# SECTION Advanced JavaScript | The language questions that separate "I use JavaScript" from "I understand the runtime".

### How does the JavaScript engine optimise your code, and how do you deoptimise it?
@short
V8 parses to bytecode, runs it in the Ignition interpreter, and profiles as it runs; hot functions get compiled to optimised machine code by TurboFan based on the types it has observed. If those assumptions are violated — a function suddenly receives a different shape of object — the code is deoptimised back to the interpreter.
@deep
The mechanism is hidden classes and inline caches. Every object gets a hidden class describing its shape, and property access sites cache the shape they have seen. A site that always sees one shape is monomorphic and compiles to a direct memory offset; a site seeing two to four shapes is polymorphic; beyond that it goes megamorphic and falls back to a hash lookup.

Adding properties in different orders, or conditionally, creates different hidden classes for objects you think are the same type. Deleting a property is worse — it forces the object into dictionary mode permanently.
@code js Shape stability — the difference is real in hot paths
// ❌ two hidden classes for what is conceptually one type
function makeA(u) { const o = {}; o.id = u.id; if (u.email) o.email = u.email; return o; }

// ✅ one shape, always: initialise every field in a consistent order
function makeB(u) { return { id: u.id, email: u.email ?? null, role: u.role ?? 'user' }; }

// ❌ delete forces dictionary mode — the object is slow forever after
delete obj.temp;
// ✅ null it out instead, or build a new object without the field
obj.temp = null;

// ❌ mixed types at one call site → polymorphic, then megamorphic
[1, 'two', {}, null].forEach(v => process(v));
@end
@senior
The honest senior framing is that this almost never matters in a web application: your bottleneck is the database, the network or the render, not V8's inline caches. It matters in genuinely hot loops — parsing a large payload, transforming a million rows, a game loop — and there the fix is usually to avoid the work rather than to micro-tune shapes. What I do apply routinely is the consistent-shape habit, because it costs nothing and also makes the code more readable and more TypeScript-friendly. I would not present this as a general optimisation strategy, and saying so is part of the right answer.
@followup
Is `for` faster than `forEach` or `map`?
@answer
Marginally, because a plain `for` loop avoids a function call per element and can be more aggressively optimised, but the difference is nanoseconds per iteration and irrelevant at typical sizes. Measured on a million-element array in a hot path it becomes visible, and there I would use a `for` loop. In application code I choose the one that expresses intent, and I optimise only what a profiler flags — anything else is guessing with worse readability as the price.
@red
- Micro-optimising V8 shapes in a CRUD app.
- Claiming `for` loops are "always faster" as a general rule.

### Explain the module resolution and bundling pipeline in a modern build.
@short
The bundler builds a module graph from the entry points, resolves each import to a file, transforms it (TypeScript stripping, JSX, PostCSS), tree-shakes unused exports, splits the graph into chunks, minifies and emits hashed filenames. Vite does this with esbuild in development for near-instant startup and Rollup for the production build.
@deep
The dev-versus-production split is the key idea in Vite. In development it serves native ES modules over HTTP with on-demand transformation — nothing is bundled, so startup does not grow with project size and HMR only touches the changed module. Dependencies are pre-bundled once with esbuild so a package with 300 internal files becomes one request.

For production it bundles with Rollup, because shipping thousands of unbundled modules would cost too many requests and lose cross-module optimisation.
@code js A production Vite config with the things that matter
export default defineConfig({
  plugins: [react(), visualizer({ filename: 'stats.html', gzipSize: true })],
  build: {
    target: 'es2022',
    sourcemap: 'hidden',                    // uploaded to Sentry, not served to users
    rollupOptions: {
      output: {
        manualChunks(id) {
          if (id.includes('node_modules')) {
            if (id.includes('react')) return 'react';       // rarely changes → long cache life
            if (id.includes('chart') || id.includes('d3')) return 'charts';  // heavy, lazy routes
            return 'vendor';
          }
        },
      },
    },
    chunkSizeWarningLimit: 300,
  },
  optimizeDeps: { include: ['react', 'react-dom'] },   // pre-bundle to avoid dev waterfalls
});

// Route-level splitting does more than any chunk tuning
const Analytics = lazy(() => import('./pages/Analytics'));
@end
@senior
When asked about a build-time reduction, the mechanism to describe is specific: Webpack with `babel-loader` and `ts-loader` re-parses and type-checks the whole graph on every change in a single-threaded JavaScript pipeline; esbuild is written in Go, parallel, and does not type-check at all, and Vite additionally avoids bundling in development entirely. So the reduction comes from three changes at once — removing Babel, removing type-checking from the hot path (moving it to a parallel `tsc --noEmit`), and removing the dev bundle step. The trade-off worth naming is that type errors no longer break the dev server, so CI must enforce them.
@followup
What is tree-shaking and why does it sometimes not work?
@answer
Tree-shaking removes exports that are provably unused, which requires static ES module imports — a CommonJS `require` or a dynamic `require(variable)` cannot be analysed. It also fails when a module has side effects the bundler cannot prove are unnecessary, which is what `"sideEffects": false` in `package.json` declares. Getting that field wrong silently drops CSS imports, and getting it missing means large libraries ship whole. The other frequent cause is importing a namespace (`import * as _ from 'lodash'`) or a package that only ships CommonJS.
@red
- Not knowing why Vite is fast in development.
- Claiming tree-shaking works with CommonJS.

### How does `AsyncLocalStorage` work and what do you use it for?
@short
`AsyncLocalStorage` provides continuation-local storage: a value stored at the start of a request remains accessible from anywhere in the async call chain, across awaits and callbacks, without being passed as a parameter. It is how request ids, tenant context and trace spans propagate without polluting every function signature.
@deep
It is built on async hooks: Node tracks the async resource chain, so a value bound with `run()` is visible to everything spawned inside that execution context. That includes promises, timers and I/O callbacks.

The alternative — threading a context object through every function — is explicit and safe but invasive; for cross-cutting concerns like logging and tracing, the implicit approach is what every observability library uses.
@code ts Request context available everywhere, with no parameter threading
import { AsyncLocalStorage } from 'node:async_hooks';

type Ctx = { requestId: string; userId?: string; tenantId?: string };
export const als = new AsyncLocalStorage<Ctx>();

app.use((req, _res, next) => {
  als.run({ requestId: req.headers['x-request-id'] ?? randomUUID(), userId: req.user?.id }, next);
});

// Deep inside a repository, five layers down — no plumbing required
export const logger = basePino.child({});
export function log(level: 'info' | 'error', msg: string, extra = {}) {
  const ctx = als.getStore();
  logger[level]({ ...ctx, ...extra }, msg);       // every line carries the request id
}

// Also how you propagate a tenant to the DB layer safely
export async function query(sql: string, params: unknown[]) {
  const { tenantId } = als.getStore() ?? {};
  const client = await pool.connect();
  try {
    if (tenantId) await client.query('SET LOCAL app.tenant_id = $1', [tenantId]);
    return await client.query(sql, params);
  } finally { client.release(); }
}
@end
@senior
The caveat I would state is that implicit context is powerful and can hide coupling — a function that silently depends on ambient state is harder to test and reason about than one that takes a parameter. So my rule is: implicit for cross-cutting infrastructure concerns (logging, tracing, request id), explicit for anything with business meaning. There is also a small performance cost from async hooks, historically significant and now modest; I would measure rather than assume. The pattern is what OpenTelemetry and Sentry use internally, which is a good indication it is the right tool for this narrow job.
@followup
What breaks `AsyncLocalStorage` context propagation?
@answer
Anything that escapes the async chain: a value captured into a long-lived object and used later from a different request, an `EventEmitter` whose listeners were registered outside the `run()` scope, some native module callbacks that do not participate in async hooks, and worker threads, which have entirely separate contexts. When context is unexpectedly `undefined`, the cause is almost always that the code path was entered from outside the `run()` — a scheduled job, a queue consumer, or a socket handler registered at startup — and those need their own `run()` wrapper.
@red
- Using it for business data instead of cross-cutting context.
- Assuming it works across worker threads.

### How do you handle very large JSON payloads efficiently?
@short
Do not parse them whole. `JSON.parse` on a 100MB body blocks the event loop for seconds and allocates several times the payload size. The options are streaming parsers, moving the work to a worker thread, pushing the transformation to the database, or — best — redesigning the API so it never sends 100MB in one response.
@deep
The specific costs are: `JSON.parse` is synchronous and blocking; the resulting object graph can be 3–10× the byte size in heap; and stringifying it back is equally expensive. On a shared Node process this means one large request stalls every other request on that instance.

Streaming parsers (`stream-json`, `JSONStream`) emit values as they are encountered so memory stays flat, at the cost of a more awkward programming model.
@code ts Streaming a huge upload instead of buffering it
import { pipeline } from 'node:stream/promises';
import StreamArray from 'stream-json/streamers/StreamArray.js';
import { parser } from 'stream-json';

// ❌ blocks the loop for seconds, allocates ~1GB heap for a 200MB body
// const items = JSON.parse(await readWholeBody(req));

// ✅ constant memory, event loop stays responsive
let inserted = 0;
const batch: Item[] = [];
await pipeline(
  req,
  parser(),
  new StreamArray(),
  new Writable({
    objectMode: true,
    async write({ value }, _enc, cb) {
      batch.push(value);
      if (batch.length >= 1000) { await repo.bulkInsert(batch.splice(0)); inserted += 1000; }
      cb();
    },
    async final(cb) { if (batch.length) await repo.bulkInsert(batch); cb(); },
  })
);
res.json({ inserted });
@end
@senior
The design-level answer is usually better than the streaming answer: a 100MB JSON request is a symptom. Bulk imports belong as a file upload to S3 plus an async job that reports progress, which removes the request timeout, the memory pressure and the retry problem in one move — and returns 202 with a job id instead of holding a connection open. If the payload must stay synchronous, I would at least bound it with a body-size limit, because an unbounded `express.json()` is a trivially exploitable denial-of-service vector.
@followup
Is there a faster JSON parser?
@answer
Yes — `JSON.parse` in V8 is already highly optimised C++, but libraries like `simdjson` bindings or `fast-json-stringify` (which compiles a serialiser from a schema) can be several times faster for serialisation specifically. Fastify uses that approach and it is a genuine win on high-throughput APIs returning consistent shapes. But the ordering matters: reduce the payload size first, then stream, then optimise the parser — swapping parsers on a 100MB body is optimising the wrong thing.
@red
- `JSON.parse` on unbounded input.
- No body size limit on the API.

### Explain event emitters, memory leaks and backpressure in event-driven code.
@short
`EventEmitter` is Node's observer implementation: `on` registers a listener, `emit` calls them synchronously in order. The two failure modes are leaks — listeners added per request and never removed — and the absence of backpressure, since `emit` has no way to slow the producer down.
@deep
Node warns at eleven listeners on one event because that usually indicates a leak, not a legitimate need. Every listener closure retains whatever it captured, so a per-request listener on a long-lived emitter retains the request context forever.

`emit` is synchronous, so a slow listener blocks the emitter and every other listener. And because there is no return signal, a fast producer emitting into a slow consumer just grows an internal queue somewhere — which is exactly why streams exist with their `drain` mechanism.
@code ts Leak, fix, and the async-consumer pattern
// ❌ leak: a listener per request on a process-lifetime emitter
app.get('/watch', (req, res) => {
  bus.on('order.updated', (o) => res.write(`data: ${JSON.stringify(o)}\n\n`));
});   // ← never removed; after 10k requests you have 10k retained closures + responses

// ✅ register and remove, tied to the connection lifetime
app.get('/watch', (req, res) => {
  const onUpdate = (o) => res.write(`data: ${JSON.stringify(o)}\n\n`);
  bus.on('order.updated', onUpdate);
  req.on('close', () => bus.off('order.updated', onUpdate));   // ← the critical line
});

// ✅ AbortSignal makes the teardown automatic and composable
const ac = new AbortController();
bus.on('order.updated', onUpdate, { signal: ac.signal });
req.on('close', () => ac.abort());

// Async consumption with backpressure, instead of firing into the void
import { on } from 'node:events';
for await (const [order] of on(bus, 'order.updated', { signal: ac.signal })) {
  await slowHandler(order);        // the loop paces itself; events queue rather than overlap
}
@end
@senior
The `{ signal }` option on `on()` is the modern idiom and worth knowing — it ties listener lifetime to an AbortController, which composes with fetch cancellation and request teardown so you have one mechanism for all cleanup. On backpressure, the point to make is that `EventEmitter` deliberately has none, so for any producer that can outpace its consumer you need a stream, a queue with a bounded buffer, or an explicit acknowledgement protocol. Fire-and-forget events between services are the same problem at a larger scale, which is why message brokers have consumer acknowledgements and prefetch limits.
@followup
Should you use events for decoupling inside a monolith?
@answer
Sparingly. In-process events do decouple modules, but they also make control flow invisible — you cannot follow the code path by reading, errors in listeners do not propagate to the emitter, and there is no retry or ordering guarantee. For side effects that must happen, I prefer an explicit call or an outbox row so failure is visible and recoverable. I use in-process events for genuinely optional concerns like analytics and cache warming, where losing one is acceptable.
@red
- Adding listeners per request without removing them.
- Assuming `emit` is asynchronous.

### How do you handle timezones, dates and money correctly?
@short
Store timestamps in UTC as `timestamptz`, transmit as ISO 8601 with an offset, and convert to the user's timezone only for display. Store money as integers in the smallest currency unit with an explicit currency code, never as floating point. Both are correctness issues that produce silent, compounding errors.
@deep
The date trap is that "a date" can mean an instant (a payment timestamp) or a calendar date (a birthday, an invoice due date). Instants belong in UTC; calendar dates should be stored as a `date` with no timezone, or they shift when read in another zone.

Recurring events are the hard case: "every Monday at 9am in Asia/Kolkata" cannot be stored as a UTC instant because daylight-saving transitions elsewhere change the offset. You store the local time plus the timezone identifier and compute occurrences.
@code ts The rules, in code
// Money: integer minor units + currency. Never Number for amounts.
type Money = { amountMinor: bigint | number; currency: 'INR' | 'USD' };
const total: Money = { amountMinor: 249900, currency: 'INR' };     // ₹2,499.00
// 0.1 + 0.2 === 0.30000000000000004  ← why floats are disqualified
const display = new Intl.NumberFormat('en-IN', { style: 'currency', currency: total.currency })
  .format(total.amountMinor / 100);

// Dates: instants in UTC, calendar dates without a zone
CREATE TABLE payments (paid_at timestamptz NOT NULL);   -- instant
CREATE TABLE users    (date_of_birth date);             -- calendar date, NO timezone

// Display in the user's zone, at the edge only
new Intl.DateTimeFormat('en-IN', { dateStyle: 'medium', timeStyle: 'short',
  timeZone: user.timezone }).format(new Date(payment.paidAt));

// Recurring: store local time + zone, expand occurrences
{ rule: 'FREQ=WEEKLY;BYDAY=MO', localTime: '09:00', timezone: 'Asia/Kolkata' }
@end
@senior
Two production stories worth having ready. Storing money as a float causes reconciliation failures that appear months later as a few paise of drift across thousands of transactions, and the fix is a migration of live financial data — expensive and risky. And storing a birthday as a `timestamptz` means a user in a different timezone sees a date one day off, which is a recurring, embarrassing bug class. I would also mention rounding policy: how you round tax and split discounts across line items must be defined and consistent, because "round each line then sum" and "sum then round" give different totals and accountants will notice.
@followup
How do you handle a report "for yesterday" across timezones?
@answer
Define whose day it is, explicitly — the customer's local day, the merchant's, or UTC — and put that decision in the API contract. Then compute the boundaries in that timezone and convert to UTC instants for the query: `WHERE paid_at >= $start AND paid_at < $end` with `$start`/`$end` derived from the zone. Postgres can do it directly with `AT TIME ZONE`, and indexing an expression on a converted timestamp is a common mistake — better to keep the index on the raw `timestamptz` and compute the bounds in application code.
@red
- Storing money as a float or a decimal string parsed with `parseFloat`.
- Storing local times without a timezone.

### What are Proxy and Reflect, and where are they used in real libraries?
@short
`Proxy` wraps an object and intercepts fundamental operations — get, set, has, deleteProperty — through a handler. `Reflect` provides the default implementations so a trap can forward correctly. They power Vue's reactivity, Immer's draft objects, MobX observables and many ORM lazy-loading implementations.
@deep
The value is transparent interception: code using the proxy cannot tell it is not the original object, so you can add change tracking, validation or lazy loading without changing call sites.

Immer is the clearest example: you mutate a draft, the proxy records every write, and it produces a new immutable object with structural sharing — unchanged subtrees keep their identity, so memoisation still works.
@code ts A change-tracking proxy — the mechanism behind Immer and MobX
function tracked<T extends object>(target: T) {
  const changes = new Map<string | symbol, unknown>();
  const proxy = new Proxy(target, {
    get(t, p, r) { return Reflect.get(t, p, r); },
    set(t, p, v, r) { changes.set(p, v); return Reflect.set(t, p, v, r); },
    deleteProperty(t, p) { changes.set(p, undefined); return Reflect.deleteProperty(t, p); },
  });
  return { proxy, changes };
}

const { proxy: user, changes } = tracked({ name: 'Ada', role: 'user' });
user.role = 'admin';
// changes → Map { 'role' => 'admin' }  →  build a minimal UPDATE, not a full-row write
await db.query('UPDATE users SET role = $1 WHERE id = $2', [changes.get('role'), id]);

// Guard against accidental reads of unloaded relations
const guarded = new Proxy(row, {
  get(t, p) {
    if (p in t) return Reflect.get(t, p);
    throw new Error(`Field "${String(p)}" was not selected — add it to the query`);
  },
});
@end
@senior
The trade-offs I would raise: proxies are meaningfully slower than plain property access, so they belong at boundaries rather than in hot loops; they break `Object.keys` assumptions if traps are inconsistent, and debugging through them is unpleasant because the debugger shows the proxy. The guarded-read pattern above is one I have found genuinely useful — it converts "this field is silently undefined because someone changed the SELECT" into a loud error at the exact access site, which is the sort of failure that otherwise ships to production and corrupts data quietly.
@followup
How does Vue's reactivity differ from React's model?
@answer
Vue 3 wraps state in a proxy and tracks which effects read which properties, so a change re-runs exactly the effects that depend on it — fine-grained and automatic. React re-renders the component and reconciles, relying on referential equality and explicit memoisation to skip work. Vue's model needs less manual optimisation; React's is simpler to reason about because rendering is a pure function of state and there is no hidden dependency graph. Signals, now appearing in Solid, Angular and Preact, bring the fine-grained model to more of the ecosystem, and React's compiler is its answer to the same problem.
@red
- Using proxies in performance-critical paths.
- Not knowing why `Reflect` exists alongside `Proxy`.

### How do you write a library that works in Node, the browser and bundlers?
@short
Ship ESM as the primary format with a CommonJS fallback, declare both in the `exports` map, ship type declarations, mark side effects, and avoid Node-only APIs in browser entry points. Test the published artefact, not the source, because packaging bugs only appear after publish.
@deep
The `exports` field is the modern mechanism and it is strict: once present, it becomes the only allowed entry surface, so deep imports that used to work will break unless you list them. Getting `types` conditions right matters for TypeScript consumers under `moduleResolution: node16`, where the wrong condition means the types resolve but the runtime does not.

Dual publishing has a real hazard — the same module loaded as both ESM and CJS produces two copies with separate state, so `instanceof` fails and singletons duplicate.
@code json A package.json that behaves correctly everywhere
{
  "name": "@acme/sdk",
  "type": "module",
  "sideEffects": false,
  "exports": {
    ".": {
      "types": "./dist/index.d.ts",
      "import": "./dist/index.js",
      "require": "./dist/index.cjs",
      "browser": "./dist/index.browser.js",
      "default": "./dist/index.js"
    },
    "./package.json": "./package.json"
  },
  "files": ["dist"],
  "peerDependencies": { "react": ">=18" },
  "peerDependenciesMeta": { "react": { "optional": true } },
  "engines": { "node": ">=20" },
  "scripts": {
    "build": "tsup src/index.ts --format esm,cjs --dts --clean",
    "check": "publint && attw --pack"
  }
}
@end
@senior
The two tools in that `check` script are the answer that shows experience: `publint` catches malformed `exports` maps and missing files, and `arethetypeswrong` catches the very common case where types resolve under one module mode and not another — problems you otherwise discover from a user's bug report. I would also emphasise peer dependencies for anything with a shared singleton (React, a state library), because bundling your own copy causes two Reacts and the "invalid hook call" error. And publishing a canary version and installing it into a real consuming app before releasing is worth the extra minute every time.
@followup
What is the dual-package hazard?
@answer
When a package ships both ESM and CJS builds and a dependency graph loads both, you get two independent module instances — separate class identities, separate module-level state, separate caches. Symptoms are `instanceof` returning false for objects that look right, and a configuration set in one place not being visible in another. The mitigations are shipping ESM only where you can, or making the CJS build a thin wrapper that re-exports the ESM build, and keeping stateful singletons out of library code entirely.
@red
- Publishing without testing the built artefact.
- Bundling React instead of declaring it as a peer dependency.

# SECTION Advanced TypeScript | Type-level tools that pay for themselves in a production codebase — and the ones that do not.

### Explain conditional types, `infer` and mapped types with a practical use.
@short
Conditional types let a type branch on another type (`T extends U ? X : Y`), `infer` captures a type from a position during that match, and mapped types transform every key of an object type. Together they let you derive types mechanically instead of maintaining parallel definitions.
@deep
`infer` is what powers `ReturnType`, `Awaited`, `Parameters` and most library type magic. Distributive conditional types are a subtlety: when the checked type is a naked type parameter and you pass a union, the condition distributes over each member — usually what you want, occasionally surprising, and suppressible by wrapping both sides in a tuple.

Mapped types with key remapping (`as`) let you rename keys, which is how you build `Getters<T>` or strip a prefix from event names.
@code ts Deriving an API client's types from the route table
const routes = {
  'GET /orders/:id':   { params: {} as { id: string }, response: {} as Order },
  'POST /orders':      { body:   {} as CreateOrderDto, response: {} as Order },
  'POST /orders/:id/refunds': { params: {} as { id: string }, body: {} as RefundDto, response: {} as Refund },
} as const;

type Routes = typeof routes;
type ResponseOf<K extends keyof Routes> = Routes[K] extends { response: infer R } ? R : never;
type BodyOf<K extends keyof Routes>     = Routes[K] extends { body: infer B } ? B : undefined;

async function call<K extends keyof Routes>(
  route: K, opts: { params?: Routes[K] extends { params: infer P } ? P : never; body?: BodyOf<K> }
): Promise<ResponseOf<K>> { /* … */ }

const order = await call('POST /orders', { body: dto });   // order: Order, body checked ✅
// await call('POST /orders', { body: { nope: 1 } });      // ❌ compile error

// Mapped type with key remapping: derive event handler props from an event map
type Events = { order_created: Order; order_refunded: Refund };
type Handlers = { [K in keyof Events as `on${Capitalize<K & string>}`]?: (e: Events[K]) => void };
// → { onOrder_created?: (e: Order) => void; onOrder_refunded?: (e: Refund) => void }
@end
@senior
The judgement I would express is about where this belongs. Type-level programming is excellent at a library or framework boundary — it buys every consumer safety with no runtime cost — and it is a liability in ordinary application code, where deeply recursive conditional types slow the compiler measurably and produce error messages nobody can read. I have seen a project where `tsc` took four minutes because of one clever generic. So: use it to make an API surface safe, keep application code boring, and measure compile time with `--extendedDiagnostics` if it starts to drag.
@followup
What is a distributive conditional type?
@answer
When a conditional type checks a naked type parameter and receives a union, it applies the condition to each member separately and unions the results — so `ToArray<string | number>` gives `string[] | number[]`, not `(string | number)[]`. That is usually the desired behaviour and it is how `Exclude` and `Extract` work. To prevent distribution you wrap both sides in a tuple: `[T] extends [U] ? … : …`, which is exactly what you need when checking whether a type *is* a union or is `never`.
@red
- Writing elaborate type gymnastics in application code.
- Not knowing why `Exclude` works on unions.

### How do you type an Express or Node application end to end?
@short
Validate at the boundary with a schema, infer types from that schema, augment `Request` for middleware-added properties, and use generics on handlers so `req.body`, `req.params` and the response type are all checked. The goal is that no `as` cast appears between the boundary and the database.
@deep
The weak points in a typical Express app are `req.body` (typed `any` by default), `req.user` (added by middleware and invisible to the type system), and the response, which `res.json` accepts as `any`. Each is fixable, and fixing all three means a schema change propagates as compile errors rather than runtime surprises.

The cleanest approach is a typed route helper that takes schemas and produces a handler whose types are inferred, which is essentially what tRPC and Fastify's type provider do.
@code ts A typed route helper — schema in, full inference out
type Handler<P, Q, B, R> = (
  req: Omit<Request, 'params' | 'query' | 'body'> & { params: P; query: Q; body: B; user: AuthUser },
  res: Omit<Response, 'json'> & { json: (body: R) => void }
) => Promise<void> | void;

export function route<P extends ZodSchema, Q extends ZodSchema, B extends ZodSchema, R>(
  spec: { params?: P; query?: Q; body?: B; response: ZodType<R> },
  handler: Handler<z.infer<P>, z.infer<Q>, z.infer<B>, R>
): RequestHandler {
  return async (req, res, next) => {
    try {
      if (spec.params) req.params = spec.params.parse(req.params);
      if (spec.query)  req.query  = spec.query.parse(req.query);
      if (spec.body)   req.body   = spec.body.parse(req.body);
      await handler(req as never, res as never);
    } catch (e) { next(e); }
  };
}

router.post('/orders/:id/refunds', authenticate, route(
  { params: z.object({ id: z.string().uuid() }), body: RefundSchema, response: RefundResponse },
  async (req, res) => {
    const refund = await refundService.execute(req.params.id, req.body, req.user);
    res.json(refund);            // ✅ response shape checked against the declared schema
  }
));
@end
@senior
The single `as never` cast inside the helper is the honest trade: you concentrate all the unsafety in one reviewed, tested place so that every call site is fully typed. That is the right shape for type-safety work generally — push the unavoidable cast to the lowest, smallest, most-tested layer. And because the same schemas generate the OpenAPI document and the client types, a field rename becomes a compile error in the frontend rather than a production 500. That end-to-end property is what makes TypeScript worth the migration cost, and it is the answer to "why did you bother?".
@followup
Would you use tRPC instead?
@answer
For a TypeScript monorepo where the same team owns both ends and there is no third-party consumer, tRPC is excellent — the client type *is* the server type, with no code generation and no drift. I would not use it for a public API, for mobile clients in another language, or where I need documented HTTP semantics, caching and status codes, because tRPC deliberately abandons REST conventions. The pragmatic combination I have seen work is tRPC for the internal web app and a versioned REST surface for anything external.
@red
- `req.body as CreateOrderDto` with no validation.
- Typing responses only on the client side.

### What are branded/nominal types and when are they worth it?
@short
TypeScript is structural, so `UserId` and `OrderId` — both strings — are interchangeable. A brand attaches a phantom property to make them distinct at compile time, so passing an order id where a user id is expected becomes an error. Worth it for identifiers, money amounts, and any value where mixing units causes real bugs.
@deep
The brand exists only in the type system; at runtime the value is still a plain string or number. Construction goes through a factory or a validator, which is what gives you the guarantee — an unvalidated string cannot become a `UserId` without passing through the check.

The same technique encodes validation state: `Email` versus `string`, `SanitisedHtml` versus `string`, `PositiveInt` versus `number`. That turns "did someone remember to validate this?" into a type question.
@code ts Branded ids and money — mixing them becomes impossible
declare const brand: unique symbol;
type Brand<T, B> = T & { readonly [brand]: B };

export type UserId  = Brand<string, 'UserId'>;
export type OrderId = Brand<string, 'OrderId'>;
export type Minor   = Brand<number, 'MinorUnits'>;     // paise/cents, never rupees/dollars

export const UserId  = (s: string): UserId  => (z.string().uuid().parse(s) as UserId);
export const OrderId = (s: string): OrderId => (z.string().uuid().parse(s) as OrderId);
export const minor   = (n: number): Minor   => (z.number().int().nonnegative().parse(n) as Minor);

function refund(orderId: OrderId, amount: Minor) { /* … */ }

const uid = UserId(req.user.id);
const oid = OrderId(req.params.id);
// refund(uid, minor(500));       // ❌ Argument of type 'UserId' is not assignable to 'OrderId'
refund(oid, minor(500));          // ✅

// Validation state in the type
type Sanitised = Brand<string, 'Sanitised'>;
const sanitise = (html: string): Sanitised => DOMPurify.sanitize(html) as Sanitised;
function render(html: Sanitised) { /* the compiler guarantees it was sanitised */ }
@end
@senior
The strongest case I have for this is money: a codebase that mixes major and minor units eventually multiplies or divides by 100 in the wrong place, and the bug surfaces as a customer charged a hundred times too much. Branding `Minor` makes the mistake a compile error. The cost is friction at boundaries — every value from the database or an API needs converting — so I apply it to the handful of types where the mistake is expensive and skip it elsewhere. Applying it universally makes the codebase tedious without proportionate benefit, and knowing where to stop is the point.
@followup
Does this have a runtime cost?
@answer
None — the brand is a phantom type erased at compile time, so the value is a plain string or number and serialises normally. The only runtime cost is whatever validation you put in the constructor, which you wanted anyway. That is the appeal: you get nominal typing's safety with zero runtime overhead, unlike wrapping values in classes, which costs allocation and breaks JSON serialisation.
@red
- Branding every type in the codebase.
- Constructing branded values with a bare `as` and no validation.

### How do you handle errors in TypeScript at scale — exceptions or Result types?
@short
Both, deliberately split: exceptions for genuinely exceptional conditions the caller cannot handle meaningfully (database down, bug, invariant violation), and a typed `Result` for expected business outcomes (invalid coupon, insufficient funds, already refunded). The second category belongs in the signature because callers must handle it.
@deep
TypeScript cannot type what a function throws, so exceptions are invisible in the type system — a caller has no way to know that `applyCoupon` can throw `ExpiredCouponError`. Returning a discriminated union makes the failure modes part of the contract and lets the compiler force exhaustive handling.

The operational benefit is signal quality: if expected failures are exceptions, your error tracker fills with business outcomes and you stop being able to see real incidents.
@code ts A Result type with exhaustive handling and a clean HTTP mapping
export type Ok<T> = { ok: true; value: T };
export type Err<E extends string, D = undefined> = { ok: false; error: E; detail?: D };
export type Result<T, E extends string, D = undefined> = Ok<T> | Err<E, D>;

type RefundError = 'ALREADY_REFUNDED' | 'NOT_PAID' | 'WINDOW_EXPIRED' | 'AMOUNT_TOO_HIGH';

async function refund(order: Order, amount: Minor): Promise<Result<Refund, RefundError>> {
  if (order.status === 'refunded')                 return { ok: false, error: 'ALREADY_REFUNDED' };
  if (order.status !== 'paid')                     return { ok: false, error: 'NOT_PAID' };
  if (daysSince(order.paidAt) > 30)                return { ok: false, error: 'WINDOW_EXPIRED' };
  if (amount > order.totalMinor)                   return { ok: false, error: 'AMOUNT_TOO_HIGH' };
  return { ok: true, value: await provider.refund(order.paymentIntentId, amount) };  // may THROW if the API is down
}

// One mapping table — the compiler demands an entry for every error code
const STATUS: Record<RefundError, number> = {
  ALREADY_REFUNDED: 409, NOT_PAID: 409, WINDOW_EXPIRED: 422, AMOUNT_TOO_HIGH: 422,
};

const r = await refund(order, amount);
if (!r.ok) return res.status(STATUS[r.error]).json({ error: r.error });
res.json(r.value);
@end
@senior
The detail that makes this work at scale is `Record<RefundError, number>`: adding a new error variant becomes a compile error at the mapping table, so you cannot ship a new failure mode that returns 500 by accident. That is the same exhaustiveness argument as discriminated unions generally, applied to the HTTP boundary. I would also note the pragmatic limit — a full functional error-handling library (neverthrow, Effect) buys composition operators but imposes a style on the whole team, so I would introduce a plain union type first and only adopt a library if the team genuinely wants the ergonomics.
@followup
How do you handle errors from a third-party SDK?
@answer
Wrap it at the adapter layer and translate: catch the provider's error types, map the ones I can act on into my domain result variants (card declined, rate limited, invalid request), and rethrow the rest as an infrastructure exception. That keeps provider-specific error shapes out of my business logic, which matters when there are three payment providers with three completely different error taxonomies. The adapter is also where I attach retry classification — which errors are transient and safe to retry, and which are terminal.
@red
- Throwing for ordinary validation failures.
- `catch (e: any)` and reading `e.message` without narrowing.

### How do you keep TypeScript build and editor performance acceptable?
@short
Measure with `tsc --extendedDiagnostics` and `--generateTrace`, avoid deeply recursive conditional types, use project references in a monorepo, keep `skipLibCheck` on, and let esbuild or SWC handle transpilation while `tsc --noEmit` type-checks in parallel. Most slowness traces to one or two clever types or an unbounded `include`.
@deep
Type checking is whole-program and single-threaded, so it grows superlinearly with type complexity rather than with line count. The specific costs are large unions (especially string-literal unions from generated code), recursive conditional types, and heavy inference on large object literals.

Project references split a monorepo into independently checked units with `.tsbuildinfo` caches, so an unchanged package is not rechecked — the difference on a large repo is minutes.
@code bash Finding what is actually slow
tsc --noEmit --extendedDiagnostics
#  Files:                  2,431
#  Types:                418,220        ← the number that matters
#  Instantiations:     14,882,110       ← >10M means a type is being instantiated pathologically
#  Check time:              84.2s

tsc --noEmit --generateTrace ./trace
npx @typescript/analyze-trace ./trace
#  → "Check file src/api/routes.ts (41.2s)"  → points at the exact type

# Monorepo: project references + incremental
{ "references": [{ "path": "../shared" }, { "path": "../db" }], "compilerOptions": { "composite": true } }
tsc --build --incremental        # unchanged packages are skipped entirely

# Editor: exclude what you never edit
{ "exclude": ["**/dist", "**/coverage", "**/.next", "**/node_modules"] }
@end
@senior
The pattern I would describe is caching the type rather than recomputing it: assigning a complex derived type to a named alias, or explicitly annotating a return type instead of relying on inference, can cut instantiations by an order of magnitude because the compiler stops re-deriving it at every use. The other big one is generated types — an OpenAPI client with a 4,000-member union will slow every file that touches it, so I split generated types by domain. And I always keep `skipLibCheck: true` unless there is a specific reason not to; checking every `.d.ts` in `node_modules` is pure cost.
@followup
Should `tsc` block the dev server?
@answer
No. Transpile with esbuild or SWC so the dev loop is instant, and run `tsc --noEmit --watch` as a separate process plus in the editor — you still see every type error immediately, just not in the critical path of a hot reload. The rule that makes this safe is that CI must run `tsc --noEmit` as a required check, otherwise a type error can reach main. That split is precisely what makes a Vite migration feel dramatically faster without giving up type safety.
@red
- Blaming TypeScript for slow builds without profiling.
- A single tsconfig covering an entire monorepo.

### How do you share types between frontend, backend and mobile?
@short
Generate them from one source of truth rather than duplicating. In a monorepo, a shared package exporting Zod schemas gives both validation and inferred types. Across repos or languages, generate from OpenAPI. Hand-written parallel interfaces always drift, and the drift is only discovered in production.
@deep
The monorepo approach with workspaces is the strongest when everything is TypeScript: `@acme/contracts` exports schemas, the API imports them for validation, the web app imports the inferred types, and a version bump is a compile error everywhere affected.

Across languages you need a wire-format contract: OpenAPI for REST, or protobuf if you want generated clients in Kotlin/Swift/Go as well. The generation must run in CI so nobody forgets.
@code ts A contracts package used by three consumers
// packages/contracts/src/orders.ts
export const CreateOrder = z.object({
  items: z.array(z.object({ sku: z.string(), qty: z.number().int().positive() })).min(1),
  currency: z.enum(['INR', 'USD']),
});
export const OrderResponse = z.object({
  id: z.string().uuid(), status: z.enum(['pending','paid','refunded']),
  totalMinor: z.number().int(), currency: z.enum(['INR','USD']),
});
export type CreateOrder   = z.infer<typeof CreateOrder>;
export type OrderResponse = z.infer<typeof OrderResponse>;

// apps/api — validates with it
router.post('/orders', validate({ body: CreateOrder }), …);

// apps/web — types the mutation with it, no duplication
const { mutate } = useMutation<OrderResponse, ApiError, CreateOrder>({ mutationFn: (b) => api.post('/orders', b) });

// apps/mobile — same package; and for a non-TS client, generate from the derived OpenAPI:
//   pnpm gen:openapi && npx openapi-generator-cli generate -g kotlin -i openapi.json
@end
@senior
The organisational point matters as much as the technical one: the contracts package must have a clear owner and a review process, because it is now the interface between teams and a careless change breaks everyone at once. I enforce that with a breaking-change check (`oasdiff` on the generated spec) in CI, so removing a field or narrowing a type fails the build unless it is an intentional version bump. Without that gate, a shared package becomes a shared foot-gun — everyone can change it and nobody notices until the mobile app crashes.
@followup
What if the backend is in Go and the frontend in TypeScript?
@answer
Then the contract must be language-neutral: OpenAPI generated from the Go handlers (via struct tags and a tool like swaggo), with TypeScript types generated from that spec in CI. The Go structs remain the source of truth, and drift is impossible because the frontend types are derived, not written. For internal service-to-service traffic I would use protobuf and gRPC instead, which gives generated clients for both languages and a stricter schema evolution story.
@red
- Maintaining parallel interfaces by hand on both sides.
- Generating types locally and committing them without a CI check.

### What are the TypeScript patterns for dependency injection in Node?
@short
Constructor injection with interfaces, wired in a composition root at startup. You do not need a DI framework — a factory function taking a dependencies object gives you the same testability with less magic. Frameworks like NestJS or tsyringe are worth it when the graph is large and the team wants convention.
@deep
The point of DI is not the container; it is that a module declares what it needs and receives it, rather than importing a concrete implementation. That is what makes it testable with a fake and swappable per environment.

TypeScript interfaces are erased at runtime, so a container cannot resolve by interface — it needs a class token or a symbol. That constraint is why Nest injects classes and why manual wiring is often simpler in plain TypeScript.
@code ts Manual composition root — no framework, fully typed, fully testable
export interface OrderRepository { insert(o: NewOrder, tx?: Tx): Promise<Order>; findById(id: OrderId): Promise<Order | null>; }
export interface PaymentProvider { createIntent(o: Order): Promise<Intent>; refund(id: string, amt: Minor): Promise<Refund>; }
export interface Clock { now(): Date; }

export function createOrderService(deps: {
  orders: OrderRepository; payments: PaymentProvider; outbox: Outbox; clock: Clock;
}) {
  return {
    async create(userId: UserId, dto: CreateOrder) {
      const totals = priceOrder(dto.items);                  // pure
      return withTransaction(async (tx) => {
        const order = await deps.orders.insert({ userId, ...totals, createdAt: deps.clock.now() }, tx);
        await deps.outbox.publish('order.created', { orderId: order.id }, tx);
        return order;
      });
    },
  };
}
export type OrderService = ReturnType<typeof createOrderService>;

// composition root — the ONLY place that knows about concrete implementations
const orderService = createOrderService({
  orders: new PgOrderRepository(pool),
  payments: providers.get(env.DEFAULT_PROVIDER)!,
  outbox: new PgOutbox(pool),
  clock: { now: () => new Date() },
});

// test — no database, no network, deterministic time
const svc = createOrderService({ orders: new InMemoryOrders(), payments: fakeProvider, outbox: fakeOutbox,
                                 clock: { now: () => new Date('2026-01-01T00:00:00Z') } });
@end
@senior
The `Clock` dependency is the small detail that pays off constantly: it makes every time-dependent rule — expiry windows, retention, refund eligibility — testable without freezing the system clock or sleeping. More generally, the composition root pattern means there is exactly one file that knows how the application is assembled, which is enormously useful when onboarding someone or when swapping an implementation. I would only reach for a DI container when the graph gets large enough that manual wiring becomes a hundred lines of boilerplate, and I would say that explicitly rather than adopting a framework by default.
@followup
When is NestJS's DI worth the overhead?
@answer
When the team is large and benefits from enforced structure, when you have many modules with overlapping dependencies, or when you want the ecosystem — guards, interceptors, pipes, generated OpenAPI — as a package. The cost is `reflect-metadata`, decorator-based indirection that makes stack traces less direct, and a heavier learning curve. For a small team on a focused service, structured Express with manual wiring gives the same architectural benefits with less machinery, and I would defend that choice on maintainability grounds rather than framework preference.
@red
- Importing the database module directly inside business logic.
- Adopting a DI container without needing one.

### How do you migrate a large JavaScript codebase to TypeScript?
@short
Incrementally, with the build green at every step: enable `allowJs` and `checkJs` off, rename files module by module starting at the leaves, keep `strict: false` initially, then turn on `noImplicitAny` and `strictNullChecks` in that order. Ban new `any` with a lint rule while allowing legacy ones, and measure the burn-down.
@deep
The order matters because `strictNullChecks` produces the largest error count and the highest value — it finds the actual `undefined` bugs. Turning it on across a large codebase at once produces thousands of errors and stalls; turning it on per-directory with `// @ts-strict-ignore` or per-package in a monorepo keeps it tractable.

Third-party types are the other friction: some packages ship none, so you write a minimal local `.d.ts` shim rather than blocking on a perfect one.
@code json A migration in phases you can actually ship
// Phase 1 — compile everything, check nothing. Build stays green.
{ "compilerOptions": { "allowJs": true, "checkJs": false, "strict": false, "noEmit": true } }

// Phase 2 — rename leaves to .ts. Add a lint rule that stops the bleeding:
//   "@typescript-eslint/no-explicit-any": ["error", { "fixToUnknown": false }]
//   with existing violations baselined, so only NEW any is blocked.

// Phase 3 — the highest-value flag, enabled per area
{ "compilerOptions": { "noImplicitAny": true } }
{ "compilerOptions": { "strictNullChecks": true } }   // ← finds the real bugs

// Phase 4 — full strict + extras
{ "compilerOptions": { "strict": true, "noUncheckedIndexedAccess": true } }

// Track progress so it does not stall:
//   npx type-coverage --detail   → "92.4% type coverage (was 61.2%)"
@end
@senior
The thing I would emphasise is that the migration must produce visible value early, or it gets deprioritised. So I start with the highest-risk modules — payments, auth, anything touching money — rather than alphabetically, and I pair the migration with schema validation at the boundaries so the types are actually true rather than asserted. The metric I report is not "percentage of files converted" but the reduction in a specific class of production errors in Sentry, because that is what justifies the time to a product manager. And `type-coverage` is more honest than file count, since a file full of `any` is converted in name only.
@followup
How do you handle a dependency with no types?
@answer
Check DefinitelyTyped first (`@types/x`). If nothing exists, write a minimal local declaration covering only the functions you actually use, in a `types/` directory included by tsconfig — a ten-line shim is far better than `any`, and it documents the surface you depend on. If the package is important, contributing the types upstream is worth the hour. What I avoid is `declare module 'x';` with no body, because that silently types everything as `any` and gives you false confidence.
@red
- A big-bang rewrite that blocks feature work for weeks.
- Converting files by adding `any` everywhere and calling it done.

# SECTION React Rendering, Reconciliation & Performance | The deepest frontend round. Answer these with mechanism, then with measurement.

### How does React's reconciliation and Fiber architecture actually work?
@short
Fiber is a linked-list representation of the component tree where each node holds the work to be done. Rendering happens in two phases: a render phase that builds a work-in-progress tree and can be interrupted, and a commit phase that applies DOM mutations synchronously. That split is what makes concurrent features possible.
@deep
Before Fiber, reconciliation was a recursive traversal that could not be paused — a large tree blocked the main thread until finished. Fiber breaks the work into units and yields between them, checking whether something more urgent has arrived.

The render phase must be pure and repeatable, because React may run it, throw the result away, and run it again with a higher-priority update. That is why StrictMode double-invokes components in development: it surfaces impure render logic that would break under concurrency.

The commit phase is not interruptible — DOM mutations, refs and layout effects run in one synchronous pass, so the user never sees a partially updated screen.
@arch The two phases and where your code runs
  UPDATE (setState, context change, props)
      │
      ▼
  ┌── RENDER PHASE (interruptible, may run 2× or be discarded) ───────────┐
  │  • your component functions run                                       │
  │  • useMemo / useState initialisers / useReducer reducers run          │
  │  • diff against current tree → build work-in-progress Fiber tree      │
  │  ⚠ must be PURE: no mutations, no subscriptions, no side effects      │
  └──────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                             │ (React yields to the browser between units)
                             ▼
  ┌── COMMIT PHASE (synchronous, never interrupted) ──────────────────────┐
  │  mutation: DOM inserts/updates/deletes, ref detach                    │
  │  layout:   useLayoutEffect + ref attach   ← blocks paint              │
  │  ──── browser paints ────                                             │
  │  passive:  useEffect                       ← after paint              │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
@end
@senior
The practical consequence I would draw out is why "my effect runs twice" is a feature, not a bug: StrictMode's double render and double effect-with-cleanup in development is React proving that your component can be mounted, unmounted and remounted safely — which is exactly what happens under Offscreen rendering and future concurrent features. Components that break under it have a real defect: a subscription without cleanup, a counter incremented during render, or an effect that assumes it runs exactly once. Treating it as noise and disabling StrictMode is how you ship a bug that only appears under load.
@followup
What decides whether React re-uses a component instance or creates a new one?
@answer
Type and position, plus the key. If the element type at a position changes — `<div>` to `<span>`, or `ComponentA` to `ComponentB` — React unmounts the old subtree entirely and mounts a new one, destroying state. If the type is the same, it reconciles and keeps state, matching children by key. This is also why defining a component inside another component's body is a serious bug: it creates a new function identity every render, so React sees a different type and remounts the whole subtree, losing state and refetching data on every keystroke.
@red
- Describing the Virtual DOM without knowing what Fiber added.
- Disabling StrictMode to silence double effects.

### What are `useTransition`, `useDeferredValue` and concurrent rendering for?
@short
They let you mark an update as non-urgent so React can interrupt it to keep the interface responsive. `useTransition` wraps a state update you initiate; `useDeferredValue` lags a value you receive. Both keep typing and clicking at full speed while an expensive derived render catches up.
@deep
Without them, every update has the same priority, so a keystroke that triggers filtering 20,000 rows blocks the input until the render finishes. Marking the expensive update as a transition tells React to render it in the background, abandon it if a newer input arrives, and keep the urgent update (the input's own value) immediate.

`isPending` gives you a way to show a subtle loading state without a jarring spinner, and React deliberately keeps showing the previous content rather than a fallback during a transition.
@code tsx Transition versus deferred value — when each fits
// useTransition: YOU trigger the update and can mark it non-urgent
function TabbedReport() {
  const [tab, setTab] = useState('summary');
  const [isPending, startTransition] = useTransition();

  const select = (next: string) => {
    startTransition(() => setTab(next));      // heavy tab content renders in the background
  };
  return (
    <>
      <TabBar value={tab} onChange={select} pending={isPending} />
      <div style={{ opacity: isPending ? 0.7 : 1 }}><ReportBody tab={tab} /></div>
    </>
  );
}

// useDeferredValue: the value ARRIVES from elsewhere (props / controlled input)
function Search({ items }) {
  const [q, setQ] = useState('');
  const dq = useDeferredValue(q);                       // lags under load
  const results = useMemo(() => heavyFilter(items, dq), [items, dq]);
  return (<>
    <input value={q} onChange={e => setQ(e.target.value)} />   {/* never blocked */}
    <ResultList results={results} stale={q !== dq} />
  </>);
}
@end
@senior
The important caveat is that these do not make the work cheaper — they reprioritise it. If `heavyFilter` takes 400ms, it still takes 400ms; the input just stays responsive while it happens. So the first fix is still to make the work smaller: filter on the server, virtualise the list, or memoise properly. I use transitions on top of that, for the residual cost. I would also mention that a transition combined with Suspense is what powers instant navigation in the App Router — the old page stays visible while the new one loads, instead of flashing a skeleton.
@followup
What is `useOptimistic` for?
@answer
It shows the expected result of a mutation immediately and reconciles when the server responds — a message appears in the chat the instant you press send, and rolls back or corrects if the request fails. It removes the perceived latency of a round trip, which is the single biggest perceived-performance win in interactive apps. The requirements are that the optimistic state is derivable locally and that failure is handled visibly, because silently reverting confuses users more than a brief spinner would.
@red
- Using transitions instead of fixing genuinely expensive work.
- Wrapping urgent updates like text input in a transition.

### How do you diagnose a slow React application methodically?
@short
Separate load performance from runtime performance. For load: Lighthouse, the network waterfall and a bundle analysis. For runtime: the React Profiler flame graph, "highlight updates" and the browser Performance panel for long tasks. Then fix the specific measured cause rather than applying memoisation broadly.
@deep
The React Profiler shows commit duration per component and why each rendered — props changed, state changed, parent rendered, or context changed. That last distinction is what tells you whether memoisation would even help: a component re-rendering because of context cannot be fixed by `React.memo`.

For interaction latency, the browser's Performance panel with the Interactions track shows INP directly and attributes it to the long task responsible.
@code js Measuring in production, not just in the lab
import { onLCP, onINP, onCLS, onTTFB } from 'web-vitals';

const report = (metric) => navigator.sendBeacon('/rum', JSON.stringify({
  name: metric.name, value: metric.value, rating: metric.rating, id: metric.id,
  // attribution tells you WHICH element/interaction was responsible
  target: metric.attribution?.interactionTarget ?? metric.attribution?.element,
  route: location.pathname, connection: navigator.connection?.effectiveType,
}));

onLCP(report); onINP(report); onCLS(report); onTTFB(report);

// In-app profiling for a specific subtree
<Profiler id="OrderTable" onRender={(id, phase, actualMs, baseMs) => {
  if (actualMs > 16) console.warn(`${id} ${phase} took ${actualMs.toFixed(1)}ms`);
}}>
  <OrderTable rows={rows} />
</Profiler>
@end
@senior
Real-user monitoring is the point I would push. Lab measurements on a fast laptop with a fibre connection systematically understate the problem; the p75 of real users on mid-range Android over 4G is what Core Web Vitals actually grades and what the business feels. Attribution data is what makes it actionable — knowing that INP is bad is useless, knowing that it is bad on the checkout button because of a 380ms long task in a third-party analytics script is a fix. I would also set a performance budget in CI (bundle size, Lighthouse score on a fixed profile) so regressions are caught at the pull request rather than discovered a month later.
@followup
Why is my Lighthouse score good but real users complain?
@answer
Lighthouse is a lab test on a simulated device with a cold cache and no third-party variability; real users have slower devices, worse networks, browser extensions, and hit different routes with different data volumes. Lighthouse also measures TBT rather than INP, and INP is what correlates with "feels slow". The resolution is always the same: instrument real users with `web-vitals`, segment by device and connection, and trust the p75 field data over the lab number.
@red
- Optimising based on a Lighthouse score alone.
- Not knowing why a component re-rendered.

### What causes a React memory leak and how do you find it?
@short
Retained references after unmount: subscriptions and event listeners without cleanup, timers that keep running, closures captured by a long-lived object, and caches that grow without bound. I find them with Chrome's heap snapshot comparison — mount and unmount a route repeatedly and look for detached DOM nodes and growing component counts.
@deep
Detached DOM nodes are the clearest signal: an element removed from the document but still referenced by JavaScript, usually by an event listener or a ref stored in a module-level structure. The retainer path in DevTools points directly at the holder.

In React specifically, the common cases are: an effect that subscribes but does not return a cleanup, a `setInterval` never cleared, a WebSocket kept in a module-level map keyed by component instance, and a global store that accumulates per-route state and never prunes it.
@code tsx The three leaks and their fixes
// ❌ interval keeps running after unmount, and holds the closure alive
useEffect(() => { setInterval(() => refetch(), 5000); }, []);
// ✅
useEffect(() => { const id = setInterval(refetch, 5000); return () => clearInterval(id); }, [refetch]);

// ❌ listener retains the component's closure forever
useEffect(() => { window.addEventListener('resize', onResize); }, []);
// ✅ one AbortController tears down everything at once
useEffect(() => {
  const ac = new AbortController();
  window.addEventListener('resize', onResize, { signal: ac.signal });
  document.addEventListener('visibilitychange', onVis, { signal: ac.signal });
  socket.addEventListener('message', onMsg, { signal: ac.signal });
  return () => ac.abort();
}, []);

// ❌ module-level cache grows for the lifetime of the tab
const cache = new Map();
// ✅ bound it, or let the query library own caching with gcTime
new QueryClient({ defaultOptions: { queries: { gcTime: 5 * 60_000, staleTime: 30_000 } } });
@end
@senior
The `AbortController` with `{ signal }` is the idiom I would highlight, because it unifies teardown for listeners, fetches and any API that accepts a signal — one cleanup path instead of four, and it is much harder to forget one. On the diagnosis side, the technique that finds these reliably is: snapshot, navigate into and out of the suspect route twenty times, force garbage collection, snapshot again, and compare — a linear growth in `Detached HTMLDivElement` or in a component's instance count is conclusive. Single-page apps that people leave open all day are where these leaks actually hurt, and dashboards are the classic case.
@followup
Does React Query or SWR leak memory?
@answer
Not by default — both garbage-collect inactive query data after `gcTime` (five minutes by default). Leaks come from misuse: query keys built from unstable objects create unlimited distinct cache entries, `gcTime: Infinity` retains everything, and subscriptions inside `queryFn` that are never torn down. The fix is stable, serialisable query keys and letting the library manage lifetime rather than holding results in your own module-level structures.
@red
- Effects with subscriptions and no cleanup.
- Blaming React for what is an application-level retention bug.

### How do you architect a large React application?
@short
Feature-sliced folders rather than technical layers, a clear boundary between server state and client state, shared UI primitives in a design-system package, and strict rules about which layer may import which. The goal is that deleting a feature is deleting a folder, and that a new engineer can find code by product concept.
@deep
The failure mode of technical-layer folders (`components/`, `hooks/`, `utils/`) is that at 300 files nobody can tell which pieces belong together, and every change touches five directories. Feature slicing keeps the blast radius local.

Import rules matter as much as folders: features may import from `shared`, never from each other directly; cross-feature communication goes through a defined public API or a route. `eslint-plugin-boundaries` or `dependency-cruiser` can enforce that mechanically.
@arch A structure that scales past 500 files
  src/
  ├── app/                    routing, providers, global error boundary
  ├── features/
  │   ├── orders/
  │   │   ├── api/            queries + mutations (the ONLY place fetching orders)
  │   │   ├── components/     order-specific UI
  │   │   ├── hooks/          useOrderFilters, useRefundFlow
  │   │   ├── model/          types, zod schemas, pure business logic
  │   │   └── index.ts        ← public API of the feature; nothing else is importable
  │   ├── payments/
  │   └── auth/
  ├── shared/
  │   ├── ui/                 Button, Table, Modal — no business logic
  │   ├── lib/                http client, formatters, date/money helpers
  │   └── config/             env, feature flags
  └── pages|routes/           thin composition of features

  Enforced rules (dependency-cruiser):
    features/* → shared/*         ✅
    features/a → features/b       ❌  (go through app/ or an event)
    shared/*   → features/*       ❌  (shared must not know about features)
@end
@senior
The rule I care most about is that data fetching for a domain lives in exactly one place. When three components each call `/api/orders` with slightly different parameters, you get inconsistent cache keys, duplicate requests and divergent error handling — and no single place to add pagination or change the endpoint. Centralising in `features/orders/api` also makes the query-key strategy consistent, which is what makes invalidation correct. Enforcing the boundaries with a linter rather than a wiki page is the difference between an architecture and an aspiration.
@followup
Monorepo or separate repos for web, mobile and API?
@answer
Monorepo when the same team ships all three and they share contracts, because atomic cross-cutting changes and shared types are worth a lot. The costs are tooling complexity, longer CI unless you use affected-project detection (Nx, Turborepo), and the temptation to couple things that should stay independent. Separate repos when teams and release cadences are genuinely independent — then the contract must be explicit and versioned, which is healthier at that scale. I would not migrate an organisation to a monorepo for its own sake; the question is whether changes routinely span the boundaries.
@red
- Folders by technical type at large scale.
- Fetching the same resource from five different components.

### How do you build a design system or component library?
@short
Start with tokens (colour, spacing, typography, radii) as the single source of truth, build unstyled accessible primitives on top (Radix or React Aria), then compose branded components. Document with Storybook, test interaction and accessibility, and version it as a package so consumers upgrade deliberately.
@deep
The highest-value decision is not to reimplement accessible behaviour. A dropdown, dialog, combobox or date picker involves focus trapping, keyboard navigation, ARIA roles and screen-reader announcements — getting that right is months of work, and headless libraries have already done it. You supply the styling.

Composition beats configuration: a component with 25 boolean props becomes unmaintainable, whereas compound components (`<Dialog.Root>`, `<Dialog.Trigger>`, `<Dialog.Content>`) let consumers compose what they need.
@code tsx Tokens → primitive → branded component, with variants
// tokens.css — one source of truth, themeable
:root { --color-brand-600:#4338ca; --space-3:12px; --radius-md:8px; --font-sans:'Inter',sans-serif; }
[data-theme='dark'] { --color-brand-600:#818cf8; }

// Variant API with type-safe props (cva / tailwind-variants)
const button = cva('inline-flex items-center rounded-md font-medium transition disabled:opacity-50', {
  variants: {
    intent: { primary: 'bg-brand-600 text-white hover:bg-brand-700',
              danger:  'bg-red-600 text-white hover:bg-red-700',
              ghost:   'bg-transparent hover:bg-slate-100' },
    size:   { sm: 'h-8 px-3 text-sm', md: 'h-10 px-4', lg: 'h-12 px-6 text-lg' },
  },
  defaultVariants: { intent: 'primary', size: 'md' },
});

export interface ButtonProps
  extends React.ButtonHTMLAttributes<HTMLButtonElement>, VariantProps<typeof button> {
  loading?: boolean;
}
export const Button = forwardRef<HTMLButtonElement, ButtonProps>(
  ({ intent, size, loading, children, ...rest }, ref) => (
    <button ref={ref} className={button({ intent, size })} aria-busy={loading}
            disabled={rest.disabled || loading} {...rest}>
      {loading && <Spinner aria-hidden />}{children}
    </button>
  ));
@end
@senior
Two points that show production experience. First, `forwardRef` and spreading the rest props are not optional in a library component — without them, consumers cannot attach refs (needed by tooltips, focus management and headless libraries) or pass `aria-*`, `data-testid` and event handlers, and they will fork your component instead. Second, the design system needs governance: a contribution process, a deprecation policy, visual regression tests (Chromatic or Playwright screenshots), and semantic versioning, or teams will diverge and you end up with three button components anyway. The technical part is the easier half.
@followup
How do you handle theming and dark mode?
@answer
CSS custom properties swapped by a `data-theme` attribute on the root, with the user's preference stored in a cookie so the server can render the correct theme and avoid a flash of the wrong colours. `prefers-color-scheme` provides the default when no explicit choice exists. Doing it with CSS variables rather than duplicating component styles means one definition per token and instant switching with no re-render, and it also supports per-brand theming in a multi-tenant product by swapping the token layer only.
@red
- Reimplementing an accessible dropdown from scratch.
- Components that do not forward refs or props.

### How do you make a React app accessible?
@short
Semantic HTML first — real buttons, headings in order, labels on inputs — then keyboard operability for everything, visible focus, correct ARIA only where semantics are missing, and adequate contrast. Test with keyboard only, with a screen reader, and with axe in CI.
@deep
The first rule of ARIA is not to use ARIA when a native element does the job: `<button>` gives you focus, keyboard activation and the correct role for free, while `<div onClick>` gives you none of them and needs `role`, `tabIndex` and key handlers to approximate it badly.

The React-specific issues are focus management on route change and modal open, announcing dynamic content with live regions, and forms where the error message must be programmatically associated with the input.
@code tsx The four things that fix most real accessibility bugs
// 1. Inputs: label association + error announcement
const id = useId();
<label htmlFor={id}>Refund amount</label>
<input id={id} aria-invalid={!!error} aria-describedby={error ? `${id}-err` : undefined} />
{error && <p id={`${id}-err`} role="alert">{error}</p>}

// 2. Route change: move focus and announce, or screen-reader users are lost
useEffect(() => { headingRef.current?.focus(); }, [pathname]);
<h1 ref={headingRef} tabIndex={-1}>{title}</h1>

// 3. Async status: a live region announces without stealing focus
<div role="status" aria-live="polite" className="sr-only">
  {isLoading ? 'Loading orders' : `${orders.length} orders loaded`}
</div>

// 4. Modals: focus trap + restore + escape (use a headless lib rather than hand-rolling)
<Dialog.Root open={open} onOpenChange={setOpen}>
  <Dialog.Content aria-labelledby="t"><Dialog.Title id="t">Confirm refund</Dialog.Title>…</Dialog.Content>
</Dialog.Root>
@end
@senior
The point I would make is that accessibility is largely a rendering-correctness problem, not a separate workstream: if you use semantic elements and label everything, most of it comes free, and the remaining work is focus management around dynamic UI. I put `jest-axe` on component tests and `@axe-core/playwright` on key journeys in CI so regressions fail the build — automated tools catch roughly a third of issues, so I also do a keyboard-only pass on any new flow. Beyond compliance, it correlates with quality: the same structure that helps a screen reader makes the DOM testable with `getByRole` and makes the markup meaningful.
@followup
What are the most common accessibility failures you find?
@answer
Clickable `<div>`s that keyboards cannot reach, missing or placeholder-only form labels, insufficient colour contrast on secondary text and disabled states, images with no alt text or decorative images not marked `alt=""`, focus outlines removed for aesthetics, and modals without focus trapping so tabbing escapes behind the overlay. Every one of those is cheap to fix during development and expensive to retrofit across a whole application, which is the argument for catching them in CI from the start.
@red
- Adding ARIA roles to compensate for non-semantic markup.
- `outline: none` with no replacement focus style.

### How do you handle forms with complex validation and multi-step flows?
@short
React Hook Form for uncontrolled inputs and subscription-based re-rendering, Zod for the schema including cross-field rules, and per-step schemas for a wizard so users see only the errors relevant to the current step. Persist the draft so a refresh or a crash does not lose their work.
@deep
The performance argument is concrete: a controlled 40-field form re-renders the entire tree on every keystroke; React Hook Form registers inputs by ref and only re-renders components that subscribe to the specific field via `useWatch`.

Cross-field validation lives in the schema with `refine`/`superRefine`, so the same rules run on the server. Async validation — "is this email already taken?" — needs debouncing and must be re-verified server-side at submission, because the answer can change between check and submit.
@code tsx A multi-step form with per-step schemas and draft persistence
const StepSchemas = {
  1: z.object({ email: z.string().email(), phone: z.string().regex(/^\+?\d{10,14}$/) }),
  2: z.object({ line1: z.string().min(3), city: z.string().min(2), pin: z.string().length(6) }),
  3: z.object({ cardHolder: z.string().min(2), saveCard: z.boolean().default(false) }),
} as const;

const Full = StepSchemas[1].merge(StepSchemas[2]).merge(StepSchemas[3])
  .superRefine((v, ctx) => {                       // cross-field / cross-step rules
    if (v.saveCard && !v.email)
      ctx.addIssue({ code: 'custom', path: ['email'], message: 'Email required to save a card' });
  });

function Wizard() {
  const [step, setStep] = useState(1);
  const form = useForm({ resolver: zodResolver(StepSchemas[step]), mode: 'onBlur',
                         defaultValues: loadDraft() });

  // persist the draft — a refresh or a crash must not lose 3 steps of typing
  useEffect(() => {
    const sub = form.watch(debounce((v) => localStorage.setItem('draft', JSON.stringify(v)), 500));
    return () => sub.unsubscribe();
  }, [form]);

  const next = form.handleSubmit(() => setStep(s => s + 1));
  const submit = form.handleSubmit(async (v) => {
    const parsed = Full.parse(v);                  // full validation before sending
    await api.post('/checkout', parsed);
    localStorage.removeItem('draft');
  });
}
@end
@senior
Two details I would add. `mode: 'onBlur'` rather than `onChange` is a deliberate UX choice: validating on every keystroke shows an "invalid email" error while the user is still typing the first character, which feels hostile. And draft persistence is the feature users notice — a checkout that survives an accidental refresh converts better, and it costs ten lines. On the server side, the same Zod schema validates the final payload, because every client-side rule is advisory; the difference between the two is that the client's job is guidance and the server's job is enforcement.
@followup
How do you handle file uploads inside a form?
@answer
Upload asynchronously as soon as the file is selected, to a presigned URL, showing progress — do not hold the bytes until submit, because a large file plus a form post is a slow, fragile request that can time out. The form then carries only the resulting object key. Validate size and type on selection for immediate feedback, and again on the server from the file's magic bytes, because client-side checks are trivially bypassed.
@red
- Fully controlled inputs on a large form.
- Validating only on the client.

### How do you implement optimistic updates correctly?
@short
Apply the expected result to the cache immediately, keep the previous value, roll back on error, and revalidate on settle so the server remains the source of truth. The pattern must also handle concurrent mutations — cancel in-flight refetches before writing, or a stale response overwrites your optimistic state.
@deep
The subtle part is ordering. If a background refetch is in flight when you apply the optimistic update, its response can land afterwards and revert the UI. TanStack Query's `cancelQueries` in `onMutate` exists precisely for that.

Rollback needs the snapshot taken before the write, and it must restore exactly — which is why you capture the whole cache entry rather than trying to reverse the operation.
@code tsx The complete optimistic mutation, including the parts people omit
const qc = useQueryClient();

const toggleFavourite = useMutation({
  mutationFn: ({ id, next }) => api.patch(`/items/${id}`, { favourite: next }),

  onMutate: async ({ id, next }) => {
    await qc.cancelQueries({ queryKey: ['items'] });          // 1. stop in-flight refetches
    const previous = qc.getQueryData(['items']);              // 2. snapshot for rollback
    qc.setQueryData(['items'], (old) =>                       // 3. optimistic write
      old?.map(i => i.id === id ? { ...i, favourite: next } : i));
    return { previous };
  },

  onError: (_err, _vars, ctx) => {
    qc.setQueryData(['items'], ctx?.previous);                // 4. exact rollback
    toast.error('Could not update — please try again');       // 5. TELL the user
  },

  onSettled: () => qc.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: ['items'] }),  // 6. reconcile with the server
});
@end
@senior
The step people skip is telling the user when the rollback happens. A silently reverted UI is worse than no optimism at all, because the user believes their action succeeded and only discovers otherwise later — in a payments or messaging context that is a trust problem.
