My rule is: optimistic updates for actions that almost always succeed and are cheap to reverse (likes, toggles, reordering, adding a comment), never for actions with financial or irreversible consequences, where an honest pending state is correct. And on mobile with an offline queue, the optimistic state must be persisted, or a restart loses it while the queued mutation still fires.
@followup
How do you handle optimistic updates for a list where the server assigns the id?
@answer
Generate the id on the client — a UUIDv7 or ULID — and send it with the request, so the optimistic entry and the server entry are the same record. That also gives you idempotency for free, since the server can dedupe on that id if the request is retried. If the server must assign the id, use a temporary key and reconcile in `onSuccess` by replacing the placeholder, but be aware that any interaction with the temporary item before reconciliation needs careful handling.
@red
- Optimistic updates without rollback.
- Not cancelling in-flight queries before writing to the cache.

# SECTION Next.js in Production | Caching, streaming, edge and the operational reality of the App Router.

### Explain the four caches in the Next.js App Router and how you debug them.
@short
Request memoisation deduplicates identical fetches within one render pass; the Data Cache persists fetch results across requests; the Full Route Cache stores rendered RSC payloads and HTML; and the client Router Cache holds prefetched segments in the browser. Stale data almost always means you are looking at the wrong one.
@deep
They have different lifetimes and different invalidation. Request memoisation lives for one render. The Data Cache survives deployments unless invalidated by tag or path. The Full Route Cache is invalidated by revalidation or by a new deployment. The Router Cache is per-session in the browser and is the reason a client-side navigation can show stale content even after a server revalidation — `router.refresh()` clears it.

Next 15 changed defaults so `fetch` and route handlers are uncached unless you opt in, which removed a large class of "why is my data stale" confusion.
@code tsx Explicit caching decisions instead of relying on defaults
// Cached with a tag — invalidated on demand, not on a timer
const product = await fetch(`${API}/products/${id}`, {
  next: { tags: [`product:${id}`, 'products'], revalidate: 3600 },
}).then(r => r.json());

// Never cached — per-user data must not be shared
const me = await fetch(`${API}/me`, { cache: 'no-store', headers: { cookie } }).then(r => r.json());

// Invalidate precisely, from a webhook or a Server Action
revalidateTag(`product:${id}`);        // this product everywhere it appears
revalidatePath('/products');           // the listing route
router.refresh();                      // client: drop the Router Cache for the current route

// Debugging: see exactly what is cached and why
// next.config.js
module.exports = { logging: { fetches: { fullUrl: true } } };
// → logs "cache: HIT/MISS/SKIP" per fetch during development
@end
@senior
The failure I would warn about is caching a request that includes an `Authorization` or cookie header without `no-store`. Because the Data Cache is keyed on the URL and options, two users hitting the same URL can share a cached response containing the first user's data — a cross-user leak from a one-word mistake. My rule is that anything reading `cookies()`, `headers()` or a user token is explicitly `no-store`, and I would rather over-fetch than risk that. For everything public, tag-based invalidation from the CMS or admin write path beats time-based revalidation because it is both fresher and cheaper.
@followup
Why does my page still show old data after `revalidateTag`?
@answer
Usually the client Router Cache: the server revalidated, but the browser is serving a prefetched segment from its own cache, which is why `router.refresh()` after a mutation is often needed. Other causes are a CDN in front with its own TTL that Next cannot invalidate, or the fetch not carrying the tag you revalidated. The debugging order is: check the server logs for a cache MISS on the next request, then check the CDN, then check the client.
@red
- Caching authenticated fetches.
- Assuming `revalidateTag` clears the browser's cache.

### How does streaming SSR work and how do you use Suspense boundaries well?
@short
The server sends the HTML shell immediately and streams each Suspense boundary's content as its data resolves, so time-to-first-byte does not wait for the slowest query. Boundary placement is the design decision: wrap the slow, independent sections so the fast ones paint instantly.
@deep
Under the hood React renders to a stream, emitting placeholder markup for suspended boundaries and later emitting the real content plus a small inline script that swaps it in. Hydration is also progressive — interactive parts become responsive as they arrive rather than all at once.

The consequence for API design is that parallel independent fetches stream independently, while sequential awaits in nested components serialise and destroy the benefit.
@code tsx Shell first, slow widgets stream in — and the waterfall to avoid
export default function Dashboard() {
  return (
    <>
      <Header />                                     {/* instant */}
      <Suspense fallback={<CardSkeleton />}>
        <RevenueCard />                              {/* 400ms query — streams in */}
      </Suspense>
      <Suspense fallback={<TableSkeleton />}>
        <RecentOrders />                             {/* 900ms query — streams in later */}
      </Suspense>
    </>
  );
}

// ❌ waterfall: 150 + 200 + 180 = 530ms
const user = await getUser(id);
const org  = await getOrg(user.orgId);
const plan = await getPlan(org.planId);

// ✅ start independent work together, await where needed
const userP = getUser(id);
const flagsP = getFlags(id);                          // does not depend on user
const user = await userP;
const [org, flags] = await Promise.all([getOrg(user.orgId), flagsP]);
@end
@senior
Two operational notes. Streaming interacts badly with anything that buffers: a CDN or Nginx with response buffering turns a streamed page into a single delayed response, so you need `no-transform` and `X-Accel-Buffering: no` for the streaming routes. And once you have started streaming a 200 response you cannot change the status code — so an error inside a streamed boundary cannot become a 500; it renders the error boundary's fallback instead. That means anything that must control the status (auth redirects, 404s) has to be resolved before the first byte, which is why I do auth checks in the layout rather than deep inside a suspended component.
@followup
Does streaming help SEO?
@answer
Yes, generally — crawlers receive the shell quickly and modern crawlers execute JavaScript and wait for streamed content. What matters is that the primary content for the page is not behind a long-suspended boundary; if the product description streams in after two seconds, some crawlers and social preview fetchers will miss it. So I stream the secondary widgets and render the SEO-critical content in the initial shell, with metadata generated server-side via `generateMetadata` so it is always present in the first response.
@red
- One Suspense boundary around the entire page.
- Sequential awaits that serialise independent data.

### When do you use Edge runtime versus Node runtime?
@short
Edge for cheap, latency-sensitive work close to the user with no Node APIs — redirects, geolocation-based rewrites, A/B assignment, setting headers, lightweight auth checks on a signed cookie. Node for anything needing a database driver, the filesystem, native modules or heavy CPU. Middleware is Edge by default, which is the constraint people hit first.
@deep
The Edge runtime is a Web-standard environment (fetch, Web Crypto, streams) without Node's built-ins. It has fast cold starts and runs in many locations, but a small bundle budget, limited execution time, and no TCP sockets — so no `pg`, no `ioredis`, no `bcrypt`.

The workaround for data access at the edge is an HTTP-based datastore (Upstash Redis over REST, a serverless Postgres HTTP driver), which changes the performance characteristics enough that it is a real architectural decision rather than a flag.
@code ts Edge middleware doing only what Edge is good at
export const runtime = 'edge';                   // in middleware.ts this is implicit

export async function middleware(req: NextRequest) {
  // ✅ geo routing — data the edge already has
  const country = req.geo?.country ?? 'IN';
  if (req.nextUrl.pathname === '/' && country !== 'IN')
    return NextResponse.rewrite(new URL(`/${country.toLowerCase()}`, req.url));

  // ✅ stable A/B bucket in a cookie
  let bucket = req.cookies.get('ab')?.value;
  if (!bucket) {
    bucket = Math.random() < 0.5 ? 'a' : 'b';
    const res = NextResponse.next();
    res.cookies.set('ab', bucket, { maxAge: 30 * 86400 });
    return res;
  }

  // ✅ cheap signature check with Web Crypto — NOT a database session lookup
  const token = req.cookies.get('session')?.value;
  if (!token && req.nextUrl.pathname.startsWith('/app'))
    return NextResponse.redirect(new URL('/login', req.url));
}
export const config = { matcher: ['/((?!_next/static|_next/image|favicon.ico).*)'] };
@end
@senior
The security point I would insist on: middleware is an optimisation, not an authorisation boundary. A missing entry in the `matcher`, a path-normalisation quirk, or a route reached by a mechanism middleware does not intercept leaves it unprotected — and there have been real CVEs around middleware bypass. So the actual authorisation check happens in the layout, page, Server Action or route handler where the data is fetched, and middleware just saves a wasted round trip for the obvious cases. Defence in depth, with the enforcement closest to the data.
@followup
Is Edge always faster?
@answer
No. Edge reduces network latency to the user, but if the request then has to reach a database in one region, you have added a hop and made it slower — the classic edge-compute-with-central-database anti-pattern. Edge wins when the work is self-contained or the data is also distributed. For a typical app with a single-region Postgres, running the API in the same region as the database and putting a CDN in front for static content usually beats moving compute to the edge.
@red
- Trying to use a Postgres driver in middleware.
- Treating middleware as the security boundary.

### How do you handle internationalisation and localisation in Next.js?
@short
Route-based locales (`/en/...`, `/hi/...`) with middleware negotiating from the `Accept-Language` header and a cookie override, message catalogues loaded per locale on the server so untranslated strings never ship to the client, and `Intl` for dates, numbers and currency rather than hand-rolled formatting.
@deep
The subtleties are pluralisation rules (which differ per language and are not "one versus many"), right-to-left layouts, date and number formats, and SEO — each locale needs its own URL with `hreflang` alternates so search engines index them separately.

Loading only the active locale's messages matters for bundle size: shipping ten languages to every user is a common and expensive mistake.
@code tsx Locale routing, server-side messages and correct formatting
// middleware.ts — negotiate once, redirect to a locale-prefixed URL
const LOCALES = ['en', 'hi', 'ml'] as const;
export function middleware(req: NextRequest) {
  const { pathname } = req.nextUrl;
  if (LOCALES.some(l => pathname.startsWith(`/${l}`))) return;
  const preferred = req.cookies.get('locale')?.value
    ?? match(req.headers.get('accept-language') ?? '', LOCALES, 'en');
  return NextResponse.redirect(new URL(`/${preferred}${pathname}`, req.url));
}

// app/[locale]/layout.tsx — load ONLY this locale's messages, on the server
export default async function LocaleLayout({ children, params }) {
  const { locale } = await params;
  const messages = (await import(`@/messages/${locale}.json`)).default;
  return (
    <html lang={locale} dir={locale === 'ar' ? 'rtl' : 'ltr'}>
      <body><NextIntlClientProvider messages={messages}>{children}</NextIntlClientProvider></body>
    </html>
  );
}

// Formatting: never hand-roll
new Intl.NumberFormat(locale, { style: 'currency', currency: 'INR' }).format(total / 100);
new Intl.RelativeTimeFormat(locale, { numeric: 'auto' }).format(-3, 'day');   // "3 days ago"
new Intl.PluralRules(locale).select(count);      // 'one' | 'other' | 'few' | 'many' …

// SEO
export const metadata = { alternates: { languages: { en: '/en/p/1', hi: '/hi/p/1' } } };
@end
@senior
The engineering point is to keep translation keys and interpolation out of concatenated strings. `"You have " + n + " orders"` cannot be translated correctly into languages with different word order or plural categories; an ICU message (`{n, plural, one {# order} other {# orders}}`) can. I would also flag the operational side: translations arrive late and incomplete, so the system needs a defined fallback chain and a way to surface missing keys in development without breaking production. And currency is not a locale property — an Indian user viewing a USD-priced product needs `en-IN` formatting of a USD amount, so locale and currency must be separate inputs.
@followup
How do you handle user-generated content in multiple languages?
@answer
Store the content with its language tag and do not machine-translate silently. For search, use per-language analysers — Postgres full-text search takes a language configuration, and using `english` on Hindi text produces nonsense stemming. For display, show the original with an optional translate action rather than replacing it, because automatic translation of reviews or messages loses nuance and users notice. Collation matters too: sorting names correctly requires a locale-aware collation, not byte order.
@red
- Shipping every locale's messages to every user.
- Building translated strings by concatenation.

### How do you monitor and debug a Next.js app in production?
@short
Sentry with source maps for both server and client errors, `web-vitals` reporting for real-user performance, structured server logs with a request id, and OpenTelemetry traces so a slow page maps to the specific fetch or query. Next's instrumentation hook is the place to wire it up once.
@deep
The Next-specific difficulty is that an error can occur on the server during a Server Component render, in a Server Action, in a route handler, or on the client after hydration — and each surfaces differently. Sentry's Next integration covers all of them, but you have to upload source maps for both bundles or your stack traces are unreadable minified noise.

Tracing matters more in the App Router because a single page render can issue many fetches across nested components; without a trace you cannot tell which one cost 600ms.
@code ts instrumentation.ts — one place to wire observability
export async function register() {
  if (process.env.NEXT_RUNTIME === 'nodejs') {
    const { NodeSDK } = await import('@opentelemetry/sdk-node');
    const { getNodeAutoInstrumentations } = await import('@opentelemetry/auto-instrumentations-node');
    new NodeSDK({
      serviceName: 'web',
      instrumentations: [getNodeAutoInstrumentations()],   // http, pg, redis spans automatically
    }).start();
  }
  await import('./sentry.server.config');
}

// Report the errors Next surfaces that would otherwise be swallowed
export const onRequestError = Sentry.captureRequestError;

// next.config.js — upload source maps, keep them off the public server
module.exports = withSentryConfig(config, {
  widenClientFileUpload: true,
  hideSourceMaps: true,
  disableLogger: true,
});
@end
@senior
The detail worth stating is release tagging: every deploy sets a release identifier that Sentry attaches to errors and that matches the uploaded source maps, so a spike is immediately attributable to a specific deploy and the stack trace resolves to real source. Without it, you get minified traces and no way to correlate a regression with a change. I would also instrument the business events, not just the technical ones — checkout started versus completed, per-provider payment success rate — because those detect the failures that leave every technical dashboard green.
@followup
How do you debug a bug that only happens in production?
@answer
Reproduce with data first: get the request id from the user or the error report, pull the full trace and log lines for that request, and check what differed — feature flag state, user tier, locale, data shape. If it is not reproducible locally, the difference is usually environment (a caching layer, a proxy, a different Node version) or data (a null field that never appears in seed data). Adding targeted structured logging behind a flag, rather than a broad log level increase, gets you the missing detail without flooding the log bill.
@red
- No source maps uploaded, so every stack trace is minified.
- Only tracking technical metrics, not business ones.

### How would you migrate a Pages Router application to the App Router?
@short
Incrementally, route by route, with both routers coexisting. Start with a low-traffic leaf route to learn the caching model, move shared layout and providers next, then migrate by business value. Expect the friction to be in third-party libraries that assume client execution and in the caching defaults.
@deep
The mechanical parts are straightforward: `getServerSideProps` becomes an async Server Component, `getStaticProps` becomes a static component with `revalidate`, `_app` and `_document` become the root layout, and API routes become route handlers. The conceptual part — deciding what is a Client Component and where the boundary sits — is the real work.

Client-only libraries need a `'use client'` wrapper module. CSS-in-JS libraries that were not designed for RSC need their own integration, which is why Tailwind and CSS Modules are the low-friction choice.
@code tsx The mechanical conversions, side by side
// Pages: getServerSideProps → App: async Server Component
// (before)
export async function getServerSideProps({ params }) {
  const order = await db.order.findUnique({ where: { id: params.id } });
  if (!order) return { notFound: true };
  return { props: { order: JSON.parse(JSON.stringify(order)) } };   // manual serialisation
}
// (after)
export default async function Page({ params }) {
  const { id } = await params;
  const order = await db.order.findUnique({ where: { id } });
  if (!order) notFound();
  return <Detail order={order} />;                                   // no serialisation dance
}

// Wrapping a client-only library once, instead of at every usage
'use client';
export { Chart } from 'some-canvas-charting-lib';     // now importable from Server Components

// api/orders.ts → app/api/orders/route.ts
export async function GET(req: NextRequest) {
  const { searchParams } = new URL(req.url);
  return NextResponse.json(await list(searchParams.get('status')));
}
@end
@senior
The advice I would give is to migrate the data layer thinking first, not the files. The biggest gain is moving data fetching into the components that need it and deleting the prop drilling from `getServerSideProps` — but that also means each component's fetch must be cached or deduplicated, or you multiply your database load. So I audit for duplicate fetches with `logging.fetches` and wrap per-request lookups (the session, the current user) in React's `cache()`. And I would set an explicit caching policy per route rather than inheriting defaults, because the defaults have changed between versions and implicit behaviour is what causes the "it was fine in staging" incidents.
@followup
What if a key dependency does not support Server Components?
@answer
Wrap it in a `'use client'` module so it is used as a client island, and keep that island as small as possible — usually just the interactive widget, with the data fetched on the server and passed as props. If the library is central (a CSS-in-JS runtime, a global state provider that must wrap everything), then either it has an official RSC integration or the migration is genuinely blocked until you replace it, and that is a real cost to put in the plan rather than discover halfway through.
@red
- A big-bang rewrite of every route at once.
- `'use client'` at the root layout, which negates the benefit.

# SECTION State Management & Data Fetching | Choosing the right tool, and the cache-invalidation questions behind it.

### How do you design query keys and cache invalidation with TanStack Query?
@short
Query keys are hierarchical arrays that describe the data: `['orders', 'list', filters]` and `['orders', 'detail', id]`. Invalidation is prefix-based, so invalidating `['orders']` refreshes every order query. Centralising key construction in a factory prevents the drift that causes "this list did not update".
@deep
The most common bug is inconsistent keys — one component uses `['orders', page]` and another `['orders', { page }]`, so they are different cache entries and invalidating one leaves the other stale. A key factory makes that impossible.

`staleTime` controls when a refetch is triggered; `gcTime` controls when unused data is discarded. Setting `staleTime: 0` (the default) means every mount refetches, which is usually more aggressive than the data needs.
@code ts A key factory plus the invalidation patterns you actually need
export const orderKeys = {
  all:      ['orders'] as const,
  lists:    () => [...orderKeys.all, 'list'] as const,
  list:     (f: Filters) => [...orderKeys.lists(), f] as const,
  details:  () => [...orderKeys.all, 'detail'] as const,
  detail:   (id: string) => [...orderKeys.details(), id] as const,
};

useQuery({
  queryKey: orderKeys.list({ status, page }),
  queryFn: ({ signal }) => api.get('/orders', { params: { status, page }, signal }),
  staleTime: 30_000,                       // don't refetch on every mount
  placeholderData: keepPreviousData,       // no flicker while paginating
});

const refund = useMutation({
  mutationFn: (id: string) => api.post(`/orders/${id}/refunds`),
  onSuccess: (data, id) => {
    qc.setQueryData(orderKeys.detail(id), data);          // write the known result directly
    qc.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: orderKeys.lists() }); // refresh lists only
  },
});

// Invalidate everything order-related after a bulk import
qc.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: orderKeys.all });
@end
@senior
Setting `setQueryData` for the detail and only invalidating the lists is the efficiency detail: you already have the authoritative response from the mutation, so refetching the detail is a wasted round trip. Beyond that, the argument I would make for a query library over a hand-rolled store is that it solves problems people underestimate — request deduplication, cancellation on unmount, retry with backoff, refetch on window focus and reconnect, and garbage collection — each of which is a bug you would otherwise ship. Moving server state out of Redux typically deletes more code than it adds.
@followup
When would you still put server data in a global store?
@answer
When the data is genuinely shared, long-lived and edited locally in ways that diverge from the server — a collaborative document's local state, an offline queue, or a complex wizard whose intermediate state is not persisted. Even then, I keep the *fetched* representation in the query cache and the *working* representation in the store, so there is one clear source for each. Copying server data into a store and then maintaining it manually is what creates the synchronisation bugs the query cache exists to prevent.
@red
- Ad-hoc query keys built inline at each call site.
- `staleTime: 0` everywhere, causing constant refetching.

### Zustand, Redux Toolkit or Context — how do you choose and why?
@short
Context for dependency injection of rarely-changing values. Zustand for global client state where I want selector-based subscriptions with minimal boilerplate. Redux Toolkit when the team benefits from strict conventions, devtools and middleware, or when the state transitions are complex enough to want a documented action log.
@deep
The deciding technical property is selective subscription. Context re-renders every consumer when the value's reference changes; Zustand lets a component subscribe to one slice with a selector, so unrelated updates cost nothing. Redux with `useSelector` has the same property plus a middleware ecosystem and time-travel debugging.

Boilerplate is the practical difference: Redux Toolkit removed most of it, but Zustand is still substantially less code for the same result in a small-to-medium app.
@code ts A Zustand store with slices, selectors and persistence
interface CartState {
  items: CartItem[];
  add: (i: CartItem) => void;
  remove: (sku: string) => void;
  totalMinor: () => number;
}

export const useCart = create<CartState>()(
  devtools(persist(immer((set, get) => ({
    items: [],
    add: (i) => set(s => {
      const found = s.items.find(x => x.sku === i.sku);
      if (found) found.qty += i.qty; else s.items.push(i);      // immer: mutate the draft safely
    }),
    remove: (sku) => set(s => { s.items = s.items.filter(x => x.sku !== sku); }),
    totalMinor: () => get().items.reduce((t, i) => t + i.unitPriceMinor * i.qty, 0),
  })), { name: 'cart', partialize: (s) => ({ items: s.items }) })   // persist items only
));

// ✅ subscribe to ONE value — this component ignores every unrelated change
const count = useCart(s => s.items.length);

// ✅ derived values need a stable selector or a shallow comparator
const { add, remove } = useCart(useShallow(s => ({ add: s.add, remove: s.remove })));
@end
@senior
The `useShallow` detail matters: returning a new object from a selector on every call defeats the subscription optimisation and re-renders on every store change, which is the most common Zustand performance mistake. On the broader choice, I would say the honest answer depends on the team as much as the code — Redux's conventions are valuable when many engineers touch the same state and you want a legible history of what changed and why; Zustand's flexibility is valuable when a small team wants to move fast. Both are correct choices with different failure modes, and articulating that is better than declaring a winner.
@followup
How do you persist and rehydrate state safely?
@answer
Persist only what should survive — a cart, preferences, a draft — never tokens or server data that could go stale and mislead. Version the persisted shape and provide a migration function, because a released schema change against an old persisted blob otherwise crashes on load for existing users. And handle the hydration gap in SSR: reading `localStorage` during render causes a hydration mismatch, so the store starts empty and rehydrates in an effect, or the value comes from a cookie the server can also read.
@red
- Selectors that return new objects every call.
- Persisting auth tokens in a store backed by localStorage.

### How do you handle real-time data in a React application?
@short
A single shared connection managed outside the component tree, with messages written into the query cache rather than into separate component state. That keeps one source of truth, so a REST refetch and a live update cannot disagree, and components subscribe through the same hooks they already use.
@deep
The mistakes are opening a socket per component, re-establishing the connection on every render because the handler is in the dependency array, and maintaining a parallel state tree for live data that then diverges from the fetched data.

Reconnection needs exponential backoff with jitter, and resumption needs a sequence number or cursor so you can replay what was missed rather than refetching everything.
@code tsx One connection, writing into the query cache
function useLiveOrders() {
  const qc = useQueryClient();
  const lastSeq = useRef<number>(0);

  useEffect(() => {
    let closed = false, attempt = 0, ws: WebSocket;

    const connect = () => {
      ws = new WebSocket(`${WS_URL}?since=${lastSeq.current}`);
      ws.onopen = () => { attempt = 0; };
      ws.onmessage = (e) => {
        const msg = JSON.parse(e.data);
        lastSeq.current = msg.seq;                      // resume point
        // write into the SAME cache the REST queries use
        qc.setQueryData(orderKeys.detail(msg.orderId), (old) => ({ ...old, ...msg.order }));
        qc.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: orderKeys.lists(), refetchType: 'none' });
      };
      ws.onclose = () => {
        if (closed) return;
        const delay = Math.min(30_000, 2 ** attempt++ * 500) * (0.5 + Math.random());  // backoff + jitter
        setTimeout(connect, delay);
      };
    };
    connect();
    return () => { closed = true; ws?.close(); };
  }, [qc]);
}
@end
@senior
Two details I would emphasise. Resuming with `?since=<seq>` rather than refetching everything is what keeps reconnection cheap on a flaky mobile network — otherwise every tunnel and lift ride triggers a full refetch storm from every client at once. And jitter on the backoff is not optional: without it, a server restart causes every client to reconnect simultaneously at exactly the same moments, which is a self-inflicted denial of service. I would also pause or close the connection when the tab is hidden for a long period, because thousands of idle connections cost real memory on the server.
@followup
WebSocket or Server-Sent Events for a live dashboard?
@answer
SSE, unless the client needs to send frequently. A dashboard is one-directional, and SSE gives automatic reconnection with `Last-Event-ID` resumption, works over plain HTTP with existing auth and compression, and passes through proxies without an upgrade negotiation. WebSockets are the right answer for chat, collaborative editing and anything with a busy upstream channel. Choosing SSE where it fits removes a whole category of operational complexity.
@red
- A socket connection per component.
- Reconnecting immediately with no backoff or jitter.

# SECTION React Native in Production | Background execution, battery, native modules and the realities of shipping to devices.

### How does background location tracking actually work on iOS and Android?
@short
It has to be a native background task, not a JavaScript timer — the OS suspends your app and JS stops running. On iOS you declare the `location` background mode and use significant-change or region monitoring with "Always" authorisation; on Android you run a foreground service with a persistent notification and request background location separately.
@deep
The platform behaviours diverge sharply. iOS will terminate a suspended app and relaunch it for a location event, so your app must be able to start cold, restore state and handle a single point. Android's Doze mode and manufacturer-specific battery optimisation (Xiaomi, Oppo, Samsung) kill background work aggressively unless the user exempts your app, which you have to guide them through.

Both require a clear rationale screen before the system prompt, and Android 10+ requires background location to be requested separately from foreground.
@code ts Permission flow and battery-aware tracking configuration
import BackgroundGeolocation from 'react-native-background-geolocation';

await BackgroundGeolocation.ready({
  desiredAccuracy: BackgroundGeolocation.DESIRED_ACCURACY_HIGH,
  distanceFilter: 50,                       // metres — no update while stationary
  stopTimeout: 5,                           // minutes before entering "stationary" state
  stopOnTerminate: false,                   // keep tracking after the app is swept away (Android)
  startOnBoot: true,
  foregroundService: true,                  // Android: mandatory, shows a persistent notification
  notification: { title: 'Trip in progress', text: 'Recording your route' },
  // batching: upload every N points instead of per point — huge battery saving
  batchSync: true, maxBatchSize: 200, autoSync: true, autoSyncThreshold: 20,
  url: `${API}/v1/locations/batch`,
  headers: { Authorization: `Bearer ${token}` },
  heartbeatInterval: 60,
});

// Adaptive accuracy: precision costs battery, so only pay for it when it matters
BackgroundGeolocation.onActivityChange(({ activity, confidence }) => {
  if (activity === 'still' && confidence > 75)
    BackgroundGeolocation.setConfig({ desiredAccuracy: BackgroundGeolocation.DESIRED_ACCURACY_LOW });
  if (activity === 'in_vehicle')
    BackgroundGeolocation.setConfig({ desiredAccuracy: BackgroundGeolocation.DESIRED_ACCURACY_HIGH, distanceFilter: 20 });
});
@end
@senior
The three things that decide whether this product succeeds are battery, permission rate and data volume, and they are all product problems as much as engineering ones. Battery: distance filtering, activity-based accuracy and batched uploads take a feature from "phone dead by 3pm" to acceptable. Permission: users deny "Always" location unless you explain the benefit first, and iOS gives you essentially one chance. Data: a point every few seconds is millions of rows a month, so downsampling and retention policy have to be designed up front. Being able to talk about those trade-offs is what makes this résumé line credible.
@followup
How do you test background behaviour?
@answer
Real devices, with simulated routes — Xcode's GPX location simulation and Android's emulator route playback for the happy path, and then real field testing, because the failure modes are network transitions, tunnels, app termination and OS-level killing, none of which the simulator reproduces. I also add instrumentation: log every state transition (tracking started, provider changed, permission revoked, batch synced) to the server with a device id, so I can debug a user's report without having their phone. Without that telemetry, background bugs are effectively undebuggable.
@red
- Using `setInterval` for background location.
- Requesting "Always" location on first launch with no explanation.

### How do you write a native module or use the new architecture?
@short
You write a native module when you need platform capability JavaScript cannot reach — a specific SDK, hardware access, or CPU-heavy work. Under the new architecture you define a TypeScript spec, Codegen generates type-safe C++/Java/ObjC bindings, and the TurboModule is loaded lazily rather than at startup.
@deep
The old bridge required manually declaring methods and passing JSON-serialisable values asynchronously. TurboModules use JSI, so calls can be synchronous and can pass richer types, and only modules actually used are initialised — which measurably improves startup time in apps with many dependencies.

Fabric is the equivalent for components: the shadow tree moves to C++ and rendering supports concurrent React features.
@code ts A TurboModule spec and its JS usage
// specs/NativeDeviceTelemetry.ts — Codegen reads this
import type { TurboModule } from 'react-native';
import { TurboModuleRegistry } from 'react-native';

export interface Spec extends TurboModule {
  getBatteryLevel(): number;                                 // sync — cheap, allowed with JSI
  isBatteryOptimisationExempt(): Promise<boolean>;           // async — Android only
  requestOptimisationExemption(): Promise<boolean>;
  readonly getConstants: () => { deviceModel: string; osVersion: string };
}
export default TurboModuleRegistry.getEnforcing<Spec>('DeviceTelemetry');

// Usage — fully typed, no manual bridge declarations
import DeviceTelemetry from './specs/NativeDeviceTelemetry';

const battery = DeviceTelemetry.getBatteryLevel();
if (Platform.OS === 'android' && !(await DeviceTelemetry.isBatteryOptimisationExempt())) {
  // this is why tracking silently dies on some Android phones — guide the user to Settings
  await DeviceTelemetry.requestOptimisationExemption();
}
@end
@senior
The judgement call is when *not* to write one. A native module means maintaining Kotlin and Swift alongside TypeScript, keeping up with SDK deprecations, and it blocks over-the-air updates for anything it touches. So I check for a maintained community package first, and only write my own when there is none or when the dependency risk is unacceptable. When I do write one, I keep the surface minimal and put as much logic as possible in TypeScript, so the native part is a thin capability shim — that keeps the OTA-updatable share of the codebase high.
@followup
What breaks when you enable the new architecture?
@answer
Any dependency that still uses the old bridge API without an interop layer, custom native components not migrated to Fabric, and code relying on synchronous bridge behaviours that changed. The interop layer covers a lot now, but in practice enabling it means auditing every native dependency and testing thoroughly on both platforms. The payoff — faster startup from lazy TurboModules, better animation and concurrent React support — is real, but I would schedule it as its own piece of work rather than bundling it with a feature release.
@red
- Writing a native module before checking for an existing package.
- Assuming JS-only OTA updates can change native behaviour.

### How do you optimise React Native startup time and bundle size?
@short
Hermes for bytecode precompilation, lazy TurboModules so unused native modules do not initialise, `inlineRequires` so JavaScript modules are evaluated on first use rather than at startup, deferring non-critical initialisation until after the first screen paints, and auditing the dependency graph for anything large that could be replaced.
@deep
Startup breaks into native initialisation, JavaScript bundle loading and evaluation, and the first render. Hermes removes parse and compile time by shipping bytecode. `inlineRequires` converts top-level imports into lazy requires at the point of use, which matters enormously when your entry file transitively imports the whole app.

Third-party SDKs — analytics, crash reporting, ads — often initialise synchronously at startup and are a common hidden cost.
@code js Metro configuration and deferred initialisation
// metro.config.js
module.exports = {
  transformer: {
    getTransformOptions: async () => ({
      transform: { experimentalImportSupport: false, inlineRequires: true },   // ← big win
    }),
  },
};

// Defer everything not needed for the first paint
useEffect(() => {
  const task = InteractionManager.runAfterInteractions(async () => {
    await Promise.all([
      analytics.init(),          // not needed to render the home screen
      remoteConfig.fetch(),
      preloadSecondaryScreens(),
    ]);
  });
  return () => task.cancel();
}, []);

// Measure it, per release, on a real low-end device
// npx react-native-performance  /  Systrace  /  Android: adb shell am start -W
@end
@senior
The measurement discipline is what I would stress: cold start on a mid-range Android device is the number that matters, and it must be tracked per release or it regresses silently as dependencies accumulate. I set a budget and check it in CI where possible. On bundle size, the biggest wins in my experience are removing moment.js and lodash in favour of `Intl` and targeted imports, importing icons individually, and checking whether a heavy SDK is actually used on the first screen. Users abandon apps that take four seconds to open, and cold start is the metric most directly tied to that.
@followup
Does code splitting exist in React Native?
@answer
Not in the web sense — there is one bundle by default. `React.lazy` with dynamic `import()` works with Metro's support for asynchronous requires and RAM bundles, which loads modules on demand from the bundle rather than evaluating everything upfront. In practice `inlineRequires` gives most of the benefit with far less complexity. For genuinely large optional features, some teams ship separate bundles fetched over the air, but that adds meaningful operational risk and I would only do it with a strong justification.
@red
- Not enabling Hermes.
- Initialising every SDK synchronously at startup.

### How do you handle errors, crashes and monitoring in a mobile app?
@short
Sentry or Crashlytics for JavaScript exceptions and native crashes, with source maps and dSYM/ProGuard mappings uploaded per release so traces are readable. Error boundaries around each screen so one broken component does not blank the app, and a global handler that reports and shows a recovery screen rather than a white screen.
@deep
Mobile crash reporting has a specific requirement: symbol files. A minified Hermes stack trace or a native crash address is meaningless without the corresponding source map or symbol file for that exact build, so uploading them must be part of the release pipeline, not a manual step.

The crash-free-users metric per release is what tells you whether to continue or halt a staged rollout, so it must be visible before you go from 10% to 100%.
@code ts Global error handling that produces actionable reports
import * as Sentry from '@sentry/react-native';

Sentry.init({
  dsn: env.SENTRY_DSN,
  release: `${pkg.version}+${Build.buildNumber}`,      // must match the uploaded symbols
  dist: String(Build.buildNumber),
  tracesSampleRate: 0.2,
  beforeSend(event) {                                   // never ship PII to the error tracker
    delete event.user?.email;
    if (event.request?.headers) delete event.request.headers.Authorization;
    return event;
  },
});

// Context that makes a report debuggable without the user's phone
Sentry.setContext('device', { batteryOptimised, networkType, storageFreeMb });
Sentry.addBreadcrumb({ category: 'sync', message: `flush ${batch.length} points`, level: 'info' });

// Per-screen boundaries: a broken widget must not blank the app
<Sentry.ErrorBoundary fallback={({ resetError }) => <ScreenError onRetry={resetError} />}>
  <TripDetail />
</Sentry.ErrorBoundary>

// Native crashes need their own upload step in CI:
//   sentry-cli upload-dif --org acme --project mobile ./ios/build/**/*.dSYM
@end
@senior
The breadcrumbs are what make mobile debugging tractable. You cannot attach a debugger to a user's phone, so the report has to carry the story: what screen they were on, whether they were offline, how many items were in the sync queue, whether battery optimisation was active. I add breadcrumbs at every state transition in the sync engine, and that has turned "it sometimes stops syncing" from unreproducible into a two-minute diagnosis. The other point is PII: stripping emails, tokens and location coordinates before sending is both a privacy obligation and something a security review will ask about.
@followup
How do you decide whether to halt a staged rollout?
@answer
On crash-free sessions and crash-free users for the new version compared with the previous one, plus the ANR rate on Android and any spike in a specific error signature. If crash-free users drops by more than a small threshold — I would use half a percentage point as a starting point — I halt and investigate before increasing the percentage. Having the OTA rollback path ready means a JavaScript-level regression can be fixed in minutes rather than waiting for a store review.
@red
- No source maps or dSYMs uploaded.
- Going straight to a 100% rollout.

### How do you handle deep links, universal links and app-to-web continuity?
@short
Universal Links on iOS and App Links on Android map real HTTPS URLs to screens, verified by a file hosted on your domain, so a link opens the app when installed and the website when not. Custom schemes are a fallback. The routing layer must handle cold start, background resume and unauthenticated states.
@deep
The verification files are the part that silently breaks: `apple-app-site-association` must be served from `/.well-known/` over HTTPS with `Content-Type: application/json` and no redirect, and `assetlinks.json` must contain the exact SHA-256 fingerprint of the signing certificate — which differs between your upload key and Google Play's app signing key, a very common cause of "links work in debug and not in production".

Deferred deep linking — preserving the destination through an install from the store — needs a third-party service, because neither platform provides it natively.
@code json The two files that must be exactly right
// https://acme.com/.well-known/apple-app-site-association   (no .json extension!)
{
  "applinks": {
    "details": [{ "appIDs": ["TEAMID.com.acme.app"],
                  "components": [{ "/": "/trips/*" }, { "/": "/pay/*" }] }]
  }
}

// https://acme.com/.well-known/assetlinks.json
[{
  "relation": ["delegate_permission/common.handle_all_urls"],
  "target": { "namespace": "android_app", "package_name": "com.acme.app",
              "sha256_cert_fingerprints": ["AB:CD:…"] }   // ← Play App Signing key, not upload key
}]
@end
@code ts Handling the link in all three app states
const pending = { current: null as string | null };

// cold start
const initialUrl = await Linking.getInitialURL();
if (initialUrl) pending.current = initialUrl;

// warm / backgrounded
Linking.addEventListener('url', ({ url }) => pending.current = url);

// consume only once the navigator is mounted AND auth is resolved
useEffect(() => {
  if (!navigatorReady || authLoading) return;
  const url = pending.current;
  if (!url) return;
  pending.current = null;
  if (!user) return saveForAfterLogin(url);        // ← the case everyone forgets
  navigateToUrl(url);
}, [navigatorReady, authLoading, user]);
@end
@senior
The "save for after login" branch is the detail that separates a working implementation from a frustrating one: a user taps a payment link in an email, the app cold-starts, they log in, and they must land on the payment — not on the home screen. I hold the pending destination through the auth flow and consume it afterwards. I would also mention that link handling is a security surface: a deep link parameter reaching a WebView or a redirect is an open-redirect and injection risk, so parameters are validated against a route allow-list exactly like any other untrusted input.
@followup
How do you test deep links?
@answer
`xcrun simctl openurl booted "https://acme.com/trips/123"` on iOS and `adb shell am start -a android.intent.action.VIEW -d "https://acme.com/trips/123"` on Android cover the mechanics. For the verification files, Apple's and Google's validators plus fetching the files with curl to confirm content type and absence of redirects. And I always test on a release build signed with the production key, because the Android fingerprint mismatch only appears there — testing only in debug is exactly how this ships broken.
@red
- Custom schemes only, no universal links.
- Wrong signing fingerprint in `assetlinks.json`.

### How do you secure a mobile app beyond storing tokens correctly?
@short
Assume the device may be compromised: short-lived tokens with server-side revocation, certificate pinning for high-value traffic, no secrets in the bundle, jailbreak and root detection as a risk signal, obfuscation as friction rather than protection, and server-side enforcement of every rule the client also enforces.
@deep
The core principle is that the client is untrusted. Any check implemented only in the app — a price calculation, a permission, a rate limit — can be bypassed by someone using the API directly with a token extracted from their own device.

App attestation (Play Integrity, App Attest) gives you a cryptographic signal that the request came from a genuine, unmodified app on a genuine device, which is the strongest available control against automated abuse.
@code ts Layered controls, none of them relied on alone
// 1. Attestation — proves the request came from a real app instance
const attestation = await PlayIntegrity.requestIntegrityToken({ nonce });
await api.post('/v1/session', { attestation });      // server verifies with Google, scores the risk

// 2. Certificate pinning for the endpoints that matter, with a backup pin
const pinned = { 'api.acme.com': ['sha256/AAA…', 'sha256/BBB…'] };   // current + next key

// 3. Compromise signals feed risk scoring — they do NOT hard-block
const risk = { rooted: await JailMonkey.isJailBroken(), emulator: await DeviceInfo.isEmulator(),
               hooked: await JailMonkey.hookDetected() };
await api.post('/v1/session', { risk });             // server may require step-up auth

// 4. The rule that actually protects you: the server recomputes everything
//    price, discount, eligibility, quota — the client's numbers are advisory only
@end
@senior
The backup pin is the detail I would insist on: pinning without a second pin for your next certificate means that when the certificate rotates, every installed app breaks and cannot be fixed remotely — a self-inflicted total outage. So I pin two keys and rotate ahead of expiry, and I ship a remote kill-switch for pinning. On root detection, treating it as a hard block is both bypassable and hostile to legitimate power users; feeding it into a risk score that can trigger step-up authentication is more proportionate and more effective. And the last line is the one that matters most: the server must recompute prices and permissions, because everything else is defence in depth around that.
@followup
How do you handle a user's data on logout or device loss?
@answer
On logout: clear the Keychain entry, wipe the local database and any cached files, revoke the refresh token server-side so it cannot be reused, and clear in-memory state. On reported device loss: revoke every session for that user from the server side so the tokens on that device stop working immediately, which is why refresh tokens must be server-tracked rather than purely stateless. For high-sensitivity apps I would also support remote wipe on next launch via a flag returned by the session endpoint.
@red
- Client-side price or permission checks with no server enforcement.
- Certificate pinning with no rotation plan.

# SECTION Node.js Internals & Performance | Where "35% API response-time reduction" comes from, mechanism by mechanism.

### How do you find and fix the bottleneck behind slow API responses?
@short
Measure the breakdown before changing anything: split total latency into time in the database, time in outbound calls, time in CPU-bound JavaScript and time waiting on the event loop. Distributed tracing gives you that directly. In my experience the order of causes is database queries, then serial awaits, then missing caching, then serialisation.
@deep
The instinct to avoid is guessing. A trace with spans per query and per outbound call tells you immediately whether a 400ms endpoint is one 380ms query or twenty 15ms queries — and those need completely different fixes.

Event-loop delay is the metric that distinguishes "we are waiting on I/O" from "we are CPU-saturated". If p99 latency rises while loop lag is flat, the dependency is slow; if loop lag rises with it, your own process is the problem.
@arch The latency budget of a typical slow endpoint, before and after
  BEFORE  GET /api/v1/orders?status=paid           p95 = 840ms
  ├── auth middleware: SELECT user by id            18ms   ← every request, uncached
  ├── SELECT orders … (no index on (user,status))  410ms   ← sequential scan
  ├── N+1: 20 × SELECT items WHERE order_id = ?    240ms   ← 20 round trips
  ├── 3 × sequential await → pricing service        90ms   ← independent, but serialised
  ├── JSON.stringify of 20 full model instances     40ms   ← whole rows, unused columns
  └── misc                                          42ms

  AFTER                                             p95 = 190ms   (−77%)
  ├── auth: JWT verify (no DB) + Redis revocation    2ms
  ├── SELECT … USING idx_orders_user_status_created 12ms   ← composite index
  ├── ONE query: items WHERE order_id = ANY($1)     14ms   ← batched
  ├── Promise.all of the 3 independent calls        32ms   ← parallel, not serial
  ├── explicit DTO: 6 columns, not 30                6ms
  └── misc + Redis list cache (30s, tag-invalidated) …
@end
@code ts The four fixes, in code
// 1. Batch instead of N+1
const orders = await repo.list(userId, status);
const items  = await repo.itemsForOrders(orders.map(o => o.id));      // ONE query
const byOrder = Map.groupBy(items, i => i.orderId);

// 2. Parallelise independent work
const [pricing, shipping, flags] = await Promise.all([svcA(), svcB(), svcC()]);

// 3. Select only what you serialise
const rows = await sql`SELECT id, total_minor, status, created_at FROM orders WHERE …`;

// 4. Reuse outbound connections — often the cheapest large win
const agent = new https.Agent({ keepAlive: true, maxSockets: 64, keepAliveMsecs: 30_000 });
@end
@senior
When an interviewer asks how I achieved a specific percentage, the answer has to be a story with numbers: this is the endpoint, this was the p95, here is the trace breakdown, here were the three changes, here is the p95 afterwards, and here is how I knew it held — a dashboard over the following week, not a single benchmark run. I would also name what I deliberately did not do: I did not add a cache in front of a query that was slow because it lacked an index, because caching a broken query hides the problem and produces a cold-cache cliff later. Fix the cause, then cache what is genuinely expensive.
@followup
How do you know the improvement was real and not noise?
@answer
Compare distributions, not single numbers: p50, p95 and p99 over a comparable window at comparable traffic, before and after, ideally with the change behind a flag so you can compare concurrently rather than across time. A single load-test run on an idle staging environment proves very little. I would also watch the downstream effects — database CPU, connection pool wait time, error rate — because an optimisation that halves latency by tripling database load has moved the problem rather than solved it.
@red
- Optimising without a trace or a profile.
- Reporting a benchmark number with no production evidence.

### How do you profile CPU usage in a Node service?
@short
Capture a CPU profile in production with `--cpu-prof` or by attaching the inspector, then read the flame graph for the widest frames — that is where the time is. The usual culprits are JSON serialisation of large payloads, synchronous crypto, regex backtracking, and unnecessary object allocation in a hot loop.
@deep
The flame graph's width is self time plus children; what you look for is a wide plateau that you did not expect. A tall narrow tower is just deep call stacks and is usually fine.

The signature failure worth knowing is catastrophic regex backtracking — a pattern with nested quantifiers against a crafted input can take exponential time and pin the CPU at 100% on a single request. That is ReDoS, and it is a denial-of-service vulnerability, not just a performance bug.
@code js Capturing a profile safely in production, and the ReDoS trap
// Option 1: signal-triggered inspector — no restart needed
// start with: node --inspect=127.0.0.1:9229 dist/index.js  (bind to localhost only!)
kill -USR1 <pid>            // opens the inspector; then port-forward and attach DevTools

// Option 2: programmatic, bounded, safe to leave in
import inspector from 'node:inspector/promises';
async function profileFor(ms = 10_000) {
  const session = new inspector.Session(); session.connect();
  await session.post('Profiler.enable');
  await session.post('Profiler.start');
  await sleep(ms);
  const { profile } = await session.post('Profiler.stop');
  await fs.writeFile(`/tmp/cpu-${Date.now()}.cpuprofile`, JSON.stringify(profile));
  session.disconnect();
}

// ❌ ReDoS: nested quantifiers — 30 chars of input can take minutes
const bad = /^(\s*\w+\s*)+$/;
// ✅ linear alternative + bounded input
const good = /^\w+(?:\s+\w+)*$/;
if (input.length > 256) throw new BadRequest('TOO_LONG');
// Better still: validate with a parser, or use a linear-time engine (RE2)
@end
@senior
Two operational notes. Binding `--inspect` to `0.0.0.0` in production is a remote code execution vulnerability, so it is always `127.0.0.1` plus an SSH tunnel. And on ReDoS, I would mention that it is usually introduced by a dependency or by a well-meaning validation regex, so I run `eslint-plugin-security` and check user-facing regexes against a scanner. The general principle behind CPU profiling in Node is that the fix is almost never micro-optimisation — it is finding the one operation that should not be in the request path at all and moving it to a worker, a queue, or the database.
@followup
What does high event-loop lag with low CPU mean?
@answer
It usually means the libuv thread pool is saturated rather than the CPU — heavy `fs` operations, DNS lookups via `getaddrinfo`, or `bcrypt`/`pbkdf2` calls, which all use the four-thread default pool. Requests queue for a thread, so latency rises while CPU sits idle. The fixes are raising `UV_THREADPOOL_SIZE`, switching to a DNS cache to avoid `getaddrinfo`, or moving password hashing off the request path. It is a genuinely counter-intuitive diagnosis and being able to name it is a strong signal.
@red
- Exposing the inspector publicly.
- Micro-optimising loops when the real cost is a database call.

### How do you find a memory leak in a production Node service?
@short
Confirm the trend first — RSS or heap-used climbing across hours and not recovering after garbage collection. Then take two heap snapshots under load, minutes apart, and diff them: the object types that grew and their retainer paths point at the cause, which in Node is almost always an unbounded cache, uncleaned listeners, or streams not destroyed.
@deep
Distinguishing a leak from normal behaviour matters. A healthy heap sawtooths — grows, then drops sharply after a major GC. A leak shows a rising floor: each post-GC minimum is higher than the last.

RSS versus heap tells you where the memory is. Growing heap points at JavaScript objects; growing RSS with flat heap points at buffers, native module allocations, or many open sockets and file descriptors.
@code js Instrumentation that lets you diagnose without guessing
import v8 from 'node:v8';
import { monitorEventLoopDelay } from 'node:perf_hooks';

const loop = monitorEventLoopDelay({ resolution: 20 }); loop.enable();

setInterval(() => {
  const m = process.memoryUsage();
  const h = v8.getHeapStatistics();
  logger.info({
    rssMb: (m.rss / 1e6) | 0,
    heapUsedMb: (m.heapUsed / 1e6) | 0,
    heapLimitMb: (h.heap_size_limit / 1e6) | 0,
    externalMb: (m.external / 1e6) | 0,          // buffers live here, not in the heap
    loopP99Ms: +(loop.percentile(99) / 1e6).toFixed(1),
    handles: process._getActiveHandles().length, // sockets/timers not cleaned up
  }, 'runtime');
  loop.reset();
}, 30_000).unref();

// On-demand snapshot, triggered by a signal so you can grab one during an incident
process.on('SIGUSR2', () => {
  const f = v8.writeHeapSnapshot(`/tmp/heap-${Date.now()}.heapsnapshot`);
  logger.warn({ f }, 'heap snapshot written');
});
@end
@senior
Tracking `_getActiveHandles().length` is a cheap and surprisingly effective leak detector: a steadily growing handle count means sockets, timers or streams are not being cleaned up, which is a leak you can localise long before a heap snapshot. I would also mention that the fix is usually structural rather than clever — bound every in-process cache with a size limit and a TTL, tie every listener to an AbortController, and use `stream.pipeline` so failures destroy the whole chain. Most Node "memory leaks" are unbounded data structures that nobody decided an eviction policy for.
@followup
Would you just restart the process periodically?
@answer
As a stopgap during an incident, yes — a scheduled rolling restart buys time and is better than a nightly OOM at peak traffic. As a permanent solution, no: it masks the growth, it drops in-flight work unless shutdown is graceful, and the underlying leak usually gets worse as traffic grows. I would use the restart to stabilise, then take snapshots from a live instance to find the cause, and remove the restart afterwards. Being honest that the stopgap is a stopgap is part of the answer.
@red
- Restarting on a cron and calling it fixed.
- Confusing normal GC sawtooth with a leak.

### How do you design a background job system in Node?
@short
A durable queue (BullMQ on Redis, or SQS), idempotent handlers because delivery is at-least-once, retries with exponential backoff and jitter, a dead-letter path for permanent failures, bounded concurrency, and monitoring on queue depth and job age. The API enqueues and returns immediately; workers scale independently.
@deep
The critical design decision is what "done" means. At-least-once delivery means a handler can run twice — after a timeout, after a worker crash, after a redelivery — so every handler needs a natural idempotency key or a processed-events table.

Retry policy needs classification: a 429 or a 503 is transient and should back off; a 422 is permanent and should go straight to the dead-letter queue rather than burning five attempts.
@code ts A worker with classification, idempotency and observability
const worker = new Worker('payments', async (job) => {
  const { paymentId, attempt } = { ...job.data, attempt: job.attemptsMade };

  // Idempotency: has this effect already happened?
  const already = await db.query(
    `INSERT INTO processed_jobs (job_key) VALUES ($1) ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING RETURNING 1`,
    [`payment:${paymentId}`]);
  if (!already.rowCount) return { skipped: 'already_processed' };

  try {
    return await provider.capture(paymentId);
  } catch (err) {
    if (err.status === 422 || err.code === 'card_declined')
      throw new UnrecoverableError(err.message);      // ← BullMQ: straight to failed, no retries
    throw err;                                        // transient: retried with backoff
  }
}, {
  connection,
  concurrency: 10,
  limiter: { max: 50, duration: 1000 },               // respect the provider's rate limit
});

await queue.add('capture', { paymentId }, {
  jobId: `capture:${paymentId}`,                      // dedupe on enqueue
  attempts: 6,
  backoff: { type: 'exponential', delay: 2000 },      // 2s,4s,8s,16s,32s,64s
  removeOnComplete: { age: 3600, count: 1000 },
  removeOnFail: false,                                // keep failures for inspection
});

// Alert on these, not on CPU:
//   queue depth growing for 15m · oldest job age > 5m · failed count > 0 · DLQ non-empty
@end
@senior
`UnrecoverableError` for permanent failures is the detail that keeps a queue healthy: retrying a declined card six times with backoff wastes two minutes per job, floods the provider, and delays every other job behind it. Classifying errors at the boundary is what makes a retry policy useful rather than harmful. I would also emphasise that job age, not queue depth, is the metric that maps to user impact — a deep queue draining fast is fine, a shallow queue where the oldest job is twenty minutes old means something is stuck. And every job payload should carry a correlation id from the originating request so a user complaint traces end to end.
@followup
How do you handle a job that must run exactly once?
@answer
You cannot get exactly-once delivery across a network, so you get exactly-once *effect*: at-least-once delivery plus an idempotent handler, usually via a unique constraint on the effect. For scheduled jobs across multiple workers, add a distributed lock or use the queue's built-in repeatable jobs with a fixed job id, so only one instance schedules it. Anyone who claims exactly-once delivery is either wrong or is describing effect-level idempotency with different words.
@red
- Handlers that are not idempotent.
- Retrying permanent failures with the same policy as transient ones.

### How do you implement graceful degradation and circuit breakers?
@short
Wrap every outbound dependency with a timeout, limited retries and a circuit breaker that opens after a failure threshold, so a failing dependency fails fast instead of consuming your resources. Then define a fallback per dependency: cached data, a reduced feature, or a clear error — chosen by what the product can tolerate.
@deep
Without a breaker, a slow dependency is worse than a dead one: every request waits for the timeout, connections and event-loop capacity are consumed, and your own service becomes unavailable for reasons unrelated to its own health. That is cascading failure.

The breaker has three states: closed (normal), open (fail immediately without calling), and half-open (allow a probe to test recovery). Combined with a bulkhead — a concurrency cap per dependency — it prevents one slow integration from starving everything else.
@code ts A breaker with a fallback that keeps the product usable
import CircuitBreaker from 'opossum';

const breaker = new CircuitBreaker(
  (id: string) => http.get(`${RECS_URL}/for/${id}`, { timeout: 800, agent }),
  {
    timeout: 800,                    // fail fast — do not hold a request for 30s
    errorThresholdPercentage: 50,    // open after 50% failures in the window
    resetTimeout: 15_000,            // then probe again
    volumeThreshold: 20,             // ignore small samples
  });

// Fallback: degraded, not broken
breaker.fallback(async (id) =>
  (await redis.get(`recs:${id}`).then(j => j && JSON.parse(j))) ?? POPULAR_DEFAULTS);

breaker.on('open',     () => { metrics.increment('breaker.open', { dep: 'recs' }); logger.warn('recs breaker OPEN'); });
breaker.on('halfOpen', () => logger.info('recs breaker probing'));

// Bulkhead: cap concurrent calls so one dependency cannot consume the whole pool
const limit = pLimit(20);
const recs = await limit(() => breaker.fire(userId));
@end
@senior
The design question the breaker forces you to answer is what the product does when a dependency is down, and that must be decided per dependency rather than globally. Recommendations missing? Show popular items — the user barely notices. Payment provider down? Fail loudly and offer an alternative provider, never silently. Auth service down? You cannot degrade, so you fail closed. Writing that table down before the incident is what turns a 3am judgement call into a configuration. I would also point out that timeouts must be shorter than your caller's timeout, or the breaker never gets a chance to help.
@followup
What timeout values do you choose?
@answer
Derived from the dependency's own latency distribution, not guessed: set the timeout somewhere above p99 — often two to three times p99 — so normal slow requests succeed but a hang is cut off quickly. Then make sure the chain is consistent: if the client times out at 5s, an internal call with a 30s timeout is pointless because nobody is waiting for the answer any more. Budget the total and allocate it downward through the call chain, leaving room for retries.
@red
- No timeouts on outbound HTTP calls.
- Retrying aggressively into an already-overloaded dependency.

### How do you handle rate limiting, quotas and abuse at the application level?
@short
Layered: coarse volumetric limits at the edge, per-user and per-endpoint limits in the application backed by Redis, and cost-based quotas for expensive operations. Return 429 with `Retry-After` and rate-limit headers, and choose fail-open or fail-closed per endpoint based on what an outage of the limiter would cost.
@deep
Keying is the design decision. IP-only is bypassed by any botnet and punishes shared NATs; user-only lets an attacker create accounts; the practical answer is layered keys — per IP, per account, and per target resource for things like login where you want to protect one account regardless of the attacker's source.

Cost-based limiting matters when operations differ by orders of magnitude: a list query and an LLM generation should not consume the same budget.
@code ts Cost-aware limiting with tiered quotas
const COST = { 'GET /orders': 1, 'POST /orders': 5, 'POST /ai/generate': 50, 'POST /exports': 100 };
const TIER = { free: { capacity: 300, refillPerSec: 1 }, pro: { capacity: 3000, refillPerSec: 10 } };

export const rateLimit: RequestHandler = async (req, res, next) => {
  const route = `${req.method} ${req.route?.path ?? req.path}`;
  const cost  = COST[route] ?? 1;
  const tier  = TIER[req.user?.plan ?? 'free'];

  const [allowed, retryAfter, remaining] = await redis.eval(
    TOKEN_BUCKET_LUA, 1, `rl:{${req.user?.id ?? req.ip}}`,
    tier.capacity, tier.refillPerSec, Date.now(), cost);

  res.set({ 'RateLimit-Limit': tier.capacity, 'RateLimit-Remaining': remaining,
            'RateLimit-Policy': `${tier.capacity};w=${tier.capacity / tier.refillPerSec}` });

  if (!allowed) {
    metrics.increment('ratelimit.blocked', { route, plan: req.user?.plan });
    return res.status(429).set('Retry-After', String(retryAfter)).json({ error: 'RATE_LIMITED', retryAfter });
  }
  next();
};

// Redis unavailable? Decide per endpoint:
//   /auth/login  → fail OPEN  (locking everyone out is worse than the attack)
//   /ai/generate → fail CLOSED (unbounded spend is worse than a brief outage)
@end
@senior
The cost model is what makes this a business control rather than a technical one — an AI endpoint that costs you real money per call deserves a quota expressed in the same units as your bill, and returning the remaining quota in every response lets well-behaved clients pace themselves instead of hammering until they are blocked. I would also point out that rate limiting is not abuse prevention on its own: credential stuffing needs account-targeted limits plus breach-password checks, and scraping needs behavioural detection. And every 429 should be a metric with route and plan labels, because the pattern of who is being limited tells you whether the limit is protecting you or breaking a legitimate customer.
@followup
How do you rate limit fairly across tenants?
@answer
Per-tenant buckets rather than a global limit, so one noisy tenant cannot consume everyone's capacity — that is the bulkhead idea applied to quotas. For shared resources like a database, I would add per-tenant concurrency caps and statement timeouts as well, because a rate limit on requests does not stop one tenant running an expensive query. And I would surface usage to customers, because a quota that is invisible until you hit it generates support tickets rather than upgrades.
@red
- IP-only rate limiting.
- Uniform cost for cheap and expensive endpoints.

### How do you handle file processing and media at scale?
@short
Never in the request path. The client uploads directly to object storage with a presigned URL, an event or a queued job triggers processing in a worker with bounded concurrency, and the result is written back to storage with the database updated on completion. The API only ever handles metadata.
@deep
The reasons are resource isolation and reliability: image or video processing is CPU and memory intensive, so doing it inline both blocks the event loop and makes your API's capacity depend on media workload. It is also slow enough to hit request timeouts, and a failed request means the user re-uploads the whole file.

Idempotency matters here too — an S3 event can be delivered more than once, so the processor must tolerate reprocessing the same object.
@arch Upload and processing pipeline
  Client ──1. POST /uploads/sign ──► API ── returns presigned PUT + key
     │
     └──2. PUT file ─────────────► S3 (bucket: uploads/, lifecycle: delete after 7d)
                                     │ 3. S3 event → SQS
                                     ▼
                              Worker service (own scaling, own CPU budget)
                                 • verify magic bytes, size, dimensions
                                 • sharp/ffmpeg → variants (thumb, web, avif)
                                 • write to S3 media/ (immutable, content-hashed key)
                                 • UPDATE assets SET status='ready', variants=… WHERE key=…
                                 • on failure → DLQ + alert; status='failed' so UI can show it
     │
     └──4. Client polls /assets/:id or receives a websocket/push event when ready
@end
@code ts The worker, with the guards that matter
export async function processImage(key: string) {
  const head = await s3.headObject({ Bucket: UPLOADS, Key: key });
  if (head.ContentLength > 20 * 1024 * 1024) return fail(key, 'TOO_LARGE');

  const body = await s3.getObject({ Bucket: UPLOADS, Key: key });
  const type = await fileTypeFromBuffer(await body.Body.transformToByteArray());
  if (!ALLOWED.has(type?.mime ?? '')) return fail(key, 'BAD_TYPE');    // magic bytes, not the header

  const pipeline = sharp(buffer, { limitInputPixels: 50_000_000 })     // ← decompression-bomb guard
    .rotate()                                                          // respect EXIF orientation
    .withMetadata({ exif: undefined });                                // ✅ strip GPS/EXIF privacy data

  for (const [name, w] of [['thumb', 200], ['web', 1200]] as const) {
    const out = await pipeline.clone().resize({ width: w }).avif({ quality: 60 }).toBuffer();
    await s3.putObject({ Bucket: MEDIA, Key: `${key}/${name}.avif`, Body: out,
                         CacheControl: 'public, max-age=31536000, immutable' });
  }
  await db.assets.markReady(key);
}
@end
@senior
Three security details in that worker are worth calling out: `limitInputPixels` prevents a decompression bomb — a 10KB PNG that expands to 50GB of pixels and OOMs the container; magic-byte validation prevents a polyglot file that claims to be an image; and stripping EXIF removes GPS coordinates that users do not realise their photos contain, which is a genuine privacy obligation. On the architecture side, the content-hashed immutable key means the CDN can cache forever with no invalidation, and reprocessing is naturally idempotent because the same input produces the same key.
@followup
How do you handle very large video files?
@answer
Multipart upload directly to S3 with presigned part URLs so the client can retry individual parts and resume, then transcoding through a dedicated service — MediaConvert or a job running ffmpeg on a queue with generous timeouts — producing HLS or DASH renditions for adaptive streaming. Delivery is via CDN with signed URLs if the content is private. What I would not do is proxy the bytes through the API or transcode synchronously; both are guaranteed to fail at scale.
@red
- Processing images inside the request handler.
- Trusting the client's content type.

### How do you structure logging so it is useful during an incident?
@short
Structured JSON with a consistent schema, a correlation id on every line, log levels used meaningfully, sensitive fields redacted centrally, and sampling for high-volume paths. The test is whether you can answer "what happened to request X" and "how many users hit this error in the last hour" without grep gymnastics.
@deep
Unstructured logs are effectively unqueryable at volume. Structured logs let you filter by user, tenant, route or error code and aggregate — which is the difference between finding a pattern in seconds and reading thousands of lines.

Redaction must be central, not per call site, or someone eventually logs a token. Pino's `redact` and a `beforeSend` hook in the error tracker cover the main paths.
@code ts A logging setup that survives an incident
import pino from 'pino';

export const logger = pino({
  level: env.LOG_LEVEL,
  redact: {
    paths: ['req.headers.authorization', 'req.headers.cookie', '*.password',
            '*.token', '*.cardNumber', 'user.email'],
    censor: '[REDACTED]',
  },
  formatters: { level: (label) => ({ level: label }) },
  base: { service: 'orders-api', version: env.VERSION, region: env.AWS_REGION },
});

// One child logger per request — every line inherits the context
app.use((req, res, next) => {
  req.log = logger.child({ reqId: req.id, route: req.route?.path, userId: req.user?.id, tenantId: req.user?.tenantId });
  const start = process.hrtime.bigint();
  res.on('finish', () => {
    req.log.info({ status: res.statusCode, durMs: Number(process.hrtime.bigint() - start) / 1e6 }, 'request');
  });
  next();
});

// Sample the noisy success path, keep every error
const shouldLog = (level: string) => level !== 'info' || Math.random() < 0.1;

// Propagate the id downstream so the trace crosses services
await http.get(url, { headers: { 'x-request-id': req.id } });
@end
@senior
Propagating the correlation id to downstream services and into queue job payloads is what makes this work end to end: a user reports a problem, you have one id, and you can follow it through the API, the worker and the third-party call. Without that, cross-service debugging is timestamp correlation and guesswork. I would also mention cost — logs are billed by volume, so an unsampled debug log on a high-traffic endpoint can cost more than the servers. Sampling successes while keeping all errors and all slow requests is the balance I use, and I make the sample rate runtime-configurable so it can be raised during an incident.
@followup
What do you log versus what do you trace?
@answer
Traces answer "where did the time go" across services and are sampled; logs answer "what exactly happened" for a specific request and carry the detail. I put timing and dependency structure in traces, and decisions, identifiers and error detail in logs, linked by the same trace and request id. Duplicating everything in both is expensive and confusing; the useful pattern is a trace that tells you which span was slow and a log line from that span explaining why.
@red
- `console.log` with string concatenation.
- Logging request bodies containing personal data or tokens.

### How do you handle configuration, feature flags and safe rollout in a Node service?
@short
Environment variables validated at boot for infrastructure configuration, and a flag service for anything that changes behaviour at runtime. Flags let you deploy code disabled, enable for internal users, ramp by percentage, and turn a feature off in seconds without a deploy — which is what makes frequent deployment safe.
@deep
The distinction matters: configuration is per environment and changes at deploy time; flags are per user or per percentage and change at runtime. Using environment variables as flags means every change is a deploy, which defeats the purpose.

Flags need lifecycle management. Without an owner and a removal date, they accumulate into a combinatorial explosion of untested code paths — a real source of bugs.
@code ts Flags with typed defaults, targeting and a kill switch
type Flags = {
  'checkout.razorpay': boolean;
  'ai.summaries': boolean;
  'orders.newPricingEngine': boolean;
  'exports.maxRows': number;
};

const DEFAULTS: Flags = {
  'checkout.razorpay': false, 'ai.summaries': false,
  'orders.newPricingEngine': false, 'exports.maxRows': 50_000,
};

export async function flag<K extends keyof Flags>(key: K, ctx: FlagContext): Promise<Flags[K]> {
  try {
    return (await client.variation(key, ctx, DEFAULTS[key])) as Flags[K];
  } catch {
    return DEFAULTS[key];                    // flag service down → safe default, never throw
  }
}

// Progressive rollout of a risky change, with both engines compared
if (await flag('orders.newPricingEngine', { userId, tenantId })) {
  const next = newPricing(order);
  const prev = legacyPricing(order);
  if (next.total !== prev.total) metrics.increment('pricing.mismatch', { tenantId });  // shadow compare
  return next;
}
return legacyPricing(order);
@end
@senior
