The cause I find most often in an ORM codebase is not a missing index at all — it is an N+1, where the slow "endpoint" is a hundred fast queries. That does not appear as a slow query anywhere; it appears as a high-count digest, which is another reason to sort by total time.
@code sql The diagnosis, in the order I actually run it
-- 1. WHERE IS THE TIME GOING? (sort by TOTAL, not average)
SELECT DIGEST_TEXT, COUNT_STAR,
       ROUND(SUM_TIMER_WAIT/1e12, 2) AS total_s,
       ROUND(AVG_TIMER_WAIT/1e9,  2) AS avg_ms,
       SUM_ROWS_EXAMINED / NULLIF(SUM_ROWS_SENT, 0) AS examined_per_returned
  FROM performance_schema.events_statements_summary_by_digest
 ORDER BY SUM_TIMER_WAIT DESC LIMIT 20;
-- examined_per_returned is the single best smell test. ~1 is a good index.
-- 4,000 means the server reads 4,000 rows to return one.

-- 2. WHAT PLAN DID IT CHOOSE?
EXPLAIN SELECT o.id, o.total_minor FROM orders o
 WHERE o.user_id = 42 AND o.status = 'paid' ORDER BY o.created_at DESC LIMIT 20;
--  type: ALL          <- full scan
--  key:  NULL         <- no index used
--  rows: 482913       <- estimate
--  Extra: Using where; Using filesort   <- and it sorted 482k rows to return 20

-- 3. WHAT ACTUALLY HAPPENED? (8.0.18+, executes the query)
EXPLAIN ANALYZE SELECT ...;
--  -> Sort: o.created_at DESC  (actual time=812..814 rows=20 loops=1)
--      -> Filter: (o.status = 'paid')  (actual time=0.1..790 rows=1204 loops=1)
--          -> Table scan on o  (actual time=0.05..610 rows=482913 loops=1)
--  The 610ms scan is the whole problem, and it is now measured, not guessed.

-- 4. THE FIX, and the verification
ALTER TABLE orders ADD INDEX idx_user_status_created (user_id, status, created_at DESC),
  ALGORITHM=INPLACE, LOCK=NONE;
--  -> type: ref, key: idx_user_status_created, rows: 40, Extra: Using where
--  -> the ORDER BY is now served by the index: no filesort.

-- 5. IF THE ESTIMATE IS WILDLY WRONG, THE STATISTICS ARE STALE
ANALYZE TABLE orders;

-- SESSION-LEVEL TRACE when the optimiser's choice is genuinely puzzling:
SET optimizer_trace = 'enabled=on'; SELECT ...;
SELECT * FROM information_schema.OPTIMIZER_TRACE\G   -- shows rejected plans and why
@end
@senior
The discipline I would show is measuring before and after on the same query with the same data, and checking that the fix did not move the cost. Adding an index makes reads faster and writes slower, and on a write-heavy table that trade can be a net loss — so after adding one I look at write latency and at whether the index is actually being used, because an unused index is pure cost. `performance_schema` and `sys.schema_unused_indexes` will tell you which indexes have never been read, and dropping those is often a bigger win than adding another. I would also name the `examined_per_returned` ratio as the metric I reach for first, because it is engine-independent reasoning that immediately separates "this query is slow because it does a lot of work" from "this query is slow because it does a lot of *unnecessary* work" — and only the second one is fixable with an index. And I would say explicitly that the slow query log alone is not enough, because the most expensive pattern in an ORM codebase is a hundred fast queries rather than one slow one, and no threshold-based log will ever show you that.
@followup
The query is fast in staging and slow in production. Why?
@answer
Almost always data volume and data distribution rather than the query. Staging has ten thousand rows where production has ten million, so a plan that scans is fine in one and catastrophic in the other — which is why I test index changes against a restored copy of production data rather than a development database. The other common causes are stale statistics leading the optimiser to a different plan, a buffer pool that holds the whole staging dataset but only a fraction of production's working set so reads that are memory-resident in staging hit disk in production, and skew: a query that is fast for the average user is slow for the one customer with two hundred thousand orders. That last one is worth checking specifically, because it produces the report where "the API is slow" is true for exactly one account and nobody can reproduce it.
@red
- Sorting queries by average latency rather than total.
- Reading `EXPLAIN` without knowing what `Using filesort` or `Using temporary` mean.
- Adding an index without measuring the write cost or checking it is used.
- Testing an index change against a development-sized dataset.

### How do you design a composite index for MySQL, and when does one stop being used?
@short
Column order is the whole design: equality predicates first, then the range or sort column last. The reason is the leftmost-prefix rule — an index on `(a, b, c)` can serve a query filtering on `a`, on `a, b`, or on `a, b, c`, but not one filtering on `b` alone, because a B+tree is only sorted by `b` within a given `a`. An index stops being used entirely when the predicate is not sargable — a function applied to the column, a leading wildcard, or an implicit type conversion — and each of those turns a fast index lookup into a full scan.
@deep
The rule that follows from leftmost-prefix is that an index on `(a, b, c)` makes a separate index on `(a)` redundant, and on a table with many indexes that is usually where the easy wins are: consolidating overlapping indexes reduces write cost and cache pressure without changing a single query.

The range-last rule needs stating precisely, because it is the part people get wrong. Once the optimiser uses a column for a range scan, no column after it in the index can be used for further filtering — the rows within that range are not sorted by the next column. So for `WHERE tenant_id = ? AND status = ? AND created_at > ?`, the correct order is `(tenant_id, status, created_at)`: two equalities, then the range. Putting `created_at` in the middle wastes the columns after it.

The same B+tree ordering is what lets an index serve `ORDER BY` without a filesort — but only if the sort direction is consistent with the index, which is why MySQL 8's descending indexes matter for a query that sorts one column ascending and another descending.

Sargability is the other half. `WHERE DATE(created_at) = '2026-01-01'` cannot use an index on `created_at`, because the index stores the column, not the function of it — rewritten as a half-open range it uses the index perfectly. `WHERE name LIKE '%smith'` cannot use an index for the same reason a phone book cannot find people by the end of their name. And the subtle one is implicit conversion: comparing a `VARCHAR` column to a number makes MySQL convert the *column*, not the literal, which silently disables the index on a query that looks completely ordinary.
@code sql Index order, and the predicates that quietly disable one
-- LEFTMOST PREFIX: one index on (a, b, c) serves a | a,b | a,b,c
CREATE INDEX idx ON orders (tenant_id, status, created_at);
  WHERE tenant_id=1                                  -- uses it
  WHERE tenant_id=1 AND status='paid'                -- uses it
  WHERE tenant_id=1 AND status='paid' AND created_at>? -- uses it fully
  WHERE status='paid'                                -- CANNOT use it
  -- => a separate INDEX(tenant_id) is now redundant. Drop it.

-- EQUALITY FIRST, RANGE LAST — this is the rule
--   (tenant_id, status, created_at)   equality, equality, range   CORRECT
--   (tenant_id, created_at, status)   equality, RANGE, equality   WRONG:
--     once created_at is used as a range, `status` after it cannot filter.

-- ORDER BY SERVED BY THE INDEX (no filesort)
CREATE INDEX idx ON orders (tenant_id, created_at DESC);   -- MySQL 8: real DESC
  WHERE tenant_id=1 ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 20;     -- index order = sort order

-- COVERING: add the selected columns to the tail to avoid the PK hop
CREATE INDEX idx ON orders (tenant_id, status, created_at, total_minor);
  SELECT total_minor ... -> Extra: Using index

-- ============ NOT SARGABLE: the index is silently ignored ============
WHERE DATE(created_at) = '2026-01-01'            -- function on the column
WHERE created_at >= '2026-01-01'                 -- FIX: half-open range
  AND created_at <  '2026-01-02'

WHERE LOWER(email) = 'a@b.com'                   -- function on the column
-- FIX (MySQL 8): a functional index
ALTER TABLE users ADD INDEX idx_email_lower ((LOWER(email)));

WHERE name LIKE '%smith'                         -- leading wildcard: full scan
WHERE name LIKE 'smith%'                         -- prefix: uses the index
-- for real substring search: FULLTEXT, or a dedicated search engine

WHERE phone = 9876543210                         -- phone is VARCHAR!
-- MySQL converts THE COLUMN to a number -> index unusable -> full scan.
WHERE phone = '9876543210'                       -- FIX: match the type.

-- LOW CARDINALITY: an index on a 2-value column is usually useless on its own
--   (status) alone -> optimiser prefers a scan. As a LEADING column in a
--   composite with a selective second column, it is fine.

-- FIND THE INDEXES THAT COST AND RETURN NOTHING
SELECT * FROM sys.schema_unused_indexes;
SELECT * FROM sys.schema_redundant_indexes;
@end
@senior
The judgement worth showing is that indexes are a budget, not a free win. Every index is written on every insert, update and delete that touches its columns, it occupies buffer pool that would otherwise cache data, and it makes bulk operations and online schema changes slower. So I design the smallest set that covers the actual query patterns rather than adding one per query — which in practice means starting from the digest list of real queries, grouping them by their equality predicates, and building composite indexes that serve several. I would also name the failure mode of the opposite instinct: tables I have inherited with fifteen indexes where `sys.schema_unused_indexes` showed nine had never been read once, and dropping those measurably improved write latency. The other thing I would raise unprompted is the implicit-conversion trap, because it is the one that produces a genuinely baffling incident — the query is correct, the index exists, the plan says full scan, and nothing looks wrong until you notice the column is a `VARCHAR` and the parameter is a number, which is exactly the kind of mismatch an ORM introduces without you writing a line of SQL.
@followup
When would you use a prefix index?
@answer
On a long string column where the leading characters are selective enough to do the filtering — indexing the first twenty characters of a URL or a long identifier rather than the full column. The reason is that MySQL has a hard index key length limit and a long `VARCHAR` index is expensive in both storage and cache, so a prefix gives most of the selectivity at a fraction of the size. The way to choose the length is to measure it rather than guess: compare `COUNT(DISTINCT LEFT(col, n)) / COUNT(*)` at several values of `n` against the full-column selectivity, and take the shortest prefix that gets close. The important caveat is that a prefix index cannot be a covering index and cannot serve an `ORDER BY`, because it stores only part of the value — so it filters, but the query still has to go back to the row. For an email column I would usually not use a prefix at all, since the leading characters are the selective part but the full column is short enough that the saving is not worth losing coverage.
@red
- Putting the range column before an equality column.
- Creating one index per query instead of composite indexes that serve several.
- Wrapping an indexed column in a function and expecting the index to work.
- Comparing a string column to a numeric literal.
- Never dropping unused indexes.

### Explain InnoDB locking, and how you would debug a deadlock in production.
@short
InnoDB does row-level locking, but it locks index records rather than rows — which is the detail that explains most surprising behaviour. Under the default `REPEATABLE READ`, a range query takes gap and next-key locks that block inserts into the gap, so two transactions can deadlock on rows that neither of them modified. To debug one, `SHOW ENGINE INNODB STATUS` reports the most recent deadlock with both transactions, the exact locks each held and waited for, and which one was rolled back; the fix is nearly always to make every transaction acquire locks in the same order, and to make them shorter.
@deep
The mechanism matters because it makes the fix obvious. If a query has no usable index, InnoDB has to examine every row, and it locks every index record it examines — so a missing index does not just make a query slow, it makes it lock the entire table in effect. That is why "add the right index" is frequently the actual answer to a locking problem, which is not where people look first.

Deadlocks themselves are not a bug to be eliminated; they are a normal outcome of concurrent transactions taking locks in different orders, and InnoDB detects the cycle and rolls back the cheaper transaction immediately rather than letting it hang. So the application must be prepared to retry a transaction that fails with error 1213, with backoff and a bounded number of attempts. An application that treats a deadlock as a fatal error will show users random failures under load.

The two structural causes are lock ordering and lock duration. Ordering: if one transaction updates order then inventory and another updates inventory then order, they will eventually deadlock — enforcing a consistent order, for example always updating by ascending primary key, makes the cycle impossible. Duration: locks are held until commit, so any external call inside a transaction holds row locks for the duration of a network round trip. Calling a payment API inside a database transaction is the version of this that causes real outages, because a slow provider converts into database lock contention.

The isolation level is worth knowing precisely. MySQL defaults to `REPEATABLE READ`, PostgreSQL to `READ COMMITTED`, so identical application code behaves differently on the two — under `REPEATABLE READ` a consistent snapshot is taken at the first read of the transaction, and gap locking is active, which is why phantom inserts are blocked and why the lock footprint is larger than people expect.
@code sql Reading a real deadlock, and the fixes that follow
-- THE DIAGNOSIS
SHOW ENGINE INNODB STATUS\G          -- section: LATEST DETECTED DEADLOCK
-- *** (1) TRANSACTION: UPDATE orders SET status='paid' WHERE id=101
--     WAITING FOR: RECORD LOCK, index PRIMARY of `inventory`, X locks rec
-- *** (2) TRANSACTION: UPDATE inventory SET qty=qty-1 WHERE sku='A1'
--     HOLDS: RECORD LOCK on `inventory`
--     WAITING FOR: RECORD LOCK on `orders`      <- the cycle
-- *** WE ROLL BACK TRANSACTION (2)
-- Read it as: who held what, who waited for what. The cycle is always visible.

SET GLOBAL innodb_print_all_deadlocks = ON;   -- log every one, not just the last
SELECT * FROM performance_schema.data_locks;         -- what is locked RIGHT NOW
SELECT * FROM sys.innodb_lock_waits;                 -- who is blocking whom

-- FIX 1: consistent lock ordering. This is the real fix.
--   BAD   tx A: orders -> inventory      tx B: inventory -> orders
--   GOOD  every transaction touches tables in a fixed order, and rows
--         in ascending primary-key order:
SELECT * FROM inventory WHERE sku IN ('A1','B2') ORDER BY id FOR UPDATE;

-- FIX 2: shorter transactions. Locks are held until COMMIT.
--   BAD                                   GOOD
--   BEGIN                                 -- call the API FIRST, outside the tx
--     UPDATE orders ...                   const r = await stripe.charge(...)
--     await stripe.charge(...)  <-- holds BEGIN
--     UPDATE ledger ...             locks   UPDATE orders ...; UPDATE ledger ...
--   COMMIT                                COMMIT

-- FIX 3: the missing index that was the actual cause
--   No index on the predicate -> InnoDB examines and LOCKS every index record.
--   UPDATE orders SET x=1 WHERE ref='abc';   -- ref unindexed = table-wide locking
ALTER TABLE orders ADD INDEX idx_ref (ref);  -- now it locks one record

-- FIX 4: RETRY. A deadlock is normal, not exceptional.
--   error 1213 ER_LOCK_DEADLOCK   -> retry the whole transaction, 3x, jittered
--   error 1205 ER_LOCK_WAIT_TIMEOUT -> a lock wait, not a cycle: usually a
--                                      long transaction holding locks. Find it:
SELECT * FROM information_schema.innodb_trx ORDER BY trx_started;
@end
@senior
The point I would lead with is that a deadlock is a symptom whose cause is usually somewhere less obvious than the two statements in the error. Three times out of four the underlying problem is a transaction that is longer than it needs to be — often because an HTTP call or a slow computation sits inside it — and shortening it removes the contention entirely without any clever lock ordering. That is also the fix that improves everything else, because long transactions in InnoDB pin the undo log and grow the history list, which degrades reads across the whole server. The second thing I would raise is that the retry has to be at the right level: retrying the failing statement is wrong because the transaction has already been rolled back, so the retry must re-run the entire unit of work, which means the business logic has to be idempotent and re-runnable. That constraint is worth designing for explicitly rather than discovering during an incident. And I would be clear that gap locking under `REPEATABLE READ` surprises people coming from Postgres — two transactions inserting different rows into the same range can block each other, which looks impossible until you know that InnoDB locks gaps rather than rows.
@followup
When would you use `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE`, and what is the alternative?
@answer
When I need to read a value, make a decision on it, and write it back with a guarantee that nobody else changed it in between — decrementing inventory, allocating a seat, consuming a voucher. `FOR UPDATE` takes an exclusive lock at read time so the read-modify-write is serialised, which is pessimistic locking, and it is the right choice when contention is genuinely likely and a conflict is expensive. The alternative is optimistic locking: read a version column with the row, and make the update conditional on the version being unchanged, so a conflicting concurrent write updates zero rows and the application retries. Optimistic is better when conflicts are rare, because it takes no locks and holds nothing across the read, and it works across a request boundary where you cannot hold a transaction open — an edit form where a user thinks for two minutes cannot use `FOR UPDATE` at all. In practice I use optimistic for user-facing edits and pessimistic for short server-side critical sections over contended rows. The one thing I would avoid is holding `FOR UPDATE` across any external call, for the reasons above.
@red
- Not knowing that InnoDB locks index records, so a missing index widens the lock.
- Treating a deadlock as a bug to eliminate rather than a condition to retry.
- Retrying the statement instead of the whole transaction.
- Any network call inside an open transaction.
- Not knowing MySQL defaults to `REPEATABLE READ` while Postgres defaults to `READ COMMITTED`.

### How do you run Sequelize against MySQL in production without it becoming the bottleneck?
@short
Three disciplines: control what SQL it emits, control the connection pool, and keep transactions explicit and short. Concretely — `include` with `separate: true` for `hasMany` so pagination is not broken by row multiplication, `attributes` specified so you are not selecting every column of every join, a pool sized to what MySQL can actually serve rather than an arbitrary number, and a query-count assertion in the integration tests so an N+1 fails CI instead of reaching production. And I read the generated SQL with `logging` on during development, because an ORM that you do not watch will write queries you would never write.
@deep
The N+1 is the dominant failure and it has two distinct forms in Sequelize. The obvious one is iterating a result set and calling an association getter per row, which produces one query per row. The subtler one is `include` on a `hasMany` without `separate: true`: Sequelize emits a single `LEFT JOIN`, which multiplies the parent rows by the number of children, so `limit: 20` returns twenty *joined* rows rather than twenty parents — the pagination is silently wrong, and the payload is far larger than expected. `separate: true` issues one query for the parents and one for all their children, which is two queries regardless of page size and is correct with `limit`.

Pool sizing is the second thing people get wrong, usually by making it too large. Each pooled connection is a thread on the MySQL side, and beyond the point where the server can execute them concurrently, more connections make everything slower rather than faster — queries queue inside the database instead of in the application, where at least you can see and control them. The number to reason from is the server's capacity and `max_connections`, divided across every application instance, with headroom left for administrative connections. And `acquire` should be short, so an exhausted pool fails fast with a clear error rather than adding a thirty-second delay to every request.

Transactions need to be explicit, and every query inside one must be passed the transaction object. The most common Sequelize bug I see is a query inside a transaction block that omits `{ transaction: t }` — it runs on a different connection, outside the transaction, so it neither sees the uncommitted writes nor rolls back with them. It produces data inconsistency that only appears when something fails, which is the worst possible time to find it. CLS/AsyncLocalStorage-based automatic transaction propagation removes the whole class of error and is worth enabling.
@code ts The Sequelize patterns that decide whether production holds
// ---------- POOL: size it to the database, not to a guess ----------
new Sequelize(url, {
  pool: {
    max: 10,          // per instance. 4 instances x 10 = 40 connections at MySQL.
                      // Check: SHOW VARIABLES LIKE 'max_connections';
                      // More is NOT faster — past the server's concurrency,
                      // queries just queue inside MySQL where you cannot see them.
    min: 2,
    acquire: 10000,   // fail fast on an exhausted pool; do not add 30s to a request
    idle: 10000,
  },
  logging: process.env.NODE_ENV !== 'production' ? console.log : false,
  benchmark: true,    // logs the duration of every query in development
  define: { underscored: true, timestamps: true },
  retry: { max: 3, match: [/ER_LOCK_DEADLOCK/] },   // deadlocks are retryable
});

// ---------- N+1, both forms ----------
// WRONG: one query per order
const orders = await Order.findAll({ limit: 20 });
for (const o of orders) o.items = await o.getItems();      // 20 extra queries

// WRONG IN A SUBTLER WAY: LEFT JOIN multiplies rows, so LIMIT is not 20 parents
await Order.findAll({ include: [OrderItem], limit: 20 });

// RIGHT: two queries total, LIMIT applies to parents, payload is what you expect
await Order.findAll({
  attributes: ['id', 'status', 'total_minor'],             // never SELECT *
  include: [{ model: OrderItem, separate: true, attributes: ['sku', 'qty'] }],
  limit: 20,
  order: [['created_at', 'DESC']],
});

// ---------- TRANSACTIONS: pass `transaction` to EVERY query ----------
await sequelize.transaction(async (t) => {
  await Order.update({ status: 'paid' }, { where: { id }, transaction: t });
  await Ledger.create({ orderId: id, amountMinor }, { transaction: t });
  // MISSING `transaction: t` = runs on a DIFFERENT connection, outside the tx.
  // It will not roll back. This is the #1 Sequelize data-integrity bug.
});
// Better: enable CLS so propagation is automatic and cannot be forgotten:
//   Sequelize.useCLS(new AsyncLocalStorage())

// ---------- THE GUARDRAIL THAT KEEPS IT FIXED ----------
test('GET /orders issues a bounded number of queries', async () => {
  const q = countQueries(sequelize);                       // hook beforeQuery
  await request(app).get('/api/v1/orders?limit=20');
  expect(q.count).toBeLessThanOrEqual(3);   // an N+1 reintroduced later FAILS CI
});

// ---------- WHEN TO DROP TO RAW SQL ----------
// Reporting aggregates, window functions, recursive CTEs, bulk upserts.
// The ORM is for the 90% of CRUD that is boring; fighting it for the other
// 10% costs more than writing the query.
await sequelize.query(sql, { replacements: [ids], type: QueryTypes.SELECT });
// replacements/bind — NEVER string interpolation. That is SQL injection.
@end
@senior
The position I would take is that an ORM is a productivity tool for the boring majority of queries and a liability for the interesting minority, and knowing which is which is the actual skill. I use it for CRUD, associations and migrations where it removes real drudgery, and I drop to raw parameterised SQL for reporting aggregates, window functions and bulk upserts, because expressing those through the ORM produces something slower and much harder to read than the SQL it is trying to avoid. The failure mode I would name is treating the ORM as a reason not to understand the database — the engineers who get into trouble are the ones who cannot say what SQL a given call emits, and the fix is simply to look: turn query logging on in development and read the output, and a whole class of problem becomes visible before it ships. I would also raise the query-count assertion specifically as the guardrail that matters most in an ORM codebase, because an N+1 is a one-line mistake to reintroduce, it does not show up in code review, and it does not appear in the slow query log — a test that fails when the count changes is the only thing that reliably catches it.
@followup
How do you handle bulk inserts through Sequelize?
@answer
`bulkCreate` with a batch size rather than a loop of `create`, because a loop is one round trip per row and a bulk insert is one statement for many — the difference at ten thousand rows is minutes versus seconds. I batch in chunks of around a thousand so a single statement does not exceed `max_allowed_packet` or hold locks for too long, and I wrap each batch in its own transaction so a failure does not roll back everything already written. For upserts, `updateOnDuplicate` maps onto MySQL's `ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE`, which is one statement rather than a select-then-insert race. Two caveats I would state: `bulkCreate` does not run individual hooks unless you ask it to, and `individualHooks: true` reverts it to per-row queries and destroys the benefit — so any logic in a hook needs to move into the batch operation. And for genuinely large loads, tens of millions of rows, I would leave the ORM entirely and use `LOAD DATA INFILE`, which is an order of magnitude faster than any statement-based approach.
@red
- Not knowing what SQL your ORM emits.
- `include` on a `hasMany` with `limit`, without `separate: true`.
- Forgetting `transaction: t` on a query inside a transaction.
- A pool sized far larger than the database can serve.
- String-interpolating values into a raw query.

### How does MySQL replication work, and how do you handle replica lag?
@short
The primary writes every change to its binary log; each replica has an I/O thread that copies that log and an applier thread that replays it locally. It is asynchronous by default, which means a commit on the primary returns before any replica has applied it — so a read routed to a replica immediately after a write can legitimately return the old value. That read-after-write problem is the one that actually shows up in an application, and the fix is to route reads that must see the write to the primary rather than to hope the lag is small.
@deep
The two things worth understanding are the format and the failure modes. Row-based binary logging, the modern default, replicates the actual before-and-after row images rather than the statement, which makes replication deterministic — statement-based logging replays the SQL text and breaks on anything non-deterministic like `NOW()` or `UUID()`. Row-based logs are larger but correct, which is the right trade.

Lag has two distinct causes that need different fixes. The first is applier throughput: historically a single applier thread replayed a multi-threaded write workload, so a busy primary could outrun its replica indefinitely — parallel replication with `binlog_transaction_dependency_tracking = WRITESET` lets independent transactions apply concurrently and largely solves it. The second is a single enormous transaction: a bulk update of a million rows commits on the primary in one operation and then has to be applied on every replica, so lag spikes for the duration. That is a schema and application problem, not a replication tuning problem, and it is why backfills are batched.

Semi-synchronous replication changes the guarantee: the primary waits for at least one replica to acknowledge receipt of the log before returning from commit. That bounds data loss on a primary failure to what no replica received, at the cost of adding a network round trip to every commit. It is a real trade rather than a strict improvement, and I would only take it where losing recent transactions is genuinely unacceptable.

GTIDs are what make failover manageable. Without them, promoting a replica means identifying a binary log file and position by hand and hoping. With global transaction identifiers each transaction has a cluster-unique id, so a replica can be pointed at a new primary and work out what it is missing automatically.
@code text Routing reads correctly, and monitoring lag properly
  THE TOPOLOGY
    primary --binlog--> replica 1  (reads)
            \--binlog--> replica 2  (reads, and analytics/reporting)
    Reporting on its own replica so a heavy analytical query cannot
    slow the replicas serving the application.

  READ-AFTER-WRITE — the bug this actually causes
    POST /orders          -> writes to primary, returns 201
    GET  /orders/:id      -> routed to a replica, 40ms later
                          -> 404 or a stale row. "It saved but it is not there."

    FIXES, in order of preference:
     1. Route to the primary for a short window after a write.
        Mark the session "recently wrote" (a request-scoped flag or a short
        Redis key keyed by user) and pin their reads to the primary for a
        few seconds. Simple, and it fixes the user-visible symptom exactly.
     2. Route by intent, not by verb: anything in a write flow, anything the
        user is about to see confirmation of, goes to the primary.
     3. Return the created resource in the write response so the client does
        not need to re-read at all. Frequently the best fix.
     4. Wait for the position: MASTER_POS_WAIT / WAIT_FOR_EXECUTED_GTID_SET.
        Correct, but it makes the request as slow as the lag.

  MONITORING — Seconds_Behind_Master is not enough on its own
    SHOW REPLICA STATUS: Seconds_Behind_Master, Replica_IO_Running,
                         Replica_SQL_Running, Last_Error
    It reads 0 when the applier has STOPPED, and it is measured against the
    transaction currently being applied, so it can look fine while an
    enormous transaction is midway through. Also track:
      - a heartbeat table written on the primary every second, and the
        difference between its timestamp and now, read on the replica.
        This is the number I would actually alert on.
      - GTID gap: retrieved vs executed set
      - replica disk and IO saturation

  THE OPERATIONAL RULES
    alert on lag > a few seconds AND on the applier not running at all
    batch every backfill — one huge transaction spikes lag on every replica
    never let the application silently fall back to a lagging replica
    know the failover story: GTIDs + an orchestrator, and that promotion
      loses whatever the replica had not received under async replication
@end
@senior
The senior framing is that replicas buy read capacity and disaster recovery, and it is important not to confuse them with either scale or a backup. They are not a backup, because a `DROP TABLE` replicates faithfully to every replica within milliseconds — only point-in-time recovery from a real backup plus binary logs protects against that, and I would say so unprompted because it is the assumption that turns a mistake into a company-ending incident. And they do not scale writes at all: every replica applies the full write workload, so adding replicas to a write-bound system makes it worse rather than better, and the answer there is sharding or a different data model. The other thing I would raise is that read-after-write is a product decision as much as a technical one — the correct behaviour differs between a payment confirmation, where stale data is unacceptable and the read goes to the primary, and a list of recommendations where three seconds of staleness is invisible. Deciding that per-endpoint rather than globally is what makes replica reads usable at all, because the alternative is either pinning everything to the primary and gaining nothing, or accepting stale reads everywhere and shipping confusing bugs.
@followup
How would you take a backup without impacting production?
@answer
Take it from a dedicated replica rather than the primary, so the cost lands somewhere that is not serving user traffic — that alone solves most of it. For the mechanism, a physical backup with Percona XtraBackup is non-blocking and much faster to restore than a logical dump at any real size, and `mysqldump --single-transaction` is fine for smaller databases because it takes a consistent snapshot without locking under InnoDB. The essential part is binary log retention alongside the backup, because that is what makes point-in-time recovery possible: restore last night's backup, replay the binary log up to the second before the bad statement. And the part I would emphasise most is that a backup that has never been restored is not a backup — I want a scheduled automated restore into an isolated environment with a row-count and checksum verification, because the failure modes are silent, and discovering during an incident that the backups have been failing for three weeks is a well-documented way for a company to die.
@red
- Calling replicas a backup.
- Expecting replicas to help with write load.
- Trusting `Seconds_Behind_Master` alone.
- No plan for read-after-write consistency.
- Never testing a restore.

# SECTION PostgreSQL Optimisation | The engine behind your pgvector and Golang projects — and where most "the API is slow" tickets are actually resolved.

### How do you read a complex execution plan and decide what to fix?
@short
Read inside-out, compare estimated rows to actual rows at every node, multiply by `loops`, and look at `Buffers` to distinguish cached reads from disk reads. Then decide between three fixes: give the planner a better index, give it better statistics, or give it a better query.
@deep
The most informative signal is estimation error. If the planner expected 1 row and got 200,000, it chose a nested loop that is now catastrophic — and the fix is statistics or a rewritten predicate, not a hint. If estimates are accurate and the plan is still slow, the fix is usually an index or genuinely too much data.

`Buffers: shared hit` versus `read` tells you whether the data was in cache. A query that is fast when warm and slow when cold points at working-set size rather than at the query itself.
@code sql Diagnosing a bad plan, step by step
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS, VERBOSE, SETTINGS)
SELECT o.id, o.total_minor, u.email
  FROM orders o JOIN users u ON u.id = o.user_id
 WHERE o.status = 'paid' AND o.created_at >= now() - interval '7 days'
 ORDER BY o.total_minor DESC LIMIT 50;

/* Limit  (actual time=2841.2..2841.3 rows=50 loops=1)
   ->  Sort  (actual rows=50 loops=1)
         Sort Key: o.total_minor DESC
         Sort Method: top-N heapsort  Memory: 41kB
         ->  Nested Loop  (actual time=0.1..2698.4 rows=184213 loops=1)
               ->  Seq Scan on orders o  (cost=… rows=421 width=…)      ← ESTIMATE 421
                                          (actual rows=184213 loops=1)  ← ACTUAL 184,213
                     Filter: (status='paid' AND created_at >= …)
                     Rows Removed by Filter: 2247769
                     Buffers: shared read=38102                          ← 300MB from disk
               ->  Index Scan using users_pkey on users u
                     (actual time=0.01..0.01 rows=1 loops=184213)        ← 184k index lookups
   Execution Time: 2841.9 ms */
@end
@code sql The two fixes the plan points to
-- 1. The estimate was 400× too low because status and created_at are correlated.
CREATE STATISTICS orders_status_created (dependencies, ndistinct)
  ON status, created_at FROM orders;
ANALYZE orders;

-- 2. Give it an index that serves the predicate AND the sort
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY idx_orders_paid_recent
    ON orders (status, created_at DESC, total_minor DESC)
 WHERE status = 'paid';          -- partial: much smaller, fits in cache

-- Result: Index Scan + top-N without a full scan; a hash join replaces the nested loop.
-- Execution Time: 24.1 ms
@end
@senior
Extended statistics are the tool most people do not know about, and correlated columns are exactly where the planner goes wrong: it assumes independence, so `status='paid' AND created_at >= …` is estimated as the product of two selectivities, which can be off by orders of magnitude. Creating a statistics object tells it the truth and often fixes the plan without any index change. I would also say what I would *not* do: disabling sequential scans or forcing a plan is a diagnostic technique, not a fix, because it will be wrong as soon as the data distribution changes.
@followup
The plan is good but the query is still slow — what next?
@answer
Then the query is doing genuinely necessary work on too much data, and the fix moves up a level: pre-aggregate into a rollup or materialised view, cache the result, paginate with a cursor instead of a deep offset, partition the table so only relevant partitions are scanned, or change the access pattern so the question is asked differently. At that point I would also check whether the work belongs in the request path at all — a dashboard query that takes two seconds should be precomputed, not optimised into taking one.
@red
- Adding indexes without reading the plan.
- Ignoring the gap between estimated and actual rows.

### How do you design indexes for a real workload?
@short
Start from the queries, not the schema. For each frequent query, build a composite index with equality columns first, then the range or sort column, and `INCLUDE` the selected columns when an index-only scan is achievable. Use partial indexes when queries always filter on a subset, and then audit for unused indexes because every one taxes writes.
@deep
Column order follows the access pattern: an index on `(a, b, c)` serves predicates on `a`, on `a, b`, and on `a, b, c`, plus sorts in that order — but not a predicate on `b` alone. Getting this wrong produces an index that looks relevant and is never used.

Index-only scans require the visibility map to be current, which means autovacuum must be keeping up; on a heavily updated table you may see an index-only scan degrade to heap fetches.
@code sql Index design driven by the query list
-- Query 1: user's orders by status, newest first
SELECT id, total_minor, status FROM orders WHERE user_id=$1 AND status=$2 ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 20;
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY ON orders (user_id, status, created_at DESC) INCLUDE (total_minor);

-- Query 2: admin queue of pending refunds (tiny subset of a huge table)
SELECT * FROM refunds WHERE status='pending' ORDER BY created_at;
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY ON refunds (created_at) WHERE status='pending';   -- 0.3% of the table

-- Query 3: case-insensitive email lookup
SELECT id FROM users WHERE lower(email)=lower($1);
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX CONCURRENTLY ON users (lower(email)) WHERE deleted_at IS NULL;

-- Query 4: JSONB containment on an event log
SELECT * FROM events WHERE payload @> '{"provider":"stripe"}';
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY ON events USING gin (payload jsonb_path_ops);

-- Query 5: time-range scan on an append-only table → BRIN is tiny and effective
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY ON location_points USING brin (recorded_at) WITH (pages_per_range = 64);

-- Then AUDIT: every index costs write throughput and disk
SELECT relname, indexrelname, idx_scan, pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size(indexrelid))
  FROM pg_stat_user_indexes WHERE idx_scan < 50 ORDER BY pg_relation_size(indexrelid) DESC;
@end
@senior
BRIN is the underused one for time-series data: on a naturally time-ordered append-only table it can be a few hundred kilobytes where a B-tree would be several gigabytes, because it stores only min/max per block range. It only works when physical order correlates with the indexed column, which is exactly the case for event and telemetry tables. The other habit I would describe is the unused-index audit — I have removed indexes that had never been scanned in six months and were adding measurable write latency to a hot table. Index design is an ongoing exercise, not a one-time schema decision.
@followup
Why did adding an index make writes slower?
@answer
Because every insert, update and delete must maintain every index on the table, and each one is additional random I/O plus WAL. A table with eight indexes pays roughly eight times the index-maintenance cost per write. There is also the HOT-update effect: Postgres can avoid touching indexes on an update if no indexed column changed and there is space in the page, so adding an index on a frequently updated column disables that optimisation and increases bloat. That is why partial and covering indexes — smaller and touching fewer updates — are often better than more indexes.
@red
- Indexing every column "just in case".
- Composite index column order that does not match the query.

### How do you handle high write throughput in Postgres?
@short
Batch writes, keep transactions short, reduce the number of indexes on hot tables, partition by time so recent data stays small, tune WAL and checkpoint settings, and move anything that does not need transactional guarantees out of the write path. If those are exhausted, shard or move the workload to a store designed for it.
@deep
The usual limiters are index maintenance, WAL volume and checkpoint spikes, and lock contention on hot rows. Each has a different fix: fewer or partial indexes; larger `max_wal_size` and spread checkpoints; and avoiding read-modify-write patterns in favour of atomic updates or per-key aggregation.

Connection count matters too — hundreds of concurrent writers cause context-switch overhead, so a pooler with a modest server-side pool often increases throughput.
@code sql Configuration and patterns for write-heavy workloads
-- Fewer, smaller indexes on the hot table; move reporting indexes to a replica
-- Partition so the "active" partition and its indexes stay in memory
CREATE TABLE events (…) PARTITION BY RANGE (created_at);

-- WAL / checkpoint tuning (postgresql.conf)
max_wal_size = '8GB'                  -- fewer, larger checkpoints
checkpoint_completion_target = 0.9    -- spread the I/O rather than spiking
wal_compression = on
synchronous_commit = off              -- ⚠️ per-transaction: faster, risks losing the last ms on crash

-- Per-session for a bulk job only: durability trade made explicitly and narrowly
SET LOCAL synchronous_commit = off;

-- Avoid hot-row contention: aggregate then apply, instead of N updates to one row
-- ❌ every request updates the same counter row → serialised
UPDATE stats SET views = views + 1 WHERE page_id = $1;
-- ✅ append and roll up periodically, or shard the counter
INSERT INTO stat_deltas (page_id, delta) VALUES ($1, 1);
-- rollup job: INSERT … SELECT page_id, sum(delta) … ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE
@end
@senior
`synchronous_commit = off` is worth discussing precisely because it is a real durability trade rather than free performance: transactions are acknowledged before the WAL is flushed, so a crash can lose the last few hundred milliseconds of commits. For telemetry and analytics writes that is entirely acceptable; for payments it absolutely is not. Setting it per-transaction rather than globally lets you make that choice per workload, which is the kind of nuance that shows you have operated a database rather than only queried one. The counter-sharding point is the other one — a single hot row is a serialisation point no amount of hardware fixes.
@followup
When do you know it is time to shard?
@answer
When a single primary cannot absorb the write rate even after batching, partitioning and index reduction, and vertical scaling has run out — or when the dataset no longer fits a manageable instance. Before that, read replicas handle read scale and partitioning handles table size. Sharding is a major architectural commitment: cross-shard queries and transactions become application problems, and rebalancing is painful. I would exhaust partitioning, archival, and moving analytical workloads to a separate system first, because most teams reach for sharding long before they need it.
@red
- Enabling `fsync = off` for performance.
- One hot counter row updated by every request.

### How do you manage bloat, vacuum and long-running transactions?
@short
Monitor dead tuples and table size, tune autovacuum per table for high-churn tables, and hunt down long-running or idle-in-transaction sessions, because they prevent vacuuming of anything newer than their snapshot. Bloat presents as a table that keeps growing while the row count is flat, and as queries getting slower for no apparent reason.
@deep
MVCC means an update writes a new row version and leaves the old one dead until vacuum reclaims it. Autovacuum triggers on a threshold proportional to table size, which on a very large table means it waits far too long — so per-table settings with a smaller scale factor are standard practice on hot tables.

An idle-in-transaction session is the classic silent killer: it holds a snapshot, so vacuum cannot remove any tuple newer than it, and bloat accumulates across the whole database.
@code sql Detecting and fixing the two most common causes
-- 1. Who is blocking vacuum?
SELECT pid, state, now() - xact_start AS xact_age, now() - state_change AS idle_for,
       left(query, 80) AS query
  FROM pg_stat_activity
 WHERE state IN ('idle in transaction', 'active') AND now() - xact_start > interval '5 minutes'
 ORDER BY xact_age DESC;

-- Prevent it globally
ALTER SYSTEM SET idle_in_transaction_session_timeout = '60s';
ALTER SYSTEM SET statement_timeout = '30s';          -- per role/session for reporting

-- 2. Which tables are bloated?
SELECT relname, n_live_tup, n_dead_tup,
       round(100.0 * n_dead_tup / NULLIF(n_live_tup + n_dead_tup, 0), 1) AS dead_pct,
       last_autovacuum, pg_size_pretty(pg_total_relation_size(relid)) AS size
  FROM pg_stat_user_tables
 WHERE n_dead_tup > 10000 ORDER BY n_dead_tup DESC;

-- 3. Tune autovacuum for a high-churn table (defaults are too lazy at scale)
ALTER TABLE orders SET (
  autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor = 0.02,     -- 2% instead of the 20% default
  autovacuum_vacuum_cost_limit = 2000,       -- let it work faster
  autovacuum_analyze_scale_factor = 0.01
);

-- 4. Reclaim space without an exclusive lock
-- ❌ VACUUM FULL locks the table completely
-- ✅ pg_repack rebuilds online
@end
@senior
`idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` is the setting I would put in place proactively rather than reactively. The pattern that causes it is almost always application code that begins a transaction and then makes a network call — to a payment provider, to an internal service — so the transaction stays open for seconds or, if the call hangs, forever. That single setting converts a database-wide bloat and lock problem into one failed request. It also pairs with the architectural rule: no external calls inside a transaction, use the outbox instead.
@followup
What is transaction ID wraparound and should you worry?
@answer
Postgres transaction ids are 32-bit and wrap around; vacuum "freezes" old rows to prevent ancient transactions from appearing to be in the future. If autovacuum cannot keep up — typically because of a long-running transaction, a stuck replication slot or a prepared transaction left behind — the database enters a protective read-only mode to avoid data loss. It is rare on a well-monitored system, but it is catastrophic when it happens, so I monitor `age(datfrozenxid)` and alert well before the threshold. Abandoned replication slots are the most common real-world cause.
@red
- Ignoring `idle in transaction` sessions.
- `VACUUM FULL` on a live table.

### How do you use partitioning effectively?
@short
Declarative range partitioning by time for append-heavy tables, list partitioning by tenant or region when queries always filter on it. The benefits are instant retention via `DROP TABLE`, partition pruning so queries touch only relevant data, and smaller indexes per partition that stay in memory.
@deep
Pruning is the main query benefit and it only works if the partition key is in the `WHERE` clause — a query without it scans every partition, which is worse than an unpartitioned table. So the partition key must match the dominant access pattern.

Maintenance needs automation: partitions must be created ahead of time, or inserts fail when the date moves past the last defined range. `pg_partman` or a scheduled job handles that.
@code sql Time partitioning with automated maintenance
CREATE TABLE events (
  id         bigserial,
  tenant_id  uuid NOT NULL,
  type       text NOT NULL,
  payload    jsonb NOT NULL,
  created_at timestamptz NOT NULL,
  PRIMARY KEY (id, created_at)              -- partition key MUST be in the PK
) PARTITION BY RANGE (created_at);

-- create ahead of time, always with a buffer
CREATE TABLE events_2026_08 PARTITION OF events
  FOR VALUES FROM ('2026-08-01') TO ('2026-09-01');
CREATE INDEX ON events_2026_08 (tenant_id, created_at DESC);

-- ✅ prunes to one partition
EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM events
 WHERE created_at >= '2026-08-10' AND created_at < '2026-08-11' AND tenant_id = $1;
-- Append → Seq Scan on events_2026_08 only

-- ❌ no partition key → scans EVERY partition
SELECT * FROM events WHERE tenant_id = $1;

-- retention: instant, no bloat, no WAL storm
DROP TABLE events_2026_02;

-- automate creation and retention
SELECT partman.create_parent('public.events', 'created_at', 'native', 'monthly',
                             p_premake := 3);
@end
@senior
The failure I would warn about is a query that omits the partition key. It is easy to write, it plans without error, and it silently scans every partition — so a table that was partitioned "for performance" gets slower. I check for it by reviewing the plans of the top queries after partitioning, not just before. The other operational note is that partitioned tables have limits worth knowing: unique constraints must include the partition key, and a very large number of partitions increases planning time, so monthly or weekly is usually right rather than daily for multi-year retention.
@followup
Partitioning or a separate time-series database?
@answer
Partitioned Postgres handles a great deal — tens of millions of rows per partition with good pruning — and keeps everything in one system you already operate. TimescaleDB is the next step: still Postgres, with automatic partition management, native compression that can reduce storage by an order of magnitude, and continuous aggregates. I would move to ClickHouse only when the workload is genuinely analytical — large scans, aggregations over billions of rows — where columnar storage changes the economics. The decision should follow measured pain, not anticipation.
@red
- Partitioning without the key in the query predicates.
- Forgetting to pre-create future partitions.

### How do you use Postgres full-text search, and when do you outgrow it?
@short
`tsvector` with a GIN index handles stemming, ranking and phrase search well, and keeps search in the database you already back up and secure. You outgrow it when you need fuzzy matching at scale, complex relevance tuning, faceting, or multi-language analysis beyond what Postgres offers — that is when Elasticsearch or OpenSearch earns its operational cost.
@deep
The implementation detail that matters is a generated column holding the `tsvector`, indexed with GIN, so you are not recomputing it per query. Weighting different fields (`setweight`) lets a title match rank above a body match.

Trigram matching (`pg_trgm`) complements it for typo tolerance and prefix search, which plain full-text search does not handle.
@code sql Weighted full-text search plus fuzzy matching
ALTER TABLE products ADD COLUMN search tsvector
  GENERATED ALWAYS AS (
    setweight(to_tsvector('english', coalesce(name, '')),        'A') ||
    setweight(to_tsvector('english', coalesce(brand, '')),       'B') ||
    setweight(to_tsvector('english', coalesce(description, '')), 'C')
  ) STORED;
CREATE INDEX ON products USING gin (search);

SELECT id, name, ts_rank_cd(search, q) AS rank,
       ts_headline('english', description, q) AS snippet
  FROM products, websearch_to_tsquery('english', $1) q      -- handles "quoted phrases" and -negation
 WHERE search @@ q
 ORDER BY rank DESC LIMIT 20;

-- Typo tolerance / prefix search
CREATE EXTENSION pg_trgm;
CREATE INDEX ON products USING gin (name gin_trgm_ops);
SELECT id, name, similarity(name, $1) s FROM products
 WHERE name % $1 ORDER BY s DESC LIMIT 10;
@end
@senior
`websearch_to_tsquery` is the function to use for user input, because it accepts the syntax people actually type — quoted phrases, `or`, leading minus for exclusion — without you writing a parser, and it does not throw on malformed input the way `to_tsquery` does. The broader point is consolidation: keeping search in Postgres means one backup, one security model, one consistency story, and no synchronisation pipeline between the database and a search index. That synchronisation is the hidden cost of Elasticsearch — a separate index that can drift, needs reindexing, and adds a failure mode. I would only pay it when the search requirements genuinely exceed Postgres.
@followup
How would you add semantic search on top?
@answer
`pgvector` in the same database: store an embedding per document, index it with HNSW, and combine vector similarity with the existing full-text score in a hybrid query — typically reciprocal rank fusion over the two result sets. That gives you keyword precision and semantic recall together, which is measurably better than either alone, and it still avoids introducing a separate vector database. That is exactly the architecture I would use for retrieval in a RAG system.
@red
- Computing `to_tsvector` at query time on every row.
- Introducing Elasticsearch before Postgres search was actually insufficient.

### How do you use `pgvector` for embeddings and similarity search?
@short
Store embeddings in a `vector(n)` column, index with HNSW for fast approximate nearest-neighbour search, and query with the distance operator matching your embedding model's metric — usually cosine. Filter with a `WHERE` clause on metadata, and tune `ef_search` to trade recall against latency.
@deep
HNSW builds a navigable small-world graph: build time and memory are higher than IVFFlat, but query performance and recall are better and it does not need a training step. `m` controls graph connectivity and `ef_construction` build quality; `hnsw.ef_search` at query time controls how hard it looks.

The subtlety is filtered search: a restrictive metadata filter combined with an approximate index can return fewer results than requested, because the index finds the nearest neighbours globally and then filters. Partial indexes per tenant, or over-fetching then filtering, are the mitigations.
@code sql A retrieval table with hybrid search
CREATE EXTENSION vector;

CREATE TABLE chunks (
  id          bigserial PRIMARY KEY,
  document_id uuid NOT NULL REFERENCES documents(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  tenant_id   uuid NOT NULL,
  content     text NOT NULL,
  tokens      int  NOT NULL,
  embedding   vector(1536) NOT NULL,
  fts         tsvector GENERATED ALWAYS AS (to_tsvector('english', content)) STORED
);

CREATE INDEX ON chunks USING hnsw (embedding vector_cosine_ops)
  WITH (m = 16, ef_construction = 64);
CREATE INDEX ON chunks USING gin (fts);
CREATE INDEX ON chunks (tenant_id);

SET hnsw.ef_search = 100;                    -- higher = better recall, slower

-- Hybrid: vector similarity + keyword, fused by reciprocal rank
WITH vec AS (
  SELECT id, row_number() OVER (ORDER BY embedding <=> $1) AS rank
    FROM chunks WHERE tenant_id = $2 ORDER BY embedding <=> $1 LIMIT 50
), kw AS (
  SELECT id, row_number() OVER (ORDER BY ts_rank_cd(fts, q) DESC) AS rank
    FROM chunks, websearch_to_tsquery('english', $3) q
   WHERE tenant_id = $2 AND fts @@ q LIMIT 50
)
SELECT c.id, c.content,
       COALESCE(1.0/(60+vec.rank), 0) + COALESCE(1.0/(60+kw.rank), 0) AS score
  FROM chunks c
  LEFT JOIN vec ON vec.id = c.id
  LEFT JOIN kw  ON kw.id  = c.id
 WHERE vec.id IS NOT NULL OR kw.id IS NOT NULL
 ORDER BY score DESC LIMIT 10;
@end
@senior
Two points I would make. Hybrid retrieval consistently outperforms pure vector search in practice, because embeddings are weak at exact identifiers — product codes, error codes, names — which keyword search handles perfectly; reciprocal rank fusion is a simple, robust way to combine them without tuning weights. And the tenant filter combined with an approximate index is a real correctness concern: if one tenant's chunks are a tiny fraction of the table, the HNSW search may return mostly other tenants' vectors which the filter then removes, leaving you with too few results. Partial indexes per large tenant, or a higher `ef_search` with over-fetching, is the fix.
@followup
When would you use a dedicated vector database instead?
@answer
When scale or features demand it — hundreds of millions of vectors, distributed sharding, or built-in features like multi-tenancy at index level and managed reranking. Below that, `pgvector` in the existing Postgres avoids a second datastore, keeps the embedding transactionally consistent with its source document, and lets you filter and join with relational data in one query, which is a genuine advantage over a separate store where you would have to fetch ids and then look them up. I would start in Postgres and measure before adding infrastructure.
@red
- Ignoring the interaction between metadata filters and approximate indexes.
- Using the wrong distance metric for the embedding model.

### How do you set up and monitor Postgres for production?
@short
Sensible memory and checkpoint settings for the instance size, `pg_stat_statements` enabled, autovacuum tuned per hot table, connection limits reconciled with your pool sizing, and monitoring on replication lag, cache hit ratio, long transactions, bloat and connection saturation. Then alert on the symptoms users feel, not on raw resource numbers.
@deep
The default configuration is deliberately conservative and is wrong for any real server. `shared_buffers` around a quarter of RAM, `effective_cache_size` around three quarters, and `work_mem` sized per-connection with the concurrency in mind are the first three to set.

`pg_stat_statements` is the single most valuable extension: it aggregates query statistics so you can rank by total time, which is how you find the query that is cheap individually and expensive in aggregate.
@code sql Baseline configuration and the monitoring queries that matter
-- Configuration for a 16GB instance
shared_buffers = '4GB'
effective_cache_size = '12GB'
work_mem = '32MB'                    -- per sort/hash node, per connection — be careful
maintenance_work_mem = '1GB'
random_page_cost = 1.1               -- SSD; the default 4.0 assumes spinning disks
effective_io_concurrency = 200
max_connections = 200                -- with a pooler in front, this is plenty
shared_preload_libraries = 'pg_stat_statements,auto_explain'
auto_explain.log_min_duration = '500ms'
auto_explain.log_analyze = on

-- Monitoring: the five queries I keep on a dashboard
-- 1. Top queries by TOTAL time
SELECT left(query,80), calls, round(total_exec_time) ms, round(mean_exec_time,2) avg
  FROM pg_stat_statements ORDER BY total_exec_time DESC LIMIT 20;
-- 2. Cache hit ratio (want > 0.99 on OLTP)
SELECT sum(blks_hit)::float/NULLIF(sum(blks_hit+blks_read),0) FROM pg_stat_database;
-- 3. Replication lag
SELECT client_addr, pg_wal_lsn_diff(pg_current_wal_lsn(), replay_lsn) lag_bytes FROM pg_stat_replication;
-- 4. Connection saturation
SELECT state, count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity GROUP BY state;
-- 5. Long / idle-in-transaction
SELECT pid, now()-xact_start age, left(query,60) FROM pg_stat_activity
 WHERE xact_start < now()-interval '1 min' ORDER BY age DESC;
@end
@senior
`random_page_cost = 1.1` is the setting that most often changes plans for the better on modern hardware: the default assumes random reads are four times the cost of sequential, which was true for spinning disks and is wrong for SSDs, so the planner under-uses indexes. `auto_explain` is the other high-value one — it logs the actual plan for any query over a threshold, so when a query is slow only in production under real data distribution, you have the plan without having to reproduce it. Together those two settings resolve a large share of "it is fast in staging" mysteries.
@followup
What is a healthy cache hit ratio and what if it is low?
@answer
Above about 99% for an OLTP workload; below that means the working set does not fit in `shared_buffers` plus the OS cache, so queries are hitting disk. The fixes in order are: reduce the working set (better indexes so fewer pages are touched, partitioning so recent data is smaller, dropping unused indexes), then add memory. A low ratio caused by a nightly analytical scan is normal and not worth chasing — which is why I look at the ratio alongside query-level `Buffers` data rather than treating it as a single target.
@red
- Running production on default configuration.
- Alerting on CPU rather than on query latency and lag.

# SECTION Redis & Caching Patterns | Beyond `GET` and `SET` — the patterns that hold under concurrency.

### How do you design a multi-layer caching strategy?
@short
Layers from cheapest to most expensive: browser and CDN for public content, an in-process LRU for tiny hot data, Redis as the shared cache, then the database. Each layer needs its own TTL and invalidation story, and the invariant is that a cache miss at every layer must still be survivable.
@deep
The in-process layer is worth having for data read on nearly every request — feature flags, tenant configuration, currency rates — because it avoids even the Redis round trip. Its danger is inconsistency across instances, so it needs a short TTL or a pub/sub invalidation channel.

Redis is the shared layer where correctness matters more, and it is where you implement stampede protection and negative caching.
@arch A layered cache with invalidation flowing outward
  Browser (Cache-Control: private/public, ETag)
      │ miss
  CDN  (s-maxage, surrogate keys)                    ← public content only
      │ miss
  App instance: in-process LRU (TTL 10–30s, or pub/sub invalidated)
      │ miss
  Redis (TTL + jitter, single-flight lock, negative cache)
      │ miss
  PostgreSQL
      ▲
      └── on WRITE: DB commit → DEL redis key → PUBLISH invalidate:<key>
                     → every instance drops its local entry
                     → revalidateTag / CDN purge for public content
@end
@code ts In-process layer with cross-instance invalidation
const local = new LRUCache<string, unknown>({ max: 5_000, ttl: 15_000 });

sub.subscribe('cache:invalidate');
sub.on('message', (_ch, key) => local.delete(key));       // every instance drops it

export async function cached<T>(key: string, ttlSec: number, load: () => Promise<T>): Promise<T> {
  const hot = local.get(key) as T | undefined;
  if (hot !== undefined) return hot;                       // layer 1

  const shared = await redis.get(key);                     // layer 2
  if (shared !== null) { const v = JSON.parse(shared); local.set(key, v); return v; }

  const fresh = await load();                              // layer 3
  await redis.set(key, JSON.stringify(fresh), 'EX', ttlSec + Math.floor(Math.random() * 30));
  local.set(key, fresh);
  return fresh;
}

export async function invalidate(key: string) {
  await redis.del(key);
  await pub.publish('cache:invalidate', key);              // fan out to all instances
}
@end
@senior
The pub/sub invalidation channel is what makes an in-process cache safe: without it, an update is visible on one instance and stale on the other five for the whole TTL, which produces the maddening "it works when I refresh twice" bug. The jitter on the Redis TTL is the other detail — without it, everything cached during a deploy expires simultaneously and you get a synchronised stampede at exactly the wrong moment. And I would always state the invariant explicitly: the system must work with an empty cache, just slower. If it cannot, the cache is not a cache, it is a database with no durability.
@followup
How do you decide the TTL?
@answer
From the business tolerance for staleness, not from a guess: how wrong can this data be before someone is harmed or confused? A product listing can be a minute stale; an inventory count at checkout cannot be stale at all; a currency rate might tolerate five minutes. Where staleness is genuinely unacceptable I do not cache the value — I cache the expensive parts around it and read the critical field live. Long TTLs with explicit invalidation on write beat short TTLs, because they give you both freshness and hit rate.
@red
- In-process caches with no cross-instance invalidation.
- Identical TTLs causing synchronised expiry.

### How do you implement a distributed lock correctly?
@short
`SET key token NX PX ttl` to acquire, a Lua compare-and-delete to release only your own lock, a TTL shorter than the work is long enough to be safe but long enough to avoid premature expiry, and — critically — the protected work must still be safe if the lock expires early. Redlock across independent nodes exists but is debated and rarely necessary.
@deep
The failure mode people miss is lock expiry during work: your process pauses for GC or a slow query, the TTL lapses, another worker acquires the lock, and now two processes are in the critical section. The token check prevents you deleting someone else's lock, but it does not prevent the overlap.

The robust answer is therefore defence in depth: use the lock to reduce contention, and make the operation idempotent or fenced so overlap is not catastrophic.
@code ts Lock with token, extension and a fencing token
const token = randomUUID();
const acquired = await redis.set(`lock:${key}`, token, 'NX', 'PX', 30_000);
if (!acquired) return { skipped: true };

// Optional watchdog: extend while we are still working (only if WE still hold it)
const EXTEND = `if redis.call('GET', KEYS[1]) == ARGV[1]
                then return redis.call('PEXPIRE', KEYS[1], ARGV[2]) else return 0 end`;
const watchdog = setInterval(() => redis.eval(EXTEND, 1, `lock:${key}`, token, 30_000), 10_000);

try {
  // ✅ fencing: a monotonically increasing token the downstream store checks
  const fence = await redis.incr(`fence:${key}`);
  await db.query(
    `UPDATE jobs SET status='running', fence=$2 WHERE id=$1 AND (fence IS NULL OR fence < $2)`,
    [key, fence]);                       // a stale holder's write is rejected by the DB
  await doWork();
} finally {
  clearInterval(watchdog);
  const RELEASE = `if redis.call('GET', KEYS[1]) == ARGV[1]
                   then return redis.call('DEL', KEYS[1]) else return 0 end`;
  await redis.eval(RELEASE, 1, `lock:${key}`, token);
}
@end
@senior
The fencing token is the part that makes this actually correct, and it is the standard critique of naive Redis locking: a lock alone cannot prevent a stalled process from writing after its lease expired, but a monotonically increasing token that the downstream store validates can. In many real cases the simpler answer is better — use the database's own locking (`SELECT … FOR UPDATE`, or an advisory lock) so the lock and the data are in the same system and the problem disappears. I reach for a Redis lock when the protected resource is not the database, such as preventing two instances from running the same scheduled job.
@followup
Is Redlock necessary?
@answer
Rarely. Redlock spreads the lock across several independent Redis nodes to survive one failing, but it depends on bounded clock drift and bounded pauses, and those assumptions have been credibly challenged. My position is: if losing the lock occasionally is merely inefficient, a single-instance lock with a TTL is fine; if it is catastrophic, you need fencing or a consensus system like ZooKeeper or etcd, not Redlock. Being able to state that trade-off honestly is better than reciting the algorithm.
@red
- `DEL` without checking the token.
- Assuming a lock guarantees mutual exclusion under pauses.

### How do you use Redis for sessions, presence and real-time fan-out?
@short
Sessions as hashes with a sliding TTL; presence as per-user keys with a short TTL refreshed by heartbeats, so a crashed instance's users expire automatically; fan-out via pub/sub for ephemeral messages or Streams when delivery must be durable. The design principle is that state must not depend on which instance a client is connected to.
@deep
Presence is the interesting one. Storing a set of online users breaks when an instance dies, because nothing removes its members. TTL-keyed presence self-heals: each connection refreshes `presence:<userId>` every 20 seconds with a 60-second TTL, so a dead instance's users disappear within a minute without any cleanup process.

For rooms, a sorted set scored by last-seen timestamp gives you both membership and automatic staleness handling in one structure.
@code ts Presence and fan-out that survive an instance dying
// Heartbeat-based presence — self-healing, no cleanup job
setInterval(async () => {
  const pipe = redis.pipeline();
  for (const userId of localConnections.keys()) {
    pipe.set(`presence:${userId}`, INSTANCE_ID, 'EX', 60);
    pipe.zadd(`room:${roomId}:members`, Date.now(), userId);
  }
  await pipe.exec();
}, 20_000);

// Who is online in this room, right now
const cutoff = Date.now() - 60_000;
await redis.zremrangebyscore(`room:${roomId}:members`, 0, cutoff);   // drop the stale
const online = await redis.zrange(`room:${roomId}:members`, 0, -1);

// Fan-out: publish once, every instance delivers to its own sockets
await pub.publish(`room:${roomId}`, JSON.stringify({ seq, type, payload }));

sub.subscribe(`room:${roomId}`);
sub.on('message', (channel, raw) => {
  const roomId = channel.split(':')[1];
  for (const ws of localRoomSockets.get(roomId) ?? []) ws.send(raw);   // only OUR sockets
});

// Durable variant when messages must not be lost on a brief disconnect
await redis.xadd(`stream:room:${roomId}`, 'MAXLEN', '~', 1000, '*', 'data', raw);
// client reconnects with its last id → XRANGE to replay the gap
@end
@senior
The TTL-based presence design is what makes the WebSocket layer genuinely stateless: any instance can crash and the system converges without a cleanup job or a leader. The Streams variant is the answer to pub/sub's fire-and-forget weakness — a client that reconnects after two seconds can replay from its last sequence id rather than losing messages, which is the difference between a chat that feels reliable and one that silently drops messages on a lift ride. I would size `MAXLEN` to the reconnection window you want to support, since it is memory you are paying for.
@followup
How do you handle a Redis failover in a WebSocket system?
@answer
The connections themselves are unaffected because they terminate on the application instances, but fan-out stops during the failover, so messages published in that window are lost with pub/sub. The mitigations are Streams with replay so clients can resume, client-side sequence numbers so gaps are detectable, and a reconnect-and-resync path. I would also make presence degrade gracefully — showing a stale online list briefly is acceptable, whereas showing everyone as offline is alarming.
@red
- A set of online users with no expiry.
- Relying on pub/sub for messages that must not be lost.

### How do you handle cache consistency with the database?
@short
Update the database first, then invalidate the cache — never the reverse. Accept that there is a small window of staleness and design for it. For anything where staleness is unacceptable, do not cache the value; cache the expensive computation around it and read the critical field live.
@deep
The classic race is: reader misses the cache, reads an old value from the database, and writes it to the cache *after* a concurrent writer has invalidated — so the stale value is now cached for the full TTL. The mitigations are a short TTL, delayed double-delete (invalidate again after a short delay), or versioned keys so a write bumps the version and old keys become unreachable.

Versioned keys are the cleanest: `product:v{version}:{id}` with the version in a fast lookup means invalidation is a single increment and there is no race at all.
@code ts Version-namespaced keys — invalidation without races
async function productKey(id: string) {
  const v = await redis.get(`ver:product:${id}`) ?? '1';
  return `product:v${v}:${id}`;
}

export async function getProduct(id: string) {
  const key = await productKey(id);
  const hit = await redis.get(key);
  if (hit) return JSON.parse(hit);
  const row = await db.product.findUnique({ where: { id } });
  await redis.set(key, JSON.stringify(row), 'EX', 3600);
  return row;
}

export async function updateProduct(id: string, data: Patch) {
  await db.product.update({ where: { id }, data });     // 1. database first
  await redis.incr(`ver:product:${id}`);                // 2. old keys become unreachable
  await redis.incr('ver:product:list');                 // derived views too
}

// Where staleness is unacceptable, do not cache the value:
const product = await getProduct(id);                    // cached description, images, etc.
const stock   = await db.stock.current(id);              // ← always live
@end
@senior
Version namespacing is my preferred pattern because it makes invalidation atomic and race-free, and it invalidates derived keys — list pages, filtered views — in one operation instead of trying to enumerate them. The cost is a second Redis read to fetch the version, which you can amortise with a short local cache of the version itself. The other point I would make is the last three lines: the correct answer to "how do you keep the cache consistent for inventory at checkout" is that you do not cache it. Choosing what *not* to cache is as much of the design as the caching itself.
@followup
What is a cache stampede and how do you prevent it?
@answer
When a hot key expires and many concurrent requests all miss and hit the origin simultaneously, potentially overwhelming it. Three defences: TTL jitter so keys do not expire together; single-flight, where the first miss takes a short lock and others wait briefly and re-read; and stale-while-revalidate, where you keep serving the expired value while one request refreshes in the background. The third is the best user experience because nobody waits, and it composes with the other two.
@red
- Invalidating the cache before committing the write.
- Caching values that must never be stale.

# SECTION Security in Depth | The controls that survive a real attacker, not just a checklist.

### How do you design a session and token architecture for web plus mobile plus third-party clients?
@short
One identity service issuing short-lived access tokens and long-lived, rotating, revocable refresh tokens. The web client holds them in httpOnly cookies with CSRF protection; mobile holds them in the Keychain and sends bearer headers; third parties use scoped API keys or OAuth client credentials. All three verify through the same middleware.
@deep
The tension is between statelessness and revocation. A pure JWT is fast but cannot be revoked; a pure session lookup is revocable but adds a datastore round trip to every request. The practical middle is a short-lived JWT plus a revocation check against Redis keyed on the token id or on a per-user "not before" timestamp — one fast lookup, immediate revocation, no database.

Refresh tokens must be stored server-side (hashed), single-use, and grouped into families so reuse detection can revoke an entire compromised chain.
@arch One identity plane, three client profiles
  ┌── Identity service ──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  issue: access JWT (RS256, 15m, aud per client) + refresh (opaque)│
  │  store: refresh_tokens(hash, family_id, used_at, expires_at)      │
  │  revoke: redis SET revoked:<jti> / user:<id>:notBefore=<ts>       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
        │                        │                        │
   WEB (browser)            MOBILE (native)          THIRD PARTY
   access  → httpOnly cookie access → Keychain       API key (hashed,
   refresh → httpOnly, Path=/auth   refresh → Keychain scoped, rotatable)
   + SameSite=Lax + CSRF token      + bearer header   or OAuth client_credentials
        └────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┘
                         ▼
              same verification middleware:
              signature → iss/aud/exp → revocation → req.user
@end
@code ts Revocation that stays fast
export async function verify(token: string): Promise<Claims> {
  const claims = jwt.verify(token, PUBLIC_KEY, {
    algorithms: ['RS256'], issuer: ISS, audience: AUD, clockTolerance: 5,
  }) as Claims;

  // Two cheap Redis reads cover both token-level and user-level revocation
  const [revoked, notBefore] = await redis.mget(
    `revoked:${claims.jti}`, `user:${claims.sub}:notBefore`);
  if (revoked) throw new Unauthorized('TOKEN_REVOKED');
  if (notBefore && claims.iat < Number(notBefore)) throw new Unauthorized('SESSION_INVALIDATED');

  return claims;
}

// Password change / suspected compromise / role downgrade → invalidate everything at once
await redis.set(`user:${userId}:notBefore`, Math.floor(Date.now() / 1000), 'EX', ACCESS_TTL_SEC);
await db.refreshTokens.revokeAllForUser(userId);
@end
@senior
The `notBefore` timestamp per user is the pattern I would highlight: it revokes every outstanding access token for a user with a single write, which is exactly what you need on password change, role downgrade or a suspected compromise — without maintaining a list of individual token ids. The TTL on that key equals the access-token lifetime, so it self-cleans. Combined with refresh-token families and reuse detection, you get near-immediate revocation while keeping the hot path to two Redis reads. And using distinct audiences per client means a web token cannot be replayed against the mobile API surface.
@followup
How do you handle token refresh races in a SPA?
@answer
Multiple concurrent 401s would each try to refresh, and with single-use rotation all but one fail and log the user out.
