The fix is a single-flight refresh in the HTTP client: the first 401 starts the refresh, subsequent requests await the same promise, and all retry with the new token. On the server side I allow a short grace window where the immediately-previous refresh token is still accepted, so a genuine race does not look like theft. Distinguishing a race from reuse is the hard part, and the grace window plus the family revocation on a clearly old token is the balance I use.
@red
- Long-lived access tokens because revocation is hard.
- Refresh tokens stored in plaintext server-side.

### How do you protect against the OWASP API Top 10 specifically?
@short
The API list differs from the web list: broken object-level authorisation, broken authentication, broken object property-level authorisation (mass assignment and excessive data exposure), unrestricted resource consumption, broken function-level authorisation, unrestricted access to sensitive business flows, SSRF, misconfiguration, improper inventory management, and unsafe consumption of third-party APIs.
@deep
The first and third are the ones automated tools cannot find, because they require knowing what data belongs to whom. They are also the most commonly exploited: an id in a URL that nobody checks ownership of, and a response that serialises the whole database row including fields the client should never see.

Business-flow abuse is the newer category and it is real: an endpoint that is correctly authorised and rate limited can still be abused if it allows automated purchase of limited stock or mass account creation.
@code ts The four controls that address most of the list
// 1. BOLA — object-level authorisation, scoped at the data layer
const order = await orders.findScoped({ id, tenantId: user.tenantId, userId: user.id });
if (!order) return res.status(404).json({ error: 'NOT_FOUND' });

// 2. Property-level — explicit allow-lists in BOTH directions
const UpdateBody = z.object({ name: z.string(), phone: z.string() }).strict();   // in: no mass assignment
const toResponse = (o: Order) => ({ id: o.id, status: o.status, totalMinor: o.totalMinor });  // out: no leakage

// 3. Resource consumption — bound everything
app.use(express.json({ limit: '100kb' }));
const List = z.object({ limit: z.coerce.number().max(100).default(20) });        // capped page size
// + per-endpoint cost-based rate limits, + statement_timeout on the DB

// 4. SSRF — never fetch a user-supplied URL naively
async function safeFetch(rawUrl: string) {
  const url = new URL(rawUrl);
  if (!['http:', 'https:'].includes(url.protocol)) throw new BadRequest('BAD_SCHEME');
  const { address } = await dns.promises.lookup(url.hostname);
  if (isPrivateIp(address)) throw new BadRequest('BLOCKED_HOST');   // 169.254.169.254, 10.x, 127.x…
  return fetch(url, { redirect: 'manual', signal: AbortSignal.timeout(5000) });  // no redirect chase
}
@end
@senior
The SSRF example deserves emphasis because it is easy to get subtly wrong: checking the hostname is not enough, since DNS can resolve to a private address, and following redirects lets an attacker bypass the check on the second hop. In cloud environments the target is usually the instance metadata endpoint, which can yield credentials — so this is a privilege-escalation vector, not just an information leak. Any feature that fetches a user-supplied URL — webhook registration, image import, link previews — needs this treatment, plus ideally an egress proxy that enforces it at the network level rather than trusting application code.
@followup
How do you prevent mass assignment in an ORM?
@answer
Explicit allow-lists rather than spreading the request body: a `.strict()` schema that rejects unknown keys, and constructing the update object field by field from validated data. Many ORMs also support a fields allow-list on update. The bug looks harmless — `await user.update(req.body)` — and the exploit is one extra JSON key, `{"role":"admin"}`. I treat any code that passes a request body directly into a persistence call as a blocking review comment.
@red
- Only checking authentication, never per-object authorisation.
- Fetching user-supplied URLs with no allow-list or IP check.

### How do you handle secrets, key rotation and encryption at rest?
@short
Secrets in a managed store injected at deploy, never in code or images; encryption keys in a KMS with envelope encryption so the data key is encrypted by a master key you never see; and rotation designed in from the start — multiple valid keys during the overlap, with a key id stored alongside every ciphertext.
@deep
Envelope encryption is the standard pattern: KMS generates a data key, you encrypt the payload with it locally, store the encrypted data key next to the ciphertext, and call KMS only to decrypt the data key. That keeps the master key in the HSM, avoids sending payloads to KMS, and makes rotation a matter of re-wrapping data keys.

Rotation only works if you record which key encrypted each record — otherwise you must re-encrypt everything atomically, which is impossible at scale.
@code ts Envelope encryption with a key id for rotation
import { KMSClient, GenerateDataKeyCommand, DecryptCommand } from '@aws-sdk/client-kms';

export async function encryptField(plaintext: string) {
  const { Plaintext: dataKey, CiphertextBlob: wrappedKey, KeyId } =
    await kms.send(new GenerateDataKeyCommand({ KeyId: MASTER_KEY_ALIAS, KeySpec: 'AES_256' }));

  const iv = crypto.randomBytes(12);
  const cipher = crypto.createCipheriv('aes-256-gcm', dataKey, iv);
  const ct = Buffer.concat([cipher.update(plaintext, 'utf8'), cipher.final()]);

  dataKey.fill(0);                                     // scrub the plaintext key from memory
  return {
    keyId: KeyId,                                      // ← which master key wrapped this
    wrappedKey: Buffer.from(wrappedKey).toString('base64'),
    iv: iv.toString('base64'),
    tag: cipher.getAuthTag().toString('base64'),
    ciphertext: ct.toString('base64'),
  };
}

// Rotation: new writes use the new master key; old records decrypt with their recorded key,
// and a background job re-wraps them at leisure. No big-bang re-encryption.
@end
@senior
Storing the key id per record is the detail that makes rotation practical — you rotate the master key, new writes use it, and a background job re-encrypts old records over days without downtime. Without it, rotation means re-encrypting the entire dataset in one operation. I would also make the point that the strongest control is not encrypting sensitive data but not holding it: card numbers belong in the payment provider's vault, and you store a token. Encryption at rest protects against stolen disks and snapshots; it does nothing against an application-level compromise, which is the far more likely attack.
@followup
What is the difference between hashing, encryption and tokenisation?
@answer
Hashing is one-way — used for passwords and integrity checks, where you never need the original back. Encryption is reversible with a key — used when you must retrieve the value, like a stored API credential for a customer integration. Tokenisation replaces the value with a meaningless reference held by a specialised vault, which is what payment providers do: you hold a token that is useless outside their system, so a breach of your database exposes nothing. Choosing the weakest sufficient mechanism is the goal, and tokenisation is usually the right answer for payment data.
@red
- Encrypting passwords instead of hashing them.
- No key id stored, making rotation impossible.

### How do you build audit logging that satisfies a security review?
@short
An append-only log of who did what to which record, when, from where, and why — written by the database or an immutable sink so application code cannot skip or alter it. It must cover authentication events, authorisation failures, privileged actions and data access to sensitive records, with retention matching your compliance obligation.
@deep
The properties that matter are completeness, immutability and usefulness. Completeness means writing at a layer nothing bypasses — a trigger or a database-level extension rather than sprinkled application calls. Immutability means the application's role cannot update or delete audit rows. Usefulness means the actor, the reason and enough context to reconstruct what happened.

Separating audit storage from application storage — shipping to CloudWatch, S3 with object lock, or a dedicated account — protects the log from an attacker who compromises the application.
@code sql Audit that the application cannot bypass or tamper with
CREATE TABLE audit_log (
  id          bigserial PRIMARY KEY,
  at          timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
  actor_type  text NOT NULL,          -- user | support | system | api_key
  actor_id    text NOT NULL,
  tenant_id   uuid,
  action      text NOT NULL,          -- order.refund | user.role_change | export.download
  resource    text NOT NULL,
  reason      text,
  ip          inet,
  user_agent  text,
  before      jsonb,
  after       jsonb
) PARTITION BY RANGE (at);

-- The application role can INSERT and SELECT — nothing else.
REVOKE UPDATE, DELETE, TRUNCATE ON audit_log FROM app_runtime;
GRANT  INSERT, SELECT ON audit_log TO app_runtime;

-- Context set once per transaction, read by triggers
SET LOCAL app.actor_id   = 'u_123';
SET LOCAL app.actor_type = 'support';
SET LOCAL app.reason     = 'customer requested refund, ticket #4821';
@end
@senior
The `reason` field is the one that turns an audit log from a compliance artefact into an investigative tool. "Support user X refunded order Y" answers what; "because of ticket #4821" answers why, and that is the question asked during an incident or a dispute. I make reason mandatory for privileged actions — the API rejects a support refund without one. The other point is that audit logs contain sensitive data by definition, so access to them is itself privileged and audited, and personal data in `before`/`after` must respect the same retention and erasure rules as the source records.
@followup
What do you audit for a data-export feature?
@answer
Every export: who requested it, the filter and the row count, the destination, and a hash of the produced file. Exports are the single most common vector for bulk data exfiltration by an insider or a compromised account, and they are usually authorised — so the control is detection rather than prevention. I would also rate-limit exports, cap the row count, require a reason, and alert on anomalies such as a support account exporting an unusual volume or exporting outside working hours.
@red
- Audit logs the application can update or delete.
- Logging the action but not the actor or the reason.

### How do you threat-model a new feature?
@short
Walk through what the feature adds — new data, new entry points, new trust boundaries — and ask what an attacker would try: who can call this, what can they see that is not theirs, what can they make expensive, what can they replay, and what happens if the input is hostile. Then decide which risks to control and which to accept, explicitly.
@deep
STRIDE gives a checklist — spoofing, tampering, repudiation, information disclosure, denial of service, elevation of privilege — but the practical version is a short exercise per feature rather than a formal document. The output should be concrete: three or four controls added to the implementation ticket.

The highest-value question is almost always about authorisation: which objects does this endpoint address, and how do we prove the caller may act on them?
@code text A worked threat model for "share a report by link"
  FEATURE: user generates a shareable link to a report
  ASSETS: report data (may contain revenue, customer names)

  Spoofing        Can someone guess a link?         → 128-bit random token, not sequential id
  Tampering       Can the token be modified?        → opaque random, server-side lookup
  Repudiation     Who created/opened it?            → audit: created_by, opened_by, ip, at
  Info disclosure Does the link leak in a Referer?  → Referrer-Policy: no-referrer on that page
                  Is it indexed by search engines?  → X-Robots-Tag: noindex, robots.txt
                  Does it expire?                   → default 7 days, max 30, revocable
  DoS             Can it be scraped/enumerated?     → rate limit per token AND per IP
                  Can it generate expensive reports?→ pre-render at share time, serve static
  Elevation       Does opening it grant more?       → read-only, scoped to that report snapshot,
                                                      never reuses the creator's session

  ACCEPTED RISK: a recipient can forward the link. Mitigated by expiry, revocation,
  optional password, and audit of opens — documented, not pretended away.
@end
@senior
The "accepted risk" line is the part I would emphasise, because a threat model that pretends every risk is eliminated is not credible. Shareable links are forwardable by design; the honest response is to bound the damage — short expiry, revocation, an audit trail of opens, and an option to require a password for sensitive reports — and to write that down so the product owner is making an informed choice. The other detail worth stating is `Referrer-Policy`: a shared link leaking through the Referer header to a third-party analytics script is a real and frequently missed disclosure path.
@followup
How do you prioritise which risks to fix?
@answer
By likelihood times impact, weighted toward anything that exposes other customers' data or grants privilege, because those are the failures that end companies rather than merely inconvenience users. Cheap controls with meaningful benefit go in immediately regardless of ranking — a rate limit or a scoped query costs minutes. Expensive controls need an explicit decision from someone accountable, recorded, so it is a business choice rather than an engineer quietly deciding not to bother.
@red
- Threat modelling as a document nobody reads.
- Claiming a design has no residual risk.

### How do you handle GDPR-style data protection requirements in an application?
@short
Know what personal data you hold and why, minimise collection, define retention per data type, and build the subject-rights operations — access, export, correction, erasure — as real features rather than manual database work. Encryption, access control and audit logging are the supporting controls.
@deep
Erasure is the technically hard one because personal data spreads: the primary table, denormalised copies, logs, backups, analytics warehouses, third-party processors and search indexes. A credible answer enumerates those and has a plan for each — usually anonymisation in place for records that must remain for financial or legal reasons, deletion elsewhere, and a documented backup expiry window.

Data minimisation is the cheapest control: data you never collected cannot leak, cannot be requested, and does not need deleting.
@code ts Erasure that accounts for every copy
export async function eraseUser(userId: UserId, reason: string) {
  await withTransaction(async (tx) => {
    // 1. Anonymise where records must be retained (financial/legal obligation)
    await tx.query(`UPDATE orders SET customer_email = NULL, customer_name = 'ERASED',
                    shipping_address = NULL WHERE user_id = $1`, [userId]);
    // 2. Delete where there is no retention obligation
    await tx.query(`DELETE FROM support_messages WHERE user_id = $1`, [userId]);
    await tx.query(`DELETE FROM location_points  WHERE user_id = $1`, [userId]);
    // 3. Tombstone the identity itself
    await tx.query(`UPDATE users SET email = concat('erased+', id, '@invalid'), name='ERASED',
                    phone = NULL, erased_at = now() WHERE id = $1`, [userId]);
    // 4. Queue downstream erasure — each processor gets its own job with retries
    await tx.query(`INSERT INTO outbox (id, topic, payload) VALUES ($1,'user.erased',$2)`,
                   [randomUUID(), JSON.stringify({ userId })]);
    // 5. Audit the erasure itself (this record is lawful to keep)
    await tx.query(`INSERT INTO audit_log (action, resource, reason, actor_type)
                    VALUES ('user.erased', $1, $2, 'system')`, [userId, reason]);
  });
}
// Consumers of user.erased: search index, analytics warehouse, email provider,
// CRM, support tool, CDN-cached exports. Backups expire on the documented schedule.
@end
@senior
The distinction between anonymising and deleting is the part that shows real understanding: you generally cannot delete an invoice, because tax law requires retaining it, so you strip the personal identifiers and keep the financial record. Backups are the other honest complication — you cannot surgically edit a backup, so the accepted answer is a documented retention window after which backups containing the data expire, disclosed in the privacy policy. And I would emphasise minimisation as the strategic control: every field you decide not to collect is a permanent reduction in risk, compliance burden and storage cost.
@followup
How do you handle personal data in logs?
@answer
Redact at the logging layer so it is structural rather than per-call-site — Pino's `redact` paths, a `beforeSend` hook in the error tracker, and a rule that identifiers rather than values go into logs (a user id, not an email). Then set a shorter retention on logs than on the primary data, because logs are copied into more systems and searched by more people. Any log that does contain personal data must be covered by the same access controls and erasure process as the database, which is a strong argument for keeping it out in the first place.
@red
- Erasure that only deletes the primary row.
- Personal data in logs with indefinite retention.

# SECTION Payments Engineering | Stripe, PayPal and Razorpay with webhook-driven order lifecycles — your strongest résumé claim, so expect the deepest questions.

### Walk me through the complete lifecycle of a card payment in your system.
@short
The client asks the server to create an intent; the server creates it with the provider, records a pending payment row keyed by the provider's intent id, and returns a client secret. The client confirms with the provider's SDK, so card details never touch our servers. The provider then sends a signed webhook, which is the authoritative event that moves the order to paid.
@deep
The critical design decision is that the *webhook* is the source of truth, not the client's success callback. The client can close the tab, lose connectivity, or be manipulated; the webhook is server-to-server, signed and retried. Treating the redirect as confirmation is the single most common payment bug.

The second decision is that the payment row exists before the money moves, in a `pending` state, so a webhook always has a record to attach to and an unmatched webhook is a detectable anomaly rather than a silent creation.
@arch The full lifecycle, with the three failure points marked
  1. POST /orders                → order(status=pending), amount computed SERVER-side
  2. POST /payments/intent       → provider.createIntent(amount, currency, idempotency-key)
                                    INSERT payments(intent_id UNIQUE, status='requires_payment')
                                 ← { clientSecret }
  3. Browser: provider SDK collects the card and confirms   ← PCI scope stays with the provider
       ├─ ⚠ FAILURE POINT A: user closes the tab here
  4a. Client redirected to /orders/:id?status=success       ← NOT authoritative, just UX
  4b. Provider → POST /webhooks/stripe  payment_intent.succeeded   ← AUTHORITATIVE
       ├─ verify signature over the RAW body
       ├─ INSERT processed_events(event_id) ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING   ← idempotency
       ├─ UPDATE payments SET status='succeeded' WHERE intent_id=…
       ├─ UPDATE orders   SET status='paid'      WHERE id=…  (only if currently pending)
       └─ INSERT outbox('order.paid')  → receipt email, fulfilment, analytics
       ├─ ⚠ FAILURE POINT B: webhook arrives BEFORE 4a, or twice, or out of order
  5. Reconciliation job (hourly): provider ledger vs our payments table
       └─ ⚠ FAILURE POINT C: webhook never arrived → repair from the provider's API
@end
@code ts The webhook handler, complete
app.post('/webhooks/stripe',
  express.raw({ type: 'application/json' }),          // RAW body — parsing breaks the signature
  async (req, res) => {
    let event: Stripe.Event;
    try {
      event = stripe.webhooks.constructEvent(
        req.body, req.get('stripe-signature')!, env.STRIPE_WEBHOOK_SECRET);
    } catch (err) {
      logger.warn({ err }, 'invalid webhook signature');
      return res.status(400).send('invalid signature');    // reject, do not process
    }

    // Ack FAST — Stripe times out at ~10s and will retry, causing duplicates
    res.json({ received: true });

    // Process asynchronously, idempotently
    await queue.add('stripe-event', event, { jobId: event.id, attempts: 8,
                                             backoff: { type: 'exponential', delay: 2000 } });
  });

// Worker
async function handleStripeEvent(event: Stripe.Event) {
  await withTransaction(async (tx) => {
    const first = await tx.query(
      `INSERT INTO processed_events (id, type, received_at) VALUES ($1,$2,now())
       ON CONFLICT (id) DO NOTHING RETURNING id`, [event.id, event.type]);
    if (!first.rowCount) return;                        // already handled — exit silently

    switch (event.type) {
      case 'payment_intent.succeeded': {
        const pi = event.data.object as Stripe.PaymentIntent;
        // Guarded transition: only pending → paid. Out-of-order events cannot regress state.
        const r = await tx.query(
          `UPDATE orders SET status='paid', paid_at=now()
            WHERE payment_intent=$1 AND status='pending' RETURNING id, user_id`, [pi.id]);
        if (!r.rowCount) { logger.info({ pi: pi.id }, 'no pending order — already paid or unknown'); break; }
        await tx.query(`INSERT INTO outbox (id, topic, payload) VALUES ($1,'order.paid',$2)`,
                       [randomUUID(), JSON.stringify({ orderId: r.rows[0].id })]);
        break;
      }
      case 'payment_intent.payment_failed': /* … */ break;
      case 'charge.refunded':               /* … */ break;
      case 'charge.dispute.created':        /* … */ break;
    }
  });
}
@end
@senior
Three things I would call out as the details that separate a working integration from a fragile one. The raw body: any global JSON parser mounted before this route silently breaks signature verification, and the failure looks like "the provider is sending bad signatures". Acknowledging before processing: providers time out quickly and retry, so a handler that does five seconds of work generates duplicate deliveries — ack fast, queue the work. And the guarded state transition `WHERE status='pending'`: webhooks arrive out of order, so a `succeeded` event landing after a `refunded` event must not resurrect the order as paid. That single `WHERE` clause is the whole out-of-order defence.
@followup
What if the webhook never arrives?
@answer
A reconciliation job closes the gap. Any payment still `pending` after a grace period — say fifteen minutes — is queried directly against the provider's API, and the state is repaired from their answer, which is authoritative. I run that continuously for recent payments and as a daily sweep over the previous day's ledger. Webhooks are best-effort even from excellent providers: outages happen, our endpoint can be down during a deploy, and a delivery can exhaust its retries. Without reconciliation, those become customers charged with no order, which is the worst failure mode in the system.
@red
- Treating the client redirect as confirmation.
- Processing synchronously inside the webhook handler.
- No reconciliation job.

### How do you make payment operations idempotent end to end?
@short
Three layers: an idempotency key on the API request so a client retry does not create two payments, the provider's own idempotency key so a retried provider call does not double-charge, and a unique constraint plus a processed-events table so a duplicate webhook is a no-op. Every layer assumes the others might fail.
@deep
Each layer covers a different retry. The client retries because the response was lost; our server retries the provider call because the connection timed out; the provider retries the webhook because our ack was lost. Without all three, one of those becomes a duplicate charge or a duplicate order.

The database constraints are what make it structural: `payments.intent_id UNIQUE` and `processed_events.id PRIMARY KEY` turn a logic race into a caught constraint violation.
@code sql The schema that makes double-charging structurally impossible
CREATE TABLE payments (
  id             uuid PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
  order_id       uuid NOT NULL REFERENCES orders(id),
  provider       text NOT NULL CHECK (provider IN ('stripe','razorpay','paypal')),
  intent_id      text NOT NULL,
  amount_minor   bigint NOT NULL CHECK (amount_minor > 0),
  currency       char(3) NOT NULL,
  status         text NOT NULL,
  created_at     timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
  UNIQUE (provider, intent_id)                    -- one payment row per provider intent
);
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX one_active_payment_per_order
  ON payments (order_id) WHERE status IN ('requires_payment','processing','succeeded');

CREATE TABLE processed_events (
  id          text PRIMARY KEY,                    -- the PROVIDER's event id
  type        text NOT NULL,
  received_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now()
);

CREATE TABLE idempotency_keys (
  key           text PRIMARY KEY,
  request_hash  text NOT NULL,
  status        text NOT NULL,                      -- in_progress | completed
  response_code int, response_body jsonb,
  created_at    timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now()
);
@end
@code ts Passing idempotency through to the provider
const key = req.header('Idempotency-Key') ?? randomUUID();

const intent = await stripe.paymentIntents.create(
  { amount: order.totalMinor, currency: order.currency, metadata: { orderId: order.id } },
  { idempotencyKey: `intent:${order.id}` }          // ← provider-side: retry returns the SAME intent
);

await db.query(
  `INSERT INTO payments (order_id, provider, intent_id, amount_minor, currency, status)
   VALUES ($1,'stripe',$2,$3,$4,'requires_payment')
   ON CONFLICT (provider, intent_id) DO NOTHING`,   // ← our side: replay is a no-op
  [order.id, intent.id, order.totalMinor, order.currency]);
@end
@senior
Deriving the provider idempotency key from the order id rather than generating a fresh UUID per attempt is the detail that makes retries genuinely safe: if our process crashes after calling Stripe but before writing the payment row, the retry produces the same intent rather than a second one, and the `ON CONFLICT` then reconciles our record. I would also mention that idempotency keys must have a TTL and a cleanup job, and that Stripe scopes them for 24 hours — so a retry a week later legitimately creates a new payment, which is why the partial unique index on active payments per order is the backstop.
@followup
A customer says they were charged twice — how do you investigate?
@answer
Start from the provider's dashboard, which is authoritative for money movement: are there genuinely two charges, or one charge and one authorisation hold that will drop off? Then match against our `payments` rows by intent id and look at the audit and request logs for two distinct intent creations — which usually means a missing idempotency key or a client that retried a create call. If it is a genuine double charge, refund immediately, then fix the cause. The reconciliation job should already have flagged it, and if it did not, that is a second bug to fix.
@red
- One layer of idempotency only.
- Generating a new provider idempotency key on each retry.

### How do you support three payment providers without three copies of your business logic?
@short
A provider interface that all three implement — create intent, verify webhook, capture, refund, translate status — with provider-specific quirks contained inside each adapter. The order lifecycle, the state machine and the database schema are provider-agnostic, keyed on a `(provider, intent_id)` pair.
@deep
The differences are real and awkward: Stripe signs webhooks with HMAC over a timestamped payload, Razorpay with HMAC over the raw body, PayPal with a certificate-based verification requiring an API call. Currency handling differs — Razorpay is paise-only, PayPal expects decimal strings. Refund semantics and status vocabularies all differ.

The adapter's job is to normalise all of that into one internal event vocabulary so the domain never branches on provider.
@code ts One interface, three adapters, one domain
export type PaymentEvent =
  | { kind: 'succeeded'; intentId: string; amountMinor: number; currency: string }
  | { kind: 'failed';    intentId: string; code: string; message: string }
  | { kind: 'refunded';  intentId: string; refundId: string; amountMinor: number }
  | { kind: 'disputed';  intentId: string; disputeId: string; reason: string }
  | { kind: 'ignored';   raw: string };

export interface PaymentProvider {
  readonly name: 'stripe' | 'razorpay' | 'paypal';
  createIntent(o: Order, idemKey: string): Promise<{ intentId: string; clientSecret: string }>;
  verifyWebhook(rawBody: Buffer, headers: IncomingHttpHeaders): Promise<{ eventId: string; event: PaymentEvent }>;
  refund(intentId: string, amountMinor: number, idemKey: string): Promise<{ refundId: string }>;
}

class RazorpayProvider implements PaymentProvider {
  readonly name = 'razorpay' as const;

  async verifyWebhook(raw: Buffer, headers) {
    const expected = crypto.createHmac('sha256', env.RAZORPAY_WEBHOOK_SECRET)
      .update(raw).digest('hex');
    const got = String(headers['x-razorpay-signature'] ?? '');
    if (!crypto.timingSafeEqual(Buffer.from(expected), Buffer.from(got)))
      throw new Unauthorized('BAD_SIGNATURE');

    const body = JSON.parse(raw.toString());
    const id = String(headers['x-razorpay-event-id']);
    switch (body.event) {
      case 'payment.captured':
        return { eventId: id, event: { kind: 'succeeded',
          intentId: body.payload.payment.entity.order_id,
          amountMinor: body.payload.payment.entity.amount,     // already paise
          currency: body.payload.payment.entity.currency } };
      case 'payment.failed':
        return { eventId: id, event: { kind: 'failed', intentId: …, code: …, message: … } };
      default:
        return { eventId: id, event: { kind: 'ignored', raw: body.event } };
    }
  }
}

// The domain never branches on provider:
const { eventId, event } = await providers.get(req.params.provider)!.verifyWebhook(req.body, req.headers);
await applyPaymentEvent(eventId, event);
@end
@senior
Normalising to an internal event vocabulary is what keeps the order state machine sane — otherwise the transition logic accumulates provider conditionals and every new provider risks breaking the existing two. The `ignored` variant matters too: providers send dozens of event types, and silently ignoring unknown ones while still recording them as processed prevents infinite retries of events you do not care about. I would also point out the currency trap: Stripe and Razorpay use minor units but PayPal uses decimal strings, and mixing them is exactly the kind of bug that charges someone a hundred times too much — which is why the internal representation is always integer minor units plus a currency code, converted only inside the adapter.
@followup
How do you choose which provider to route a payment to?
@answer
By method and geography first — UPI and netbanking in India route to Razorpay, international cards to Stripe, and PayPal where the customer prefers it — and by cost and success rate second. Routing rules live in configuration, not code, so they can change without a deploy, and I record the chosen provider on the payment row so reconciliation and support know where to look. Some teams add automatic failover to a second provider on a technical decline, which is valuable but needs care: retrying a genuine decline across providers looks like card testing and can get you flagged.
@red
- Provider conditionals scattered through the order logic.
- Assuming all providers use minor units.

### How do you design the order and payment state machine?
@short
Explicit states with explicit allowed transitions, enforced in the database with guarded updates rather than in application `if` statements. Payment state and order state are separate machines that influence each other, because a payment can succeed while fulfilment fails and both facts must be representable.
@deep
Modelling the transitions explicitly is what makes out-of-order webhooks safe. Every update is conditional on the current state, so an event that would cause an illegal transition simply affects zero rows and is logged rather than corrupting state.

Separating payment from order state avoids the trap of a single status column that has to encode both "money received" and "goods dispatched", which quickly becomes fifteen ambiguous values.
@arch Two machines, clearly separated
  PAYMENT                                     ORDER
  requires_payment ──► processing ──► succeeded    pending ──► paid ──► fulfilled ──► completed
        │                   │             │            │         │          │
        └──► failed ◄───────┘             ├──► refunded│         └──► cancelled
                                          └──► disputed│              (restock, notify)
                                                        └──► refund_pending ──► refunded

  Rules:
   • order.paid requires payment.succeeded          (never the reverse)
   • refunded is terminal for the payment, not necessarily for the order (partial refunds)
   • disputed does NOT change the order state — it opens a separate case record
   • every transition writes an order_events row: from, to, cause, actor, event_id
@end
@code sql Transitions the database enforces
CREATE TABLE order_events (
  id         bigserial PRIMARY KEY,
  order_id   uuid NOT NULL REFERENCES orders(id),
  from_status text NOT NULL,
  to_status   text NOT NULL,
  cause       text NOT NULL,        -- webhook event id, user action, reconciliation job
  actor       text NOT NULL,
  at          timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now()
);

-- Guarded transition: illegal or duplicate transitions affect ZERO rows
WITH allowed AS (
  SELECT * FROM (VALUES
    ('pending','paid'), ('paid','fulfilled'), ('fulfilled','completed'),
    ('pending','cancelled'), ('paid','refund_pending'), ('refund_pending','refunded')
  ) AS t(from_status, to_status)
), moved AS (
  UPDATE orders o SET status = $2, updated_at = now()
    FROM allowed a
   WHERE o.id = $1 AND o.status = a.from_status AND a.to_status = $2
  RETURNING o.id, a.from_status
)
INSERT INTO order_events (order_id, from_status, to_status, cause, actor)
SELECT id, from_status, $2, $3, $4 FROM moved
RETURNING order_id;      -- zero rows = transition rejected, log and move on
@end
@senior
The `order_events` table is the artefact that makes payment support tractable: when a customer disputes what happened, you can show the exact sequence with the causing webhook event id at each step. It is also how you debug an out-of-order webhook — the event log shows the rejected transition rather than silently swallowing it. And separating dispute handling from the order state is a real modelling decision: a chargeback is a case with its own lifecycle, evidence deadlines and outcomes, and collapsing it into the order status loses all of that.
@followup
How do you handle partial refunds and partial fulfilment?
@answer
Refunds become their own records with their own amounts and states, and the order derives its refunded total by summing them rather than holding a single boolean. The same applies to fulfilment: a shipment table with line items lets an order be partially shipped. Encoding these as single columns on the order is what forces the eventual painful migration. The invariant to enforce is that the sum of refunds never exceeds the captured amount, which is a check constraint or a guarded update, not an application-level check.
@red
- One status column encoding payment and fulfilment together.
- Unguarded status updates that out-of-order events can regress.

### How do you handle refunds, disputes and chargebacks?
@short
Refunds are initiated by us and confirmed by webhook, exactly like payments — request, pending state, webhook confirms, order updated. Disputes are initiated by the customer's bank, arrive as a webhook with a deadline, require evidence submission, and can result in funds being withdrawn regardless of the merits.
@deep
The operational difference is that a dispute has a clock. You typically have a limited window to submit evidence, and missing it means automatic loss. So a dispute webhook must create a task with a deadline, notify the right people, and gather the evidence the provider expects — delivery confirmation, customer communication, the terms accepted at checkout.

Chargebacks also carry fees and, if the rate gets too high, the risk of losing your merchant account — which makes prevention a business concern, not just an engineering one.
@code ts Refund with idempotency, and a dispute that creates a deadline-bound case
export async function requestRefund(orderId: OrderId, amountMinor: Minor, actor: Actor, reason: string) {
  return withTransaction(async (tx) => {
    const order = await orders.findForUpdate(orderId, tx);
    const refunded = await refunds.sumSucceeded(orderId, tx);
    if (refunded + amountMinor > order.capturedMinor) return err('AMOUNT_EXCEEDS_CAPTURED');

    const refund = await refunds.insert({ orderId, amountMinor, status: 'pending', actor, reason }, tx);
    await orders.transition(orderId, 'refund_pending', `refund:${refund.id}`, actor, tx);
    await outbox.publish('refund.requested', { refundId: refund.id }, tx);   // provider call happens async
    return ok(refund);
  });
}

// Dispute webhook → a case with a deadline, not a status flag
case 'charge.dispute.created': {
  const d = event.data.object as Stripe.Dispute;
  await tx.query(
    `INSERT INTO disputes (id, order_id, amount_minor, reason, status, evidence_due_at)
     VALUES ($1,$2,$3,$4,'needs_response',to_timestamp($5))
     ON CONFLICT (id) DO NOTHING`,
    [d.id, orderId, d.amount, d.reason, d.evidence_details.due_by]);
  await outbox.publish('dispute.opened', { disputeId: d.id, dueAt: d.evidence_details.due_by }, tx);
  break;                        // ← order status unchanged; this is a separate case
}
@end
@senior
Two points. Refunds must go through the same asynchronous, webhook-confirmed path as payments — calling the provider inside the request transaction is the classic mistake, because a timeout leaves you unsure whether the refund happened while your transaction rolled back. The outbox makes the provider call idempotent and retryable outside the transaction. On disputes, the deadline is the thing to engineer around: an alert at creation, a reminder before the due date, and evidence assembled automatically from data you already have. Losing a dispute by default because nobody saw the webhook is an avoidable and expensive failure.
@followup
How do you reduce chargebacks?
@answer
Mostly through product and operations rather than code: a clear billing descriptor so customers recognise the charge, an obvious and easy refund path so they contact you instead of their bank, order confirmation emails with support contact details, delivery tracking as evidence, and 3D Secure for risky transactions, which shifts liability to the issuer. On the engineering side, radar-style fraud rules and velocity checks catch card testing. The metric to watch is the dispute rate against the network thresholds, because exceeding them threatens the merchant account itself.
@red
- Calling the provider's refund API inside a database transaction.
- Treating a dispute as just another order status.

### How do you reconcile your records with the provider's ledger?
@short
A scheduled job that pulls the provider's transactions for a window and compares them with our payments table in both directions: charges they have that we do not, and payments we think succeeded that they have no record of. Every mismatch becomes an alert with enough context for a human to resolve it.
@deep
Both directions matter and they indicate different bugs. A charge at the provider with no local record means a webhook was lost or our handler failed — the customer paid and has nothing, which is urgent. A local success with no provider charge means a state bug on our side, or a test-mode key in production.

Amount and currency mismatches are the third category and usually indicate a rounding or unit-conversion bug, which is exactly why amounts are stored in minor units.
@code ts Two-way reconciliation with actionable output
export async function reconcile(day: Date) {
  const [providerTxns, localPayments] = await Promise.all([
    stripe.charges.list({ created: { gte: startOf(day), lt: endOf(day) }, limit: 100 }).autoPagingToArray({ limit: 10_000 }),
    db.payments.forDay(day, 'stripe'),
  ]);

  const local = new Map(localPayments.map(p => [p.intentId, p]));
  const remote = new Map(providerTxns.map(c => [c.payment_intent as string, c]));
  const issues: Issue[] = [];

  for (const [intentId, charge] of remote) {
    const p = local.get(intentId);
    if (!p) { issues.push({ severity: 'critical', kind: 'MISSING_LOCALLY', intentId,
                            amountMinor: charge.amount, note: 'customer charged, no local record' });
              await repairFromProvider(charge); continue; }          // self-heal where safe
    if (p.amountMinor !== charge.amount)
      issues.push({ severity: 'critical', kind: 'AMOUNT_MISMATCH', intentId,
                    ours: p.amountMinor, theirs: charge.amount });
    if (p.status === 'succeeded' && charge.refunded && !p.refundedAt)
      issues.push({ severity: 'high', kind: 'REFUND_NOT_RECORDED', intentId });
  }

  for (const [intentId, p] of local) {
    if (p.status === 'succeeded' && !remote.has(intentId))
      issues.push({ severity: 'critical', kind: 'MISSING_AT_PROVIDER', intentId,
                    note: 'we think it succeeded but the provider has no charge' });
  }

  await db.reconciliationReports.insert({ day, provider: 'stripe', issues });
  if (issues.some(i => i.severity === 'critical')) await alert.page('payments-reconciliation', issues);
  metrics.gauge('reconciliation.issues', issues.length, { provider: 'stripe' });
}
@end
@senior
Self-healing where it is safe is the part I would emphasise — a charge with no local record can usually be repaired automatically by fetching the intent and applying the same handler the webhook would have run, because that path is already idempotent. Anything ambiguous goes to a human with full context rather than being guessed at. The other point is that reconciliation should also run continuously for recent payments, not only daily: a payment stuck in `pending` for fifteen minutes is queried directly, which catches a lost webhook while the customer is still on the page rather than the next morning.
@followup
What do you do when the numbers do not match at month end?
@answer
Work from the provider's settlement report, which is authoritative for money actually moved, and reconcile down: gross charges, refunds, disputes, fees and payouts. Common causes of apparent mismatch are fees not modelled, currency conversion, payments captured in one period and settled in another, and disputes reversing an earlier charge. Modelling fees and settlement timing explicitly in the payments schema from the start avoids most of it, and finance will ask for that anyway.
@red
- One-directional reconciliation.
- Reconciliation that produces a report nobody is paged about.

### How do you handle subscriptions and recurring billing?
@short
Let the provider own the recurring schedule and the dunning logic, and mirror the state locally from webhooks. Model the subscription, its current period, and entitlements separately, so access is derived from a locally-known state rather than from a live API call on every request.
@deep
The events that matter are `customer.subscription.created/updated/deleted`, `invoice.paid`, `invoice.payment_failed` and the trial-ending notification. Proration on plan change, grace periods on failure, and cancellation at period end versus immediately are all product decisions that must be encoded explicitly.

Entitlement should be a local, cached fact — "this tenant has feature X until date Y" — because checking the provider on every request is slow and creates a hard dependency on their availability.
@code ts Subscription state mirrored locally, entitlement derived locally
CREATE TABLE subscriptions (
  id                 text PRIMARY KEY,             -- provider subscription id
  tenant_id          uuid NOT NULL,
  plan               text NOT NULL,
  status             text NOT NULL,                -- trialing|active|past_due|canceled|unpaid
  current_period_end timestamptz NOT NULL,
  cancel_at_period_end boolean NOT NULL DEFAULT false,
  grace_until        timestamptz                   -- our policy, not the provider's
);

// Webhook keeps it in sync
case 'invoice.payment_failed': {
  const inv = event.data.object as Stripe.Invoice;
  await tx.query(
    `UPDATE subscriptions SET status='past_due', grace_until = now() + interval '7 days'
      WHERE id = $1`, [inv.subscription]);
  await outbox.publish('billing.payment_failed', { tenantId, attempt: inv.attempt_count }, tx);
  break;
}

// Entitlement check: local, fast, and forgiving during the grace window
export function hasAccess(sub: Subscription, now = new Date()): boolean {
  if (sub.status === 'active' || sub.status === 'trialing') return sub.current_period_end > now;
  if (sub.status === 'past_due') return !!sub.grace_until && sub.grace_until > now;   // don't cut them off instantly
  return false;
}
@end
@senior
The grace period is a product decision with real revenue impact: cutting off access the moment a renewal fails loses customers whose card simply expired, whereas a seven-day grace window with escalating emails recovers a large share of them. Providers have their own dunning retries, and your grace window should span them rather than fight them. The other point is proration on upgrades and downgrades — deciding whether a mid-cycle upgrade charges immediately with proration or at the next cycle is a policy that must be written down, because getting it wrong produces refund requests and support load rather than a technical error.
@followup
How do you handle a plan change mid-cycle?
@answer
Delegate the money to the provider's proration engine — it handles credits and the immediate invoice correctly — and mirror the resulting subscription state locally. What we own is the entitlement transition: an upgrade takes effect immediately, a downgrade usually takes effect at period end so the customer keeps what they paid for. I also record the change in an audit trail with the actor, because "who downgraded our plan" is a question customers ask.
@red
- Calling the provider's API to check entitlement on every request.
- Cutting off access instantly on the first failed payment.

### How do you handle multi-currency and international payments?
@short
Store every amount as an integer in the smallest unit of an explicit currency, never convert for storage, and be clear about which currency the customer is charged in versus what you settle in. Display conversions are presentation only; the authoritative amount is the one the provider charged.
@deep
Zero-decimal currencies break naive assumptions: JPY and KRW have no minor unit, so multiplying by 100 produces a charge a hundred times too large. Some currencies have three decimals. A currency-aware helper rather than a hardcoded `× 100` is the fix.

Settlement is the other complication: you may charge in USD and settle in INR, with a conversion applied by the provider at their rate plus a fee, so your revenue reporting must use settlement figures, not charge figures.
@code ts Currency-aware amounts and presentation
const ZERO_DECIMAL = new Set(['JPY', 'KRW', 'VND', 'CLP']);
const THREE_DECIMAL = new Set(['BHD', 'KWD', 'OMR', 'TND']);

export function toMinor(amount: number, currency: string): number {
  const exp = ZERO_DECIMAL.has(currency) ? 0 : THREE_DECIMAL.has(currency) ? 3 : 2;
  return Math.round(amount * 10 ** exp);          // ⚠️ never `amount * 100` unconditionally
}
export function format(minor: number, currency: string, locale: string) {
  const exp = ZERO_DECIMAL.has(currency) ? 0 : THREE_DECIMAL.has(currency) ? 3 : 2;
  return new Intl.NumberFormat(locale, { style: 'currency', currency })
    .format(minor / 10 ** exp);
}

-- Store the charge AND the settlement, never derive one from the other
ALTER TABLE payments
  ADD COLUMN settled_amount_minor bigint,
  ADD COLUMN settled_currency     char(3),
  ADD COLUMN fx_rate              numeric(18,8),
  ADD COLUMN provider_fee_minor   bigint;
@end
@senior
Storing the settlement amount, the FX rate and the fee alongside the charge is what makes financial reporting correct rather than approximate. Deriving revenue by converting charge amounts at today's rate produces numbers that never reconcile with the bank, and finance will notice. On the customer side, the decision worth stating is presentment currency: charging in the customer's local currency improves conversion but you take the FX risk, whereas charging in your own currency pushes the risk and a bad rate onto the customer's bank. That is a business decision the engineering has to support either way.
@followup
How do you handle India-specific payment requirements?
@answer
UPI as a first-class method rather than an afterthought, since it dominates volume, plus netbanking and wallets — which is why Razorpay is the right primary provider there. RBI's tokenisation rules mean card details cannot be stored by merchants and recurring card mandates require an e-mandate flow with pre-debit notifications. Recurring payments therefore behave differently from a Stripe subscription, and modelling "mandate" as a distinct entity rather than assuming a stored card is the design consequence.
@red
- `amount * 100` for every currency.
- Reporting revenue from charge amounts rather than settlements.

### How do you keep PCI scope minimal?
@short
Never let card data touch your servers. Use the provider's hosted fields, Elements or Checkout so the card is posted directly from the browser to them and you receive only a token. That keeps you in the lightest self-assessment tier instead of a full audit.
@deep
The moment card data passes through your infrastructure — even in transit, even unlogged — your servers, logs, backups and everyone with access are in scope, which is an enormous compliance burden. Provider-hosted input fields render in an iframe from the provider's origin, so your JavaScript cannot read them.

The related discipline is not accidentally capturing card-like data elsewhere: a support form, a free-text note field, or a logged request body can pull you back into scope.
@code tsx Card data never reaches your origin
// ✅ Stripe Elements — the input lives in Stripe's iframe; your JS cannot read it
const stripe = useStripe(); const elements = useElements();

async function pay() {
  const { error, paymentIntent } = await stripe.confirmPayment({
    elements,
    confirmParams: { return_url: `${origin}/orders/${orderId}/confirm` },
    redirect: 'if_required',
  });
  if (error) return setError(error.message);
  // ⚠️ this is UX only — the webhook is what actually marks the order paid
  setStatus('processing');
}

// ❌ Anything like this puts your entire stack in PCI scope
// app.post('/pay', (req) => charge(req.body.cardNumber, req.body.cvv))

// Defensive: refuse to log anything that looks like a PAN
logger = pino({ redact: { paths: ['*.cardNumber', '*.cvv', '*.pan'], censor: '[REDACTED]' } });
const looksLikeCard = (s: string) => /\b(?:\d[ -]?){13,19}\b/.test(s);
@end
@senior
The point I would make is that PCI scope is a design decision made early and expensive to reverse. Choosing hosted fields at the start keeps you in SAQ A territory; deciding later that you want a custom card form pulls your whole infrastructure into scope and typically means an audit, quarterly scans and a set of controls that a small team cannot absorb. The related operational habit is scanning your own logs for card-like patterns, because the usual way merchants end up in scope accidentally is a support ticket or a debug log capturing a number a customer typed into the wrong field.
@followup
What about storing cards for repeat purchases?
@answer
Store the provider's token, never the card. Stripe's customer and payment-method objects, Razorpay's tokenisation, and PayPal's billing agreements all let you charge a returning customer without holding card data. In India, RBI rules make merchant card storage effectively prohibited, so tokenisation is mandatory rather than optional. The token is scoped to your account, so a breach of your database yields something useless elsewhere — which is exactly the property you want.
@red
- Building a custom card form to "own the experience".
- Logging full request bodies on payment endpoints.

### How do you test a payment integration?
@short
Provider test mode with their documented test cards for the happy path and each decline reason, signed webhook fixtures replayed against the real handler including duplicates and out-of-order delivery, and integration tests asserting the resulting database state. Never call the live API from CI.
@deep
The valuable tests are the adversarial ones: a duplicate webhook must not create two orders; a `succeeded` arriving after a `refunded` must not resurrect the order; an unsigned or wrongly signed request must be rejected; a payment with no matching local record must be flagged rather than silently creating one.

Provider CLIs (`stripe trigger`, `stripe listen`) let you replay real event shapes locally, which is far better than hand-written fixtures that drift from the actual payload.
@code ts The payment tests that actually catch bugs
const signed = (payload: object) => {
  const body = JSON.stringify(payload);
  const ts = Math.floor(Date.now() / 1000);
  const sig = crypto.createHmac('sha256', WEBHOOK_SECRET).update(`${ts}.${body}`).digest('hex');
  return { body, headers: { 'stripe-signature': `t=${ts},v1=${sig}` } };
};

it('is idempotent across duplicate deliveries', async () => {
  const evt = signed(paymentIntentSucceeded({ id: 'evt_1', intent: 'pi_1', orderId }));
  await post('/webhooks/stripe', evt).expect(200);
  await post('/webhooks/stripe', evt).expect(200);
  await drainQueue();
  expect(await db.orderEvents.count({ orderId, to: 'paid' })).toBe(1);
});

it('does not resurrect a refunded order from a late succeeded event', async () => {
  await post('/webhooks/stripe', signed(chargeRefunded({ id: 'evt_2', intent: 'pi_1' }))).expect(200);
  await post('/webhooks/stripe', signed(paymentIntentSucceeded({ id: 'evt_3', intent: 'pi_1' }))).expect(200);
  await drainQueue();
  expect((await db.orders.find(orderId)).status).toBe('refunded');   // ← the guarded transition
});

it('rejects an invalid signature', async () => {
  await post('/webhooks/stripe')
    .set('stripe-signature', 't=1,v1=deadbeef')
    .send(JSON.stringify(paymentIntentSucceeded({}))).expect(400);
  expect(await db.processedEvents.count()).toBe(0);
});
@end
@senior
Signing the fixtures with the real HMAC rather than stubbing the verification is the detail that makes these tests meaningful — otherwise you never test the raw-body handling, which is the single most common integration bug. I would also mention staging: a full end-to-end run against the provider's sandbox with their CLI forwarding real webhooks, executed before every release that touches payments, because the mechanics of signature headers and event shapes change occasionally and a fixture-only suite will not notice. Payments is the area where I would happily spend more test effort than anywhere else, because the cost of a bug is money and trust.
@followup
How do you test the reconciliation job?
@answer
By constructing the mismatch cases deliberately: a provider charge with no local row, a local success with no provider charge, an amount mismatch, and a refund recorded at the provider but not locally. Each should produce the expected issue with the expected severity, and the self-healing path should be verified to actually repair the safe case. I stub the provider's list API with recorded fixtures so the test is deterministic, and I assert on the alert being raised, not just on the report being written.
@red
- Testing only the happy path.
- Stubbing signature verification instead of signing fixtures.

### What does a payments observability dashboard look like?
@short
Business metrics first: authorisation success rate per provider and per method, payment volume and value, decline reasons, webhook processing lag, reconciliation mismatches, refund and dispute rates. Technical metrics support them. Alerts fire on the business signals, because those detect failures that leave every technical dashboard green.
@deep
The classic invisible failure is a provider-side change — a new decline code, a certificate rotation, a policy change — that causes success rates to fall while your API returns 200 for every request. Latency, error rate and CPU all look perfect. Only the authorisation success rate reveals it.

Webhook lag is the second critical signal: if events are queuing, orders are not being marked paid, and customers are seeing "processing" while their money has moved.
@code ts The metrics I would put on the wall
const authOutcome = new client.Counter({
  name: 'payment_authorisation_total',
  labelNames: ['provider', 'method', 'currency', 'outcome', 'decline_code'],
});
const webhookLag = new client.Histogram({
  name: 'webhook_processing_lag_seconds',
  labelNames: ['provider', 'type'],
  buckets: [1, 5, 15, 60, 300, 900],
});
const reconIssues = new client.Gauge({
  name: 'reconciliation_open_issues', labelNames: ['provider', 'kind'],
});

// on every webhook, measure provider-created → we-processed
webhookLag.observe({ provider, type: event.type }, (Date.now() / 1000) - event.created);

/* Alerts that page:
   • auth success rate for any provider drops >5pp vs the 7-day baseline for 15m
   • webhook lag p95 > 120s for 10m
   • reconciliation critical issues > 0
   • pending payments older than 30m > 10
   • dispute rate above the network threshold (rolling 30d)
   Dashboards, not alerts:
   • volume/value by provider, method, currency
   • decline reason breakdown (insufficient_funds vs do_not_honor vs 3DS abandonment)
*/
@end
@senior
Comparing against a rolling baseline rather than a fixed threshold is what makes the success-rate alert usable: authorisation rates vary by time of day, day of week and traffic mix, so a fixed "below 90%" either fires constantly or never. A five-point drop against the same period last week is a real signal. And the decline-reason breakdown is genuinely actionable in a way a single rate is not — a spike in `do_not_honor` for one issuer is a different problem from a spike in 3DS abandonment, and the second one is usually a UX regression on your own checkout page that no backend metric would catch.
@followup
What is the first thing you check when payment success rate drops?
@answer
Whether it is one provider or all of them, and whether it is one method, one currency or one issuer — that segmentation immediately separates "our deploy broke something" from "a provider is having an incident". Then I check the provider's status page and our recent deploys in parallel. If it is our side, the fastest mitigation is usually routing traffic to the alternative provider while investigating, which is the operational reason multi-provider support is worth its complexity.
@red
- Only technical dashboards, no business metrics.
- Fixed thresholds on a metric that varies naturally.

# SECTION Docker, CI/CD & Cloud in Production | Build once, deploy safely, recover fast.

### How do you optimise Docker builds for a large TypeScript monorepo?
@short
Order layers so dependencies are cached separately from source, use BuildKit cache mounts for the package manager, build only the affected workspace, and produce a minimal runtime image with multi-stage builds. In a monorepo the biggest win is pruning the workspace so a change in one app does not invalidate every other app's cache.
@deep
The default failure is copying the whole repository before installing, which invalidates the dependency layer on any source change and turns every build into a full install. Copying manifests first fixes that.

For monorepos, `turbo prune` or `pnpm deploy` produces a subset containing only the target workspace and its internal dependencies, so the build context and the cache key are scoped correctly.
@code dockerfile Monorepo-aware, cache-friendly, small
# syntax=docker/dockerfile:1.7
FROM node:22-alpine AS base
RUN corepack enable
WORKDIR /app

# 1. Prune the monorepo to just this app and its internal deps
FROM base AS pruner
COPY . .
RUN pnpm dlx turbo prune --scope=@acme/api --docker

# 2. Install with a cache mount — dependencies cached independently of source
FROM base AS deps
COPY --from=pruner /app/out/json/ .
RUN --mount=type=cache,id=pnpm,target=/pnpm/store \
    pnpm install --frozen-lockfile

# 3. Build
FROM base AS build
COPY --from=deps /app/node_modules ./node_modules
COPY --from=pruner /app/out/full/ .
RUN pnpm --filter @acme/api build

# 4. Runtime: production deps only, non-root, small
FROM node:22-alpine AS runtime
ENV NODE_ENV=production
WORKDIR /app
RUN addgroup -S app && adduser -S app -G app
COPY --from=build --chown=app:app /app/apps/api/dist ./dist
COPY --from=build --chown=app:app /app/node_modules ./node_modules
USER app
EXPOSE 3000
CMD ["node", "dist/index.js"]
@end
@senior
The `--mount=type=cache` for the package store is the change that most improves CI times, because it survives across builds without being baked into a layer — a cold-cache install of a few minutes becomes seconds. I would also mention `.dockerignore` as the unglamorous prerequisite: without it, `node_modules`, `.git` and build output are uploaded as build context on every build, which can dominate the time entirely. And I always tag images with the commit SHA rather than `latest`, because `latest` makes it impossible to say what is actually running during an incident.
@followup
How do you handle a native dependency that fails to build on Alpine?
@answer
Alpine uses musl rather than glibc, so prebuilt binaries for packages like `sharp`, `bcrypt` or `canvas` often do not match and it falls back to compiling from source — slow, and sometimes broken. The options are installing the build toolchain in the build stage only, switching to a `slim` Debian base which has glibc and better prebuilt coverage, or replacing the dependency. I usually take `node:22-slim`: slightly larger than Alpine but far fewer surprises, and the size difference matters much less than build reliability.
@red
- `COPY . .` before installing dependencies.
- Tagging production images `latest`.

### How do you design a deployment strategy with safe rollback?
@short
Immutable artefacts promoted through environments, rolling or blue-green deploys behind a load balancer with readiness checks, automatic verification after deploy, and one-command rollback to the previous image. The schema is decoupled from the code so a rollback does not break on a migration.
@deep
Rolling deploys replace instances gradually and are the default; blue-green runs a full parallel environment and switches traffic, giving an instant rollback at the cost of double capacity; canary routes a small percentage to the new version and watches metrics before proceeding.

The constraint that makes any of them safe is backwards compatibility: during the rollout, old and new code run simultaneously against the same database and the same queues, so both must work with the current schema and the current message formats.
@code yaml Canary with automatic rollback on error rate
# ECS / CodeDeploy canary — 10% for 5 minutes, then all
DeploymentConfiguration:
  CanaryPercentage: 10
  CanaryInterval: 5
  Alarms:
    - api-5xx-rate-high          # ← breaching these triggers automatic rollback
    - api-p99-latency-high
    - payments-auth-rate-drop

# Post-deploy verification, run by the pipeline
- run: |
    ./scripts/wait-healthy.sh $ENV
    npm run test:smoke -- --base-url https://$ENV.acme.com
    ./scripts/compare-metrics.sh --window 5m --baseline 1h \
        --max-error-delta 0.5 --max-p99-delta 200
- if: failure()
  run: ./scripts/rollback.sh $ENV        # redeploy the previous image tag
@end
@senior
The point I would make is that rollback must be tested, not merely available. A rollback path that has never been exercised will fail exactly when you need it — usually because a migration was not backwards-compatible, or because the previous image was garbage-collected from the registry. So I keep a retention policy on images, I run a rollback in staging as part of the release process, and I keep the "can we revert?" question as the first thing asked in an incident. Rolling forward under pressure is how a five-minute problem becomes an hour-long one.
@followup
When is blue-green worth the extra cost?
@answer
When rollback speed matters more than infrastructure cost — a payment or checkout service where minutes of degradation are expensive — or when the deploy carries risk that gradual rollout cannot de-risk, such as a runtime upgrade. The trade is double capacity during the switch and the need for the database to serve both versions simultaneously, which is the same backwards-compatibility requirement as rolling deploys. For most services a rolling deploy with a good readiness probe and fast rollback is sufficient and much cheaper.
@red
- Deploying a migration and its dependent code together.
- A rollback procedure that has never been executed.

### How do you manage infrastructure as code?
@short
Terraform for cloud resources, versioned in git and applied only through CI with a plan reviewed in the pull request. State in a remote backend with locking, environments separated by workspace or directory, and no manual changes in the console — because drift between code and reality is what makes incidents unrecoverable.
@deep
The discipline that matters is that the plan output is part of the review. A one-line change to a security group can open a database to the internet, and that is visible in the plan and invisible in the diff.

Secrets never go in Terraform state in plaintext — state contains resolved values, so it must be encrypted, access-controlled, and secrets should be referenced from a secret manager rather than passed as variables.
@code hcl Terraform with the guards that matter
terraform {
  backend "s3" {
    bucket         = "acme-tfstate"
    key            = "prod/api/terraform.tfstate"
    dynamodb_table = "acme-tflock"          # state locking — no concurrent applies
    encrypt        = true
  }
}

resource "aws_db_instance" "primary" {
  identifier             = "acme-prod"
  engine                 = "postgres"
  engine_version         = "16.4"
  instance_class         = "db.r6g.xlarge"
  multi_az               = true
  storage_encrypted      = true
  publicly_accessible    = false                       # ← reviewed on every plan
  backup_retention_period = 14
  deletion_protection    = true                        # ← cannot be destroyed by accident
  performance_insights_enabled = true

  lifecycle { prevent_destroy = true }
}

# CI: plan on PR (posted as a comment), apply only on merge to main
# checkov / tfsec run on the plan to catch open security groups, unencrypted volumes, public buckets
@end
@senior
`prevent_destroy` and `deletion_protection` on stateful resources are cheap insurance against the worst possible Terraform accident — a refactor that changes a resource's identity and causes a replace rather than an update. I have seen that nearly destroy a production database. I would also mention policy-as-code scanning in the pipeline (tfsec, checkov) so a publicly accessible bucket or an unencrypted volume fails the build rather than relying on a reviewer noticing. And drift detection on a schedule, because someone will eventually change something in the console during an incident and never bring it back into code.
@followup
How do you handle secrets in Terraform?
@answer
Reference them, never define them: create the secret container in Terraform, populate the value out of band through the secret manager, and have the application read it at runtime. Passing a secret as a Terraform variable puts it in the state file, which is then a credential store you must protect as carefully as the secrets themselves. For values Terraform must know, I use a data source that reads from the secret manager at plan time so the value is not stored in code.
@red
- Manual changes in the cloud console.
- Secrets passed as Terraform variables into state.

### How do you keep cloud costs under control?
@short
Tag everything so cost is attributable, right-size instances from actual utilisation, use lifecycle policies on storage, avoid cross-AZ and NAT data transfer where possible, and put a budget alarm and a cost review in the regular engineering rhythm. Most overspend is idle capacity and forgotten resources, not clever inefficiency.
@deep
The commonly overlooked costs are data transfer — NAT gateway charges for every byte a private-subnet service sends to the internet, cross-AZ traffic between services, and CloudFront-to-origin egress — and storage growth from logs, backups and old S3 objects that nobody expires.

Compute savings come from right-sizing (most services are provisioned for a peak that never arrives), Graviton instances, and Savings Plans for predictable baseline load with on-demand for the spikes.
@code text Where the money actually goes, and the lever for each
  Compute      over-provisioned tasks        → right-size from p95 CPU/memory, not from guesswork
               always-on non-prod            → schedule staging to scale to zero overnight
               x86 where arm64 works         → Graviton: ~20% cheaper, usually faster for Node/Go
  Database     oversized instance            → right-size; move analytics to a replica
               unbounded storage growth      → partition + drop old partitions, archive to S3
  Data transfer NAT gateway for S3/ECR       → VPC endpoints (gateway endpoints are FREE for S3)
               cross-AZ chatter              → co-locate chatty services; cache locally
  Storage      logs kept forever             → retention policy per log group (30/90/365 days)
               S3 with no lifecycle          → Intelligent-Tiering or explicit transitions
  Observability high-cardinality metrics     → drop unused labels; sample successful traces
  Forgotten    unattached EBS, old snapshots → scheduled report of unattached/unused resources
@end
@senior
The VPC endpoint for S3 is the specific one I would name, because it is a common and invisible cost: every byte a private-subnet service pulls from S3 or ECR goes through the NAT gateway and is billed per gigabyte, and a gateway endpoint eliminates that entirely for free. Similarly, high-cardinality metric labels — a user id or a request id as a label — can cost more than the compute they measure. The broader habit is making cost visible to engineers: a dashboard by service and environment, and a monthly review, changes behaviour far more than a policy document.
@followup
How do you decide between serverless and containers on cost?
@answer
By the utilisation curve. Lambda is cheaper for spiky, low-duty-cycle workloads because you pay nothing when idle; containers are cheaper once utilisation is sustained, because per-request pricing at high volume exceeds the cost of a reserved instance. The crossover is workload-specific, so I model it with real request volumes and durations rather than reasoning by reputation. I would also weigh operational cost: Lambda removes patching and scaling work, which has real value for a small team even when the compute bill is slightly higher.
@red
- No tagging, so cost cannot be attributed.
- Ignoring data transfer and storage growth.

### How do you manage environments and configuration across dev, staging and production?
@short
Identical infrastructure definitions parameterised per environment, the same container image promoted through all of them, and configuration supplied at runtime rather than baked in. Staging mirrors production's topology closely enough that a problem there is a real signal, with production-like data volumes where feasible.
@deep
The most common failure is staging that differs structurally — a single instance instead of three, no CDN, a tiny database, no read replica — so it never reproduces the problems production has. It is better to have a smaller but structurally identical staging than a large but differently shaped one.

Data is the other issue: testing with production-like volume matters for query plans, and production data in staging is a privacy risk, so anonymised or synthetic data at realistic scale is the compromise.
@code hcl One module, three environments, no drift
module "api" {
  source = "../../modules/service"

  environment     = var.environment
  image           = var.image_tag           # ← the SAME image, promoted
  desired_count   = var.environment == "prod" ? 6 : 2
  cpu             = var.environment == "prod" ? 1024 : 512
  db_instance     = var.environment == "prod" ? "db.r6g.xlarge" : "db.t4g.medium"
  multi_az        = var.environment == "prod"
  # structure identical: same VPC layout, same ALB, same endpoints, same alarms
}

# Runtime configuration, not build-time
environment = [
  { name = "NODE_ENV",   value = "production" },
  { name = "LOG_LEVEL",  value = var.environment == "prod" ? "info" : "debug" },
]
secrets = [
  { name = "DATABASE_URL", valueFrom = aws_secretsmanager_secret.db.arn },
  { name = "JWT_SECRET",   valueFrom = aws_secretsmanager_secret.jwt.arn },
]
@end
@senior
Promoting the identical image is the property that makes staging meaningful — if you rebuild per environment, you are testing a different artefact than the one you ship. The Next.js caveat is worth mentioning here: `NEXT_PUBLIC_*` variables are inlined at build time, so a frontend with different API URLs per environment cannot use a single image unless you read that configuration at runtime on the server and pass it down. That is a concrete architectural consequence of the build-once principle, and it is worth designing for rather than discovering during a promotion.
@followup
Should staging use production data?
@answer
Not raw. The privacy exposure is real — staging typically has weaker access controls, more people with access, and less monitoring — and in many jurisdictions it is a compliance violation. What I use is a masked or synthetic dataset at production-like scale: real distributions and volumes so query plans behave the same, with personal data replaced deterministically so referential integrity holds. Generating it is work, but it is the only way to catch performance problems before production without creating a data-protection liability.
@red
- Staging with a fraction of production's structure.
- Rebuilding the image per environment.

# SECTION Observability & Reliability | Knowing something is wrong before your users tell you.

### How do you define and use SLOs and error budgets?
@short
An SLI is a measurement of user-visible correctness — the proportion of requests that succeed within a latency threshold. An SLO is the target for that SLI over a window. The error budget is the allowed shortfall, and it turns "should we ship this risky change?" into a data question rather than an argument.
@deep
The value is in the conversation it enables. If you are well within budget, you can ship faster and take more risk; if you have burned most of it, the team stops feature work and fixes reliability. That removes the perpetual tension between velocity and stability by making the trade explicit.

Alerting on burn rate rather than instantaneous failure is what keeps pages meaningful: a short spike that consumes 1% of the monthly budget is not worth waking someone; a sustained rate that will exhaust it in two hours is.
@code yaml SLO definition and multi-window burn-rate alerts
slo:
  name: checkout-availability
  sli: >
    sum(rate(http_requests_total{route="/checkout",status!~"5.."}[5m]))
      / sum(rate(http_requests_total{route="/checkout"}[5m]))
  objective: 0.999               # 43 minutes of budget per 30 days
  window: 30d

# Fast burn: consuming the month's budget in ~2 days → page
- alert: CheckoutBudgetFastBurn
  expr: (1 - sli_5m) > 14.4 * 0.001 and (1 - sli_1h) > 14.4 * 0.001
  for: 2m
  labels: { severity: page }

# Slow burn: consuming it in ~10 days → ticket, not a page
- alert: CheckoutBudgetSlowBurn
  expr: (1 - sli_6h) > 6 * 0.001 and (1 - sli_3d) > 6 * 0.001
  for: 15m
  labels: { severity: ticket }
@end
@senior
The two-window condition is what removes false pages: requiring both a short and a longer window to be burning means a thirty-second blip does not page anyone, while a sustained problem does immediately. I would also stress choosing SLIs that reflect what users care about — checkout availability rather than aggregate API availability, because a 99.9% overall figure can hide a completely broken checkout. And SLOs are only useful if the organisation actually honours the error budget; if the response to exhausting it is "ship anyway", the whole framework is theatre and I would rather have honest alerting than a decorative SLO.
@followup
What availability target would you pick?
@answer
The lowest one that keeps users happy, because each additional nine multiplies cost. 99.9% is 43 minutes a month, achievable with multi-AZ and good deployment practice; 99.99% is four minutes a month, which requires multi-region, automated failover and a mature on-call practice. I would derive it from the product: an internal admin tool does not need what a payment flow needs, and setting a target you cannot meet just teaches people to ignore it.
@red
- Alerting on every error rather than on budget burn.
- SLOs measured on aggregate rather than on critical journeys.

### How do you implement distributed tracing?
@short
OpenTelemetry auto-instrumentation for HTTP, database and queue clients, with trace context propagated through headers and message metadata so a request is one trace across every service and worker. Then sample: keep all errors and slow traces, sample the rest.
@deep
Context propagation is the part that requires care. HTTP is handled automatically by the `traceparent` header, but queue jobs are not — you must inject the context into the job payload and extract it in the worker, or the asynchronous half of the flow becomes an orphan trace.

Sampling strategy matters for cost: head-based sampling decides at the start and is cheap but may drop the interesting traces; tail-based sampling decides after seeing the whole trace and can keep every error, at the cost of buffering.
@code ts Trace context across an HTTP → queue → worker boundary
