import { context, propagation, trace } from '@opentelemetry/api';

// Producer: inject the current context into the job payload
const carrier: Record<string, string> = {};
propagation.inject(context.active(), carrier);
await queue.add('send-receipt', { orderId, _otel: carrier }, { jobId: `receipt:${orderId}` });

// Consumer: extract and continue the SAME trace
new Worker('emails', async (job) => {
  const parent = propagation.extract(context.active(), job.data._otel ?? {});
  return context.with(parent, async () => {
    const span = tracer.startSpan('send-receipt', { attributes: { 'order.id': job.data.orderId } });
    try {
      await sendReceipt(job.data.orderId);
      span.setStatus({ code: SpanStatusCode.OK });
    } catch (e) {
      span.recordException(e as Error);
      span.setStatus({ code: SpanStatusCode.ERROR });
      throw e;
    } finally { span.end(); }
  });
});

// Link logs to traces so one click goes from a trace to its log lines
const { traceId, spanId } = trace.getActiveSpan()?.spanContext() ?? {};
logger.info({ traceId, spanId, orderId }, 'receipt queued');
@end
@senior
Injecting the trace context into queue payloads is the step teams usually miss, and it is what turns "the API was fast but the email never arrived" from two disconnected investigations into one trace. Attaching `traceId` to every log line is the other high-leverage move: it makes traces and logs navigable in both directions, so you go from a slow span straight to the log lines emitted inside it. On sampling, I keep 100% of errors and anything above a latency threshold, and a small percentage of the rest — that keeps the bill sane while guaranteeing that the traces you actually want are always there.
@followup
Is tracing worth it for a monolith?
@answer
Yes, though the value shifts. In a monolith you are not tracing across services, but you are tracing across layers: which query, which cache call, which third-party request consumed the time within one endpoint. That answers the same question — where did the 400ms go — without correlating log lines by hand. Auto-instrumentation gives you that almost for free, so the cost is low and the diagnostic value is high from day one.
@red
- Tracing HTTP only, leaving async work uninstrumented.
- Sampling uniformly and losing every error trace.

### How do you run an on-call rotation and handle incidents?
@short
A documented severity scale, a single incident commander per incident, a communication channel separate from the debugging channel, runbooks linked from every alert, and a blameless post-mortem with concrete follow-up actions that are actually scheduled. The goal is to make incidents boring.
@deep
The two failure modes are alert fatigue — so many pages that people stop reading them — and hero culture, where one person knows everything and incidents depend on their availability. Both are fixed by the same things: ruthless alert tuning, runbooks written by whoever resolved the last incident, and rotating responsibility.

Post-mortems must be blameless in practice, not just in name. The question is "what made this failure possible and easy to miss", not "who pushed the button".
@code text An incident runbook that someone can follow at 3am
  ALERT: payments-auth-rate-drop            SEVERITY: SEV-2
  MEANING: card authorisation success is >5pp below the 7-day baseline for 15 minutes.
  IMPACT:  customers cannot complete checkout. Revenue is being lost right now.

  FIRST 5 MINUTES
   1. Dashboard → Payments → auth rate by provider/method/currency
      • one provider only?  → check their status page, consider routing to the other
      • all providers?      → likely our side; check recent deploys (last 2h)
   2. /version on prod → what is running? does it match the last green pipeline?
   3. Error tracker → new signatures in the last hour, filtered to payment routes

  MITIGATE BEFORE DIAGNOSING
   • Recent deploy suspected  → ./scripts/rollback.sh prod    (≈90s)
   • One provider down        → flag `payments.route.default = razorpay`  (no deploy)
   • Webhook backlog          → scale the worker service, check DLQ

  ESCALATE: @payments-oncall → @eng-lead after 15 min → @cto for SEV-1
  COMMS: post in #incidents every 15 min even if nothing changed. Status page if >10 min.
  AFTER: create the post-mortem doc from the template before leaving the incident.
@end
@senior
"Mitigate before diagnosing" is the most important line in any runbook. The instinct of engineers is to understand the problem first, but users do not care why they cannot pay — restoring service comes first, understanding second, and rollback or a feature-flag kill switch is usually the fastest mitigation. That is also why I invest in making those two levers fast and reliable. On post-mortems, the measure of a good one is whether the follow-up actions get done: if they sit in a backlog forever, the same incident recurs, and the process becomes ritual rather than improvement.
@followup
How do you reduce alert fatigue?
@answer
Delete alerts that have never led to an action, convert cause-based alerts into symptom-based ones, require every paging alert to have a runbook and an owner, and route non-urgent signals to a ticket queue rather than a phone. I review the alert log periodically and ask, for each firing, whether a human did something differently as a result — if not, it is tuned or removed. An on-call rotation with more than a couple of pages a week is unsustainable and will cause a real incident to be missed.
@red
- Alerts with no runbook.
- Post-mortems that assign blame or produce no scheduled actions.

### How do you do chaos and load testing responsibly?
@short
Load test against a production-like environment with realistic data volumes and traffic shapes, testing the whole system including the database and third parties, not just the API in isolation. Chaos testing starts small and in non-production: kill an instance, add latency to a dependency, fill a disk — and verify the system degrades as designed.
@deep
The common mistake in load testing is unrealistic traffic: hammering one endpoint with identical parameters gives you a perfect cache hit rate and tells you nothing. Realistic tests use a mix of endpoints, varied parameters, think time, and a ramp that reflects real arrival patterns.

Chaos testing's value is in verifying the assumptions you wrote down: that a circuit breaker actually opens, that a replica failover completes, that the queue drains after a worker outage.
@code js A k6 test with realistic mix and meaningful thresholds
import http from 'k6/http';
import { check, sleep } from 'k6';

export const options = {
  scenarios: {
    browse:   { executor: 'ramping-vus', startVUs: 0,
                stages: [{ duration: '2m', target: 200 }, { duration: '10m', target: 200 },
                         { duration: '2m', target: 0 }] },
    checkout: { executor: 'constant-arrival-rate', rate: 20, timeUnit: '1s',
                duration: '14m', preAllocatedVUs: 50 },
  },
  thresholds: {
    'http_req_duration{scenario:browse}':   ['p(95)<400'],
    'http_req_duration{scenario:checkout}': ['p(95)<1200', 'p(99)<2500'],
    'http_req_failed': ['rate<0.01'],
    'checks': ['rate>0.99'],
  },
};

export default function () {
  const id = ids[Math.floor(Math.random() * ids.length)];      // ← vary params: real cache behaviour
  const res = http.get(`${BASE}/api/v1/products/${id}`, { headers: auth() });
  check(res, { 'status 200': (r) => r.status === 200 });
  sleep(Math.random() * 3);                                     // ← think time
}
@end
@senior
The thing I watch during a load test is not the throughput number but where the system bends first: connection pool saturation, event-loop lag, database CPU, or a third-party rate limit. That tells you the real capacity constraint, which is almost never the one people assume. I would also insist on testing the failure path — what happens when the load exceeds capacity? A system that degrades gracefully by shedding load with 429s is far better than one that queues until everything times out, and you only find out which one you have by pushing past the limit deliberately.
@followup
What is load shedding and when do you implement it?
@answer
Deliberately rejecting some requests when the system is beyond capacity, so the requests you do accept still succeed. Without it, an overloaded service accepts everything, queues grow, latency exceeds every client's timeout, and effectively 100% of requests fail — you get zero useful throughput instead of degraded throughput. Implementations range from a queue-depth check that returns 503 immediately, to priority-based shedding where checkout traffic is served and analytics is dropped. I would add it to any service where overload is plausible, which is most user-facing ones.
@red
- Load testing one endpoint with identical parameters.
- Chaos testing in production before non-production.

# SECTION Clean Architecture & Design Patterns | Structure that survives four years and six engineers.

### What is Clean Architecture and how do you apply it pragmatically in Node?
@short
Dependencies point inward: the domain knows nothing about the database, HTTP or the framework; the application layer orchestrates use cases; adapters implement the interfaces the domain declares. Applied pragmatically that means a service layer that takes interfaces, and infrastructure that implements them — not four folders of ceremony for a CRUD app.
@deep
The value is testability and replaceability. If the order service depends on an `OrderRepository` interface, you can test it with an in-memory implementation and swap Sequelize for raw SQL without touching business logic. If it imports the Sequelize model directly, both are impossible.

The cost is indirection, and the honest position is that it should be proportional: a domain with real rules deserves the separation, a thin CRUD endpoint does not.
@arch Dependency direction, and what belongs where
   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │ DOMAIN            entities, value objects, pure policy        │
   │                   priceOrder(), canRefund(), Money, OrderId   │
   │                   ← knows NOTHING about DB/HTTP/Stripe        │
   ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
   │ APPLICATION       use cases: createOrder, refundOrder         │
   │                   declares interfaces: OrderRepository,       │
   │                   PaymentProvider, Clock, Outbox              │
   ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
   │ ADAPTERS          PgOrderRepository, StripeProvider,          │
   │                   ExpressController, BullWorker, CliCommand   │
   ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
   │ FRAMEWORKS        Express, Sequelize, Redis, AWS SDK          │
   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
     dependencies point UP.  The domain is the stable core.

   PRAGMATIC VERSION for most services:
     modules/<feature>/{ routes, controller, service, repository, schema, policy }
     — same dependency direction, one third the ceremony.
@end
@code ts The pragmatic version, with the boundary that actually matters
// application layer declares what it needs
export interface OrderRepository {
  findForUpdate(id: OrderId, tx: Tx): Promise<Order | null>;
  insert(o: NewOrder, tx: Tx): Promise<Order>;
}

// domain policy — pure, exhaustively testable, no I/O
export function canRefund(actor: Actor, order: Order, amount: Minor, now: Date): Result<true, RefundError> { … }

// use case — orchestration only
export function makeRefundOrder(deps: { orders: OrderRepository; outbox: Outbox; clock: Clock }) {
  return async (input: RefundInput): Promise<Result<Refund, RefundError>> => { … };
}

// adapter — the only file that imports Sequelize
export class PgOrderRepository implements OrderRepository { … }
@end
@senior
The line I would draw is that the *policy* functions — the ones encoding business rules — must be pure and free of infrastructure, and everything else is negotiable. That single rule gives you the majority of the benefit: rules become exhaustively testable, reviewable by a domain expert, and reusable from any entry point. Beyond that, adding an interface for every collaborator when there will only ever be one implementation is ceremony, and I would say so in a design review. Architecture should be justified by a change you expect to make, not by a diagram.
@followup
Is Clean Architecture overkill for a startup?
@answer
The full four-layer structure with mappers between every layer usually is — it slows early iteration when the domain is still being discovered. What is not overkill at any size is keeping business rules out of controllers and I/O out of business rules, because that costs nothing and preserves your options. My approach is to start with feature-sliced modules and pure policy functions, and to introduce explicit ports and adapters only where a second implementation appears or the domain complexity justifies it.
@red
- Four layers of mappers for a CRUD endpoint.
- Business rules that import the ORM.

### Which design patterns do you actually use, and where?
@short
Repository and service layer for structure; strategy for interchangeable behaviour like payment providers; adapter for wrapping third-party SDKs; factory for constructing configured clients; observer for events; decorator for cross-cutting concerns; and the outbox and circuit breaker as architectural patterns. Each appeared because a specific pain existed.
@deep
The distinction worth making is between patterns that structure code and patterns that solve distributed-systems problems. The Gang of Four patterns are about coupling within a process; outbox, saga, circuit breaker, bulkhead and idempotency key are about correctness across processes — and in a modern backend the second group matters more.

Applying patterns pre-emptively is the anti-pattern: they add indirection, and indirection without a reason is cost.
@code ts Strategy + adapter + factory, in one realistic slice
// Adapter: the third-party SDK is wrapped, so its types never leak into the domain
class StripeAdapter implements PaymentProvider {
  constructor(private sdk: Stripe) {}
  async refund(intentId: string, amountMinor: number, idem: string) {
    const r = await this.sdk.refunds.create(
      { payment_intent: intentId, amount: amountMinor }, { idempotencyKey: idem });
    return { refundId: r.id };                       // our shape, not Stripe's
  }
}

// Factory: constructs configured instances; the only place credentials are read
export function makeProviders(env: Env): Map<string, PaymentProvider> {
  return new Map([
    ['stripe',   new StripeAdapter(new Stripe(env.STRIPE_KEY))],
    ['razorpay', new RazorpayAdapter(new Razorpay({ key_id: env.RZP_ID, key_secret: env.RZP_SECRET }))],
  ]);
}

// Strategy: chosen at runtime by configuration, not by an if-chain in the domain
const provider = providers.get(routing.decide(order)) ?? fail('UNSUPPORTED_PROVIDER');

// Decorator: cross-cutting retry/metrics without touching the adapters
const instrumented = withMetrics(withRetry(provider, { attempts: 3, backoff: 'exponential' }));
@end
@senior
The adapter boundary is the one I would defend hardest: wrapping a third-party SDK means their types, their error shapes and their breaking changes are contained in one file. When Stripe changes an API version, I change one adapter rather than fifty call sites — and I can write a fake adapter for tests that behaves like the real one without a network. The decorator composition on top is how I add retries and metrics uniformly across all providers without duplicating that logic three times, which is a concrete example of why composition beats inheritance here.
@followup
What patterns do you avoid?
@answer
Singletons with mutable global state, because they make testing and reasoning about order-dependence painful — Node's module cache already gives you the useful part. Deep inheritance hierarchies, for the fragile-base-class reasons. Service locators, which hide dependencies instead of declaring them. And anything applied speculatively: an abstraction with one implementation and no expected second one is a cost with no benefit, and I would rather add it later when the need is real than carry it for a year.
@red
- Applying patterns because they are patterns.
- Third-party SDK types spread across the domain.

### How do you approach a large legacy refactor safely?
@short
Characterise the existing behaviour with tests first, then change in small reversible steps behind a flag, with shadow comparison for anything computing values. Strangler-fig the boundaries: route new work through the new implementation while the old one continues serving, until nothing calls the old path.
@deep
The rule is never to refactor and change behaviour at the same time. A refactor that also fixes a bug makes it impossible to tell whether a difference in output is the fix or a regression.

Characterisation tests are the entry point for untested legacy code: capture the current behaviour, including its quirks, so you have a safety net — even if some of that behaviour is wrong, you learn about the change when the test fails and can decide deliberately.
@code ts Strangler + shadow comparison — migrate without a big bang
// 1. New implementation behind a flag, old one still authoritative
export async function priceOrder(order: Order): Promise<Totals> {
  const legacy = legacyPricing(order);

  if (await flag('pricing.v2.shadow', { tenantId: order.tenantId })) {
    try {
      const next = pricingV2(order);
      if (next.totalMinor !== legacy.totalMinor) {
        metrics.increment('pricing.mismatch', { tenant: order.tenantId });
        logger.warn({ orderId: order.id, legacy: legacy.totalMinor, next: next.totalMinor,
                      diff: next.totalMinor - legacy.totalMinor }, 'pricing shadow mismatch');
      }
    } catch (e) { metrics.increment('pricing.v2.error'); }   // shadow errors never affect users
  }
  return legacy;                                             // 2. old path still serves
}

// 3. once mismatch rate is zero for a week across real traffic, flip the flag
// 4. remove the legacy path and the flag in the following release
@end
@senior
The shadow phase is what converts a risky rewrite into a measured one: you validate the new implementation against real production inputs — including all the edge cases nobody documented — without any user-visible risk. The discipline is to be strict about what counts as ready: zero mismatches across a full business cycle, not "close enough". I would also insist on deleting the old path promptly once switched, because a codebase with two pricing engines and a flag is worse than either one alone, and the temptation is always to leave it "just in case".
@followup
How do you handle a refactor that touches the database schema?
@answer
Expand and contract, exactly as for any migration, with the shadow phase running against both representations: write to both, read from the old, compare, then switch reads, then stop writing the old and finally drop it. Each step is a separate deploy and independently reversible. The failure mode to avoid is a "migration branch" that accumulates for weeks — it becomes unmergeable and the eventual big-bang cutover is exactly the risk the whole approach exists to avoid.
@red
- Refactoring and fixing behaviour in the same change.
- A long-lived rewrite branch merged in one go.

### How do you decide what to build versus what to buy?
@short
Build what is your differentiator; buy the rest. Authentication, payments, email delivery, error tracking, search infrastructure and observability are solved problems where a vendor's version is better than what you would build and maintain. Build when the requirement is genuinely specific to your product or when the vendor's constraints break your model.
@deep
The cost people underestimate is not building it but running it for three years: security patches, scaling, an on-call burden, and the opportunity cost of the engineers maintaining it instead of building the product.

The cost people underestimate on the buy side is lock-in and unit economics at scale — a per-seat or per-event price that is trivial at launch can dominate the cost base later, so the exit path matters.
@code text A build-versus-buy decision framework
  BUY when:                                    BUILD when:
   • it is a commodity (auth, email, logs)      • it IS the product differentiator
   • the vendor's security > ours (payments)    • vendor pricing breaks at our scale
   • it needs 24/7 expertise we lack            • the requirement is genuinely unusual
   • time to market matters more than cost      • data residency/compliance forbids the vendor
   • the exit path is reasonable                • integration cost ≈ build cost

  ALWAYS: wrap the vendor behind YOUR interface (adapter), so switching is a rewrite
          of one module rather than of the application.

  Worked example — search:
   Postgres FTS      → free, one system, good enough to ~10M docs     ← start here
   Managed OpenSearch→ facets, fuzzy, scale; +$400/mo, +sync pipeline ← when FTS is measurably short
   Algolia/Typesense → best UX, fastest to ship; per-record pricing   ← when search IS the product
@end
@senior
The adapter rule is the part I would emphasise: whatever you buy, put it behind your own interface. That single decision means a vendor's price change, acquisition or shutdown is a contained problem rather than a rewrite — and it also lets you run a fake in tests. I would also make the argument in terms of engineer-months rather than licence cost, because "we can build it in two weeks" almost always means two weeks to a prototype and a year of maintenance nobody accounted for. The strongest build case is when the thing is your product; the strongest buy case is anything involving other people's money or credentials.
@followup
When would you build your own authentication?
@answer
Rarely — Auth0, Clerk, Cognito and Auth.js cover almost every case, and getting authentication subtly wrong is expensive. I would build when requirements are genuinely unusual: complex multi-tenant identity federation, regulatory constraints on where credentials may live, or per-seat vendor pricing that becomes untenable at scale. Even then I would use well-audited libraries for the primitives — password hashing, token signing, TOTP — rather than implementing cryptography, and I would treat the whole area as needing external review.
@red
- Building commodity infrastructure to "avoid lock-in".
- Buying without wrapping the vendor behind an interface.

### How do you handle technical debt deliberately?
@short
Make it visible and attach it to a cost: which debt slows which work, and by how much. Then pay it down continuously as part of feature work rather than in a "refactor sprint" that never gets scheduled. Not all debt is worth repaying — some code is stable, ugly and untouched, and that is fine.
@deep
The useful distinction is between debt that is being paid interest on and debt that is not. A messy module nobody touches costs nothing; a messy module in the path of every new feature costs a slice of every estimate. Prioritising by change frequency — which files have the highest churn and the most bugs — targets the debt that actually hurts.

Making it visible means recording it where the work is planned, with a concrete cost, rather than in a wiki page of good intentions.
@code text A debt register that gets acted on
  ITEM      order.service.ts is 1,400 lines; refunds, pricing and fulfilment tangled
  INTEREST  every payments change takes ~2 extra days; 4 of the last 9 bugs originated here
  CHURN     modified in 31 of the last 40 PRs                    ← highest in the repo
  PLAN      extract pricing (done) → extract refunds → extract fulfilment
  COST      ~5 days, split across the next 3 feature tickets
  RISK OF   NOT doing it: payments changes stay slow and bug-prone

  Prioritise by:  churn × bug density × blast radius
  Pay down by:    the boy-scout rule on touched code + one explicit item per sprint
  Accept:         stable, untouched, ugly code. It costs nothing to leave alone.
@end
@senior
Framing debt in terms of the interest being paid is what makes the conversation work with product stakeholders: "this module costs two days on every payments change and caused four of the last nine bugs" is an argument; "the code is messy" is not. Churn data from git is the objective input — the files changed most often are where cleanliness pays compound returns. And I would be explicit that some debt should be accepted permanently, because a blanket "fix all the debt" position loses credibility fast and takes the legitimate cases down with it.
@followup
How do you stop new debt accumulating?
@answer
Make the right thing the easy thing: lint rules and type strictness that block known problems, templates and generators for the standard shapes so the good pattern is the default, and code review that treats structure as seriously as correctness. A definition of done that includes tests and observability stops the most common shortcut. And I would keep an eye on delivery pressure — most debt is created by deadlines, so the honest fix is often a scope conversation rather than a technical one.
@red
- A refactor sprint that never gets scheduled.
- Treating all debt as equally urgent.

### How do you onboard onto an unfamiliar codebase quickly?
@short
Run it first, then follow one real request end to end — from the route through the service to the database and back. Read the tests, the migrations and the deployment configuration, because those tell you what the system actually does rather than what the README claims. Then make a small change and ship it.
@deep
The fastest way to build a mental model is a vertical slice: pick one user-visible feature and trace every layer it touches. That gives you the conventions, the layering, the error handling style and the data model in one pass, whereas reading files alphabetically gives you nothing.

Migrations are underrated as documentation — they are a chronological record of how the domain evolved and what the team learned.
@code text A first-week plan that produces a mental model, not just familiarity
  DAY 1  Run it locally. Fix whatever is broken in the setup docs — first PR, real value.
  DAY 1  Read: README, package.json scripts, docker-compose, CI config, .env.example
  DAY 2  Trace ONE request end to end with a debugger:
           route → middleware → controller → service → repository → SQL → response
         Note every convention you see (errors, validation, logging, auth).
  DAY 2  Read the migrations in order. The schema is the domain model.
  DAY 3  Read the tests for the module you will work on — they document intended behaviour.
  DAY 3  Map the external dependencies: which third parties, which queues, which caches.
  DAY 4  Ship something small and real. Feel the whole pipeline: review, CI, deploy, verify.
  WEEK 2 Sit with on-call. Read the last three post-mortems. That is where the truth is.
@end
@senior
Reading the last few post-mortems is the highest-signal thing on that list and the one nobody suggests: they tell you where the system is actually fragile, which parts are load-bearing, and what the team has already learned the hard way. Fixing the setup documentation as the first pull request is the second — it is genuinely useful, it is low risk, and it exercises the full pipeline so you learn how work ships here. I would also write down every question I had to ask someone, because that list becomes the onboarding guide for the next person and it is the one document that is always out of date.
@followup
How do you evaluate whether a codebase is healthy?
@answer
I look at the feedback loops: how long does CI take, how often do they deploy, how long from commit to production, and how quickly can they roll back. Then at the test suite — does it run fast, is it trusted, or do people retry failures. Then at the alerting and the on-call load. Those tell you more about health than any amount of code style, because a codebase with fast feedback and reliable tests can be improved, and one without them cannot be changed safely no matter how clean the code looks.
@red
- Reading code alphabetically instead of tracing a request.
- Proposing a rewrite in the first month.

# SECTION Real-Time Systems & Collaboration | Your stateless WebSocket fan-out with Redis Pub/Sub and Yjs — a self-initiated project, and expect it examined closely anyway.

### How do you design a WebSocket server that scales horizontally?
@short
Keep the servers stateless with respect to *which* client is connected where: each instance holds its own sockets, subscribes to the channels its clients care about, and messages are published to Redis so every instance delivers to its own connections. Shared state — presence, room membership, sequence numbers — lives in Redis, not in process memory.
@deep
The core problem is fan-out across instances. Without a shared bus, a message sent by a client on instance A never reaches a client on instance C. Redis pub/sub solves that: publish once, every subscribed instance receives it and writes to its local sockets.

Connection distribution needs care too. Load balancers with least-connections routing plus long-lived connections mean a restarted instance receives all new connections, so reconnection storms concentrate. Jittered reconnect and gradual instance rotation mitigate that.
@arch Stateless fan-out — any instance can serve any client
   clients ──► ALB (sticky at connection level only)
        ┌──────────┬──────────┬──────────┐
        ▼          ▼          ▼          ▼
     ws-1       ws-2       ws-3       ws-4      ← each holds ONLY its own sockets
        │          │          │          │
        └──────────┴────┬─────┴──────────┘
                        ▼
                  Redis pub/sub          channel: room:<id>
                  Redis keys             presence:<user>  TTL 60s (heartbeat)
                  Redis sorted set       room:<id>:members  score = last seen
                  Redis stream           stream:room:<id>   MAXLEN ~1000 (replay)
                        │
                        ▼
                  Postgres (durable history, permissions, audit)

   Properties: any instance can die → its users reconnect elsewhere; presence
   self-heals via TTL; a client that reconnects replays from its last seq id.
@end
@code ts The instance-local registry plus the shared bus
const rooms = new Map<string, Set<WebSocket>>();          // LOCAL sockets only

sub.on('message', (channel, raw) => {
  const roomId = channel.slice('room:'.length);
  const sockets = rooms.get(roomId);
  if (!sockets) return;
  for (const ws of sockets) if (ws.readyState === ws.OPEN) ws.send(raw);
});

export async function join(ws: WebSocket, roomId: string, userId: string) {
  if (!rooms.has(roomId)) { rooms.set(roomId, new Set()); await sub.subscribe(`room:${roomId}`); }
  rooms.get(roomId)!.add(ws);
  await redis.zadd(`room:${roomId}:members`, Date.now(), userId);

  // replay what they missed while disconnected
  const since = ws.lastSeq ?? '0';
  const missed = await redis.xrange(`stream:room:${roomId}`, `(${since}`, '+', 'COUNT', 500);
  for (const [id, fields] of missed) ws.send(fields[1]);
}

export async function leave(ws: WebSocket, roomId: string, userId: string) {
  const set = rooms.get(roomId);
  set?.delete(ws);
  if (set && set.size === 0) { rooms.delete(roomId); await sub.unsubscribe(`room:${roomId}`); }
  await redis.zrem(`room:${roomId}:members`, userId);
}
@end
@senior
Unsubscribing when the last local socket for a room leaves is the detail that keeps this efficient at scale — otherwise every instance receives every room's traffic and you have built a broadcast system rather than a fan-out. The Redis stream alongside pub/sub is the other important piece: pub/sub alone loses anything sent while a client was disconnected, which on mobile networks is constantly, so a bounded stream gives you replay from a sequence id without unbounded storage. Together those two decisions are what make the design genuinely stateless and genuinely reliable.
@followup
How do you authenticate a WebSocket connection?
@answer
On the upgrade request, before accepting: read the token from a cookie or a subprotocol header, verify it, and reject the upgrade with a 401 if it fails — putting the token in the query string works but leaks it into access logs and proxy logs. Because the connection is long-lived, I also re-check authorisation periodically or on privileged actions, and I handle token expiry by requiring a refresh message rather than silently allowing an expired session to persist for hours. Revocation must reach the socket too, which is why a Redis-published revocation event closes affected connections.
@red
- Storing room membership in process memory only.
- Tokens in the WebSocket URL query string.

### How do CRDTs and Yjs make collaborative editing work?
@short
A CRDT is a data structure whose concurrent updates can be merged in any order and always converge to the same result, with no server-side conflict resolution. Yjs implements shared types — text, arrays, maps — as CRDTs, so each client applies local edits instantly and exchanges compact binary updates that merge deterministically.
@deep
Yjs uses a variant of YATA: each inserted item carries a unique id (client id plus a clock) and a reference to its neighbours, so concurrent insertions at the same position are ordered deterministically by those ids rather than by arrival time. Deletions are tombstones, compacted over time.

The consequence is that the server does not need to understand the document at all — it can be a dumb relay that broadcasts updates and persists them, which is exactly what makes the WebSocket layer stateless.
@code ts A Yjs provider with awareness, persistence and a dumb relay server
// Client
const doc = new Y.Doc();
const provider = new WebsocketProvider(WS_URL, `doc:${docId}`, doc, {
  params: { token },                         // auth on the upgrade
  connect: true,
});
const ytext = doc.getText('body');

// Presence/cursors ride alongside the document but are NOT part of it
provider.awareness.setLocalStateField('user', { name, colour, cursor: null });
provider.awareness.on('change', () => renderRemoteCursors(provider.awareness.getStates()));

// Offline edits merge automatically on reconnect — this is the CRDT property
new IndexeddbPersistence(`doc:${docId}`, doc);

// Server: relays and persists opaque binary updates. It never parses the document.
ws.on('message', async (data: Buffer) => {
  await redis.publish(`doc:${docId}`, data);                    // fan out to other instances
  await redis.xadd(`stream:doc:${docId}`, 'MAXLEN', '~', 5000, '*', 'u', data);
  debouncedPersist(docId, data);                                // append to Postgres periodically
});
@end
@senior
The property worth stating clearly is that convergence is guaranteed by the data structure, not by the server — which means the server needs no locking, no operational transform, and no per-document ownership, so it scales by adding instances. Awareness data (cursors, selections, who is online) is deliberately *not* part of the CRDT, because it is ephemeral and should not be persisted or replayed; conflating them is a common design mistake that bloats documents forever. The main costs to acknowledge are document size growth from tombstones, which needs periodic snapshot compaction, and the fact that CRDT convergence guarantees a consistent result, not a semantically *correct* one.
@followup
What does "converges but is not necessarily correct" mean in practice?
@answer
If two users concurrently edit the same sentence in incompatible ways, the CRDT will produce one deterministic merged result that both see — but that result may be a nonsensical interleaving of both edits. There is no conflict marker and no user is asked to choose. For prose that is usually acceptable and users fix it; for structured data — a form field, a price, a status — it is not, and those fields need either a last-writer-wins field with a visible indicator, or explicit locking. Knowing which parts of your document tolerate merging is a design decision, not something the library makes for you.
@red
- Claiming CRDTs eliminate all conflicts.
- Persisting awareness/cursor data into the document.

### How do you handle ordering, delivery guarantees and reconnection in a real-time system?
@short
Assign a monotonic sequence number per room or document at the server, have clients track the last sequence they applied, and on reconnect request everything after it. Delivery is at-least-once, so clients must be idempotent, and gaps must be detectable rather than silently ignored.
@deep
Ordering across a distributed fan-out is not free. Redis pub/sub does not guarantee ordering across publishers, so the sequence number must be assigned at a single point per room — an atomic `INCR` per room key, or the stream id from Redis Streams, which is naturally monotonic.

Clients detect gaps by comparing the received sequence with the expected next one, and request a resync when they diverge — which is also the recovery path after a long disconnection where the stream has trimmed past their position.
@code ts Sequencing, gap detection and resync
// Server: one authoritative sequence per room
const seq = await redis.incr(`room:${roomId}:seq`);
const envelope = JSON.stringify({ seq, type, payload, at: Date.now() });
await redis.xadd(`stream:room:${roomId}`, 'MAXLEN', '~', 1000, '*', 'e', envelope);
await redis.publish(`room:${roomId}`, envelope);

// Client: detect gaps, never silently skip
let expected = lastSeq + 1;
ws.onmessage = (e) => {
  const msg = JSON.parse(e.data);
  if (msg.seq < expected) return;                    // duplicate → ignore (idempotent)
  if (msg.seq > expected) return resync(lastSeq);    // GAP → fetch what we missed
  apply(msg); lastSeq = msg.seq; expected = msg.seq + 1;
};

async function resync(from: number) {
  const res = await fetch(`/api/rooms/${roomId}/events?since=${from}`);
  const { events, truncated } = await res.json();
  if (truncated) return fullReload();                // stream trimmed past us → snapshot
  for (const ev of events) { apply(ev); lastSeq = ev.seq; }
  expected = lastSeq + 1;
}

// Reconnect with exponential backoff AND jitter — otherwise every client returns at once
const delay = Math.min(30_000, 2 ** attempt * 500) * (0.5 + Math.random());
@end
@senior
The `truncated` flag is the detail that makes this robust: a client offline for an hour will have fallen off the bounded stream, and quietly applying only the events that remain would leave it permanently inconsistent in a way nobody notices. Detecting that and falling back to a full snapshot is the correct behaviour. The jitter on reconnection is equally important operationally — I have seen a rolling restart of a WebSocket fleet cause a synchronised thundering herd that made the recovery far worse than the original blip, and jitter is a one-line fix for that.
@followup
How do you keep a Yjs document from growing forever?
@answer
Periodic snapshot compaction: encode the current document state (`Y.encodeStateAsUpdate`) and store it as a new baseline, then discard the older incremental updates. Tombstones from deletions are what accumulate, and a snapshot collapses them. I run compaction when the update count or size crosses a threshold, keep the last snapshot plus subsequent updates for recovery, and store snapshots in object storage rather than Postgres if documents get large. Version history, if the product needs it, is a separate concern built from periodic named snapshots rather than from the raw update log.
@red
- Ignoring sequence gaps.
- Reconnecting immediately without backoff or jitter.

### How do you handle presence, typing indicators and cursors at scale?
@short
Treat them as ephemeral: never persist them, never route them through the durable path, and apply aggressive throttling. Presence lives in Redis with a TTL refreshed by heartbeats so it self-heals; cursors and typing indicators are broadcast at a bounded rate and dropped when the connection is under pressure.
@deep
The volume problem is real: a cursor position update per mouse move across fifty participants is thousands of messages per second for information that is worthless a moment later. Throttling to ten or twenty updates per second per user, and coalescing to only the latest value, reduces that by an order of magnitude with no perceptible difference.

Presence at scale also has a fan-out problem: a room with a thousand participants means every join and leave is a thousand messages. Aggregating presence into a periodic snapshot rather than per-event broadcast is the fix.
@code ts Throttled ephemeral channel, separate from the durable one
// Client: coalesce to the latest value, bounded rate
const sendCursor = throttle((pos: Pos) => {
  ws.send(JSON.stringify({ ch: 'ephemeral', type: 'cursor', pos }));   // ← never persisted
}, 50);                                                                 // ≤20/s

// Server: ephemeral messages bypass the stream and the database entirely
if (msg.ch === 'ephemeral') {
  await redis.publish(`room:${roomId}:ephemeral`, raw);      // fan out, no xadd, no persist
  return;
}

// Presence: heartbeat + TTL, aggregated snapshot rather than per-event fan-out
setInterval(async () => {
  const pipe = redis.pipeline();
  for (const [userId] of localUsers) pipe.zadd(`room:${roomId}:members`, Date.now(), userId);
  await pipe.exec();
}, 20_000);

setInterval(async () => {                                    // one snapshot per 5s beats N events
  await redis.zremrangebyscore(`room:${roomId}:members`, 0, Date.now() - 60_000);
  const members = await redis.zrange(`room:${roomId}:members`, 0, -1);
  await redis.publish(`room:${roomId}:ephemeral`,
    JSON.stringify({ type: 'presence', members }));
}, 5_000);
@end
@senior
Separating the ephemeral channel from the durable one is the architectural decision that matters, because it means cursor traffic can be dropped freely under load without any risk to document correctness. That gives you a natural load-shedding lever: when an instance is under pressure, stop broadcasting cursors and keep delivering document updates. The presence snapshot rather than per-event fan-out is the other scaling decision — it converts an O(n) message storm on every join into a fixed-rate O(1) broadcast, which is what makes large rooms viable.
@followup
How do you show "who is viewing this" across many rooms efficiently?
@answer
Aggregate rather than subscribing per room. A single Redis hash or sorted set per resource with TTL-scored members gives you counts cheaply, and the client polls or receives a batched update for all the rooms it is displaying rather than opening a subscription for each. For a dashboard showing fifty documents, one aggregated query every few seconds is far cheaper than fifty live subscriptions, and the freshness difference is imperceptible.
@red
- Persisting cursor and typing events.
- Broadcasting presence per join/leave event in large rooms.

# SECTION AI & RAG in Production | Building LLM features that are correct, cheap and observable — your pgvector and Gemini work, with its provenance stated honestly.

### How do you build a RAG pipeline end to end?
@short
Ingest and chunk documents, embed the chunks, store them with metadata in a vector store, then at query time embed the question, retrieve the top candidates with hybrid search, rerank, assemble a prompt within the context budget, and generate with citations. Every stage needs evaluation, because retrieval quality dominates answer quality.
@deep
The stage that decides quality is retrieval, not generation. A strong model given the wrong three paragraphs will confidently produce a wrong answer; a weaker model given the right paragraphs will usually be correct. So effort belongs in chunking, hybrid retrieval and reranking before prompt engineering.

Chunking strategy matters more than people expect: chunks that split mid-sentence or mid-table destroy meaning, and chunks that are too large dilute the embedding. Structure-aware splitting with overlap, plus a small contextual header on each chunk, measurably improves retrieval.
@arch The pipeline, with the quality lever marked at each stage
  INGEST      parse (PDF/HTML/MD) → clean → structure-aware split
              ├─ chunk 500–800 tokens, 10–15% overlap
              └─ prepend context: "Document: X · Section: Y" ← big retrieval win
  EMBED       batch → embedding model → vector(1536)
              store: chunks(content, embedding, tenant_id, doc_id, section, tokens)
  INDEX       HNSW on embedding + GIN on tsvector + btree on tenant_id
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  QUERY       (optional) rewrite/expand the question using conversation history
  RETRIEVE    hybrid: vector top-50 ∪ keyword top-50, fused by reciprocal rank
  RERANK      cross-encoder over the top ~30 → keep top 5     ← biggest accuracy lever
  ASSEMBLE    budget: system + history + context + answer ≤ model limit
              drop oldest history first, never the system prompt
  GENERATE    stream tokens; require citations; low temperature for factual tasks
  VERIFY      check that citations exist; flag unsupported claims; log everything
@end
@code ts Retrieval with tenant isolation and citation-ready chunks
export async function retrieve(question: string, tenantId: string, k = 5) {
  const qEmbedding = await embed(question);

  const { rows } = await db.query(`
    WITH vec AS (
      SELECT id, content, doc_id, section,
             row_number() OVER (ORDER BY embedding <=> $1) AS rank
        FROM chunks WHERE tenant_id = $2
       ORDER BY embedding <=> $1 LIMIT 50
    ), kw AS (
      SELECT id, row_number() OVER (ORDER BY ts_rank_cd(fts, q) DESC) AS rank
        FROM chunks, websearch_to_tsquery('english', $3) q
       WHERE tenant_id = $2 AND fts @@ q LIMIT 50
    )
    SELECT c.id, c.content, c.doc_id, c.section,
           COALESCE(1.0/(60+vec.rank),0) + COALESCE(1.0/(60+kw.rank),0) AS score
      FROM chunks c
      LEFT JOIN vec ON vec.id=c.id LEFT JOIN kw ON kw.id=c.id
     WHERE (vec.id IS NOT NULL OR kw.id IS NOT NULL) AND c.tenant_id = $2
     ORDER BY score DESC LIMIT 30`, [toSql(qEmbedding), tenantId, question]);

  const reranked = await rerank(question, rows);          // cross-encoder
  return reranked.slice(0, k);
}
@end
@senior
Two points I would make. Reranking is the highest return per unit of effort in most RAG systems: bi-encoder embeddings are optimised for recall at scale, and a cross-encoder that actually reads the question and the passage together is far more precise — retrieving thirty and reranking to five typically beats retrieving five directly by a wide margin. And tenant isolation must be enforced in the SQL, not by filtering after retrieval, because a RAG system that leaks another customer's documents into an answer is a data breach with a very visible artefact. I would test that specifically with a cross-tenant query in the test suite.
@followup
How do you evaluate whether retrieval is any good?
@answer
Build a small golden set — questions paired with the chunks that should be retrieved — and measure recall at k and mean reciprocal rank on every change to chunking, embeddings or retrieval. That turns "this prompt feels better" into a number. For end-to-end answer quality I use an LLM-as-judge over a fixed question set with a rubric, calibrated against human ratings on a sample, and I track it per release. Without an evaluation set, every RAG change is a guess, and regressions are invisible until users complain.
@red
- Pure vector search with no keyword component.
- No evaluation set, tuning by vibes.

### How do you manage context windows, cost and latency for LLM features?
@short
Budget the context explicitly — system prompt, retrieved context, conversation history, expected output — and enforce it with a token counter before every call. Control cost with prompt caching, model routing by task difficulty, aggressive output limits, and caching of repeated queries. Stream so perceived latency is low even when total latency is not.
@deep
Costs scale with tokens in and out, and the two biggest wastes are stuffing more context than the model needs and re-sending an identical system prompt on every turn. Prompt caching addresses the second directly, and it changes the economics substantially for long system prompts.

Model routing is the other lever: a cheap fast model handles classification, extraction and simple answers, and only genuinely hard requests go to the expensive model. Deciding that with a cheap classifier is usually worth it.
@code ts Explicit budget, caching, routing and cancellation
const LIMITS = { context: 128_000, reserveForOutput: 2_000 };

function assemble(system: string, history: Msg[], chunks: Chunk[], question: string) {
  let budget = LIMITS.context - LIMITS.reserveForOutput - count(system) - count(question);

  const context: Chunk[] = [];
  for (const c of chunks) { if (count(c.content) > budget) break; context.push(c); budget -= count(c.content); }

  const kept: Msg[] = [];
  for (const m of [...history].reverse()) {              // newest first; drop oldest
    if (count(m.content) > budget) break;
    kept.unshift(m); budget -= count(m.content);
  }
  return { system, history: kept, context, question };
}

// Cache the stable prefix so repeated turns do not re-pay for the system prompt
const res = await client.messages.create({
  model: routeModel(question),                            // cheap model for easy questions
  max_tokens: 1024,                                       // ← hard cap on output cost
  system: [{ type: 'text', text: system, cache_control: { type: 'ephemeral' } }],
  messages,
  stream: true,
}, { signal: abortController.signal });                    // ← stop paying if the user leaves

// Semantic cache for repeated questions
const hit = await cache.findSimilar(qEmbedding, { threshold: 0.97, tenantId });
if (hit) return hit.answer;
@end
@senior
Aborting the upstream call when the client disconnects is the cost control people forget: without it you keep generating tokens for a user who closed the tab, and at scale that is a measurable share of the bill. The semantic cache is the other big lever for support-style workloads where many users ask the same question in different words — a similarity threshold high enough to avoid wrong hits still catches a lot of repetition. I would also track cost per request as a first-class metric with tenant and feature labels, because AI spend is the one line item that can grow ten times overnight from a single bad loop, and you want an alert rather than an invoice.
@followup
How do you decide between a large and a small model?
@answer
By measuring on your own task, not by benchmarks. I build an evaluation set, run both models, and compare quality against cost and latency — often a smaller model is indistinguishable for extraction, classification and summarisation, which are the bulk of production AI work. Routing gives you both: a cheap model by default, escalating to the larger one when a confidence signal or the task type warrants it. The mistake is defaulting to the most capable model for everything, which is easy and expensive.
@red
- No token budgeting, relying on the model to truncate.
- No output cap, so a runaway generation costs unboundedly.

### How do you deal with hallucination and make AI answers trustworthy?
@short
Ground every answer in retrieved sources, require inline citations, verify after generation that the cited passages exist and support the claims, and design the interface so the model can say it does not know. Low temperature for factual work, and a hard rule that the model never invents identifiers, prices or dates.
@deep
Hallucination is not eliminable, so the engineering goal is containment and detectability. Grounding reduces it, citations make it checkable, and post-generation verification catches the residual cases automatically.

The interface matters as much as the model: showing the source passages next to the answer lets the user verify in a glance, and an explicit "I could not find this in your documents" path is far better than a confident fabrication.
@code ts Citation enforcement and post-generation verification
const SYSTEM = `Answer ONLY from the provided context.
Cite every factual claim with [chunk_id]. If the context does not contain the answer,
reply exactly: "I could not find this in the available documents."
Never invent identifiers, prices, dates or names.`;

const answer = await generate(SYSTEM, context, question);

// 1. Every cited id must actually be in the context we supplied
const cited = [...answer.matchAll(/\[([a-z0-9_-]+)\]/gi)].map(m => m[1]);
const supplied = new Set(context.map(c => c.id));
const invalid = cited.filter(id => !supplied.has(id));
if (invalid.length) {
  metrics.increment('llm.invalid_citation');
  logger.warn({ invalid, question }, 'model cited chunks that were not provided');
  return FALLBACK;                                   // do not show an answer we cannot back
}

// 2. Claims with no citation at all are a warning sign
const sentences = split(answer);
const uncited = sentences.filter(s => isFactual(s) && !/\[[a-z0-9_-]+\]/i.test(s));
if (uncited.length > sentences.length * 0.3) metrics.increment('llm.low_grounding');

// 3. For high-stakes answers, a second model checks entailment against the sources
const verdict = await judge(`Does the context support every claim? Answer SUPPORTED or NOT_SUPPORTED
with the offending claim.`, { context, answer });
@end
@senior
Rejecting an answer whose citations do not exist is the concrete, automatable check, and it catches a real failure mode — models do fabricate plausible-looking identifiers. I would also emphasise product design as a mitigation: for anything consequential the AI should draft and a human should approve, rather than acting autonomously. And I would measure grounding as a metric over time, because a change to chunking or a model upgrade can quietly degrade it, and without the metric you find out from a customer complaint about a confidently wrong answer.
@followup
How do you handle the case where the answer genuinely is not in the documents?
@answer
Make that a first-class outcome rather than an edge case: an explicit refusal string the model is instructed to use, detected by the application and rendered as a distinct UI state that offers next steps — refine the query, browse the source documents, contact support. I also log those cases, because a cluster of unanswerable questions is the highest-quality signal about what content is missing from the knowledge base. Treating "I don't know" as a product feature rather than a failure is what makes users trust the answers they do get.
@red
- Free-form answers with no citations.
- Treating hallucination as purely a prompt-engineering problem.

### How do you secure an LLM feature against prompt injection and abuse?
@short
Treat all model input as untrusted and all model output as untrusted. Never let the model's output directly trigger a privileged action — tool calls go through the same authorisation as a user request. Separate instructions from data, sanitise retrieved content, cap cost per user, and log everything.
@deep
Indirect prompt injection is the serious one: a document in your knowledge base, or a web page the model fetches, contains instructions that hijack the model's behaviour. Since the model cannot reliably distinguish instructions from data, the defence is architectural — restrict what the model is *able* to do, rather than trying to convince it not to.

Tool calling is where this becomes concrete. If the model can call `refundOrder`, then an injected instruction can attempt a refund, so that tool must enforce the calling user's permissions and its own limits independently.
@code ts Tools that enforce authorisation regardless of what the model was told
const tools = [{
  name: 'refund_order',
  description: 'Refund an order the CURRENT user owns',
  input_schema: { type: 'object', required: ['orderId', 'amountMinor'],
                  properties: { orderId: { type: 'string' }, amountMinor: { type: 'integer' } } },
}];

async function executeTool(call: ToolCall, ctx: { userId: string; tenantId: string }) {
  const input = ToolSchemas[call.name].parse(call.input);        // 1. validate, never trust

  // 2. authorise as the USER, not as the model. An injected instruction gets nowhere.
  const order = await orders.findScoped(input.orderId, ctx.tenantId, ctx.userId);
  if (!order) throw new Forbidden('NOT_YOURS');

  // 3. independent limits, whatever the prompt said
  if (input.amountMinor > 50_00) return { needsApproval: true, reason: 'AMOUNT_OVER_AI_LIMIT' };

  // 4. audit with the fact that an AI initiated it
  await audit.record({ action: 'order.refund', actor: ctx.userId, via: 'ai_assistant',
                       reason: call.rationale, resource: input.orderId });
  return refundOrder({ ...input, actor: { type: 'ai', onBehalfOf: ctx.userId } });
}

// Retrieved content is DATA, not instructions — delimit it clearly
const contextBlock = context.map(c =>
  `<document id="${c.id}" source="${escapeAttr(c.source)}">\n${stripControlChars(c.content)}\n</document>`
).join('\n');
@end
@senior
The principle to state plainly is that prompt-level defences are mitigations, not controls — you cannot prompt your way to security, because the model has no reliable notion of trust boundaries. The control is that the tool executes with the user's permissions and its own independent limits, so the worst an injection can achieve is what the user could already do. I would also mention output handling: model output rendered as HTML is an XSS vector, and model output used to construct a SQL query or a shell command is exactly as dangerous as user input, so it gets the same treatment.
@followup
How do you control AI cost abuse?
@answer
Per-user and per-tenant quotas measured in tokens or in currency rather than in request counts, because one request can be a thousand times more expensive than another; hard output caps; input length limits; and a circuit breaker on the spend rate that fails closed. I also monitor cost per tenant and alert on anomalies, because the realistic abuse case is not malicious — it is a customer's integration in a retry loop. Failing closed on an expensive endpoint is the right default, unlike a login endpoint where failing open is safer.
@red
- Letting the model's tool calls run with elevated privileges.
- Relying on "ignore previous instructions" style prompt defences.

### LangChain is on your CV. Do you build on a framework or write the pipeline yourself?
@short
I use LangChain for the parts where its ecosystem is genuinely a time-saver — document loaders, splitters, and integration glue while prototyping — and I do not put its abstractions on the critical path in production. The reason is debuggability: a RAG pipeline is about eight explicit steps, each of which I want to log, time, cache and test individually, and a chain hides exactly those seams.
@deep
The value a framework provides is front-loaded. On day one it saves you writing a PDF loader, a token-aware splitter, and four provider adapters, and that is real. By month three the questions you are asking are "why did this query retrieve that chunk", "which stage burned 400ms", and "can I replay last Tuesday's failing request against the new prompt" — and answering those means opening the abstraction anyway.

The specific problem is that the useful production seams and the framework's abstraction boundaries are not in the same places. I want to intercept between retrieval and reranking, cache at the embedding call, and swap the generation model per tenant. A chain is a single call that does all of it, so the interception points become callbacks and subclasses rather than functions.

So my split is: borrow the ecosystem, own the orchestration. Loaders and splitters are pure functions over documents with no runtime coupling, and they are worth taking. The pipeline itself is maybe two hundred lines of my own code, and it is the two hundred lines I most need to be able to read at 2am.
@code ts The pipeline as explicit steps — each one loggable, timeable, testable
// Not a chain. A function, where every stage is visible and independently testable.
export async function answer(q: string, ctx: Ctx): Promise<Answer> {
  const t = tracer.start('rag', { tenant: ctx.tenantId });

  const query      = await t.step('rewrite',  () => rewriteForRetrieval(q, ctx.history));
  const candidates = await t.step('retrieve', () => hybridSearch(query, ctx, { k: 60 }));
  const chunks     = await t.step('rerank',   () => rerank(query, candidates, { k: 5 }));

  if (!chunks.length) return refuse('no_relevant_context');   // ✅ an explicit branch,
                                                              //    not a silent empty prompt
  const out = await t.step('generate', () => generate(ANSWER_V7, { q, chunks }, {
    model: ctx.model, signal: ctx.signal,
  }));

  t.end({ chunks: chunks.map(c => c.id), promptVersion: 'ANSWER_V7', tokens: out.usage });
  return { text: out.text, citations: chunks.map(toCitation) };
}

// ✅ Borrowed from the ecosystem — no runtime coupling, pure input → output:
//      loaders (pdf, docx, html), token-aware splitters, tokenizers
// ❌ Not borrowed — this is where production questions get answered:
//      orchestration, retry, caching, tracing, cost attribution, prompt versioning
@end
@senior
The version-churn argument is worth making because it is concrete rather than aesthetic: LLM framework APIs have moved fast and broken things repeatedly, and taking a breaking upgrade to fix an unrelated bug is a bad position to be in for code that is on a revenue path. Owning two hundred lines is cheaper than owning that dependency risk.

Where I would happily reverse this is agent orchestration with genuinely complex state — multi-step planning with branching, retries and human approval steps. Writing your own state machine there is real work with real bugs, and that is a case where a framework is earning its abstraction rather than just wrapping a fetch call. I would also say plainly that the framework debate matters far less than chunking and evaluation, so I would not let an interview spend ten minutes on it.
@followup
What do you use for prompt and pipeline observability?
@answer
Structured tracing over the same stack as everything else, rather than an AI-specific tool by default. Every stage emits a span with its duration, and the trace carries the retrieved chunk ids, the prompt version, token counts in and out, computed cost, the model, and the tenant. That makes "which chunks produced this answer" a query rather than an investigation, and it makes cost attribution per tenant a group-by instead of a spreadsheet. The one thing I would add on top is a store of the full request and response payloads with a short retention, because replaying real failing inputs against a changed prompt is the fastest debugging loop available — with the caveat that those payloads are user data and need the same retention and deletion rules as anything else.
@red
- "We use LangChain" as the whole architecture answer.
- Chains so deeply nested you cannot say what was sent to the model.
- Writing your own loaders and splitters to avoid a dependency that costs nothing.

### Why `pgvector` and not a dedicated vector database?
@short
Because one datastore is dramatically simpler to operate than two, and Postgres already holds the documents, the tenants and the permissions the retrieval has to respect. `pgvector` keeps the vector and its metadata in the same transaction, so the index cannot drift from the source of truth. I would move to a dedicated store when the corpus outgrows what one machine can index in memory, and I would want the numbers before making that call.
@deep
The argument is mostly about joins and transactions rather than about vector math. Real retrieval is never pure similarity — it is similarity filtered by tenant, by document permissions, by date, by status, joined to metadata for display. In Postgres that is one query. Split across a vector store and a relational database it becomes two round trips plus reconciliation, and the classic bug appears immediately: the vector store still serves a document the relational database deleted, because the two writes were not atomic.

Where dedicated stores genuinely win is scale and specialisation — distributed sharding beyond one machine, memory tiering so cold vectors sit on disk, and purpose-built features like built-in reranking. Those are real, and none of them matter at a corpus of thousands to a few million chunks, which is where most products actually live.

The honest limit is memory. HNSW wants to be resident, so the index size versus available RAM is the number that decides this, and it is knowable in advance rather than a matter of opinion.
@arch The decision, with the thresholds written down
  DIMENSION            pgvector                    Dedicated (Pinecone/Qdrant/…)
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  operational cost     zero new services           a datastore to run, back up,
                                                    monitor and pay for
  metadata filtering   a SQL WHERE + join          per-product, often limited
  transactional sync   ✅ same transaction         ✅ never — eventual, needs
                        as the document write       reconciliation
  permissions          RLS, same as everything     reimplemented in the app
  backup / PITR        it is just Postgres         a second, separate story
  scale ceiling        one machine's memory        shards horizontally
  cold storage         all resident                memory tiering built in

  MY THRESHOLD, stated as a number rather than a feeling:
    index size ≈ rows × dims × 4 bytes × 1.4  (HNSW overhead)
      2M chunks × 1536 dims → ~17 GB  → comfortable on one Postgres box
     20M chunks × 1536 dims → ~172 GB → shrink it first (halfvec, 768-dim,
                                         partial indexes), then reconsider
@end
@senior
The framing I would use is that adding a datastore is not a feature decision, it is an operational commitment — backups, failover, an access-control model, a second consistency story, and one more thing that can be down at 3am. That cost is paid forever and is invisible in a benchmark, which is why "vector DB benchmarks faster" is not on its own a reason to adopt one.

I would also point out that the migration path is not frightening, which lowers the stakes of choosing Postgres first. The retrieval layer is one interface — embed, upsert, search, delete — so swapping the implementation is contained if the day comes, and the embeddings themselves are portable. Starting with the simpler option and keeping the seam clean is strictly better than adopting the complex one on a forecast that may never arrive.
@followup
What would actually push you off `pgvector`?
@answer
Three things, in order of likelihood. First, index size exceeding what one instance can hold after I have already tried `halfvec`, dimension reduction and partial indexes — that is the real ceiling and it is arithmetic, not opinion. Second, index build time becoming operationally intolerable: if a full rebuild takes eight hours and I need to re-embed on a model change, that is a maintenance window I cannot get, and distributed builds solve it. Third, a workload where the vector traffic and the transactional traffic genuinely conflict for the same resources — a heavy write path competing with heavy ANN queries on one primary is a real contention problem, though a read replica solves the milder version of it. What would not push me off is a benchmark showing lower query latency in isolation, because at my scale both are comfortably under the budget and the difference is invisible next to the LLM call.
@red
- Adopting a vector database before measuring whether Postgres is a problem.
- Two datastores with no reconciliation, so deleted documents keep being cited.
- Reimplementing tenant permissions in application code because the vector store cannot join.

### How do you keep the vector index in sync with the source of truth?
@short
By making the index a derived artefact with an explicit contract, not a second source of truth. Chunk changes are detected by content hash so only changed chunks are re-embedded, deletes are transactional with the document delete, and a periodic reconciliation job proves the two agree rather than assuming it. Every chunk carries the embedding model version, so a model change is a migration and not a corruption.
@deep
Three drift paths cause almost all of the bugs. Deletes that never reach the index, so a removed document keeps being cited — the worst one, because it is a compliance failure as well as a quality failure. Updates that re-embed the whole document, which is merely expensive, or that update the text and not the embedding, which is silent and much worse: retrieval matches on the old meaning and the model reads the new text. And model changes, where new and old embeddings coexist in one index and their distances are not comparable, so ranking degrades in a way that looks like a mysterious quality regression.

The design that avoids all three is to treat the document row as authoritative and everything vector-shaped as rebuildable. Writes go to the document inside a transaction that also enqueues the reindex; a re-embedding failure retries against a job, so the index converges. A hash per chunk makes the common case — a typo fix in a 200-page document — cost one embedding call instead of four hundred.
@code ts Content-hash diffing, transactional deletes, versioned embeddings
export async function reindexDocument(docId: string) {
  const doc = await db.documents.get(docId);
  const chunks = chunkDocument(doc);
  const existing = await db.chunks.byDoc(docId);            // id → contentHash
  const seen = new Set<string>();

  const toEmbed: Chunk[] = [];
  for (const c of chunks) {
    c.hash = sha256(c.embedText);
    seen.add(c.hash);
    const prev = existing.find(e => e.hash === c.hash);
    if (prev && prev.model === EMBEDDING_MODEL) continue;   // ✅ unchanged: skip
    toEmbed.push(c);                                        //    typo fix = 1 call, not 400
  }

  const vectors = await embedBatch(toEmbed.map(c => c.embedText));

  await db.tx(async trx => {
    // ✅ delete and insert in ONE transaction — retrieval never sees a half-updated doc
    await trx.chunks.deleteWhere({ docId, hashNotIn: [...seen] });
    await trx.chunks.upsert(toEmbed.map((c, i) => ({
      ...c, embedding: vectors[i], model: EMBEDDING_MODEL, docId,
    })));
    await trx.documents.update(docId, { indexedAt: new Date(), indexVersion: doc.version });
  });
}

// Deleting a document — the path that must never be best-effort:
await db.tx(async trx => {
  await trx.documents.delete(docId);
  await trx.chunks.deleteWhere({ docId });      // same transaction, or it drifts
  await trx.outbox.insert({ type: 'purge_caches', docId });   // semantic cache too
});

// Reconciliation, nightly — trust nothing, prove it:
//   documents with indexVersion < version      → stale, enqueue reindex
//   chunks whose docId no longer exists        → orphaned, delete and alert
//   chunks with model != EMBEDDING_MODEL       → migration is incomplete
@end
@senior
The point I would push is that the semantic cache is part of the index and is routinely forgotten. Delete a document, purge its chunks correctly, and a cached answer from yesterday still quotes it — so cache entries need to record which chunk ids produced them, and a chunk delete has to invalidate the answers derived from it. Without that, "we deleted the customer's data" is not true, and it is the kind of untrue that ends up in a compliance conversation.

On model migration, the workable pattern is a second column rather than a rewrite in place: add `embedding_v2`, backfill it in the background while retrieval keeps using v1, compare both against the golden set, then cut over and drop the old column. It costs disk temporarily and it means the migration is reversible, which for something that takes hours to recompute is worth far more than the disk.
@followup
How do you reindex a large corpus without downtime or a huge bill?
@answer
As a background job with a resume point, not a script someone runs in a terminal. It reads in batches ordered by id, records progress durably so a crash resumes rather than restarts, and rate-limits itself against the embedding provider's quota so it does not starve live traffic — the ingestion path and the user-facing path compete for the same quota, and live traffic must win. Writing into a new column means retrieval keeps serving from the old one throughout, so there is no window where the index is half-migrated and results are incoherent. I would run it at low concurrency over days rather than at high concurrency over hours, because there is no deadline that justifies degrading production, and I would check the golden set against the new column before cutting over rather than after.
@red
- Best-effort deletes outside the transaction that removed the document.
- Re-embedding whole documents on every edit.
- Mixing embeddings from two models in one index.

### How do you version and test prompts so a change cannot silently break production?
@short
Prompts are code: they live in the repository, they are versioned, they go through review, and every change runs against an evaluation set before it ships. The reason this matters more than it does for ordinary code is that a bad prompt change does not throw — it produces slightly worse answers, which nobody notices for weeks. So the safety net has to be a measurement, not an exception.
@deep
The failure mode is specific and it is what makes prompt work feel unserious to people who have only seen it done badly. Someone tweaks a sentence to fix one reported case, ships it, and accuracy on a different class of question drops by fifteen points. Nothing errors, no alert fires, and the regression is discovered from support tickets a fortnight later.

Everything follows from that. The evaluation set is the test suite, and it grows the way a test suite grows — every production failure that gets fixed is added as a case, so it can never silently return. The prompt version is recorded on every generation, so an answer in the logs can be traced to the exact text that produced it. And meaningful changes go out behind a flag with a comparison rather than as a straight replacement, because offline evaluation catches regressions but does not tell you which version users prefer.

I would also keep prompts out of the database, tempting as it is to make them editable. Once they are editable at runtime by anyone, they are outside review, outside the diff, and outside the deploy history — and the first hard incident is one nobody can reconstruct.
@code ts Prompts as versioned artefacts, with a regression gate in CI
// prompts/answer.ts — a normal reviewable, diffable module.
export const ANSWER_V7 = {
  version: 'answer@7',
  text: `You answer strictly from the provided context.
If the context does not contain the answer, say so — do not infer.
Cite every claim as [doc_id:section].`,
  changedFor: 'v6 over-inferred from partially relevant chunks (INC-412)',
} as const;

// evals/answer.eval.ts — the test suite. Grows with every production failure.
const CASES = [
  { q: 'refund window for enterprise?', mustCite: 'contract-12:Billing > Refunds',
    mustContain: ['30 days'] },
  { q: 'do you support SOC2?',          mustRefuse: true,      // ← not in the corpus
    note: 'INC-412: v6 invented an answer here' },
];

test.each(CASES)('$q', async (c) => {
  const out = await answer(c.q, testCtx);
  if (c.mustRefuse) return expect(out.text).toMatch(REFUSAL);
  expect(out.citations.map(x => x.id)).toContain(c.mustCite);
  for (const s of c.mustContain ?? []) expect(out.text).toContain(s);
});

// CI gate — a prompt change that drops the aggregate score does not merge.
//   deterministic assertions first (citations, refusals, JSON shape) — free and stable
//   LLM-as-judge only for the subjective remainder — and the judge prompt is
//   ALSO versioned, or your baseline moves under you
@end
@senior
The distinction I would draw is between deterministic checks and judged ones, because teams often reach for an LLM judge for everything and end up with a noisy, expensive gate they stop trusting. Whether the right document was cited, whether the model refused when it should have, whether the JSON parsed, whether a forbidden phrase appeared — all of that is an assertion, it is free, it is stable, and it catches most real regressions. The judge is for the residue: tone, completeness, helpfulness. And the judge's own prompt has to be pinned, or your measuring instrument drifts and every historical score becomes incomparable.

The organisational point is that this is what makes prompt engineering reviewable. Once a change is a diff with a score attached, a non-engineer can propose one and it goes through the same gate as everything else, which is the only version of "let the domain expert edit the prompt" that survives contact with production.
@followup
How large does the evaluation set need to be?
@answer
Smaller than people expect to be useful, and it matters far more that it is representative than that it is large. Fifty to a hundred cases drawn from real production queries — including the awkward ones, the ambiguous ones, and the ones where the correct answer is "I do not know" — will catch the large regressions, which are the ones that hurt. The most valuable cases are not the ones you write at the start; they are the ones added after each production failure, because those are by definition the mistakes your system actually makes rather than the ones you imagined. I would rather have eighty curated cases that someone maintains than a thousand generated ones nobody has read, because an eval set with wrong expected answers in it is worse than none — it trains you to ignore the gate.
@red
- Editing the production prompt directly to fix a single reported case.
- Prompts stored in a database and edited at runtime, outside review and deploy history.
- An LLM judge whose own prompt is unversioned, so the baseline drifts.

# SECTION Frontend Build & Web Performance | The Create React App to Vite migration, Lighthouse 62 to 89, and every number you claim on your résumé.

### How exactly did you reduce build time by migrating to Vite?
@short
Three changes at once: esbuild replaced Babel for transpilation, type-checking moved out of the build into a parallel `tsc --noEmit`, and development stopped bundling entirely — Vite serves native ES modules and transforms on demand. The production build uses Rollup with better defaults for code splitting.
@deep
The Webpack pipeline re-parsed and re-transformed the whole module graph in JavaScript on every change, with `ts-loader` type-checking in the same pass. Cold start and rebuild time therefore grew with the project.
