Vite's dev server pre-bundles dependencies once with esbuild and then serves your source as ES modules the browser imports directly — so cold start is roughly constant and hot updates touch one module.

For production, Rollup produces the bundle; the gain there is smaller and comes mainly from esbuild-based minification and better default chunking.
@code text What actually changed, and how to measure it honestly
  BEFORE (Webpack 5 + babel-loader + ts-loader + fork-ts-checker)
    cold dev server start   ~95s      ← full graph parse + transform + typecheck
    HMR after one edit      ~4–8s     ← re-bundles the affected graph
    production build        ~6m 20s

  AFTER (Vite 5 + esbuild dev transform + Rollup build + tsc --noEmit in parallel)
    cold dev server start   ~2.5s     ← deps pre-bundled once, source served as ESM
    HMR after one edit      ~120ms    ← single module invalidated
    production build        ~2m 10s   ← esbuild minify, parallel, no Babel

  MEASURED WITH:  hyperfine over 5 runs, cold cache, same machine, same commit
  TRADE-OFF ACCEPTED: type errors no longer break the dev server
                      → `tsc --noEmit` is a REQUIRED CI check
  MIGRATION COST: CommonJS-only deps needed `optimizeDeps.include`;
                  `process.env.X` → `import.meta.env.VITE_X`;
                  Jest → Vitest for a consistent transform pipeline
@end
@senior
When I am asked to justify a percentage, the credible answer names the measurement method and the trade-off, not just the number. So: measured with hyperfine over five cold runs on the same machine and commit; the reduction comes from removing Babel, removing type-checking from the hot path, and eliminating the dev bundle; and the cost is that a type error no longer breaks the dev server, which is why the CI gate is mandatory. I would also mention what did not improve — the production build gains less than development, because Rollup still has to bundle and minify everything — because being precise about the limits of a claim makes the rest of it believable.
@followup
What broke during the migration?
@answer
The usual three: CommonJS-only dependencies that needed explicit pre-bundling, environment variables changing from `process.env.REACT_APP_*` to `import.meta.env.VITE_*` with the prefix rule enforced, and the test runner — Jest's transform pipeline diverged from Vite's, so tests passed while the app broke and vice versa, which is why moving to Vitest was part of the work rather than optional. There were also a couple of libraries importing Node built-ins that Webpack had been polyfilling silently, which Vite correctly refuses to do.
@red
- Quoting a percentage with no measurement method.
- Not knowing that Vite skips type-checking in development.

### How do you improve Core Web Vitals in a real application?
@short
Measure real users first with the `web-vitals` library and attribution data, then attack the specific cause. LCP is usually an unoptimised hero image or a render-blocking chain; INP is usually a long JavaScript task; CLS is usually images without dimensions or late-injected banners.
@deep
Lab tools tell you what is possible; field data tells you what is happening. The p75 across real devices and networks is what Core Web Vitals grades, and it is typically much worse than a developer's laptop suggests.

Attribution is what makes the data actionable — knowing which element is the LCP, and which interaction and which script caused a slow INP, turns a score into a task.
@code text A concrete before/after with the specific fixes
  BEFORE (field p75, mobile)     LCP 4.1s   INP 380ms   CLS 0.24
   • hero image: 1.8MB JPEG, no dimensions, lazy-loaded  → LCP + CLS
   • 620KB of JS on the landing route (charts + date lib bundled in)
   • analytics + chat widget loaded synchronously in <head>
   • web font from a third-party CDN, no display strategy → FOIT + CLS

  FIXES
   • <Image priority sizes="(max-width:768px) 100vw, 50vw"> → AVIF, 96KB, reserved space
   • route-level code splitting; charts lazy-loaded on the dashboard route only
   • date-fns instead of moment; icons imported individually        (620KB → 180KB)
   • third-party scripts: `next/script` strategy="lazyOnload"
   • next/font self-hosted, display: swap, adjusted fallback metrics → CLS ≈ 0
   • preconnect to the API origin; preload the LCP image

  AFTER (field p75, mobile)      LCP 1.6s   INP 140ms   CLS 0.02
@end
@code js Measuring with attribution so the number becomes a task
import { onLCP, onINP, onCLS } from 'web-vitals/attribution';

onINP(m => beacon({
  name: 'INP', value: m.value, rating: m.rating,
  target: m.attribution.interactionTarget,      // WHICH element
  inputDelay: m.attribution.inputDelay,         // waiting for the main thread
  processingDuration: m.attribution.processingDuration,   // our handler
  presentationDelay: m.attribution.presentationDelay,     // render after
  route: location.pathname, device: navigator.deviceMemory,
}));
@end
@senior
The INP attribution breakdown is the most useful diagnostic in the set, because it separates three very different problems: a long input delay means the main thread was busy with something else — usually a third-party script or hydration; a long processing duration means your own handler is slow; a long presentation delay means the resulting render is expensive. Each has a different fix, and without the breakdown people guess. I would also state that the biggest wins were not React-specific at all — image format and size, third-party script loading, and route-level splitting moved the numbers far more than any component-level optimisation.
@followup
How do you stop performance regressing after you have fixed it?
@answer
Budgets enforced in CI: a bundle-size limit per route that fails the build when exceeded, and a Lighthouse CI run against a fixed device and network profile with thresholds. Then continuous field monitoring so real-user p75 is on a dashboard with an alert. Without the CI gate, every new dependency erodes the gains silently, and you end up re-doing the work in a year — which is the usual pattern when performance is treated as a project rather than a constraint.
@red
- Optimising for Lighthouse rather than for users.
- No CI budget, so gains erode.

### How do you approach code review as a senior engineer?
@short
Read the description and tests first to understand intent, then review for correctness, security and operability — error paths, concurrency, authorisation, migrations, and what happens on retry or partial failure. Style belongs to the linter. I ask questions where I am unsure and give clear, actionable feedback where I am not.
@deep
The highest-value comments are about things a tool cannot see: a missing tenant scope, an N+1 that only appears at scale, a migration that will lock a table, an operation that is not idempotent, an error swallowed silently.

Tone matters because review is where culture is set. Blocking comments should be reserved for genuine problems, and everything else marked as a suggestion, or people learn to dread review and start batching changes to avoid it.
@code text A review checklist that catches real problems
  CORRECTNESS   • error paths handled? what happens on partial failure / retry?
                • concurrency: can two requests interleave here? is the update atomic?
                • boundary cases: empty list, null, zero, very large input
  SECURITY      • is the query scoped to the user AND the tenant?
                • is input validated at the boundary? unknown fields rejected?
                • any secret, PII or token that could reach a log or a response?
  DATA          • migration safe under load? lock timeout? backwards compatible?
                • N+1? missing index for the new query? unbounded result set?
  OPERABILITY   • is a failure here visible? metric, log with request id, alert?
                • is it reversible? behind a flag? what does rollback look like?
  CLARITY       • will someone understand this in six months without the author?
                • does the test express the intent, or just cover the lines?

  NOT IN REVIEW: formatting, import order, naming bikesheds → Prettier + ESLint
@end
@senior
The operability line is the one most reviews miss and the one that matters most at 3am: if this code fails in production, will anyone know, and will there be enough context to act? A pull request that adds a feature with no metric, no meaningful log and no flag is incomplete even if the logic is perfect. I would also say something about size — reviews degrade sharply past a few hundred lines, so the most useful feedback on a two-thousand-line pull request is usually to split it, and saying that early is kinder than a superficial approval.
@followup
How do you handle disagreement in a review?
@answer
Distinguish preference from correctness. If it is a correctness or security issue, I explain the failure scenario concretely — "if two requests arrive simultaneously, both read the same stock value" — because a specific scenario is much harder to hand-wave than an abstract objection. If it is preference, I state it as a suggestion and defer to the author. If we genuinely disagree on something significant, a short conversation resolves it faster than a comment thread, and I record the decision so it does not recur.
@red
- Reviewing formatting instead of correctness.
- Blocking on personal preference.

### How do you estimate work and communicate with non-engineers?
@short
Break the work down until each piece is something you have done before, estimate ranges rather than points, state the assumptions and the unknowns explicitly, and communicate progress in terms of user-visible outcomes rather than tasks completed. When something slips, say so early with a revised plan.
@deep
Most estimation error comes from unlisted work: migration, testing, review, deployment, monitoring, documentation and the inevitable integration surprises. Making those explicit line items surfaces them rather than compressing them into "and then it's done".

For genuinely uncertain work, a timeboxed spike that produces an estimate is more honest than a number invented on the spot.
@code text An estimate that survives contact with reality
  FEATURE: add Razorpay as a third payment provider

  KNOWN (I have done this shape before)
    provider adapter + webhook verification            2d
    routing config + admin toggle                      1d
    integration tests incl. duplicate/out-of-order      2d
    reconciliation extension for the new provider       1d
    dashboards, alerts, runbook update                  1d
                                                       ───
                                                        7d

  UNKNOWN → timeboxed spike (1d) before committing to a number
    • how their webhook signature and event vocabulary differ
    • UPI/mandate flows: do they fit our existing state machine?

  ASSUMPTIONS (stated, so they can be corrected)
    • sandbox credentials available on day 1
    • no change to the order state machine
    • one engineer, no parallel incident load

  RANGE: 7–11 working days.  CONFIDENCE: medium until the spike lands.
  RISK:  if mandates need a new entity, add ~4d — I will know after the spike.
@end
@senior
Giving a range with stated assumptions rather than a single number is what makes estimates useful to a product owner: it tells them what could change the answer and what they could do about it. The second habit is early escalation — the cost of saying "this will take three more days" on day two is trivial; on day nine it is a broken commitment and a replanned quarter. And when communicating with non-engineers I talk about user-visible outcomes and risks rather than tasks, because "the refund flow is working end to end in staging, remaining risk is the reconciliation edge cases" is information they can act on, while "the adapter is done" is not.
@followup
What do you do when asked to hit a date you do not believe in?
@answer
Say so once, clearly, with the reasoning and the specific risk — then offer options rather than only an objection: reduce scope, add people to a parallelisable part, ship behind a flag to a subset, or accept a known gap to be closed after. If the decision is still to commit to the date, I commit and make the risks visible in writing so nobody is surprised later. What I avoid is silent agreement followed by a late surprise, which destroys trust far more than an unwelcome estimate does.
@red
- Single-point estimates with no assumptions.
- Discovering a slip only at the deadline.

### How do you mentor and grow other engineers?
@short
Pair on real work rather than lecturing, review with explanations rather than instructions, hand over increasing ownership with a safety net, and be explicit about the reasoning behind decisions so it transfers. The measure of success is that they make good decisions when I am not there.
@deep
The most effective mechanism I have seen is narrating the reasoning: not "add an index here" but "this query filters on user_id and sorts by created_at, so a composite index in that order lets Postgres skip the sort — here is the plan before and after". The second time, they find it themselves.

Ownership transfer needs to be real: giving someone a feature but reviewing every line and overriding decisions teaches nothing. The safety net is a design conversation up front and support when it goes wrong, not control.
@code text How I structure growth for a mid-level engineer
  WEEK 1–2   pair on a payments change: I drive, narrating every decision
             they write the tests, I review with reasoning, not instructions
  WEEK 3–4   they drive, I navigate: I ask questions rather than giving answers
             ("what happens if this webhook arrives twice?")
  MONTH 2    they own a small feature end to end: design doc → build → deploy → monitor
             I review the design BEFORE the code, which is where mistakes are cheap
  MONTH 3    they take a shift of on-call with me as backup
             they write the post-mortem for anything that fires
  ONGOING    they present one thing they learned to the team each month
             (teaching is the fastest way to consolidate)

  MY JOB: make the failure modes safe (flags, rollback, review) so they can take
          real risk and learn from real consequences.
@end
@senior
Reviewing the design before the code is the highest-leverage part: a wrong approach caught in a fifteen-minute conversation costs nothing, and caught after three days of implementation costs three days plus the emotional weight of throwing work away. The other principle is asking questions instead of giving answers — "what happens if this webhook arrives twice?" teaches the habit of thinking about duplicates, whereas "add an idempotency key" teaches one fact. And I would be honest that mentoring is a real time cost that has to be planned for, not something absorbed alongside a full delivery load.
@followup
How do you handle an engineer whose work is consistently below standard?
@answer
First establish whether it is skill, clarity or context — often the expectations were never made explicit, or they are missing information everyone else has. I give specific, timely, private feedback with examples rather than general impressions, agree what "good" looks like concretely, and pair to close the gap. If it does not improve after genuine support, that becomes a management conversation rather than something I handle informally, and I would raise it early rather than let it damage the team and the person's own prospects.
@red
- Rewriting a junior's code instead of explaining.
- Feedback only at review time, never in the moment.

# SECTION Résumé Deep-Dive Defence | Every number and every claim on your CV, turned into the question an interviewer will actually ask.

### "SQL query and index optimisation cut average API response time by 35%" — how did you measure it, and what did you change?
@short
This was the Express and Sequelize API on MySQL. I took average and p95 latency per route from structured request logs over a week before and a week after at comparable traffic — 35% is the weighted average across the top endpoints, not a uniform improvement. The reduction came from three specific changes on the heaviest routes: a composite covering index that removed a full table scan, collapsing a Sequelize N+1 into one batched query, and parallelising three independent downstream calls that had been awaited in sequence.
@deep
The method matters as much as the number. A single benchmark run proves nothing; comparing average, p95 and p99 across comparable windows does, and I watched the downstream effects — database CPU, connection-pool wait time, error rate — to be sure I had not simply moved the cost somewhere else. I lead with the caveat that average is the weaker statistic, because it hides the tail; I quote it because it is the number on my CV, and I give p95 alongside it unprompted.

The diagnosis came first, from the slow query log and per-route timing middleware: an 840ms endpoint was spending 410ms in one query, 240ms across twenty near-identical item queries, and 90ms in three serial HTTP calls. Each number pointed at its own fix.
@code sql The exact before/after I would put on a whiteboard — MySQL 8, InnoDB
-- 1. Full table scan on orders (410ms)
--    EXPLAIN showed type=ALL, rows≈480k, Extra: Using where; Using filesort
ALTER TABLE orders
  ADD INDEX idx_orders_user_status_created (user_id, status, created_at DESC, total_minor),
  ALGORITHM=INPLACE, LOCK=NONE;          -- online, no write blocking
--    -> type=ref, rows≈40, Extra: Using index  (covering)  => 12ms
--    Column order is equality-first, then range/sort: user_id, status, then
--    created_at so the ORDER BY is served by the index instead of a filesort.
--    total_minor is trailing purely to make the index cover the SELECT list.
--    Verified with EXPLAIN ANALYZE before and after, not by guessing.

-- 2. N+1: Sequelize emitted 20 x SELECT ... WHERE order_id = ?   (240ms)
--    Cause: iterating orders and calling order.getItems() per row.
--    Fix A: one batched query, grouped in memory
SELECT * FROM order_items WHERE order_id IN (?);          -- 14ms
--    Fix B (Sequelize): findAll({ include: [{ model: OrderItem, separate: true }] })
--    -> exactly two queries regardless of page size, and no row multiplication
--       from a JOIN against a hasMany, which is what breaks LIMIT.

-- 3. Three independent service calls awaited in series (90ms)
--    await a; await b; await c  ->  await Promise.all([a, b, c])   => 32ms

-- 4. Outbound HTTP with no keep-alive agent
--    new https.Agent({ keepAlive: true }) removed a TLS handshake per call.
@end
@arch The result, and the proof it was real
  ENDPOINT  GET /api/v1/orders?status=paid     avg 840ms -> 190ms   (-77% on this route)
  FLEET     weighted over the top 12 endpoints avg       -> -35%    (p95 -41%)

  MEASURED  per-route timing middleware -> structured logs -> 7 days before
            vs 7 days after, comparable traffic and comparable data volume.
  CHECKED   MySQL CPU 62% -> 38%, pool wait p95 140ms -> 9ms, error rate flat.
            The win was real, not relocated.
@end
@senior
The part I volunteer without being asked is what I deliberately did not do: I did not put Redis in front of the slow query. It was slow for a fixable reason, and caching would have hidden that while creating a cold-cache cliff later — so I fixed the query and cached only the genuinely expensive read-heavy endpoints afterwards, which is the order those two bullets actually happened in. I would also be precise that 35% is a weighted average, not a uniform improvement: the worst endpoint improved by 77%, several improved by nothing at all, and one got marginally slower because the new index added write cost. An interviewer who has done this work knows uniform improvements are not how it goes, and volunteering the endpoint that got worse buys more credibility than the one that got better.
@followup
How do you know it stayed fixed?
@answer
A dashboard with average and p95 per route and an alert on regression, plus a query-count assertion in the integration tests so an N+1 reintroduced by a future change fails CI rather than reaching production — that one is the important guardrail, because Sequelize makes it a one-line mistake to reintroduce. I also review the top statements by total latency in `performance_schema.events_statements_summary_by_digest` periodically, because new features add new slow queries and the list changes month to month. Performance work that is not protected by a gate erodes within a couple of quarters.
@red
- A percentage with no measurement method.
- Claiming a uniform improvement across every endpoint.
- Quoting an average and being unable to give the tail.
- Reciting PostgreSQL syntax for work you did on MySQL.

### "Migrated the codebase to TypeScript" — how did you do it without stopping feature work?
@short
Incrementally, with the build green at every step: `allowJs` on, files renamed module by module starting at the leaves, `strict: false` initially, then `noImplicitAny`, then `strictNullChecks`. New `any` was blocked by lint while legacy `any` was baselined, and I tracked type coverage rather than file count.
@deep
Sequencing was chosen by risk rather than convenience: payments, auth and anything touching money went first, because that is where a type error is expensive. Each converted module also got schema validation at its boundary, so the types were true rather than asserted.

The measurable outcome I reported was not "80% of files converted" but the drop in a specific class of Sentry errors — `undefined is not a function` and `Cannot read properties of undefined` — because that is what justified the time.
@code text The migration plan, phased and shippable
  PHASE 0  tsconfig with allowJs, checkJs:false, strict:false, noEmit — build stays green
           CI: tsc --noEmit added as a NON-blocking check first, then blocking
  PHASE 1  leaf modules first (utils, formatters, validators) — no dependents to break
           add Zod at every HTTP boundary as each module is converted
  PHASE 2  domain + services, highest-risk first: payments → auth → orders
  PHASE 3  noImplicitAny on (≈400 errors, fixed over 2 sprints alongside features)
  PHASE 4  strictNullChecks on, per-directory (the big one: ≈1,100 errors)
  PHASE 5  strict:true + noUncheckedIndexedAccess

  GUARDRAIL  @typescript-eslint/no-explicit-any: error, with a baseline file
             → legacy `any` allowed, NEW `any` blocked
  METRIC     type-coverage: 61% → 94%
  OUTCOME    "cannot read properties of undefined" errors in Sentry: −72% over 3 months
@end
@senior
Reporting the Sentry reduction rather than the conversion percentage is the part that made the work defensible to a product owner. It also kept me honest — converting a file to TypeScript with `any` everywhere raises the file count and changes nothing, whereas type coverage and error rate reflect real safety. The other decision I would defend is validating at the boundaries as part of the migration: types you assert about untrusted input are a lie, and converting a codebase without fixing that gives you confidence without safety.
@followup
What was the hardest part?
@answer
`strictNullChecks`, by a distance — it produced over a thousand errors and each one is a genuine question about whether a value can be absent, so it cannot be mechanically fixed. I did it directory by directory rather than globally, starting where the risk was highest. The second hardest was dependencies with wrong or missing types, where I wrote minimal local declarations covering only the surface we used rather than blocking on perfect ones.
@red
- Claiming a big-bang migration with no feature impact.
- Measuring success by files converted.

### "Designed and built 40+ RESTful API endpoints" — what makes the 41st consistent with the first?
@short
Shared conventions enforced by shared code: one validation middleware, one error shape, one pagination pattern, one auth pattern, one route helper that types the handler from its schemas. Plus a generated OpenAPI spec, so the contract cannot drift from the implementation.
@deep
Forty endpoints written by several people over two years will diverge unless the conventions are code rather than documentation. The route helper is the mechanism: because it takes the schemas and produces the typed handler, using it correctly is the path of least resistance, and using it wrong does not compile.

Consistency then pays off in the client, in the documentation, and in support — one error shape means one error handler on the frontend.
@code ts The conventions, expressed as reusable code
// One route helper: validation, typing, error handling, OpenAPI registration — all at once
export const route = defineRoute({
  method: 'get', path: '/orders', tags: ['Orders'], auth: 'bearer',
  query: ListOrdersQuery,                 // → validated, coerced, capped limit
  response: { 200: PaginatedOrders, 422: ApiError },
}, async (req) => {
  const page = await orderService.list(req.user.id, req.query);
  return { status: 200, body: page };     // ← response shape checked against the schema
});

// Shared primitives every endpoint reuses
export const Paginated = <T extends ZodTypeAny>(item: T) => z.object({
  data: z.array(item),
  page: z.object({ nextCursor: z.string().nullable(), hasMore: z.boolean() }),
});
export const ApiError = z.object({ error: z.object({
  code: z.string(), message: z.string(), fields: z.record(z.array(z.string())).optional(),
  requestId: z.string() }) });

// Checked in CI: every endpoint appears in the spec, no breaking changes vs the published one
//   npm run gen:openapi && npx oasdiff breaking openapi.prev.json openapi.json
@end
@senior
The claim worth making about forty endpoints is not the count but the consistency, because count is a measure of activity and consistency is a measure of engineering. The specific things I would name are: one error contract so the frontend has one handler; capped pagination on every list so no endpoint can be turned into a denial-of-service; cursor pagination on anything that grows; and an authorisation test matrix per endpoint so the fortieth is as safe as the first. Those are the things that stop an API surface degrading as it grows.
@followup
How do you keep the documentation accurate across 40 endpoints?
@answer
By generating it from the same schemas that validate requests, so it cannot drift, and by validating responses against the spec in the test suite so an undocumented change fails the build. Hand-maintained documentation for forty endpoints is out of date within a month — I have never seen an exception. The generation plus the contract test is what makes accuracy structural rather than a discipline problem.
@red
- Counting endpoints as an achievement.
- Documentation maintained separately from the code.

### "Payment integration with Stripe, PayPal and Razorpay for 2,000+ monthly transactions" — what was hardest?
@short
Reconciling three different webhook models into one order lifecycle without the domain knowing which provider it was dealing with. The hard parts were signature verification differing per provider, out-of-order and duplicate deliveries, and building reconciliation so a lost webhook did not leave a customer charged with no order.
@deep
Each provider signs differently — Stripe with a timestamped HMAC, Razorpay with an HMAC over the raw body, PayPal with certificate-based verification requiring an API call — and each has its own event vocabulary and currency conventions. The adapter normalises all of that into one internal event type so the order state machine has no provider branches.

The correctness backbone is three layers of idempotency plus guarded state transitions, so duplicates are no-ops and out-of-order events cannot regress state.
@code text The architecture in one picture, and what each piece defends against
  client ─► POST /payments/intent ─► adapter.createIntent(idem = "intent:<orderId>")
                                     INSERT payments(provider, intent_id UNIQUE)
                                     ↑ defends: retried create → same intent, one row

  provider ─► POST /webhooks/:provider (raw body)
              adapter.verifyWebhook() → normalised PaymentEvent
              INSERT processed_events(event_id) ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING
                ↑ defends: duplicate delivery → no-op
              UPDATE orders SET status='paid' WHERE ... AND status='pending'
                ↑ defends: out-of-order delivery → cannot resurrect a refunded order
              INSERT outbox('order.paid')
                ↑ defends: email/fulfilment happen exactly once, retried until they do

  reconciliation (15 min + daily)
              provider ledger ⟷ payments table, BOTH directions
                ↑ defends: webhook never arrived → repaired automatically or paged

  METRICS  auth success rate per provider/method · webhook lag p95 · pending >30m
           · reconciliation open issues · dispute rate
@end
@senior
The claim I would make and be able to defend is that at that volume, the risk is not throughput — 2,000 transactions a month is trivial load — it is correctness under partial failure. So the engineering effort went into idempotency, guarded transitions and reconciliation, not into performance. I would also volunteer a specific incident: a webhook delivery that arrived while a deploy was in progress, was retried, and would have double-fulfilled without the processed-events table — because a concrete near-miss demonstrates that the defences are there for a reason rather than copied from a blog post.
@followup
What would you do differently if you rebuilt it?
@answer
Model the dispute lifecycle properly from the start rather than adding it later — disputes have deadlines, evidence and outcomes that do not fit an order status column, and retrofitting that was more work than doing it upfront. I would also add the reconciliation job on day one instead of after the first lost webhook, and I would put the provider routing rules in configuration from the beginning rather than in code, because the first time you need to fail over to another provider you want it to be a flag, not a deploy.
@red
- Describing only the happy path.
- Not being able to name a concrete failure you handled.

### "Stateless WebSocket fan-out with Redis Pub/Sub and Yjs" — how does it actually work?
@short
Each server instance holds only its own sockets and subscribes to the Redis channels its clients need. A message published anywhere reaches every instance, which delivers to its local connections. Shared state — presence, room membership, sequence numbers — lives in Redis with TTLs, so an instance dying is self-healing. Yjs handles document convergence, so the server never parses the document.
@deep
"Stateless" is precise here: the servers hold connections, which is unavoidable, but no *authoritative* state. Any client can reconnect to any instance and resume from its last sequence id, because the recovery data is in Redis and Postgres.

Yjs is what removes conflict resolution from the server entirely. Updates are opaque binary blobs that merge deterministically on the clients, so the server is a relay plus a persistence layer.
@code text The design decisions and what each one buys
  DECISION                          BUYS                              COST
  per-instance local socket map     no cross-instance routing table   none
  subscribe only to needed rooms    O(rooms in use) not O(all rooms)   sub/unsub churn
  Redis pub/sub for fan-out         any instance can publish          no delivery guarantee
  Redis Stream (MAXLEN ~1000)       replay after reconnect            bounded memory
  presence via TTL + heartbeat      crashed instance self-heals       ~60s staleness window
  server-assigned seq per room      gap detection + resync            one INCR per message
  Yjs CRDT on the client            no server-side conflict logic     tombstone growth →
                                    server never parses documents      periodic snapshot compaction
  ephemeral channel for cursors     can be dropped under load         cursors may lag

  FAILURE HANDLING
   instance dies      → clients reconnect (jittered backoff), resume from lastSeq
   Redis fails over   → fan-out pauses; clients detect a gap and resync from the stream/API
   client offline 1h  → stream trimmed past them → `truncated` → full snapshot reload
@end
@senior
The two details I would lead with are the sequence-number gap detection and the jittered reconnect, because they are what make it survive real conditions rather than a demo. Without gap detection a client silently misses messages and diverges; without jitter, a rolling restart produces a synchronised reconnection storm that makes recovery worse than the outage. And I would be explicit about the limitation: Redis pub/sub is fire-and-forget, so the Stream is what provides the actual delivery guarantee, and anything that must never be lost is written to Postgres, not just published.
@followup
How many concurrent connections did it handle, and what was the limiting factor?
@answer
I would give the honest measured figure rather than a headline number, and name what bound it — typically memory per connection and file descriptors per instance rather than CPU, since idle WebSockets cost little CPU but each carries buffers and an fd. The practical limits I would raise are `ulimit -n`, the load balancer's connection limits and idle timeout, and Redis subscription fan-out when the number of distinct rooms per instance grows. Being able to say "it was bound by X and here is how I measured it" is worth more than a large number with no context.
@red
- Calling it stateless without explaining what state moved to Redis.
- Claiming CRDTs solve all conflicts.

### "Offline-first geolocation tracking in React Native" — what were the real problems?
@short
Battery, OS background restrictions, and data volume — in that order. The engineering answers were distance-filtered and activity-aware accuracy, a native background task rather than a JavaScript timer, batched idempotent uploads through a durable SQLite outbox, and downsampling with a retention policy so storage stayed bounded.
@deep
The naive implementation drains a phone in hours and is killed by the OS within minutes of backgrounding. High-accuracy GPS at a fixed interval is the single most expensive thing an app can do, and Android's Doze plus manufacturer battery optimisers will stop a JavaScript timer entirely.

On the server side, a point every few seconds per user is millions of rows a month, which forces partitioning, batching and a downsampling policy from the start rather than as a later optimisation.
@code text The problems and the specific engineering response
  BATTERY      continuous high-accuracy GPS drains a phone in ~4h
   → distanceFilter 50m; accuracy reduced when `still`, raised when `in_vehicle`
   → batched upload (200 points) instead of one request per point
   → measured on real devices: ~4h → full working day

  OS KILLS     iOS suspends the app; Android Doze + OEM optimisers kill background work
   → native background task with the location entitlement, not setInterval
   → Android foreground service with a persistent notification
   → detect battery-optimisation state and guide the user to the exemption screen

  CONNECTIVITY tunnels, lifts, aeroplane mode, hours offline
   → SQLite outbox (NOT AsyncStorage), status pending|inflight|acked
   → exponential backoff + jitter, cap 5 min; 4xx → dead-letter, 5xx → retry
   → client-generated UUID per point = idempotency key; server has a unique index

  DATA VOLUME  1 point / 5s = ~17k rows/user/day
   → server: partitioned by month, dropped by DROP TABLE, BRIN index on time
   → downsample: full resolution 7d → 1/min for 90d → hourly rollup forever
   → device: outbox capped; oldest points downsampled if offline for days

  PERMISSIONS  "Always" location is denied by most users if asked cold
   → rationale screen first, system prompt only after they agree
   → measured: acceptance roughly doubled
@end
@senior
The permission-rationale result is the one I would lead with in a product-minded interview, because it is an engineering-adjacent decision with a measurable business outcome: asking for "Always" location on first launch gets denied, and iOS effectively gives you one chance. The battery number is the second, because it is the difference between a product users keep and one they uninstall. And I would name the storage discipline explicitly — the downsampling policy was designed before launch, because retrofitting retention onto a table with a billion rows is a much harder project than defining it upfront.
@followup
How did you debug problems you could not reproduce?
@answer
Telemetry from the sync engine: every state transition — tracking started, provider changed, permission revoked, batch synced, backoff entered — logged with a device id, OS version and battery-optimisation state, sent with the next batch. That turned "it sometimes stops syncing" into a query. Without it, background bugs on Android across a dozen manufacturer skins are effectively undebuggable, because you cannot attach a debugger and the user cannot tell you what happened.
@red
- Not mentioning battery.
- Using AsyncStorage as the durable queue.

### "Python data-ingestion pipelines and pgvector retrieval, HNSW-indexed semantic search over thousands of documents" — what did you learn?
@short
That retrieval quality dominates everything. Chunking strategy and hybrid search plus reranking moved answer quality far more than prompt engineering or model choice. And that an evaluation set is not optional — without one, every change is a guess and regressions are invisible. I say upfront that this is a self-initiated project with no real users, so every number I quote is from my own golden set, not from production traffic.
@deep
The specific improvements that mattered, in order: structure-aware chunking with a contextual header on each chunk, hybrid retrieval fusing vector and keyword results, and a cross-encoder reranker over the top thirty. Each was measurable against a golden set of questions with known correct sources.

The operational lessons were about cost and isolation: aborting generation when the client disconnects, capping output tokens, caching semantically similar questions, and enforcing tenant filters inside the SQL rather than after retrieval.
@code text What moved the numbers, measured on a 120-question golden set
  BASELINE   naive 1000-char chunks, pure vector top-5
             recall@5 = 0.58   ·  answer accuracy (LLM judge, human-calibrated) = 0.61

  +1  structure-aware chunking (headings/tables respected, 15% overlap)
      + contextual header "Document: X · Section: Y" prepended to each chunk
      recall@5 = 0.71   accuracy = 0.69

  +2  hybrid retrieval: vector ∪ keyword, reciprocal rank fusion
      → fixed the failure class where users search by product/error CODE
      recall@5 = 0.83   accuracy = 0.76

  +3  cross-encoder rerank of top-30 → top-5
      recall@5 = 0.91   accuracy = 0.85          ← biggest single jump

  +4  citation enforcement + "not in the documents" refusal path
      accuracy unchanged, but WRONG-and-confident answers fell sharply
      (the metric I actually optimised for)

  COST   semantic cache (0.97 threshold) removed ~30% of calls on a replayed
         question set; output cap + client-disconnect abort removed more waste
  CAVEAT self-hosted Postgres, my own corpus, my own 120-question golden set.
         No production traffic — I say so before I am asked.
@end
@senior
The finding I would emphasise is that keyword search fixed a failure class embeddings simply cannot handle — exact identifiers like error codes and SKUs, where semantic similarity is meaningless. That is why hybrid retrieval is not an incremental tweak but a correctness fix. The second is that the metric worth optimising was not accuracy but the rate of confident wrong answers, and the refusal path plus citation enforcement improved that without changing accuracy at all. Choosing the right metric was as important as any technical change.
@followup
Why pgvector rather than a dedicated vector database?
@answer
Because the corpus fitted comfortably in Postgres, and keeping embeddings in the same database as the source documents meant transactional consistency — a document update and its re-embedding commit together — plus the ability to filter and join with relational data in one query, which a separate store cannot do without a second round trip. It also avoided a whole operational surface: another datastore to back up, monitor, secure and keep in sync. I would move to a dedicated store at a scale where HNSW build time and memory in Postgres became the constraint, and I would want a measurement, not an assumption.
@red
- Attributing quality gains to prompt engineering alone.
- No evaluation set.
- Letting a self-initiated project sound like it served real users.

### "Moved the React build from Create React App to Vite, cutting build times 60%+ (~90s to ~35s)" — prove it.
@short
Measured with hyperfine over five cold runs on the same machine and the same commit, before and after: the production build went from about 90 seconds to about 35 seconds, a 61% reduction. The gain came from esbuild replacing Babel for transpilation, type-checking moving out of the build into a parallel `tsc --noEmit`, and dropping the ejected Create React App webpack config with its full source-map and polyfill defaults.
@deep
Two numbers improved for different reasons, and it matters that I separate them. The production build is the 90s → 35s figure and it is bounded, because Rollup still has to bundle, tree-shake and minify — the win there is esbuild-based transform and minification instead of Babel plus Terser. The development experience improved far more than 60%, because Vite does not bundle in development at all: it serves native ES modules and only transforms the file you actually requested, so cold start went from roughly 40 seconds to about 1.5 seconds and hot reload became effectively instant.

Quoting one blended number without that distinction is where these claims lose credibility, so I quote the production build — the smaller and more defensible number — and mention the dev-server gain separately.
@code bash The measurement, reproducible
# Same machine, same commit, cold cache, 5 runs each
hyperfine --warmup 1 --runs 5 --prepare 'rm -rf node_modules/.cache build dist .vite' \
  'npm run build:cra' 'npm run build:vite'

# Benchmark 1: react-scripts build    89.6 s ± 2.7 s
# Benchmark 2: vite build             34.8 s ± 1.1 s   → 2.57x faster  (-61%)

# Dev server cold start, measured to first byte of the app shell
# react-scripts start   ~40 s        (webpack bundles the whole graph first)
# vite                  ~1.5 s       (native ESM, transform on request)

# What actually moved the production number
#   babel-loader -> esbuild transform            ~28 s
#   fork-ts-checker in-build -> parallel tsc      ~14 s
#   source-map 'source-map' -> 'hidden-source-map' ~8 s
#   terser -> esbuild minify                      ~5 s
@end
@senior
The honest caveat I volunteer is that Create React App was already an unmaintained baseline — it is deprecated, it was pinning us to an old webpack and Babel toolchain, and beating it is not the same as beating a well-tuned modern webpack config. So the real argument for the migration was never the 60%: it was that CRA was a dead dependency we could not upgrade, that `react-scripts` hid the config so we could not fix anything ourselves, and that the build number was the measurable side effect that made the case fundable. Leading with the maintenance argument and treating the number as evidence is what makes this read as engineering judgement rather than a benchmark chase.
@followup
Did anything get worse?
@answer
Two things. Type errors stopped breaking the dev server, because Vite transpiles without type-checking, so a developer can now run code that does not compile — which is why `tsc --noEmit` became a required CI check and a pre-push hook. And the development and production pipelines diverged, since development uses esbuild over native ESM while production uses Rollup, so a bug can in principle appear only in the production bundle; I mitigate that by running a real production build and a smoke test in CI on every pull request rather than only on merge. There was also a one-off migration cost: `process.env` became `import.meta.env`, `require` and Jest module mocks had to move, and SVG and worker imports needed plugins.
@red
- A percentage with no methodology.
- Claiming the production build improved as much as the dev server did.
- Selling it as a performance win when the real driver was an unmaintained toolchain.

### "Code-splitting and CDN assets lifted Lighthouse 62 to 89 and page load 3.8s to 2.1s" — which metric, and how?
@short
Lighthouse mobile went 62 to 89 and the load number I quote is LCP at the p75 of real users, 3.8 seconds to 2.1 seconds. Three changes did almost all of it: route-level code splitting so the landing route stopped downloading the dashboard's charting library, static assets moved to a CDN with long-lived immutable cache headers, and the hero image re-encoded to WebP at the right dimensions with the size reserved so it stopped shifting layout.
@deep
The lab score is a proxy; the p75 of real users is what Google grades and what the business feels. So I instrumented with the `web-vitals` library first, which named the LCP element and the specific interaction behind slow INP, and fixed those rather than applying general advice.

The largest contributors were not React-level at all: image bytes, JavaScript shipped to a route that did not need it, and assets served from the origin behind the same slow connection as the API. This was a Vite build after the Create React App migration, so `manualChunks` and dynamic `import()` were the splitting mechanism and the CDN was an S3 origin behind CloudFront with hashed filenames.
@code text What moved, and why — lab score and field p75, mobile
  BEFORE   Lighthouse (lab, mobile) 62   LCP 3.8s   TBT 410ms   CLS 0.19
  AFTER    Lighthouse (lab, mobile) 89   LCP 2.1s   TBT 150ms   CLS 0.03

  1. CODE SPLITTING                                       LCP -0.9s, TBT -190ms
     One bundle, 620KB gz, every route's code on first paint.
     -> React.lazy + Suspense per route; charts, the rich-text editor and the
        PDF viewer became dynamic import()s reached only on their own routes
     -> vite manualChunks: react/react-dom in a stable vendor chunk so an app
        deploy does not invalidate the framework chunk
     -> moment -> date-fns, per-icon imports instead of the whole set
     => landing route 620KB -> 180KB gz

  2. CDN ASSETS                                           LCP -0.5s
     JS, CSS and images served from the app origin, Cache-Control: no-cache.
     -> S3 + CloudFront, hashed filenames, immutable, max-age=31536000
     -> index.html stays no-cache so a deploy is picked up immediately
     -> preconnect to the API origin; modulepreload for the entry chunk

  3. IMAGES + RESERVED SPACE                              LCP -0.3s, CLS -0.16
     1.8MB JPEG hero, lazy-loaded, no width/height.
     -> WebP at the served size, eager with fetchpriority="high", explicit
        width/height, srcset for mobile                    -> 110KB
     -> font-display: swap with a metric-matched fallback

  GUARDRAIL  size-limit budget per route in CI + Lighthouse CI on a fixed
             device/network profile + field RUM so regressions show within a day
@end
@senior
The point I would make is that a Lighthouse score is a means, not the goal — it is possible to chase the lab number with tricks that do not help real users, and it is possible to have a mediocre lab score with excellent field data. So I quote both, and I lead with the field p75 because that is what the business cares about. I would also be precise that 89 is not 100 deliberately: the remaining gap was third-party analytics and a chat widget the business wanted, and pushing past that would have meant removing features rather than doing engineering — knowing where to stop is part of the work. The other honest note is that the biggest wins were unglamorous: not shipping a charting library to a page with no charts, and putting a cache header on a static file.
@followup
How do you prevent regression?
@answer
A bundle-size budget per route enforced in CI so a new dependency fails the build, Lighthouse CI against a fixed device and network profile with thresholds, and field monitoring with an alert on p75 regression. Without the CI gate the gains erode within a couple of quarters, because every feature adds a little and nobody notices until the total is bad again — I have seen a single unguarded `import` of a date library put 90KB back on a route. Performance has to be a constraint in the pipeline, not a project that finishes.
@red
- Optimising the lab score without field data.
- Quoting a score with no device and network profile attached.
- No budget in CI.

### "Designed relational schemas in MySQL — versioned migrations shipped without downtime" — how do you ship a schema change with no downtime?
@short
The rule is that the schema and the code must be compatible in both directions during the rollout, because for a period both the old and new versions are running. So every change is expand-then-contract: add the new thing as nullable with a default, backfill in batches, dual-write, switch reads, and only drop the old column in a later release. On MySQL 8 the mechanics matter too — `ALGORITHM=INPLACE, LOCK=NONE` for the changes that support it, and a tool like gh-ost or pt-online-schema-change for the ones that do not.
@deep
The failure everyone hits once is deploying a migration that drops or renames a column in the same release as the code that stops using it. During a rolling deploy the old instances are still selecting that column, so you take an outage for the length of the rollout. Renames are the worst case: there is no version of the code that works against both names, which is why a rename is always implemented as add, backfill, dual-write, switch, drop — four releases, never one.

The second failure is a migration that holds a metadata lock. In MySQL an `ALTER TABLE` needs the metadata lock, and a single long-running `SELECT` blocks it — and every query queueing behind that blocked ALTER then blocks too, so one slow report can take the whole table down. That is why `lock_wait_timeout` gets set low for migrations: fail fast and retry rather than pile up.

Backfills are batched and throttled for the same reason. `UPDATE orders SET x = ... WHERE x IS NULL` across half a million rows is one enormous transaction: it holds locks, inflates the undo log, and blows out replica lag. Batches of a few thousand by primary key, with a pause between them and a check on `Seconds_Behind_Master`, are slower to write and dramatically safer.
@code sql Expand → migrate → contract, the MySQL 8 mechanics
-- GOAL: orders.customer_ref (VARCHAR) -> orders.customer_id (BIGINT FK)
-- NEVER as one migration. Four releases.

-- R1 EXPAND — additive only, old code is unaffected
ALTER TABLE orders
  ADD COLUMN customer_id BIGINT NULL,
  ALGORITHM=INSTANT;                     -- MySQL 8.0: adding a nullable column
                                         -- at the end of the row is metadata-only
ALTER TABLE orders
  ADD INDEX idx_orders_customer (customer_id),
  ALGORITHM=INPLACE, LOCK=NONE;          -- online, writes keep flowing

-- R1 also: application DUAL-WRITES both columns. Reads still use customer_ref.

-- R2 BACKFILL — batched, throttled, resumable, idempotent
--   run as a job, not as a migration step in the deploy
UPDATE orders SET customer_id = (SELECT id FROM customers c WHERE c.ref = orders.customer_ref)
 WHERE customer_id IS NULL AND id BETWEEN ? AND ?     -- 2000-row windows by PK
 ORDER BY id LIMIT 2000;
--   between batches: sleep 100ms, and pause if replica lag > 5s
--   verify: SELECT COUNT(*) FROM orders WHERE customer_id IS NULL;  -- must be 0

-- R3 SWITCH — reads move to customer_id. Still dual-writing. Fully reversible:
--   a rollback to R2 code works, because both columns are still correct.

-- R4 CONTRACT — only after R3 has been stable in production
ALTER TABLE orders ADD CONSTRAINT fk_orders_customer
  FOREIGN KEY (customer_id) REFERENCES customers(id);
ALTER TABLE orders DROP COLUMN customer_ref;

-- The ones that are NOT online in MySQL 8 (need gh-ost / pt-osc):
--   changing a column TYPE, adding a column in the MIDDLE of the row,
--   adding a PRIMARY KEY, converting the character set
SET SESSION lock_wait_timeout = 5;       -- never queue behind a long SELECT
@end
@senior
The judgement I would show is that the deploy pipeline has to enforce this, not the engineer's memory. Migrations run as a separate step before the application rollout and are required to be backward compatible, so a migration that drops a column fails review by policy rather than by luck. I also keep migrations out of application startup entirely — if every instance runs migrations on boot, a rolling deploy runs them concurrently and you rely on the advisory lock working correctly under a partial failure, which is not a thing to rely on. The other point worth making is about foreign keys: I do use them, because the alternative is discovering orphaned rows two years later, but I am precise that they cost write throughput and make bulk operations harder, so on the highest-volume tables I have used an index and a nightly integrity check instead. That is a trade-off with a reason, not a rule.
@followup
How do you roll back a migration that has already run?
@answer
Mostly, you do not — you roll forward. Additive changes need no rollback at all, which is the main argument for expand-and-contract: at every intermediate step the previous release still works, so the rollback is a code deploy rather than a schema change. For a genuinely bad migration, a `DROP COLUMN` cannot be undone by re-adding it because the data is gone, so the real recovery is point-in-time recovery from backup plus binlog, which is an outage. That asymmetry is exactly why destructive steps are separated by releases and why I want a column to have been unused in production for a full release cycle before it is dropped. I also test the migration against a restored copy of production data, not against a small development database, because a migration that takes 200ms on 10,000 rows can take 40 minutes on 20 million.
@red
- Renaming or dropping a column in the same release that changes the code.
- Running migrations on application startup.
- An unbatched backfill in a single transaction.
- Never having tested the migration against production-sized data.

### "Added Redis caching for read-heavy endpoints and session data" — what did you cache, and how did you invalidate it?
@short
Two different things with two different designs. Read-heavy endpoints got cache-aside with a short TTL and explicit invalidation on write, keyed by the exact query parameters and scoped by tenant. Sessions got Redis as the primary store rather than a cache, because in-process sessions do not survive multiple PM2 instances or a restart — that one was about correctness under horizontal scaling, not performance.
@deep
The important distinction is that a cache must be allowed to disappear. If losing Redis logs everyone out or corrupts data, it is not a cache, it is a database with no durability guarantees — so I am explicit that session data in Redis is persisted with AOF and treated as a store, while endpoint caches are pure derived data that can be flushed at any time.

For invalidation, TTL alone is a correctness compromise you accept knowingly: a 60-second TTL means up to 60 seconds of stale reads, which is fine for a product listing and unacceptable for an account balance. So writes delete the affected keys, and TTL is only the backstop for the invalidations I inevitably miss. Key design is what makes that possible — a key that encodes the tenant and every parameter that changes the result, with a version prefix so I can invalidate an entire class of keys by bumping the prefix rather than scanning for them.

The two failure modes worth naming are the stampede and the scan. A stampede is what happens when a hot key expires under load: every concurrent request misses, and they all hit the database at once — a single-flight lock or a probabilistic early refresh prevents it. The scan is the operational trap: `KEYS pattern` is O(n) and blocks the single-threaded server, so invalidating by pattern in production is how you cause the incident you were trying to prevent.
@code ts Cache-aside with single-flight, and the invalidation that goes with it
// KEY DESIGN — version prefix, tenant scope, every parameter that changes the result
const key = `v3:t${tenantId}:products:${category}:${page}:${sort}`;

async function getProducts(p: Params) {
  const hit = await redis.get(key(p));
  if (hit) return JSON.parse(hit);

  // SINGLE FLIGHT: only one request per key rebuilds; the rest wait briefly.
  // Without this, a hot key expiring under load sends every concurrent
  // request to MySQL at the same instant.
  const lock = await redis.set(`lock:${key(p)}`, '1', 'NX', 'PX', 5000);
  if (!lock) {
    await sleep(50);
    const second = await redis.get(key(p));
    if (second) return JSON.parse(second);
    // still nothing: fall through and query rather than wait forever
  }

  const rows = await db.query(...);                       // the expensive read
  await redis.set(key(p), JSON.stringify(rows), 'EX', 60); // TTL = backstop only
  return rows;
}

// INVALIDATION on write — delete, do not update. An update races with a
// concurrent rebuild and can leave a stale value with a fresh TTL.
async function updateProduct(id: string, patch: Patch) {
  await db.transaction(async t => { await Product.update(patch, { where: { id }, transaction: t }); });
  await redis.del(`v3:t${tenantId}:product:${id}`);        // exact keys
  await redis.incr(`v3:t${tenantId}:products:epoch`);      // list keys: bump the epoch
}
// Lists embed the epoch in their key, so bumping it invalidates every list
// query for that tenant in O(1) and the dead keys expire on their own.
// NEVER: `KEYS v3:t1:products:*` — O(n) on a single-threaded server.
//        SCAN with a cursor if you truly must, never KEYS.

// SESSIONS are a different contract — a store, not a cache:
//   connect-redis, AOF everypsec, TTL = session lifetime, sliding on activity.
//   Losing this logs everyone out, so it is monitored and backed up like a database.
@end
@senior
The thing I would volunteer is what I chose not to cache. Caching is a correctness liability that buys latency, so the first question is always whether the read is slow for a fixable reason — an unindexed query behind a cache is a bug with a timer on it, and it surfaces as a cliff the first time the cache is cold after a restart. The order I actually worked in was: fix the query, measure again, then cache what was still genuinely expensive. I would also name the operational discipline that goes with it: a hit-rate metric per key class, because a cache with a 30% hit rate is adding a network round trip to 70% of requests and making things worse; a memory policy set deliberately — `allkeys-lru` for the cache, `noeviction` for sessions, and separate databases or separate instances so an eviction sweep cannot delete anybody's session; and a test that runs with Redis switched off, because the application must degrade to slow, not to broken.
@followup
What happens when Redis goes down?
@answer
For the endpoint caches, every request becomes a miss and goes to MySQL — so the real question is whether the database survives the full uncached load, and the honest answer is that it does not, unless you plan for it. That is why the client has a short timeout and a circuit breaker: if Redis is unreachable, fail open to the database immediately rather than adding a five-second timeout to every request, and shed or queue load if the database saturates. For sessions, failing open is not an option — everybody is logged out — which is why that instance runs with AOF persistence and is monitored as a critical dependency rather than treated as disposable. Separating the two concerns is what makes each answer possible.
@red
- Treating session data and cached reads as the same problem.
- `KEYS` in production.
- Updating a cache entry on write instead of deleting it.
- No plan for the cold-cache or Redis-down case.

### "Containerised services with Docker and deployed to Linux/VPS and AWS behind Nginx and PM2, with GitHub Actions CI/CD" — walk me through a deploy.
@short
Push to main runs the pipeline: install with a lockfile, lint, typecheck, unit and integration tests against a real MySQL and Redis in services containers, then a multi-stage Docker build tagged with the commit SHA and pushed to the registry. Deploy pulls that exact image on the host, starts the new container alongside the old one, waits for its health check to pass, and only then does Nginx switch upstream and the old container stop. Rollback is redeploying the previous SHA, which is roughly ninety seconds.
@deep
The two things that make this safe rather than hopeful are the immutable tag and the health gate. Tagging by commit SHA rather than `latest` means the artefact that passed CI is byte-for-byte the artefact that runs, and it makes rollback a deterministic operation rather than a rebuild that might pick up a different transitive dependency. The health gate means a container that cannot reach MySQL never receives traffic — without it, a rolling deploy cheerfully replaces a working service with a broken one.

I should be precise about PM2 versus Docker, because it looks like duplication. Historically the services ran under PM2 directly on the VPS in cluster mode, which is what gives you multiple Node processes across cores and restart-on-crash. As services moved into containers, the process supervision moved to Docker's restart policy and the clustering moved to running multiple containers, and PM2 stayed only where it was still earning its place. Running PM2 inside a container as well is usually the wrong answer — you end up with two supervisors disagreeing about what a crash means, and Docker cannot see the real process health.

Nginx is doing three separate jobs and it is worth naming them separately: TLS termination with certificates from Let's Encrypt on an auto-renew timer, reverse proxying to the application upstreams, and serving static assets directly so Node never handles a file request. The WebSocket routes need the `Upgrade` and `Connection` headers passed through explicitly and a long `proxy_read_timeout`, which is the specific detail that catches people.
@code yaml The pipeline, and the deploy step that makes rollback trivial
# .github/workflows/deploy.yml
jobs:
  test:
    services:                              # real dependencies, not mocks
      mysql:  { image: "mysql:8", env: { MYSQL_ROOT_PASSWORD: test }, ports: ["3306:3306"] }
      redis:  { image: "redis:7", ports: ["6379:6379"] }
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-node@v4
        with: { node-version: 20, cache: npm }
      - run: npm ci                        # lockfile, never npm install
      - run: npm run lint && npx tsc --noEmit
      - run: npm run migrate && npm test   # migrations must apply cleanly too

  build:
    needs: test
    steps:
      - run: docker build -t $REG/api:${{ github.sha }} .   # SHA, never :latest
      - run: docker push $REG/api:${{ github.sha }}

  deploy:
    needs: build
    environment: production                # requires an approval
    steps:
      - run: ssh $HOST "IMAGE=$REG/api:${{ github.sha }} ./deploy.sh"
# deploy.sh, on the host:
#   docker pull  $IMAGE
#   docker run -d --name api-new --health-cmd 'curl -f localhost:3000/health/ready' $IMAGE
#   wait for healthy (60s timeout) --------------> if unhealthy: rm api-new, EXIT 1
#   nginx: switch upstream to api-new, reload (graceful, drains in-flight)
#   docker stop --time=30 api-old   # SIGTERM, 30s to finish in-flight requests
#   ROLLBACK = re-run deploy.sh with the previous SHA. ~90s.
@end
@arch The Dockerfile decisions that matter, and why
  MULTI-STAGE
    stage 1  node:20  -> npm ci (all deps) -> tsc build
    stage 2  node:20-slim -> npm ci --omit=dev -> COPY --from=1 /dist
    => the compiler, the dev dependencies and the source never ship.
       ~1.1GB image -> ~180MB, and a smaller attack surface.

  LAYER ORDER   COPY package*.json  ->  npm ci  ->  COPY src
                (dependencies re-install only when the lockfile changes)

  USER node     never root. A container escape should not be a root escape.

  SIGNALS       no shell-form CMD: `CMD ["node","dist/main.js"]` so the process
                is PID 1 and receives SIGTERM. With shell form, sh is PID 1,
                it does not forward the signal, and every deploy kills
                in-flight requests after a 10s timeout instead of draining.

  GRACEFUL      SIGTERM -> stop accepting new connections, finish in-flight,
                close the MySQL pool and the Redis client, then exit 0.

  HEALTH        /health/live  = the process is up          (restart if failing)
                /health/ready = MySQL + Redis reachable    (route traffic if passing)
                Conflating them means a database blip restart-loops the app.

  SECRETS       injected as environment variables at run time from the CI
                secret store. Never in the image; a layer is public forever
                to anyone who can pull it.
@end
@senior
The honest framing I would give is about scale and what it justifies. A single VPS with Nginx, a couple of containers and a health-gated swap is the right architecture for the traffic I was running, and reaching for Kubernetes there would have been resume-driven development — the operational cost would have exceeded the benefit by a wide margin. What I would want to show instead is that I know where this architecture stops: it has no horizontal capacity beyond that box, the deploy has a brief window where both versions are live so the database schema must be compatible with both, and there is a single point of failure I mitigated with monitoring and a fast rebuild rather than with redundancy. Being able to name the limits of your own architecture is a stronger signal than having used a bigger one. The other thing I would raise is that the deploy is only half of it — the pipeline also has to make bad deploys visible, so error rate and p95 latency are watched for the ten minutes after a rollout and a spike triggers an alert that names the deploy as the suspect.
@followup
How do you handle secrets and configuration across environments?
@answer
Configuration comes from environment variables, validated at startup against a schema so the process refuses to boot with a missing or malformed value rather than failing at 3am on the first request that needs it — that check has caught more misconfigurations than any amount of documentation. Secrets live in the GitHub Actions secret store and are injected at deploy time into the container environment; they are never in the image, never in the repository, and `.env` is gitignored with a committed `.env.example` listing the keys and no values. Each environment has its own credentials with its own database user and its own least-privilege grants, so a staging leak cannot touch production. If I were tightening this further the next step is AWS Secrets Manager or SSM Parameter Store with IAM-scoped access and rotation, which removes the standing copy of the secret in the CI store — I would name that as the known gap rather than pretend the current setup is the end state.
@red
- Deploying `:latest`.
- No health gate before switching traffic.
- Secrets baked into the image or committed to the repository.
- Shell-form CMD, so SIGTERM never reaches Node and every deploy drops requests.
- Not knowing how to roll back.

### "Built a shared API-contract and component library for React, Next.js and React Native clients" — how did you keep three clients in sync?
@short
One package holding the types and the runtime validation schemas, imported by the server and by every client, so the contract is a compile-time dependency rather than a document. The schemas are the single source of truth: the server validates requests and responses against them, the clients infer their types from the same definitions, and a breaking change fails the typecheck in CI on every consumer rather than surfacing as a runtime error in the mobile app three weeks later.
@deep
The failure this solves is drift. Three clients against one API, with types hand-copied into each, means the moment the server renames a field the clients still compile and fail at runtime — and React Native is the worst case, because a shipped app cannot be fixed by a deploy. Deriving all three from one definition converts that class of bug into a build error.

The component library is a separate problem and the important thing is knowing where sharing stops. React and React Native do not share primitives — `div` and `View` are not interchangeable, styling is CSS versus a style object, and pretending otherwise produces an abstraction that is worse than duplication. So what is genuinely shared is the layer below the components: types, validation schemas, API client functions, formatting and currency helpers, and the design tokens. The components themselves are shared between React and Next.js, where the platform really is the same, and the React Native components are separate implementations against the same tokens and the same hooks.

Next.js adds one specific constraint: a shared component that uses hooks or event handlers cannot be a Server Component, so the package has to mark client entry points with `'use client'` and keep the pure helpers free of it. Getting that wrong turns the whole library into client-side JavaScript and quietly deletes the benefit of Server Components.
@code ts One schema, one contract, three clients that cannot drift
// packages/contracts/src/order.ts — the single source of truth
export const OrderSchema = z.object({
  id: z.string().uuid(),
  status: z.enum(['pending', 'paid', 'shipped', 'cancelled']),
  totalMinor: z.number().int(),          // minor units, never a float
  currency: z.string().length(3),
  createdAt: z.string().datetime(),
});
export type Order = z.infer<typeof OrderSchema>;      // types DERIVED, never written twice

export const ListOrdersQuery = z.object({
  status: OrderSchema.shape.status.optional(),
  cursor: z.string().optional(),
  limit: z.coerce.number().int().min(1).max(100).default(20),
});

// SERVER — the same schema is the validation middleware
router.get('/v1/orders', validate({ query: ListOrdersQuery }), async (req, res) => {
  // req.query is typed AND verified. Not asserted — verified.
});

// CLIENTS — React, Next.js and React Native import the identical package
const orders = await api.get('/v1/orders', { query: { status: 'paid' } });
// Rename `status` on the server -> tsc fails in all three clients in CI.
// Without this, all three still compile and the mobile app breaks in the field.

// WHAT IS SHARED, AND WHAT IS DELIBERATELY NOT
// packages/contracts   types + zod schemas + typed api client   -> all three
// packages/core        formatting, currency, date, hooks        -> all three
// packages/tokens      colour, spacing, type scale (plain JSON)  -> all three
// packages/ui-web      React components ('use client' where needed) -> React + Next
// packages/ui-native   React Native components, same tokens      -> RN only
//   ^ NOT one component library pretending div and View are the same thing.
@end
@senior
The judgement I would show is knowing what not to share. A shared component library across web and native is a classic over-abstraction: you spend months building a lowest-common-denominator API, every platform-specific requirement becomes a prop, and eventually both platforms are worse than if they had been written separately. Sharing the contract and the tokens gets most of the consistency benefit at a fraction of the cost. The second thing I would raise is versioning, because a shared package inside a monorepo is easy and a shared package consumed by a mobile app is not — an app in the store runs old code for as long as users decline to update, so the API has to stay compatible with contract versions that shipped months ago. That means additive changes only for anything a released app depends on, a minimum-supported-version check that can force an upgrade when it truly matters, and treating the mobile client as a permanently lagging consumer rather than one you can deploy.
@followup
How did you handle a breaking API change with an app already in the store?
@answer
By not making it breaking. The field is added alongside the old one, both are populated, and the old one is removed only once telemetry shows the versions that read it have fallen below a threshold — which for a mobile app can be a couple of quarters, not a sprint. When a change genuinely cannot be made additive, it goes behind a new API version and the old version keeps working, with a deprecation window measured in months. I also want a minimum-supported-version gate in the client from day one — a lightweight config call that can tell an old build to show an upgrade screen — because without it there is no mechanism at all to retire a version, and you discover that on the day you need one.
@red
- Hand-copied types in each client.
- One component library pretending web and native are the same platform.
- Treating a released mobile app as something you can deploy a fix to.
- Making a breaking change without a deprecation window.

### "Shipped React Native apps to the Google Play Store and Apple App Store" — what does that involve beyond writing the app?
@short
The engineering is signing, versioning, staged release and crash monitoring. Concretely: managed signing keys that are backed up because losing the Android upload key is unrecoverable, a build number that increments on every upload, a staged rollout starting at a small percentage with crash-free rate watched before it advances, and Sentry with source maps uploaded so a stack trace from a minified production bundle is readable.
@deep
The part that surprises people moving from web is that you cannot deploy a fix. A bad release is in users' hands until they update, and the App Store review adds a day or more to any correction, so the release process has to be conservative in a way web deploys do not need to be. That is what staged rollout and the crash-free-sessions metric are for — you expose 5% of users, watch, and halt if the number moves.

The second is that the store review process is a real engineering constraint, not paperwork. An app with background location has to justify the entitlement with a specific user-facing purpose string, and it is rejected if the justification is vague — which for an offline-first tracking app was the single most likely rejection reason. Both stores also require a privacy declaration that must match what the app actually collects.

Version discipline is the third. The user-facing version and the internal build number are different things: the build number must be unique and increasing per upload or the store rejects the binary outright, so it is derived from CI rather than edited by hand.
@code text The release pipeline, and the parts that are unrecoverable if you get them wrong
  SIGNING
    Android  upload key + Play App Signing. Google holds the app signing key;
             you hold the upload key. LOSE THE UPLOAD KEY -> account recovery
             with Google, and there is no self-service path. Backed up, encrypted,
             never in the repository.
    iOS      certificates + provisioning profiles, managed with fastlane match
             in a private repo so CI and every machine use the same identity.

  VERSIONING
    versionName / CFBundleShortVersionString  = user-facing  (1.4.0)
    versionCode / CFBundleVersion             = CI build number, ALWAYS
                                                increments, never reused

  RELEASE
    internal track -> closed testing -> staged rollout 5% -> 20% -> 50% -> 100%
    gate at each step: crash-free sessions >= 99.5%, ANR rate flat, no spike
    in the error rate on the API. Halt-and-hold, not halt-and-panic.

  OBSERVABILITY
    Sentry with SOURCE MAPS uploaded per build. Without them a production
    stack trace is minified line numbers and is worthless.
    Track crash-free SESSIONS and crash-free USERS separately: one bad screen
    can crash 100% of the users who reach it and still look fine by sessions.

  STORE REVIEW — the rejections you can predict
    background location  -> purpose string must name a concrete user benefit
    privacy declaration  -> must match what the SDKs actually collect
    account deletion     -> if there are accounts, in-app deletion is required
    permissions          -> anything requested and unused is a rejection

  THE POINT: there is no rollback. A bad build is fixed by shipping a new one
  and waiting for review, so the release process carries the risk instead.
@end
@senior
What I would emphasise is the operational asymmetry between web and mobile, because it changes engineering decisions upstream. On the web a bug is a deploy away from fixed; on mobile the broken version is permanent for some slice of users, so anything that could be wrong belongs behind a server-controlled flag rather than compiled into the binary. That single principle — put the decision on the server, put the mechanism in the app — is what makes a mobile release recoverable, and it is why the API has to keep supporting old clients essentially forever. I would also be honest that over-the-air update tooling covers only the JavaScript bundle: it is genuinely useful for a bad JavaScript-level bug, it does nothing for a native crash, and both stores have rules about what can be changed that way. Knowing where it stops is more useful than knowing that it exists.
@followup
How did you test on devices you did not have?
@answer
Internal and closed testing tracks first, so real users on real hardware hit the build before any public rollout, and a device-farm run across a matrix of Android versions and manufacturer skins for the smoke path — because Android background behaviour genuinely differs between OEMs and no emulator reproduces a manufacturer's battery optimiser. Beyond that, the substitute for a device you cannot hold is telemetry: device model, OS version, available memory and battery-optimisation state attached to every crash and to the sync-engine events, so a report of "it stops tracking on some phones" becomes a query that names the manufacturer. That is how the background-location bugs were actually found, and it is the answer I would give, because pretending you tested on forty devices is not credible.
@red
- No staged rollout.
- Sentry without source maps.
- Not knowing the difference between a version name and a build number.
- Assuming a bad release can be rolled back.
