### "Built a Golang backend on Clean Architecture — repository and service-layer separation with dependency injection" — why Go, and what did that architecture actually buy you?
@short
This is the self-initiated IoT analytics platform, self-hosted Postgres and Redis with no commercial users, and I say that before I am asked. I chose Go for the ingestion path deliberately: high-frequency telemetry is a concurrency problem, and goroutines with bounded worker pools plus a single static binary made the write path cheap and the deployment trivial. Clean Architecture bought me one thing I can demonstrate rather than assert — handlers, services and repositories are separated by interfaces, so the service logic is tested against an in-memory repository with no database running at all.
@deep
The dependency rule is the whole point: the service layer defines the interface it needs, and the Postgres repository implements it. Dependencies point inward, so nothing in the business logic imports GORM or Gin. Wiring happens once in `main`, which is the only place that knows a concrete database exists.

The monorepo splits backend, frontend and infrastructure, which matters more than it sounds: the Docker Compose file that brings up Postgres, Redis and the broker lives beside the code it runs, so the project is reproducible with one command rather than a README full of prerequisites.
@code text The layering, and where the dependency arrows point
  cmd/api/main.go        ← the ONLY place that knows Postgres exists
     │  builds: pgRepo := postgres.NewDeviceRepo(db)
     │          svc    := service.NewDeviceService(pgRepo, cache)
     │          h      := http.NewHandler(svc)
     ▼
  internal/http/         Gin handlers — decode, validate, call service, encode
     │                   knows NOTHING about SQL
     ▼
  internal/service/      business rules; defines the interface it needs:
     │                     type DeviceRepo interface {
     │                       Save(ctx, Reading) error
     │                       Latest(ctx, id) (Reading, error)
     │                     }
     ▼
  internal/repository/   GORM + Postgres implements DeviceRepo
                         swappable for an in-memory fake in tests

  RESULT   service tests run with an in-memory DeviceRepo — no container,
           no fixtures, no truncate-between-tests. Full suite in ~2s.
@end
@code text The ingest path — where Go actually earned its place
  device ──MQTT──► broker ──► subscriber goroutine
                                  │
                                  ▼
                         buffered channel (cap 10k)   ← backpressure lives here
                                  │
                    ┌─────────────┴─────────────┐
                    ▼                           ▼
              worker pool (N=GOMAXPROCS)   drop + count if full
                    │                      (a metric, never a silent loss)
                    ▼
         batched COPY / multi-row INSERT every 200 readings or 1s
                    │
                    ▼
         readings(device_id, ts, metric, value)
           PRIMARY KEY (device_id, ts, metric)
           BRIN index on ts          ← time-ordered inserts, tiny index
           partitioned BY RANGE (ts) ← old months dropped, not DELETEd

  WHY BATCHING   one INSERT per reading was the first version and it was
                 the bottleneck — per-statement round-trip and WAL overhead
                 dominated. Batching was ~20x on my hardware.
  CACHE          Redis holds "latest reading per device" — the dashboard
                 polls that, so the hot read never touches Postgres.
@end
@senior
The honest senior answer is that Clean Architecture is over-engineering for a small service, and I would not defend it as universally correct — I would defend the one property I actually got from it, which is that the business logic is testable without infrastructure. That is worth the indirection; the folder ceremony on its own is not. If I were doing it again at this size I would keep the interface boundary and flatten the directory structure.

The second point is the drop counter. A bounded channel that silently discards telemetry under load is a data-loss bug disguised as resilience, so the drop path increments a metric and alerts. Knowing you dropped readings is a different situation from discovering it a week later in a report.
@followup
Why not use Node, which you know better?
@answer
For most of this Node would have been fine, and I would say so rather than pretending Go was necessary. The specific reason was the fan-in write path: thousands of concurrent device connections with CPU-bound decoding is where Node's single-threaded event loop needs worker threads or clustering, and Go's scheduler handles it with goroutines as the default programming model rather than as an escape hatch. The static binary and the memory profile were secondary benefits. The honest framing is that I chose it partly to learn Go properly on a real workload, and it turned out to fit the problem.
@red
- Presenting a self-initiated project as production experience.
- Reciting Clean Architecture layers without naming a benefit you actually got.
- An unbounded channel, or a bounded one that drops silently.

### "Built the Node.js API layer for a voice-first AI assistant — streaming LLM responses, speech-to-text and TTS orchestration, server-side tool/function calling" — walk me through a single utterance.
@short
Again, self-initiated and no real users. A single utterance is a pipeline where latency is the product: audio streams up while the user is still speaking, partial transcripts come back continuously, and the moment the endpoint-detector says they have stopped, I send the final transcript to the model and start streaming tokens out. Time-to-first-audio is the metric that decides whether it feels like a conversation or a form submission, so TTS starts on the first complete clause rather than the complete answer.
@deep
The orchestration problem is that three streams run at different rates — audio in, tokens out, audio out — and the server is the only place that can coordinate them. Doing it client-side means the round trips serialise and the assistant feels slow.

Barge-in is the requirement that shapes the design: if the user starts speaking while the assistant is talking, everything in flight must be cancelled — the LLM request aborted, the TTS queue flushed, and playback stopped — otherwise the assistant talks over the user and the session is unusable.
@code text One utterance, with the latency budget that matters
  t=0     mic opens; audio chunks stream to server over WebSocket
          │  STT is streaming — partial transcripts arrive continuously
          │  (partials drive the on-screen text; they are NOT sent to the LLM)
          ▼
  t=~0    endpoint detected (silence + VAD) → FINAL transcript
          │
          ▼
  +120ms  LLM request opens with AbortController; tokens stream back
          │
          │  first COMPLETE CLAUSE (~", " or sentence end) ──┐
          ▼                                                  ▼
  +380ms  ── TIME TO FIRST AUDIO ──               TTS synthesises clause 1
          │                                        while clause 2 is still
          │                                        being generated
          ▼
          audio chunks stream to client, played in order

  BARGE-IN  user speaks mid-answer →
              abortController.abort()   (stop token generation, stop billing)
              ttsQueue.clear()          (drop unsynthesised clauses)
              client stops playback     (drop buffered audio)
            all three, or the assistant talks over the user.

  DISCONNECT  client socket closes → same abort path. Without it you pay
              for tokens nobody will ever hear.
@end
@senior
The design decision I would defend hardest is sentence-level TTS chunking. Waiting for the full LLM answer before synthesising is the obvious implementation and it roughly triples perceived latency; chunking on clause boundaries overlaps generation with synthesis and is the single biggest win in the pipeline. The cost is prosody — the speech engine cannot apply sentence-level intonation it has not seen yet — so it is a genuine trade, not a free optimisation, and I would say that rather than pretending it is strictly better.

The second is that partial transcripts must never reach the model. They change as the recogniser revises, so acting on them means acting on words the user did not say — and if a tool call fires from a partial, you have taken a real action on a misheard instruction.
@followup
How do you keep the cost of this bounded?
@answer
Aborting on disconnect and on barge-in, which is the largest saving because abandoned utterances are common in voice. Then a hard output-token cap, because a runaway answer is both expensive and unlistenable. Then choosing the model per turn rather than globally — a short conversational reply does not need the largest model, and routing simple turns to a cheaper one cut my costs substantially with no quality complaint on my own test set. I also cap conversation history by tokens rather than by message count, since one long tool result can otherwise blow the context budget on its own.
@red
- Sending partial transcripts to the model.
- No abort path on disconnect or barge-in.
- Waiting for the complete answer before starting TTS.

### "Secured the API with JWT refresh-token rotation and Redis rate limiting" — what does rotation actually protect against, and how do you detect theft?
@short
Rotation turns a stolen refresh token from permanent access into a detectable event. Every refresh issues a new refresh token and invalidates the old one, so if a token is ever presented twice, either the legitimate client or the attacker is using a token that has already been spent — and that reuse is the signal. On detection I revoke the entire token family, which logs out the attacker and the user together, deliberately, because a forced re-login is a far better outcome than silent ongoing access.
@deep
The mechanism needs a family identifier. Each refresh token carries the id of its lineage, and the server stores the currently valid token per family. Reuse of a superseded token means the lineage is compromised, and the whole family is killed rather than just that token.

Access tokens stay short-lived and stateless — fifteen minutes — because checking a denylist on every request would put Redis in the path of every call. The refresh endpoint is where state lives, and it is called rarely, so the cost is acceptable there.
@code text Rotation, reuse detection, and what the limiter actually protects
  REFRESH FLOW
    client → POST /auth/refresh  { rt_7 }
    server:  lookup family F of rt_7
             is rt_7 the CURRENT token of F?
               yes → issue at_new (15m) + rt_8; current(F) = rt_8; store used(rt_7)
               no  → rt_7 was already spent  ══► REUSE DETECTED
                       revoke ENTIRE family F
                       force re-login, alert, log device + IP

  WHY IT WORKS
    attacker steals rt_7 and uses it first  → user's next refresh fails → detected
    user refreshes first, attacker uses rt_7 → server sees spent token → detected
    either way, the theft surfaces on the NEXT refresh, not months later

  STORAGE   Redis: rt:<jti> → {family, used}   TTL = refresh lifetime
            revocation is per family, so it is one key delete, not a scan

  RATE LIMITING — different limits for different failure modes
    /auth/login    5 / 15min per (IP + account)   ← credential stuffing
    /auth/refresh  10 / min per family            ← token abuse
    LLM endpoints  per-user token budget, not request count
                   (one request can cost 1000x another)
    general API    sliding-window per user; 429 + Retry-After

  ALGORITHM  sliding-window counter in Redis, not fixed-window — a fixed
             window lets 2x the limit through across a boundary.
             Increment and expiry in one Lua script so it is atomic.
@end
@senior
The trade-off worth naming is the race between rotation and concurrent requests. A client that fires three requests at once on an expired access token will attempt three refreshes, two of which present a token that is about to be superseded — and a naive implementation reads that as theft and logs the user out. The fix is a short grace window where the immediately-previous token is accepted without rotating again, plus a single-flight refresh on the client so only one refresh is ever in progress. I have seen this exact bug reported as "the app randomly logs me out", and it is the reason rotation is harder than it looks on a diagram.

On rate limiting, the point I would make is that limiting LLM endpoints by request count is meaningless, because one request can cost a thousand times another. Budgeting tokens rather than calls is what actually protects the bill.
@followup
Where do you store the refresh token on the client, and why?
@answer
An httpOnly, Secure, SameSite cookie scoped to the refresh path, so JavaScript cannot read it and XSS cannot exfiltrate it directly. Local storage is readable by any script on the page, which makes a single XSS a permanent account takeover. The cookie scoping matters — it is sent only to the refresh endpoint, not attached to every API call — and because it is a cookie I need CSRF protection on that endpoint, which SameSite plus a double-submit token handles. On mobile it goes in the platform keystore, Keychain or EncryptedSharedPreferences, not AsyncStorage.
@red
- Rotation without reuse detection — that is just a shorter-lived token.
- Revoking only the reused token instead of the family.
- Refresh tokens in local storage.
- Fixed-window rate limiting presented as equivalent to sliding-window.

### "Wrote end-to-end Playwright tests for multi-user editing, reconnection and conflict resolution" — how do you test a system whose whole point is concurrency?
@short
With multiple independent browser contexts in one test, driven deterministically. Playwright gives each user a separate context with its own storage and its own socket, so two real clients edit the same document in the same test. The hard part is not opening two browsers — it is asserting convergence without sleeping, so every assertion polls until both documents agree rather than waiting a fixed time and hoping.
@deep
Network conditions are the interesting cases and they have to be simulated deliberately: CDP lets me take a client offline mid-edit, let it accumulate local changes, then bring it back and assert that both sides converge on the same text.

The assertion that matters is not "user B sees user A's text" — it is that both users' documents are byte-identical after the dust settles, in both directions, including the case where the edits overlapped at the same position.
@code text Two real clients, one document, no sleeps
  test('offline edits converge on reconnect', async ({ browser }) => {
    const a = await browser.newContext();   // separate storage, separate socket
    const b = await browser.newContext();
    const pa = await a.newPage(), pb = await b.newPage();

    await pa.goto(DOC); await pb.goto(DOC);
    await expect(pa.locator('.peer-count')).toHaveText('2');   // real barrier

    await pa.locator('#editor').type('Hello ');
    await expect(pb.locator('#editor')).toHaveText('Hello ');  // A → B

    await a.setOffline(true);                 // ── partition ──
    await pa.locator('#editor').type('from A');   // queued locally
    await pb.locator('#editor').type('from B');   // committed by server

    await a.setOffline(false);                // ── heal ──

    await expect.poll(async () =>             // convergence, not equality-now
      await pa.locator('#editor').innerText()
      === await pb.locator('#editor').innerText(),
      { timeout: 10_000 }
    ).toBe(true);
  });

  THE RULES THAT KEEP IT NON-FLAKY
   ✔ never waitForTimeout — poll on the condition itself
   ✔ barrier on peer-count before editing (both sockets actually joined)
   ✔ assert CONVERGENCE (a === b), never a hard-coded expected string —
     with concurrent inserts at one position, both orderings are CORRECT
   ✔ one document id per test, generated per run → tests are parallel-safe
   ✘ asserting "Hello from Afrom B" — that bakes in one valid interleaving
@end
@senior
The insight I would lead with is that the correctness property of a CRDT system is convergence, not a specific output — so a test asserting an exact final string is asserting something the system never promised, and it will fail intermittently the moment timing shifts. That distinction is what makes these tests stable, and getting it wrong is why most people conclude real-time systems "cannot be tested reliably".

The second is scope. End-to-end tests are slow and I keep few of them: the merge semantics themselves are unit-tested against the CRDT directly, where I can drive thousands of randomised concurrent operation sequences in milliseconds and assert convergence. Playwright covers the wiring — sockets, auth, reconnection, presence — which is exactly the layer unit tests cannot reach.
@followup
How do you keep these from being flaky in CI?
@answer
By removing every implicit wait. Explicit barriers on observable state rather than sleeps, polling assertions with a generous timeout, a unique document per test so runs never share state, and the whole stack — app, Postgres, Redis — brought up by Docker Compose in CI so timing is consistent. When one does flake, I treat it as a real bug until proven otherwise, because in a distributed system an intermittent test failure is usually an intermittent product failure that the user will eventually hit. Retries hide exactly the class of bug these tests exist to find, so I record them rather than silently passing on the second attempt.
@red
- A single browser context with two tabs — shared storage, not two real users.
- waitForTimeout as the synchronisation mechanism.
- Asserting one specific interleaving as the correct merge result.
- Auto-retrying flaky real-time tests until green.

### Your degree is a BBA, not computer science. How did you get here, and what are the gaps?
@short
I came to engineering through building things, and I closed the theory gap deliberately rather than hoping it would not come up. The practical answer is three years of production work — REST APIs, relational schema design, payments, containerised deployment — plus self-directed study of the fundamentals a CS degree gives you: data structures and complexity, operating-system concepts like scheduling and memory, networking from TCP up, and database internals. I would rather you test me on those than take my word for it.
@deep
The honest framing is that the degree is not the qualification; it is a proxy for the qualification, and I can be assessed directly. What a CS degree reliably provides is vocabulary and the theory underneath — complexity analysis, concurrency primitives, how a database actually stores and finds a row, how memory and scheduling work. Those matter, and I have deliberately learned them because not knowing them shows up quickly in senior interviews and in production: you cannot reason about an index without understanding B-trees, or about the event loop without understanding scheduling and I/O.

What a business degree gave me that I do use is not decorative. I read a requirement and ask what it costs and who it is for before I ask how to build it, I am comfortable arguing an engineering trade-off in terms a product owner can act on, and I write and present clearly — which is most of what a senior engineer's day actually consists of.

The thing I would not do is be defensive about it. A candidate who is uncomfortable with this question invites more of it; a candidate who answers it in thirty seconds with evidence and moves on ends it.
@code text How I would close the question, concretely
  THE ANSWER SHAPE
    1. Where I actually came from, in one sentence — no apology.
    2. The gap, named honestly, and what I did about it — specifically.
    3. An invitation to test it, which only a confident answer can offer.
    4. What the non-CS background gives me that is genuinely useful.

  EVIDENCE I CAN POINT AT, NOT CLAIM
    complexity/DSA    "the N+1 fix was recognising an O(n x m) loop —
                       I build the index map instead, that is the habit"
    OS / scheduling   event loop phases, libuv thread pool, why a CPU-bound
                       handler blocks every other request on that process
    networking        TCP handshake, TLS, keep-alive, why HTTP/2 changed
                       bundling advice, head-of-line blocking
    DB internals      B+tree structure, why a covering index avoids the
                       row lookup, MVCC, isolation levels and what each permits
    distributed       CAP as a trade you make per-operation, idempotency,
                       at-least-once delivery, the reasons exactly-once is a lie

  WHAT I DO NOT SAY
    "the degree does not matter"      -> dismissive, and untrue in their eyes
    "I am basically self-taught"      -> undersells three years of production work
    a long story about switching      -> the question deserves 30 seconds
@end
@senior
The senior version of this answer inverts the question. A hiring manager asking it is not checking a credential — they are checking whether there is a hole in the fundamentals that will surface at staff level, and whether I am self-aware about my own gaps. So I answer the real question: here is what I know because I needed it in production, here is what I studied deliberately because I would not otherwise have met it, and here is where I am still weaker than someone who has done it for a decade. Naming a genuine remaining gap — for me, formal distributed-systems theory beyond the practical patterns, and low-level performance work like profiling native code — is what makes the rest of the answer believable. Every candidate has gaps; the difference between mid and senior is knowing which ones you have. And I would let three years of shipped, running, operated systems do most of the arguing, because a degree predicts an entry-level hire and production track record predicts everything after that.
@followup
How do you keep learning, given you did not learn this formally?
@answer
Deliberately and with a bias toward building, because reading about a distributed system and operating one produce different knowledge. Concretely, the self-initiated projects on my CV are how I learn the things my day job does not reach — Golang and Clean Architecture in one, RAG and vector retrieval in another, CRDTs and WebSocket fan-out in the third — and each of those exists because I wanted to understand something properly rather than have an opinion about it. Alongside that I read primary sources over blog posts: the Postgres and MySQL documentation, the Node and libuv internals, papers when a topic warrants it. And the most reliable teacher has been production incidents — the things I understand best are the ones that broke at an inconvenient hour and made me read the manual properly.
@red

### You have three years of experience and you are interviewing for a senior role. Why should we consider you at that level?
@short
Because level is about scope of ownership and quality of judgement, not time served — and I would rather be measured on that than argue about the number. @deep
The strongest form of this answer is specific and unbothered. Three years at a company where I owned systems outright is a different three years from three years on a large team writing tickets within someone else's architecture — and that is the actual argument, not the number. Payments in particular is a domain where the responsibility is real: money moves, and getting idempotency or reconciliation wrong is not a bug report, it is a customer who was charged twice.

The mistake candidates make is either apologising for the number or bluffing past it. Both lose. The interviewer already read the dates; the question is whether I can locate myself accurately, because a candidate who over-claims their level is a hiring risk regardless of ability.

I would also make the practical point that I am not asking to be paid for a title. I am asking to be assessed on the work, and if the outcome is that I am strong mid rather than senior, that is a fair result from a fair process — which is a much more persuasive position than insisting on a label.

@senior
The senior behaviour here is calibration, and the question is really testing for it. So the answer that works names the specific dimensions where I do operate at a senior level — ownership through production, judgement about what not to build, systems whose failure modes I can enumerate from memory because I have handled them — and names the dimensions where I do not, without hedging the whole thing into mush. I would also reframe the risk from their side, because that is what they are actually weighing: the risk in hiring me is scale exposure and team leadership, and both are addressable in a role with senior engineers around me. The risk in hiring an over-claiming candidate is discovering the gap after they have designed something. Making that comparison explicitly is a more useful answer than a list of my achievements.

I would expect to be genuinely useful within weeks on anything API, database or payments related because that is where my experience is concentrated, and to need longer before I would want to be the one making an architectural decision that is expensive to reverse. Saying that plainly is not a weakness in the answer — it is the answer.

### Your strongest projects are self-initiated with no users. Why should I weigh them?
@short
Weigh them for what they actually demonstrate and discount them for what they do not — and I will do that discounting myself before you have to. What they show is that I can design and build a Clean Architecture Go service, a CRDT-based collaboration server and a RAG pipeline from nothing, which is real engineering evidence. What they cannot show is behaviour under production traffic, real users doing unexpected things, or a system I have operated for two years and watched decay. My commercial work is where that evidence is, and I keep the two categories clearly separated on my CV for exactly that reason.
@deep
The distinction I hold to is between engineering skill and operational experience. A side project proves the first: the code exists, the architecture is a choice I made and can defend, and every trade-off in it was mine. It cannot prove the second, because nothing has ever been at 3am under load with a customer on the phone.

So the framing that works is to be the one who says this. The label on my CV — self-initiated, self-hosted, no commercial users — is deliberate, and stating it before being asked converts a potential credibility problem into a credibility signal. An interviewer who discovers that a "platform" had no users is now doubting everything else on the page; an interviewer who was told upfront is not.

What genuinely raises a project's evidentiary value is being able to discuss its limits as sharply as its features. I know what my WebSocket server does at ten thousand connections because I load-tested it, and I know which numbers I am inferring rather than measuring. I know the RAG numbers come from a 120-question golden set I built, not from user feedback. Being precise about the provenance of every number is the whole game.
@code text How I present a project so it survives scrutiny
  SAY THIS FIRST, UNPROMPTED

     take from them is the design work; the operational experience is from
     my day job."
